Part: Graphs (83)

1. Graphs (83)

no-f-001 · level facile · notes
An undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ has $7$ vertices, with degrees $\deg(v)$ (number of edges incident to $v$) equal to $3,3,2,2,2,1,1$. How many edges does $G$ have?
  1. $7$
  2. $14$
  3. $6$
  4. $8$
Explanation. By the handshake lemma $\sum_{v \in V}\deg(v)=2|E|$, since every edge counts in two degrees: the sum is $14$, so $|E|=7$. $14$ is the sum of the degrees, not the number of edges.
no-f-002 · level facile · notes
Let $G=(N,A)$ be a directed graph and $\mat{B}$ its node–arc incidence matrix (one row per node, one column per arc), with the convention of the course. What does the column of the arc $(4,3)$, with tail $4$ and head $3$, look like?
  1. $-1$ in row $4$, $+1$ in row $3$, $0$ elsewhere
  2. $+1$ in rows $3$ and $4$, $0$ elsewhere
  3. $+1$ in row $4$, $-1$ in row $3$, $0$ elsewhere
  4. $+1$ in row $4$ and in column $3$, $0$ elsewhere
Explanation. In the course the column of an arc has $+1$ in the row of the tail and $-1$ in the row of the head: $+1$ in row $4$, $-1$ in row $3$. The last option describes the adjacency matrix, which is $|N| \times |N|$.
no-f-003 · level facile · notes
A directed graph $G=(N,A)$ has $|N|=8$ nodes and $|A|=11$ arcs, and its underlying undirected graph (the arcs taken as edges) has $k=3$ connected components. What is the rank of the node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$?
  1. $8$
  2. $5$
  3. $7$
  4. $8$ minus the number of cycles
Explanation. $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|-k=8-3=5$. The rank is never $|N|$, since the rows of each component add up to zero; $7=|N|-1$ holds only for a connected graph.
no-f-004 · level facile · notes
Let $G$ be an undirected graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges. A tree is a connected acyclic graph. Which condition is not enough to say that $G$ is a tree?
  1. $G$ is connected and $m=n-1$
  2. $G$ is acyclic and $m=n-1$
  3. $G$ is connected and acyclic
  4. $G$ has exactly $m=n-1$ edges
Explanation. Any two of «connected», «acyclic» and «$n-1$ edges» imply the third; $m=n-1$ alone does not: a triangle plus an isolated vertex has $4$ vertices and $3$ edges, but it is not a tree.
no-f-005 · level facile · notes
An acyclic undirected graph (a forest) has $n=10$ vertices and $m=7$ edges. How many connected components $k$ does it have?
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $7$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Every graph has $k \ge n-m$ components, with equality if and only if it is acyclic (each component is a tree with one vertex more than its edges): $k=10-7=3$.
no-f-006 · level facile · notes
In the generic search of a directed graph from a node $s$, every labelled node $j \neq s$ gets a predecessor $\mathit{pred}(j)$. Which search gives, in the predecessor tree, paths from $s$ with the minimum number of arcs to every reachable node?
  1. The depth-first search (DFS), with $\mathit{LIST}$ handled as a stack
  2. The breadth-first search (BFS), with $\mathit{LIST}$ handled as a queue
  3. Both searches, as long as the adjacency lists are sorted
  4. Neither of them, since the lengths of the arcs are needed
Explanation. In the BFS $\mathit{LIST}$ is a queue and the number of arcs of the tree path, $d(j)=d(\mathit{pred}(j))+1$, is the minimum possible. The DFS goes deep first and can give longer paths, whatever the order of the lists.
no-f-007 · level facile · notes
With the graph stored in adjacency lists, what is the running time of the generic search (from a node $s$) of a directed graph $G=(N,A)$?
  1. $O(|N|\,|A|)$
  2. $O(|N|^2)$
  3. $O(|N|+|A|)$
  4. $O(|A|\log|N|)$
Explanation. Every node is labelled and removed from the list of nodes to be examined $\mathit{LIST}$ at most once, and the pointer of the adjacency list $L(i)$ moves at most $\deg^+(i)$ times (the arcs leaving $i$): $O(|N|+|A|)$ in total.
no-f-008 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph $G=(N,A)$, let $R(i)$ be the set of the nodes reachable from $i$ by a directed path and $R'(i)$ the set of the nodes from which $i$ is reachable. What is the strongly connected component of $i$?
  1. $R(i) \cup R'(i)$
  2. $R(i)$
  3. $N \setminus R'(i)$
  4. $R(i) \cap R'(i)$
Explanation. $j$ is strongly connected to $i$ if $j \in R(i)$ and $i \in R(j)$, that is $j \in R'(i)$: the component is $R(i) \cap R'(i)$.
no-f-009 · level facile · notes
Let $T$ be a spanning tree of a connected undirected graph $G$, and $e$ an edge of $G$ that is not in $T$. How many cycles does the graph $T \cup \{e\}$ contain?
  1. Exactly one
  2. None
  3. At least two
  4. As many as the edges of $T$
Explanation. $T \cup \{e\}$ contains exactly one cycle, the fundamental cycle $C(T,e)$: the edge $e$ plus the unique path in $T$ between its endpoints.
no-f-010 · level facile · notes
How much space do the adjacency lists (for every node, the list of the heads of its leaving arcs) of a directed graph with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs take?
  1. $O(n^2)$
  2. $O(n+m)$
  3. $O(n\,m)$
  4. $O(m^2)$
Explanation. The lists store one entry per arc plus the start of every list: $n+m$. The adjacency matrix takes $n^2$ and the incidence matrix $n\,m$.
no-f-011 · level media · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,1)$, $(3,4)$, $(4,5)$, $(5,4)$, $(5,6)$. Two nodes $i,j$ are strongly connected if there are directed paths from $i$ to $j$ and from $j$ to $i$. How many strongly connected components does the graph have?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $3$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The components are $\{1,2,3\}$ (cycle $1,2,3,1$), $\{4,5\}$ (cycle $4,5,4$) and $\{6\}$: from $4$ there is no way back to $3$, and no arc leaves $6$. The graph is weakly connected, which gives $1$ connected component, not strongly connected.
no-f-012 · level teorema · notes
Let $G=(N,A)$ be a directed graph whose underlying undirected graph has $k$ connected components, and $\mat{B}$ its node–arc incidence matrix. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|$ if and only if $G$ is connected
  2. $\rank(\mat{B})=|A|-k$ for every directed graph
  3. $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|-k$ for every directed graph
  4. $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|-1$ for every directed graph
Explanation. This is the theorem on the rank of the incidence matrix: $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|-k$. The rows of each component add up to zero, so the rank is never $|N|$; $|N|-1$ holds only for $k=1$.
no-f-013 · level teorema · notes
Let $G=(N,A)$ be a directed graph with connected underlying undirected graph, and $\mat{B}$ its node–arc incidence matrix. Which statement on the columns of $\mat{B}$ is correct?
  1. $|N|-1$ columns are independent if and only if their arcs form a spanning tree
  2. $|N|-1$ columns are independent if and only if their arcs form a directed path
  3. Any $|N|-1$ columns of $\mat{B}$ are always linearly independent
  4. $\mat{B}$ has full row rank, that is, rank equal to $|N|$
Explanation. Corollary «bases of the incidence matrix»: in a connected graph the bases of $\mat{B}$ are exactly the spanning trees (they need not be directed); the rank is $|N|-1$ and one row is redundant.
no-f-014 · level teorema · notes
A leaf of a tree is a vertex of degree $1$. Which statement on the leaves of a tree with $n$ vertices is correct?
  1. Every tree with $n \ge 2$ vertices has exactly two leaves
  2. Every tree with $n \ge 2$ vertices has at least two leaves
  3. Every tree with $n \ge 3$ vertices has at least three leaves
  4. A tree with $n \ge 2$ vertices has leaves only if $n$ is even
Explanation. The endpoints of a path of maximum length have degree $1$: every tree with at least $2$ vertices has at least two leaves. A star with $n$ vertices has $n-1$ of them, a path exactly two.
no-f-015 · level ipotesi · notes
Let $G=(N,A)$ be a directed graph with $k$ connected components (of the underlying undirected graph) and incidence matrix $\mat{B}$. Which results are used to prove $\rank(\mat{B}) \ge |N|-k$?
  1. The handshake lemma and the total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$
  2. The lemma «columns of a cycle» and the correctness of the search
  3. Bellman's equations and the lemma «leaves» on trees
  4. A spanning forest with $|N|-k$ arcs and the lemma «columns of a forest»
Explanation. A spanning forest has $|N|-k$ arcs and the columns of the arcs of a forest are linearly independent: the rank is at least $|N|-k$. The columns of a cycle are used for the other direction of the corollary on bases, not for this inequality.
no-f-016 · level ipotesi · notes
In the generic search from $s$ every labelled node $j \neq s$ has a predecessor $\mathit{pred}(j)$. To prove that the arcs $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ form a tree on the labelled nodes, what is used?
  1. $\mathit{pred}(j)$ is labelled before $j$; a connected graph with $r$ nodes and $r-1$ arcs is a tree
  2. The BFS labels the nodes in non-decreasing order of $d$; the cut property
  3. The rank theorem for $\mat{B}$; the fact that $\mathit{LIST}$ is handled as a queue
  4. The adjacency lists are sorted; every tree with at least two nodes has two leaves
Explanation. Following the predecessors (always labelled earlier) one reaches $s$, so the $r-1$ arcs connect the $r$ labelled nodes and, by the characterization of trees, they form a tree. It holds for every search, not only for the BFS.
no-f-017 · level ipotesi · notes
The generic search from $s$ removes a node from the list $\mathit{LIST}$ when it has no admissible arcs left (arcs towards unlabelled nodes). In the proof that it labels all the nodes reachable from $s$, which fact gives the contradiction?
  1. The breadth-first search finds the paths with the minimum number of arcs
  2. The adjacency lists are sorted by increasing index of the head
  3. A node leaves $\mathit{LIST}$ only without admissible arcs, and labels stay
  4. The underlying undirected graph of the directed graph is connected
Explanation. If $i_t$ is the first unlabelled node of a path from $s$, the arc $(i_{t-1},i_t)$ stays admissible until the end, but $i_{t-1}$ has left $\mathit{LIST}$: a contradiction. No assumption on BFS, list order or connectivity is needed.
no-f-018 · level facile · notes
We traverse a cycle of the underlying undirected graph of a directed graph (arcs may be crossed in both directions). When is the arc $(i,j)$, with tail $i$ and head $j$, a backward arc of the cycle?
  1. When $i>j$
  2. When the cycle traverses it from the head $j$ to the tail $i$
  3. When its cost is negative
  4. When it is the last arc of the cycle
Explanation. An arc is forward if it is traversed from tail to head, backward otherwise: it depends on the direction of traversal, not on the indices or the costs.
no-f-019 · level facile · notes
An undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ has $k=4$ connected components. An edge $\{u,v\} \notin E$ is added, with $u$ and $v$ in the same component. What happens?
  1. The components become $3$ and no cycle appears
  2. The components become $5$
  3. The components stay $4$ and a cycle through $\{u,v\}$ appears
  4. The components stay $4$ and no cycle appears
Explanation. Lemma «adding an edge»: with $u,v$ in the same component, a path from $u$ to $v$ already exists and closes a cycle with $\{u,v\}$; the components do not change. Between different components, instead, $k$ drops by one and no cycle appears.
no-f-020 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph with node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$, the arcs $C$ form a cycle of the underlying undirected graph. What can be said about the columns $\mat{B}_a$, $a \in C$?
  1. They are linearly dependent: with sign $+1$ on forward arcs and $-1$ on backward arcs they add up to $\vet{0}$
  2. They are linearly independent, since every column has one $+1$ and one $-1$
  3. They are dependent only if the cycle is directed, that is, with all arcs forward
  4. They are dependent only if the cycle has an even number of arcs
Explanation. Lemma «columns of a cycle»: $\varepsilon_t\mat{B}_{c_t}=\vet{e}_{v_{t-1}}-\vet{e}_{v_t}$ and the sum along the cycle telescopes to zero. It holds for every cycle, directed or not, of any length.
no-f-021 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph, a directed walk may repeat nodes, a directed path may not. The walk $1,3,2,4,3,5$ goes from $1$ to $5$. Which directed path from $1$ to $5$ is obtained by deleting the closed piece?
  1. $1,3,2,4,5$
  2. $1,2,4,3,5$
  3. $1,3,2,4,3$
  4. $1,3,5$
Explanation. Lemma «a walk contains a path»: node $3$ repeats, and deleting the closed piece $3,2,4,3$ leaves $1,3,5$, which uses only arcs of the walk. The other sequences use arcs the walk does not contain or do not end at $5$.
no-f-022 · level teorema · notes
In a directed graph, two nodes $i,j$ are strongly connected if there are directed paths from $i$ to $j$ and from $j$ to $i$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The relation is symmetric but not transitive, so the components may overlap
  2. The strongly connected components coincide with those of the underlying undirected graph
  3. It is an equivalence relation, so the strongly connected components partition the nodes
  4. It is an equivalence relation only if the graph is acyclic
Explanation. The relation is reflexive (path of length $0$), symmetric by definition and transitive (two paths in a row give a walk, which contains a path): the classes partition $N$. In an acyclic graph every class is a single node.
no-f-212 · level facile · notes
In an undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ every edge is a subset $\{u,v\}$ of two distinct vertices. With $n=|V|$, how many edges can $G$ have at most?
  1. $n^2$
  2. $n(n-1)$
  3. $n(n-1)/2$
  4. $n-1$
Explanation. The edges are the unordered pairs of distinct vertices: $\binom{n}{2}=n(n-1)/2$. $n(n-1)$ counts the ordered pairs, that is, the arcs of a simple directed graph; $n-1$ is the number of edges of a tree.
no-f-213 · level facile · notes
A directed graph $G=(N,A)$ has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(3,2)$, $(2,4)$, $(4,3)$. The outgoing star $\delta^+(i)$ contains the arcs with tail $i$, the incoming star $\delta^-(i)$ those with head $i$. What are $\deg^+(3)=|\delta^+(3)|$ and $\deg^-(3)=|\delta^-(3)|$?
  1. $\deg^+(3)=2$, $\deg^-(3)=1$
  2. $\deg^+(3)=1$, $\deg^-(3)=2$
  3. $\deg^+(3)=3$, $\deg^-(3)=0$
  4. $\deg^+(3)=1$, $\deg^-(3)=1$
Explanation. Only $(3,2)$ leaves node $3$, so $\deg^+(3)=1$; $(1,3)$ and $(4,3)$ enter it, so $\deg^-(3)=2$. Swapping tail and head gives the first option; $3$ is the degree in the underlying undirected graph.
no-f-214 · level teorema · notes
Let $G=(V,E)$ be an undirected graph with $m$ edges and degrees $\deg(v)$ (number of edges incident to $v$). Which statement is correct?
  1. $\sum_{v \in V}\deg(v)=m$, since every edge is counted once
  2. The number of vertices of even degree is always even
  3. $\sum_{v \in V}\deg(v)=2m$ only if $G$ is connected
  4. $\sum_{v \in V}\deg(v)=2m$, so the number of vertices of odd degree is even
Explanation. Every edge $\{u,v\}$ counts once in $\deg(u)$ and once in $\deg(v)$: the sum is $2m$ for every graph, connected or not. An even sum forces the odd degrees to be an even number; there is no constraint on the even degrees.
no-f-215 · level facile · notes
In an undirected graph a walk $v_0,v_1,\dots,v_k$ may repeat vertices, a path has distinct vertices, a cycle is a closed walk with $k \ge 3$ and $v_0,\dots,v_{k-1}$ distinct. In the graph with edges $\{1,2\},\{2,3\},\{3,1\},\{3,4\}$, what is the sequence $1,2,3,1$?
  1. A cycle of length $3$
  2. A path of length $3$
  3. A walk that is not closed
  4. A cycle of length $4$
Explanation. The sequence returns to $1$ after $k=3$ edges and $1,2,3$ are distinct: it is a cycle of length $3$. It is not a path, since $1$ appears twice; the length is the number of edges, not of written vertices.
no-f-216 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: if a graph has a walk from $u$ to $v$ (vertices may repeat), it also has a path from $u$ to $v$ (distinct vertices) with vertices of the walk. The proof chooses a walk from $u$ to $v$ of minimum length. Which argument concludes it?
  1. Every walk is already a path, since the graph is simple
  2. The graph is connected, so a path between two vertices always exists
  3. If a vertex appeared twice, removing the closed piece between its two occurrences would give a shorter walk
  4. The shortest walk is a tree, and a tree has a unique path
Explanation. In a walk of minimum length no vertex repeats: otherwise the closed piece between the two occurrences is removed and a shorter walk with the same endpoints is obtained. Connectivity of the graph is not needed, and a walk with repetitions is not a path even in a simple graph.
no-f-217 · level facile · notes
A graph is simple if it has no loops (edges $\{v,v\}$ or arcs $(i,i)$) and no parallel edges or arcs (two copies of the same pair). Which graph is not simple?
  1. A directed graph with the arcs $(1,2)$ and $(2,1)$
  2. An undirected graph with two copies of the edge $\{1,2\}$
  3. An undirected graph with an isolated vertex
  4. A directed graph without arcs
Explanation. Two copies of $\{1,2\}$ are parallel edges: the graph is a multigraph. The arcs $(1,2)$ and $(2,1)$ are different ordered pairs, so they are not parallel; isolated vertices and the absence of arcs do not matter.
no-f-218 · level facile · notes
In the network optimization module all directed graphs are simple (no loops, no parallel arcs). Which consequence of this convention do the notes use?
  1. Every node has at least one outgoing arc
  2. The number of arcs is at most $n-1$
  3. The underlying undirected graph is a tree
  4. An arc is identified by the pair $(i,j)$ of tail and head, and one can write $x_{ij}$, $c_{ij}$
Explanation. Without parallel arcs there is at most one arc for each ordered pair $(i,j)$, so the data of an arc are indexed by $ij$. Simplicity says nothing about outgoing arcs (beyond the bound $n(n-1)$) nor about the structure of the graph.
no-f-219 · level facile · notes
A simple directed graph (no loops $(i,i)$, no parallel arcs) has $n$ nodes. How many arcs can it have at most?
  1. $n(n-1)$
  2. $n(n-1)/2$
  3. $n^2$
  4. $2n$
Explanation. Every ordered pair $(i,j)$ with $i \neq j$ can be an arc, once: $n(n-1)$ arcs. $n(n-1)/2$ is the maximum for an undirected graph, $n^2$ would also count the loops.
no-f-220 · level facile · notes
The adjacency matrix $\mat{M}$ of a directed graph $G=(N,A)$ with $n$ nodes has $m_{ij}=1$ if $(i,j) \in A$ and $0$ otherwise. What does the sum of the entries of row $i$ of $\mat{M}$ count?
  1. The in-degree $\deg^-(i)$
  2. The number of nodes reachable from $i$
  3. The out-degree $\deg^+(i)$
  4. The number of arcs of the graph
Explanation. Row $i$ has a $1$ for every arc $(i,j)$ leaving $i$: the sum is $\deg^+(i)$. The in-degree is the sum of column $i$; reachable nodes need paths, not just arcs.
no-f-221 · level facile · notes
The adjacency lists of a directed graph store, for every node $i$, the list $L(i)$ of the heads of the arcs leaving $i$, sorted by increasing index. The graph has the arcs $(1,3)$, $(2,1)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,2)$, $(3,4)$. What is $L(1)$?
  1. $L(1)=(2)$
  2. $L(1)=(2,3)$
  3. $L(1)=(3,2)$
  4. $L(1)=(2,3,2)$
Explanation. The arcs leaving $1$ are $(1,3)$ and $(1,2)$; the sorted heads give $L(1)=(2,3)$. $(2)$ lists instead the nodes entering $1$; $(3,2)$ is not sorted.
no-f-222 · level facile · notes
Four representations of a directed graph with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs are compared: adjacency matrix, node–arc incidence matrix, arc list, adjacency lists. How many entries does the node–arc incidence matrix store?
  1. $n^2$
  2. $2m$
  3. $n+m$
  4. $n\,m$
Explanation. The incidence matrix has one row per node and one column per arc: $n\,m$ entries. $n^2$ is the adjacency matrix, $2m$ the arc list (tail and head), $n+m$ the adjacency lists.
no-f-223 · level facile · notes
A directed graph with $n$ nodes is stored with the adjacency matrix $\mat{M}$ ($m_{ij}=1$ if $(i,j)$ is an arc). What does it cost to test whether $(i,j)$ is an arc, and to list the successors of $i$?
  1. $O(1)$ and $O(n)$
  2. $O(n)$ and $O(1)$
  3. $O(1)$ and $O(\deg^+(i))$
  4. $O(m)$ and $O(m)$
Explanation. The test reads the entry $m_{ij}$, in constant time; the successors require scanning the whole row $i$, of length $n$. $O(\deg^+(i))$ is the cost with adjacency lists, $O(m)$ with the arc list.
no-f-224 · level facile · notes
A directed graph is sparse when the number $m$ of arcs is much smaller than $n^2$, as in the networks of applications. Why do the notes use adjacency lists in network algorithms?
  1. Because they test an arc $(i,j)$ in time $O(1)$
  2. Because they are the only representation storing the capacities
  3. Because they take space $n+m$ and give the successors of $i$ in time $O(\deg^+(i))$
  4. Because they take space $O(n)$ whatever $m$ is
Explanation. With $m$ much smaller than $n^2$ the space $n+m$ is much less than $n^2$, and scanning the successors costs only $\deg^+(i)$: searches become linear. Testing an arc in $O(1)$ is the strength of the adjacency matrix, not of the lists.
no-f-225 · level facile · notes
Let $\mat{B}$ be the node–arc incidence matrix of a directed graph ($+1$ in the row of the tail, $-1$ in that of the head) and $\vet{x}$ a vector with a value $x_{ij}$ for every arc. What is component $i$ of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$?
  1. What enters $i$ minus what leaves it
  2. What leaves $i$ minus what enters it
  3. The sum of the values on the arcs incident to $i$
  4. The largest value on the arcs leaving $i$
Explanation. $(\mat{B}\vet{x})_i=\sum_{(i,j)}x_{ij}-\sum_{(j,i)}x_{ji}$: outgoing arcs have $+1$ in row $i$, incoming ones $-1$. It is the net outflow, which the conservation constraints set equal to the balance $b_i$.
no-f-226 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,4)$, and $\mat{B}$ is its node–arc incidence matrix ($+1$ in the tail, $-1$ in the head). With $x_{ij}=1$ on every arc, what is $\mat{B}\vet{x}$?
  1. $(2,1,1,1)'$
  2. $(-2,0,1,1)'$
  3. $(2,0,-1,-1)'$
  4. $(0,0,0,0)'$
Explanation. With all values equal to $1$, $(\mat{B}\vet{x})_i=\deg^+(i)-\deg^-(i)$: node $1$: $2-0$, node $2$: $1-1$, node $3$: $1-2$, node $4$: $0-1$. The components add up to $0$, as for every $\mat{B}\vet{x}$.
no-f-227 · level teorema · notes
Let $\mat{B}$ be the node–arc incidence matrix of a directed graph ($+1$ in the tail, $-1$ in the head of every arc) and $\vet{x}$ any vector of values on the arcs. Which statement is correct?
  1. The sum of the components of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$ is always $0$
  2. The sum of the components of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$ is $\sum x_{ij}$
  3. $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{0}$ for every $\vet{x}$
  4. $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{0}$ only if $\vet{x}=\vet{0}$
Explanation. Every column of $\mat{B}$ has one $+1$ and one $-1$, so the rows add up to zero and $\vet{1}'\mat{B}\vet{x}=0$: every unit leaving a node enters another one. $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{0}$ does not hold for every $\vet{x}$, and a cycle gives nonzero solutions.
no-f-228 · level facile · notes
A directed graph $G=(N,A)$ is weakly connected if the underlying undirected graph (arcs taken as edges) is connected. The graph with nodes $1,2,3,4$ has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(3,2)$, $(3,4)$. What can be said?
  1. It is strongly connected
  2. It is not weakly connected, since $4$ does not reach $1$
  3. It is weakly connected but not strongly connected
  4. It has $4$ connected components
Explanation. The edges $\{1,2\},\{2,3\},\{3,4\}$ form a path, so the underlying graph is connected. It is not strongly connected: from $1$ only $2$ is reached. Weak connectivity ignores the directions of the arcs.
no-f-229 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: in a directed graph «being strongly connected» ($j$ reachable from $i$ and $i$ from $j$) is an equivalence relation, so the strongly connected components partition the nodes. Which result is needed for transitivity?
  1. Every node has at least one outgoing arc
  2. Every directed walk contains a directed path with the same endpoints
  3. The underlying undirected graph is connected
  4. Every tree has at least two leaves
Explanation. A path from $i$ to $j$ followed by one from $j$ to $h$ is only a walk (it may repeat nodes); the walk lemma extracts from it a path from $i$ to $h$. The other assumptions are not true in general or are not needed.
no-f-230 · level facile · notes
In an undirected graph $G=(V,E)$ a forest is an acyclic graph, a tree a connected acyclic graph, and a spanning tree of a connected $G$ is a tree $T=(V,F)$ with $F \subseteq E$. What distinguishes a spanning tree from any tree contained in $G$?
  1. It contains all the vertices of $G$
  2. It has minimum weight
  3. It contains all the edges of $G$
  4. It has exactly two leaves
Explanation. «Spanning» means that the tree touches all the vertices $V$ of $G$, so it has $|V|-1$ edges. Minimum weight concerns the minimum spanning tree; a tree with all the edges exists only if $G$ itself is a tree.
no-f-231 · level facile · notes
A spanning forest of an undirected graph $G$ is a forest with all the vertices of $G$ and the same connected components. A graph has $n=12$ vertices, $m=20$ edges and $k=3$ connected components. How many edges does each of its spanning forests have?
  1. $11$
  2. $17$
  3. $8$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Each component with $n_c$ vertices has a spanning tree with $n_c-1$ edges; in total $n-k=12-3=9$. $11=n-1$ holds only for a connected graph; $17=m-k$ and $8=m-n$ use the number of edges, which does not matter.
no-f-232 · level ipotesi · notes
Corollary: every connected undirected graph has a spanning tree. The proof removes, as long as there is one, an edge of a cycle. Why does removing that edge not disconnect the graph?
  1. Because the removed edge is the heaviest of the cycle
  2. Because the graph has at least $n$ edges
  3. Because the rest of the cycle still connects the two endpoints of the removed edge
  4. Because every vertex has degree at least $2$
Explanation. The endpoints $u,v$ of the removed edge stay connected along the rest of the cycle, so the components do not change. Weights do not matter (they are needed only for the minimum spanning tree); the number of edges and the degrees are not assumptions of the corollary.
no-f-233 · level teorema · notes
Let $G=(V,E)$ be an undirected graph with $n$ vertices. Which statement is equivalent to «$G$ is a tree» (connected and acyclic)?
  1. $G$ has $n-1$ edges
  2. Between every pair of vertices there is exactly one path
  3. Every vertex has degree at most $2$
  4. $G$ is connected and has at least $n-1$ edges
Explanation. A tree has a unique path between two vertices, and conversely: existence gives connectivity, uniqueness excludes cycles. $n-1$ edges alone are not enough; «degree at most $2$» describes paths and cycles; a connected graph always has at least $n-1$ edges.
no-f-234 · level facile · notes
Let $T$ be a spanning tree of a connected undirected graph $G$ with $n=7$ vertices and $m=11$ edges. Every edge $e \notin T$ forms with $T$ a fundamental cycle $C(T,e)$. How many fundamental cycles does $G$ have with respect to $T$?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $4$
  4. $11$
Explanation. There is one fundamental cycle for every edge outside $T$: $m-(n-1)=11-6=5$. $6=n-1$ is the number of edges of $T$, $4=m-n$ forgets the $+1$.
no-f-235 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph, the spanning tree $T$ has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,4)$. The fundamental cycle of the arc $(4,3) \notin T$ is traversed in the direction of $(4,3)$: an arc of $T$ is forward if traversed from tail to head, backward otherwise. Which arcs of $T$ are backward?
  1. $(1,3)$
  2. $(1,2)$ and $(2,4)$
  3. $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$ and $(2,4)$
  4. None
Explanation. The cycle is $4,3,1,2,4$: from $3$ to $1$ it traverses $(1,3)$ from head to tail (backward), then $(1,2)$ and $(2,4)$ from tail to head (forward). The second option swaps the two directions.
no-f-236 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: if the arcs $F$ of a directed graph form a forest, their columns in the node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ are linearly independent. The proof is by induction on $|F|$. Which fact makes it possible to cancel one coefficient at each step?
  1. Every column of $\mat{B}$ adds up to zero
  2. The underlying graph is connected
  3. $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular
  4. A tree with at least $2$ nodes has a leaf, whose row contains a single arc of $F$
Explanation. In the row of a leaf $\ell$ only the arc $a'$ incident to $\ell$ appears, so the zero combination gives $\lambda_{a'}=0$; removing $a'$ leaves a forest. The zero column sums are used for the rank $|N|-k$, not for this lemma; connectivity is not required.
no-f-237 · level facile · notes
The generic search of a directed graph from a source $s$ labels the reached nodes, records $\mathit{pred}(j)$ and keeps in $\mathit{LIST}$ the labelled nodes still to be processed. When is an arc $(i,j)$ called admissible?
  1. When $i$ and $j$ are both labelled
  2. When $i$ is labelled and $j$ is not
  3. When $j$ is labelled and $i$ is not
  4. When $(i,j)$ belongs to the predecessor tree
Explanation. An admissible arc leaves an already reached node and leads to a new one: traversing it labels $j$ with $\mathit{pred}(j)=i$. Arcs between two labelled nodes are no longer useful.
no-f-238 · level facile · notes
In the generic search of a directed graph from $s$, at every iteration a node $i$ of $\mathit{LIST}$ (list of the labelled nodes to be processed) is selected. What is done if $i$ has no admissible arcs (arcs to unlabelled nodes)?
  1. The label of $i$ is removed
  2. The search restarts from $i$
  3. $i$ is removed from $\mathit{LIST}$
  4. The node with the lowest index is labelled
Explanation. A node without admissible arcs cannot label anyone any more, so it leaves $\mathit{LIST}$ (and stays labelled: labels are never removed). The search ends when $\mathit{LIST}$ is empty.
no-f-239 · level teorema · notes
Let $R(s)$ be the set of nodes reachable from $s$ by a directed path. Which statement about the generic search from $s$ (BFS and DFS included) is correct?
  1. The BFS labels all of $R(s)$, the DFS may stop earlier
  2. At the end all the nodes of the graph are labelled
  3. The labelled nodes depend on the order of the adjacency lists
  4. At the end the labelled nodes are exactly those of $R(s)$
Explanation. Every labelled node is reachable (along the predecessor tree) and every reachable node gets labelled, whatever the selection rule. The order of the lists changes the tree and the labelling order, not the final set.
no-f-240 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: at the end of the generic search from $s$ every node reachable from $s$ is labelled. The proof takes the first unlabelled node $i_t$ of a path from $s$ and the previous node $i_{t-1}$, which is labelled. Which fact of the search gives the contradiction?
  1. A node leaves $\mathit{LIST}$ only when it has no admissible arcs, and labels are never removed
  2. The nodes are processed in the order in which they are labelled
  3. The adjacency lists are sorted by increasing index
  4. The graph has no directed cycles
Explanation. $i_{t-1}$ left $\mathit{LIST}$, but the arc $(i_{t-1},i_t)$ stayed admissible until the end since $i_t$ was never labelled: a contradiction. The FIFO order (BFS) and the order of the lists are not needed, and the graph may have cycles.
no-f-241 · level facile · notes
A directed graph with $n$ nodes is stored with the adjacency matrix $\mat{M}$ instead of adjacency lists. In what time does the generic search from a node $s$ run?
  1. $O(n+m)$, as with the lists
  2. $O(m \log n)$
  3. $O(n^2)$
  4. $O(n\,m)$
Explanation. With the matrix, the successors of a node are found by scanning a row of length $n$, and every node is processed once: $O(n^2)$. $O(n+m)$ needs adjacency lists, where scanning costs $\deg^+(i)$.
no-f-242 · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the (sorted) adjacency lists $L(1)=(2,3)$, $L(2)=(4)$, $L(3)=(4,5)$, $L(4)=(5)$, $L(5)=\emptyset$. In the BFS from $1$ ($\mathit{LIST}$ is a queue, the first unlabelled head is taken), let $d(j)$ be the number of arcs of the path from $1$ to $j$ in the predecessor tree. What is $d(5)$?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $1$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The BFS labels $2,3$ from $1$, then $4$ from $2$, then $5$ from $3$: $\mathit{pred}(5)=3$ and $d(5)=d(3)+1=2$. $3$ is the length of the path $1,2,4,5$, which the BFS does not use.
no-f-243 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: in the predecessor tree of the BFS from $s$, $d(j)=d(\mathit{pred}(j))+1$ is the minimum number of arcs of a directed path from $s$ to $j$. Which property of the BFS is the core of the proof?
  1. The adjacency lists are sorted by increasing index
  2. The graph is acyclic
  3. The nodes are labelled in nondecreasing order of $d$, because $\mathit{LIST}$ is a queue
  4. Every node has at most one incoming arc
Explanation. With the queue the values $d$ in $\mathit{LIST}$ grow by at most $1$ and the nodes are labelled by nondecreasing $d$; hence $d(j) \le d(i)+1$ for every arc $(i,j)$, which gives minimality. The order of the lists and acyclicity are not needed.
no-f-244 · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the (sorted) adjacency lists $L(1)=(2,3)$, $L(2)=(4)$, $L(3)=(5)$, $L(4)=(3)$, $L(5)=\emptyset$. In the DFS from $1$ ($\mathit{LIST}$ is a stack: the last node is always processed, and the first unlabelled head is labelled), what is $\mathit{pred}(3)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $5$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The DFS goes from $1$ to $2$, then to $4$, then from $4$ it labels $3$ before going back to $1$: $\mathit{pred}(3)=4$. $\mathit{pred}(3)=1$ is the result of the BFS, which labels $3$ right away from $1$.
no-f-245 · level facile · notes
The BFS ($\mathit{LIST}$ a queue) and the DFS ($\mathit{LIST}$ a stack) are run from the same source $s$ of a directed graph, with the same adjacency lists. What do they always have in common?
  1. The set of labelled nodes
  2. The predecessor tree
  3. The labelling order
  4. The depth of the predecessor tree
Explanation. Both label exactly $R(s)$, the nodes reachable from $s$. The trees differ in general: on the reference graph the DFS reaches node $6$ with $5$ arcs, the BFS with $3$.
no-f-246 · level facile · notes
In the DFS from $s$ on a directed graph ($\mathit{LIST}$ is a stack), when does the search go back to a node labelled earlier?
  1. After every traversed arc
  2. When it meets a node with a smaller index
  3. When the node on top of the stack has no admissible arcs and leaves $\mathit{LIST}$
  4. Never: the DFS does not go back
Explanation. The DFS always restarts from the last labelled node still in $\mathit{LIST}$; it goes back to the previous node of the stack only when that node has no more arcs to unlabelled nodes and is removed.
no-f-247 · level facile · notes
In an undirected graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges all the connected components are to be found with searches. How, and in what time?
  1. A search from every vertex, in time $O(n(n+m))$
  2. A single search from any vertex, in time $O(n+m)$
  3. A search in the graph and one in the reversed graph, in time $O(n+m)$
  4. A search from every vertex still unlabelled, in total time $O(n+m)$
Explanation. Each search from an unlabelled vertex labels its whole component; the searches touch every vertex and edge once, so the total is $O(n+m)$. A single search finds only one component; the reversed graph is needed for strongly connected components.
no-f-248 · level facile · notes
Let $G'$ be the reversed graph of a directed graph $G$ (every arc $(i,j)$ becomes $(j,i)$), $R(i)$ the nodes reachable from $i$ in $G$ and $R'(i)$ those reachable from $i$ in $G'$. What is $R(i) \cap R'(i)$?
  1. The set of nodes from which $i$ is reachable
  2. The strongly connected component of $i$
  3. The connected component of $i$ in the underlying undirected graph
  4. The empty set, if $G$ has cycles
Explanation. $R'(i)$ contains the nodes from which $i$ is reachable in $G$; the intersection contains the nodes reachable from $i$ that also reach $i$, that is, the strongly connected component $S(i)$. $R'(i)$ alone is the first option.
no-f-249 · level facile · notes
A search (BFS or DFS) is run from any vertex of a connected undirected graph with $n$ vertices. What do the edges $\{\mathit{pred}(j),j\}$ of the labelled vertices $j$ other than the source form?
  1. A spanning tree of the graph
  2. A minimum spanning tree
  3. A path touching all the vertices
  4. A forest with two components
Explanation. The search labels all $n$ vertices (the graph is connected) and the predecessors form a tree on the labelled vertices: a spanning tree. It is not of minimum cost (the search ignores weights) and in general it is not a path.
no-f-250 · level teorema · notes
Let $\mat{B}$ be the node–arc incidence matrix of a directed graph $G=(N,A)$ and $F \subseteq A$ a set of arcs. Which statement is correct?
  1. The columns of $F$ are independent if and only if $F$ is a spanning tree
  2. The columns of $F$ are independent if and only if $F$ is a path
  3. The columns of $F$ are independent if and only if $F$ contains no cycle (of the underlying undirected graph)
  4. The columns of $F$ are always independent, since every column has one $+1$ and one $-1$
Explanation. The columns of a forest are independent and those of a cycle dependent, so independence is equivalent to acyclicity. A spanning tree is the maximal case ($|N|-1$ columns, connected graph); a path is only an example of a forest.
no-f-251 · level facile · notes
A multigraph allows loops and parallel edges or arcs. Why is there no need, in the network optimization module, to consider parallel arcs $(i,j)$ with different costs?
  1. Because the costs must all be equal
  2. Because a multigraph has no feasible flows
  3. They can be made simple by splitting a parallel arc with an intermediate node
  4. Because parallel arcs always form a negative cycle
Explanation. A parallel arc $(i,j)$ is replaced by two arcs $(i,k)$, $(k,j)$ through a new node $k$: the graph becomes simple and every arc is still identified by its tail–head pair. Costs need not be equal, and two parallel arcs do not form a directed cycle.
no-f-252 · level facile · notes
The reference graph of the chapter on graphs has $n=6$ nodes and $m=9$ arcs. How many entries do its adjacency lists (one entry per node plus one per arc) and its arc list (tail and head of every arc) store?
  1. $36$ and $54$
  2. $15$ and $18$
  3. $18$ and $15$
  4. $9$ and $9$
Explanation. The adjacency lists take $n+m=15$ entries, the arc list $2m=18$. $36=n^2$ is the adjacency matrix and $54=n\,m$ the node–arc incidence matrix.
no-f-253 · level facile · notes
In a network, a value $x_{ij}$ on every arc respects flow conservation at node $i$ if what leaves $i$ minus what enters it equals the balance $b_i$. With $\mat{B}$ the node–arc incidence matrix, how are the constraints for all nodes written?
  1. $\mat{B}'\vet{x}=\vet{b}$
  2. $\mat{B}\vet{x} \le \vet{b}$
  3. $\vet{x}'\mat{B}=\vet{b}$
  4. $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$
Explanation. Row $i$ of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$ is the outflow minus the inflow of $i$, so conservation is $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$, one row per node. $\mat{B}'$ appears in the dual constraints, one row per arc.
no-f-254 · level teorema · notes
Let $T=(V,F)$ be a spanning tree of a connected graph $G=(V,E)$ and $e=\{u,v\} \in E \setminus F$. Which statement about the graph $(V,F \cup \{e\})$ is correct?
  1. It contains at least two cycles, one for each endpoint of $e$
  2. It is still a tree, with $n$ edges
  3. It contains exactly one cycle, made of $e$ and the unique path from $u$ to $v$ in $T$
  4. It contains a cycle only if $e$ is heavier than the edges of $T$
Explanation. Every cycle must use $e$ ($T$ is acyclic), and removing $e$ leaves a path from $u$ to $v$ in $T$, which is unique: the fundamental cycle is unique. A connected graph with $n$ vertices and $n$ edges is not a tree; weights do not matter.
no-f-255 · level facile · notes
BFS and DFS are two cases of the generic search of a directed graph from $s$, which at every step selects a node $i$ of the list $\mathit{LIST}$ of labelled nodes to be processed. What distinguishes them?
  1. The selected node: the BFS takes the first of $\mathit{LIST}$ (a queue), the DFS the last (a stack)
  2. The BFS uses adjacency lists, the DFS the adjacency matrix
  3. The BFS labels only the nodes close to $s$, the DFS all of them
  4. The BFS needs arc lengths, the DFS does not
Explanation. The only difference is the selection rule: queue (first in, first out) for the BFS, stack (last in, first out) for the DFS. Both label all reachable nodes, with any representation and without lengths.
no-f-256 · level facile · notes
In the generic search of a directed graph with adjacency lists sorted by increasing index, the selected node $i$ has several admissible arcs (arcs to unlabelled nodes). Which arc does the search traverse?
  1. A random one
  2. The one to the node with the largest index
  3. All of them, in a single step
  4. The first admissible one of the list $L(i)$, that is, to the unlabelled node of smallest index
Explanation. With sorted lists the search takes the first admissible arc of the list, so the result (order and tree) is unique. One iteration labels a single node.
no-f-257 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph the BFS from $s$ gives every labelled node $j$ the number $d(j)$ of arcs of the path from $s$ to $j$ in its predecessor tree. What is $d(j)$ with respect to the directed paths from $s$ to $j$?
  1. It is the maximum number of arcs of a path from $s$ to $j$
  2. It is the length of the shortest path with the lengths $c_{ij}$
  3. It has no relation with paths, it depends on the order of the lists
  4. It is the minimum number of arcs of a path from $s$ to $j$
Explanation. The BFS gives paths with the minimum number of arcs (shortest paths with all lengths equal to $1$). With general lengths Dijkstra or Bellman–Ford is needed; the order of the lists changes the tree, not the values $d(j)$.
no-f-258 · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,4$ has the (sorted) adjacency lists $L(1)=(2,3)$, $L(2)=(3)$, $L(3)=(4)$, $L(4)=\emptyset$. In which order does the DFS from $1$ ($\mathit{LIST}$ a stack, first unlabelled head) label the nodes, and which tree does it get?
  1. $1,2,3,4$, with the tree $1,2,3,4$ (a path)
  2. $1,2,3,4$, with the arcs $(1,2),(1,3),(3,4)$
  3. $1,3,2,4$
  4. $1,2,4,3$
Explanation. The DFS goes from $1$ to $2$, from $2$ to $3$, from $3$ to $4$: order $1,2,3,4$ and tree $(1,2),(2,3),(3,4)$, a path. The arcs $(1,2),(1,3),(3,4)$ are the tree of the BFS, which has the same labelling order.
no-f-259 · level facile · notes
To find all the strongly connected components of a directed graph with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs, the notes repeat, for a node of each component not yet found, a search in the graph and one in the reversed graph. How long does this method take in the worst case?
  1. $O(n+m)$
  2. $O(n(n+m))$
  3. $O(m \log n)$
  4. $O(n^2 m)$
Explanation. Each pair of searches costs $O(n+m)$ and is repeated at most $n$ times (once per component): $O(n(n+m))$. More refined algorithms get down to $O(n+m)$, but they are not those of the notes.
no-f-528 · level teorema · notes
A leaf of a tree is a vertex of degree $1$. Which statement about the leaves of a tree with $n \ge 2$ vertices is correct?
  1. It has at least two leaves; it has exactly two if and only if it is a path
  2. It has at most two leaves
  3. It has exactly $n/2$ leaves
  4. It may have no leaves if it is large
Explanation. The endpoints of a longest path are leaves, so there are at least two; a star with $n$ vertices has $n-1$. A tree with exactly two leaves is a path.
no-f-529 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: every tree with $n \ge 2$ vertices has at least two leaves (vertices of degree $1$). The proof takes a path of maximum length. Why are its endpoints leaves?
  1. Because the graph has $n-1$ edges
  2. Because the degrees add up to $2(n-1)$
  3. Because the path contains all vertices
  4. Another edge at an endpoint would lengthen the path or close a cycle
Explanation. If an endpoint had another neighbour: outside the path, the path would get longer; on the path, a cycle would close, impossible in a tree. The longest path does not necessarily touch all vertices.
no-f-530 · level teorema · notes
Let $G=(V,E)$ be an undirected graph with $k$ connected components and $e=\{u,v\} \notin E$. Which statement about the graph $(V,E \cup \{e\})$ is correct?
  1. It always has $k-1$ components
  2. It always has a cycle through $e$
  3. If $u,v$ are in different components it has $k-1$ components and no cycle through $e$; if in the same one it has $k$ components and a cycle through $e$
  4. It has $k+1$ components if $u,v$ are in the same component
Explanation. An edge between two components merges them without creating cycles; an edge inside a component closes a cycle with the existing path between $u$ and $v$. It is the lemma behind the counts on trees and forests and behind Kruskal.
no-f-531 · level ipotesi · notes
Corollary: a graph with $n$ vertices, $m$ edges and $k$ components has $k \ge n-m$, with equality if and only if it is acyclic. How is it proved?
  1. By induction on the vertices, removing a leaf
  2. By adding the edges one at a time starting from $n$ isolated vertices: each lowers the components by $1$ or by $0$, and by $0$ only if it closes a cycle
  3. With the handshake lemma
  4. With the rank theorem
Explanation. One starts with $n$ components; by the edge-addition lemma every edge removes at most one component, so $k \ge n-m$, and it removes exactly one when it closes no cycle.
no-f-532 · level teorema · notes
Let $\mat{B}$ be the node–arc incidence matrix of a directed graph and $C$ a set of arcs forming a cycle of the underlying undirected graph. Which statement is correct?
  1. The columns of $C$ are independent if the cycle is directed
  2. The columns of $C$ always add up to zero, without signs
  3. The columns of $C$ are independent if $|C| \ge 3$
  4. The columns of $C$ are dependent: with sign $+1$ on forward arcs and $-1$ on backward ones they add up to zero
Explanation. Going around the cycle, $\pm\mat{B}_c=\vet{e}_{v_{t-1}}-\vet{e}_{v_t}$ and the sum telescopes to zero. Without signs the sum is nonzero if the cycle has backward arcs.
no-f-533 · level ipotesi · notes
Corollary: for a directed graph with connected underlying graph, $n-1$ columns of the incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ are independent if and only if their arcs form a spanning tree. Which results are needed?
  1. Only the handshake lemma
  2. The lemma on the columns of a forest (independent) and the one on the columns of a cycle (dependent), plus «acyclic with $n-1$ edges $\Rightarrow$ tree»
  3. Total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$
  4. The correctness of breadth-first search
Explanation. A spanning tree is a forest, so its columns are independent; independent columns exclude cycles, and $n-1$ acyclic arcs form a tree. Total unimodularity is about determinants, not needed here.
no-f-534 · level teorema · notes
In the generic search of a directed graph from $s$ every labelled node $j \neq s$ has a predecessor $\mathit{pred}(j)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The arcs $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ may form cycles in the DFS
  2. At every iteration the arcs $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ form a tree on the labelled nodes, with a directed path from $s$ to every labelled node
  3. The predecessor arcs form a tree only at the end of the search
  4. The predecessor tree contains all the nodes of the graph
Explanation. Every node is labelled after its predecessor, so tracing back the predecessors reaches $s$ without repetitions; $r$ nodes and $r-1$ connected arcs form a tree. It contains only the reachable nodes.
no-f-535 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: with adjacency lists the generic search takes time $O(|N|+|A|)$. Which device is essential in the proof?
  1. Every node keeps a pointer into its list $L(i)$ that moves forward and never back, because labels are never removed
  2. The lists are sorted
  3. A priority queue is used
  4. The graph is acyclic
Explanation. The pointer of node $i$ advances at most $\deg^+(i)$ times in total, and $\sum_i \deg^+(i)=|A|$; there are at most $2|N|$ iterations. The order of the lists only makes the result unique.
no-f-556 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has $n=6$ nodes and $m=9$ arcs. How many rows and columns does its node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ have, and how many nonzero entries?
  1. $6 \times 6$, with $9$ nonzero entries
  2. $9 \times 6$, with $9$ nonzero entries
  3. $6 \times 9$, with $9$ nonzero entries
  4. $6 \times 9$, with $18$ nonzero entries
Explanation. One row per node and one column per arc: $6 \times 9$. Every column has one $+1$ and one $-1$, so there are $2m=18$ nonzero entries. The $6 \times 6$ matrix is the adjacency matrix.
no-f-557 · level facile · notes
An undirected graph has the edges $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$, $\{2,3\}$, $\{3,4\}$. In the adjacency lists of an undirected graph every edge appears in the lists of both endpoints. What is the list $L(3)$?
  1. $(1,2,4)$
  2. $(4)$
  3. $(1,2)$
  4. $(1,2,3,4)$
Explanation. Vertex $3$ is adjacent to $1$, $2$ and $4$. $(4)$ would list only the neighbours with larger index, as if the edge were oriented from $3$.
no-f-558 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,1)$, $(3,4)$, $(4,5)$, $(5,4)$. The strongly connected components are the maximal sets of nodes pairwise reachable in both directions. How many are there?
  1. $1$
  2. $5$
  3. $2$
  4. $3$
Explanation. $\{1,2,3\}$ (cycle $1,2,3,1$) and $\{4,5\}$ (arcs $(4,5)$ and $(5,4)$): two components. From $4$ there is no way back to $3$, so the two components stay separate; the underlying graph is instead connected.
no-f-559 · level facile · notes
A directed graph with $|N|=6$ nodes has a connected underlying undirected graph, and $\mat{B}$ is its node–arc incidence matrix. At most how many columns of $\mat{B}$ can be chosen so that they are linearly independent?
  1. $6$
  2. $5$
  3. As many as there are arcs
  4. $4$
Explanation. The rank is $|N|-1=5$, and $5$ independent columns correspond to a spanning tree. $6$ columns always contain a cycle, so they are dependent.
no-f-560 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph with the arcs $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,1)$, $(3,4)$, what is the sequence $1,2,3,1,2$?
  1. A directed walk of length $4$, not a path
  2. A directed path of length $4$
  3. A directed cycle
  4. A forbidden sequence, since it repeats arcs
Explanation. Every step follows an arc from tail to head, so it is a directed walk, of $4$ arcs; it repeats nodes $1$ and $2$, so it is not a path, and it does not return to its first node. Walks may repeat arcs.

Part: Trees and paths (112)

2. Minimum spanning trees (54)

no-f-023 · level facile · notes
Kruskal's algorithm for the minimum spanning tree scans the edges by increasing weight and builds a set $F$ of accepted edges. When does it accept the scanned edge $e_k$?
  1. When at least one of its two endpoints is not yet touched by $F$
  2. When it has minimum weight among the edges incident to the current vertex
  3. Always, as long as $F$ has fewer than $n-1$ edges
  4. When its endpoints are in different components of the graph $(V,F)$
Explanation. Kruskal accepts $e_k$ if and only if it joins two components of $(V,F)$, that is, it closes no cycle with $F$. An edge can be accepted even when both endpoints are already touched by $F$.
no-f-024 · level facile · notes
Prim's algorithm grows a tree on the vertices $S$ and keeps a label $d(u)$ for every $u \notin S$. The vertex $v$ enters $S$, and $u \notin S$ is adjacent to $v$ by an edge of weight $w_{vu}$. How is $d(u)$ updated?
  1. $d(u):=w_{vu}$ if $w_{vu}<d(u)$
  2. $d(u):=d(v)+w_{vu}$ if $d(v)+w_{vu}<d(u)$
  3. $d(u):=\max\{d(u),w_{vu}\}$
  4. $d(u):=w_{vu}$ in every case
Explanation. In Prim $d(u)$ is the minimum weight of an edge between $S$ and $u$: the single weight $w_{vu}$ is compared. With $d(v)+w_{vu}$ one gets Dijkstra's algorithm for shortest paths.
no-f-025 · level facile · notes
On a connected undirected graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges, what are the running times of Kruskal's algorithm (with a union–find structure) and of Prim's algorithm (labels in an array)?
  1. Kruskal $O(n^2)$, Prim $O(m\log n)$
  2. Kruskal $O(m\log n)$, Prim $O(n^2)$
  3. Kruskal $O(n+m)$, Prim $O(n+m)$
  4. Kruskal $O(n\,m)$, Prim $O(n^2)$
Explanation. Kruskal is dominated by the sorting of the edges, $O(m\log n)$; Prim makes $n-1$ selections, each in $O(n)$, for $O(n^2)$.
no-f-026 · level facile · notes
In a connected undirected graph with edge weights, the edge $e$ of a cycle $C$ is strictly heavier than all the other edges of $C$. What do we conclude on the minimum spanning trees (minimum total weight)?
  1. $e$ belongs to every minimum spanning tree
  2. $e$ belongs to at least one minimum spanning tree
  3. $e$ belongs to no minimum spanning tree
  4. Nothing, without knowing the weights of all the edges
Explanation. Cycle property: the strictly heaviest edge of a cycle is in no minimum spanning tree; removing it from a tree and replacing it with another edge of the cycle lowers the weight.
no-f-027 · level facile · notes
For a set of vertices $S$, the cut $\delta(S)$ is the set of edges with one endpoint in $S$ and one outside. The edge set $F$ is contained in a minimum spanning tree. Which edge can be added to $F$ while staying inside some minimum spanning tree?
  1. An edge of minimum weight among all the edges outside $F$
  2. An edge of minimum weight in a cut $\delta(S)$ with $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$
  3. An edge of minimum weight in any cut $\delta(S)$
  4. An edge of maximum weight in a cycle of the graph
Explanation. Cut property: the lightest edge of a cut not crossed by $F$ extends $F$. If the cut is crossed by $F$ the guarantee is lost; the overall lightest edge may close a cycle with $F$.
no-f-028 · level facile · notes
In an undirected graph, $\delta(S)$ is the cut of the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and one outside. A cycle $C$ contains an edge of $\delta(S)$. What can be said about the number $|C \cap \delta(S)|$ of cycle edges in the cut?
  1. It is even, hence at least $2$
  2. It is exactly $1$
  3. It is odd
  4. It equals $|S|$
Explanation. Walking along the cycle, the side changes at every edge of the cut and the walk ends on the starting side: the crossings are even.
no-f-029 · level facile · notes
When does a connected undirected graph with edge weights have exactly one minimum spanning tree?
  1. Only if the graph itself is a tree
  2. Only if all the weights are positive
  3. Always, since Kruskal and Prim give the same tree
  4. Certainly when the weights are pairwise distinct
Explanation. With distinct weights the minimum spanning tree is unique. With ties other optima may exist, even though Kruskal and Prim return a single one; the sign of the weights does not matter.
no-f-030 · level facile · notes
In a weighted graph, the spanning tree $T$ weighs $20$. The edge $e \notin T$ weighs $5$, and the fundamental cycle of $e$ (the cycle of $T \cup \{e\}$) contains the edges of $T$ of weight $2$, $6$, $3$. What is the weight of the best tree obtained by adding $e$ and removing an edge of the cycle?
  1. $19$
  2. $22$
  3. $23$
  4. $25$
Explanation. By the exchange lemma $T \cup \{e\} \setminus \{f\}$ is a tree for every edge $f$ of the cycle; the best choice removes the heaviest one: $20+5-6=19$. Hence $T$ is not optimal.
no-f-031 · level facile · notes
Let $T$ be a spanning tree of a connected undirected graph. What is the fundamental cut of an edge $e=\{u,v\}$ of $T$?
  1. The set of the edges of $T$ sharing an endpoint with $e$
  2. The cycle created by adding $e$ to the tree $T$
  3. $\delta(S_e)$, where $S_e$ is the component of $T \setminus \{e\}$ that contains $u$
  4. $\delta(\{u\})$, the set of the edges incident to $u$
Explanation. Removing $e$ splits the tree into two components; the fundamental cut is $\delta(S_e)$, the edges of the graph between the two parts, and $e$ is the only edge of $T$ crossing it.
no-f-032 · level facile · notes
How many edges does a minimum spanning tree of a connected undirected graph with $12$ vertices and $30$ weighted edges have?
  1. $12$
  2. $11$
  3. $19$
  4. It depends on the edge weights
Explanation. Every spanning tree of a graph with $n$ vertices has $n-1=11$ edges, whatever the weights.
no-f-033 · level media · notes
In a weighted graph, $T$ is a minimum spanning tree. The edge $e \notin T$ weighs $7$, and the edges of $T$ in its fundamental cycle (the cycle of $T \cup \{e\}$) weigh $3$, $7$, $5$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $T$ is not optimal, because $e$ is not strictly the heaviest of its cycle
  2. $e$ belongs to no minimum spanning tree
  3. Another minimum spanning tree contains $e$
  4. $e$ belongs to every minimum spanning tree
Explanation. The cycle condition holds ($7 \ge 3,7,5$), so $T$ is optimal; but the cycle edge of weight $7$ ties with $e$: exchanging them gives another tree of the same weight containing $e$. Excluding $e$ would require the strict inequality.
no-f-034 · level teorema · notes
Let $T$ be a spanning tree of a connected graph with weights $w$, and for $e \notin T$ let $C(T,e)$ be the fundamental cycle of $e$ (the cycle of $T \cup \{e\}$). Which statement is correct?
  1. $T$ is optimal if and only if $w_e \ge w_f$ for every $e \notin T$ and every edge $f$ of $C(T,e)$
  2. $T$ is optimal if and only if $w_e \le w_f$ for every $e \notin T$ and every edge $f$ of $C(T,e)$
  3. $T$ is optimal if and only if it contains an edge of minimum weight of the graph
  4. $T$ is optimal if and only if $w_e \ge w_f$ for every $e \in T$ and every $f$ in the fundamental cut of $e$
Explanation. Cycle condition: every edge outside $T$ is the heaviest of its fundamental cycle (equivalently, every edge of $T$ is the lightest, not the heaviest, of its fundamental cut). Containing the lightest edge is necessary but not sufficient.
no-f-035 · level teorema · notes
Let $F$ be an edge set contained in a minimum spanning tree, and $\delta(S)$ the cut of the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and one outside. Which statement of the cut property is correct?
  1. For every cut $\delta(S)$, an edge of minimum weight in $\delta(S)$ extends $F$ within an optimal tree
  2. If $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$, an edge of maximum weight in $\delta(S)$ extends $F$ within an optimal tree
  3. If $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$, every edge of $\delta(S)$ extends $F$ within an optimal tree
  4. If $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$ and $e$ has minimum weight in $\delta(S)$, $F \cup \{e\}$ lies in an optimal tree
Explanation. The cut property needs a cut not crossed by $F$ and the lightest edge of that cut. Without the assumption $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$ the statement is false.
no-f-036 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof of the cut property, the lightest edge $e$ of a cut $\delta(S)$ (not crossed by $F$) is added to an optimal tree $T^\star$ containing $F$ and exchanged with an edge $f$ of $T^\star$. Which results are used?
  1. The rank theorem for the incidence matrix and the correctness of Kruskal
  2. A cycle crosses a cut an even number of times, and the exchange lemma
  3. The lemma «leaves» on trees and the cycle property
  4. The uniqueness of the optimal tree with distinct weights and the correctness of Prim
Explanation. The fundamental cycle $C(T^\star,e)$ crosses $\delta(S)$ at $e$, hence also at another edge $f$, which is not in $F$; by the exchange lemma $T^\star \cup \{e\} \setminus \{f\}$ is a tree, not heavier because $w_e \le w_f$.
no-f-037 · level ipotesi · notes
In Prim's algorithm the label $d(v)$ of a vertex $v \notin S$ is the minimum weight of an edge between $S$ and $v$. To prove that Prim finds a minimum spanning tree, which results are combined?
  1. The cycle property and the rank theorem for the incidence matrix
  2. The correctness of Dijkstra's algorithm and Bellman's equations
  3. The meaning of the labels $d(v)$ and the correctness of the generic greedy algorithm
  4. The uniqueness of the optimal tree with distinct weights and the exchange lemma
Explanation. The labels give the lightest edge of the cut $\delta(S)$, which the current set $F$ does not cross: every step of Prim is a step of the generic greedy, correct by the cut property. Distinct weights are not needed.
no-f-038 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that, with pairwise distinct weights, a connected graph has only one minimum spanning tree, assume by contradiction that there are two, $T_1 \neq T_2$. Which step is essential?
  1. Exchange the lightest edge $e$ of $T_1 \setminus T_2$ with a heavier edge of the cycle $C(T_2,e)$
  2. Show that Kruskal and Prim, on the same graph, return the same tree
  3. Show that a graph with distinct weights has only one spanning tree
  4. Use the total unimodularity of the incidence matrix of the graph
Explanation. The lightest edge $e$ of the symmetric difference of $T_1$ and $T_2$ lies, say, in $T_1 \setminus T_2$; the cycle $C(T_2,e)$ contains an edge $f \in T_2 \setminus T_1$ with $w_f>w_e$, and the exchange would give a tree lighter than $T_2$.
no-f-039 · level facile · notes
The generic greedy algorithm for the minimum spanning tree starts from $F=\emptyset$. What does it add to $F$ at every step ($\delta(S)$ is the cut of the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and one outside)?
  1. The lightest edge not yet scanned, in every case
  2. Any edge that closes no cycle with $F$
  3. An edge of minimum weight in a cut $\delta(S)$ not crossed by $F$
  4. The heaviest edge of a cycle of the graph
Explanation. The step chooses a cut $\delta(S)$ with $F \cap \delta(S)=\emptyset$ and adds an edge of minimum weight of that cut; Kruskal and Prim are two ways to choose the cut.
no-f-040 · level facile · notes
Prim's algorithm takes $O(n^2)$ time with the labels in an array and $O(m\log n)$ with the labels in a binary heap, on a graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges. On which kind of graph is the array better?
  1. On dense graphs, with $m$ of the order of $n^2$
  2. On sparse graphs, with $m$ of the order of $n$
  3. On trees, with $m=n-1$
  4. On no graph: the heap is always faster
Explanation. With $m$ of the order of $n^2$ the heap costs $O(n^2\log n)$ and the array $O(n^2)$, linear in the size of the graph. On sparse graphs, instead, $O(m\log n)$ is better.
no-f-041 · level facile · notes
In the complete graph with vertices $1,2,3,4,5$ (one edge for every pair), how many edges does the cut $\delta(S)$ with $S=\{1,2\}$ have, that is, the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and the other outside?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Every vertex of $S$ is joined to the $3$ vertices outside $S$: $|\delta(S)|=|S|\,(5-|S|)=2 \cdot 3=6$. The edge $\{1,2\}$ is inside $S$ and does not count.
no-f-042 · level facile · notes
Kruskal scans the edges by increasing weight and accepts those that close no cycle with the already accepted edges $F$. On a connected graph with $n=8$ vertices, when can it stop without scanning the other edges?
  1. After scanning $n=8$ edges
  2. As soon as $F$ contains $n-1=7$ edges
  3. As soon as all the vertices are touched by $F$
  4. Never: it must scan all the edges
Explanation. A spanning tree has $n-1$ edges: with $|F|=7$ the graph $(V,F)$ is connected and every other edge would close a cycle. All vertices being touched is not enough, since $F$ may be a forest with several components.
no-f-198 · level facile · notes
The generic greedy algorithm for the minimum spanning tree adds at every step the lightest edge of a cut $\delta(S)$ not crossed by the edges chosen so far. Prim grows a single tree from the start vertex. Which cut does Prim use at every step?
  1. $\delta(S)$, with $S$ the component of an endpoint of the lightest unscanned edge
  2. $\delta(S)$, with $S$ the set of the vertices of the tree built so far
  3. The fundamental cut of the last edge added to the tree
  4. Any cut containing the lightest edge of the graph
Explanation. Prim chooses the lightest edge between the current tree and the rest, that is, of $\delta(S)$ with $S$ the vertices of the tree. The component of an endpoint of the lightest unscanned edge is the cut used by Kruskal.
no-f-199 · level facile · notes
In a connected undirected graph with $n$ vertices and weights $w_e$ of any sign, the same constant $k$ is added to the weight of every edge. What happens to the minimum spanning trees (minimum total weight)?
  1. They stay the same, since every tree grows by $(n-1)\,k$
  2. They change, since trees with more edges grow more
  3. They stay the same only if $k>0$
  4. They stay the same only if after the addition all weights are positive
Explanation. Every spanning tree has exactly $n-1$ edges, so its weight grows by $(n-1)\,k$: the order among the trees does not change, and negative weights are no problem. For shortest paths, instead, the same operation changes the solution.
no-f-260 · level facile · notes
In the minimum spanning tree (MST) problem on a connected undirected graph with weights $w_e$, a spanning tree of maximum weight is wanted instead. How is it obtained?
  1. It cannot be: Kruskal's algorithm works only for the minimum
  2. An MST is found and the lightest edges are removed
  3. An MST is found with weights $1/w_e$
  4. An MST is found with weights $-w_e$
Explanation. Maximizing $\sum w_e$ is the same as minimizing $\sum(-w_e)$, and negative weights are allowed in the MST problem. The weights $1/w_e$ do not preserve the order of the sums and are undefined for $w_e=0$.
no-f-261 · level facile · notes
$n$ offices must be connected by cables; $w_{uv}$ is the cost of the direct cable between offices $u$ and $v$, and two offices communicate if they are joined by a sequence of cables. Which problem gives the cheapest network?
  1. A minimum spanning tree
  2. A shortest path from one office to all the others
  3. A maximum flow between two offices
  4. A minimum cost assignment
Explanation. The network must connect all the vertices at minimum total cost: without cycles (they would be useless cables) it is a minimum spanning tree. The shortest path tree minimizes the distances from a source, not the total cost.
no-f-262 · level facile · notes
In single-linkage clustering, $n$ points are the vertices of a complete graph with weights $w_{uv}$ equal to the distances. How are the points split into $k$ groups with a minimum spanning tree $T$?
  1. The $k-1$ lightest edges are removed from $T$
  2. The $k$ vertices of largest degree in $T$ are taken
  3. The $k-1$ heaviest edges are removed from $T$
  4. Any $k$ edges are removed from $T$
Explanation. Removing $k-1$ edges from a tree leaves $k$ components; removing the heaviest ones keeps nearby points together. Removing the lightest would separate exactly the closest points; removing $k$ edges gives $k+1$ groups.
no-f-263 · level facile · notes
Minimum spanning trees appear as a subproblem of other optimization problems on graphs. For which problem do they give a lower bound (a bound below the optimal value)?
  1. The maximum flow problem
  2. The travelling salesman problem
  3. The shortest path problem
  4. The transportation problem
Explanation. Removing one edge from a Hamiltonian tour gives a spanning tree (a path), so the cost of a minimum spanning tree is a lower bound on the cost of the optimal travelling salesman tour.
no-f-264 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: a spanning tree $T$ is minimum if and only if the cycle condition holds ($w_e \ge w_f$ for every $e \notin T$ and every $f$ of the fundamental cycle $C(T,e)$). For (cycle)$\Rightarrow$(optimal) one takes an MST $T^\star$ with the largest number of edges in common with $T$. Which step leads to the contradiction?
  1. An exchange $T^\star \cup \{f\} \setminus \{e\}$ with $w_f \le w_e$ gives an MST with one more edge in common with $T$
  2. One shows that $T^\star$ has fewer than $n-1$ edges
  3. One shows that the weights of $T$ are distinct
  4. Prim's algorithm is applied to $T$
Explanation. With $e \in T^\star \setminus T$ and $f \in T \setminus T^\star$ in the fundamental cut of $e$, the exchange does not increase the weight (cycle condition for $e \notin T$) and increases the common edges: this contradicts the choice of $T^\star$. Distinct weights are not an assumption of the theorem.
no-f-265 · level facile · notes
To certify that a spanning tree $T$ of a weighted graph is minimum with the cycle condition, what is checked?
  1. That $T$ contains the lightest edge of the graph
  2. That every edge of $T$ is lighter than every edge outside $T$
  3. That every edge $e \notin T$ weighs at least as much as every edge of its fundamental cycle $C(T,e)$
  4. That $T$ has $n-1$ edges
Explanation. One check per outside edge is enough: $m-n+1$ fundamental cycles. Every tree edge lighter than every outside edge is sufficient but not necessary; $n-1$ edges only says that $T$ is a tree.
no-f-266 · level facile · notes
In a weighted graph, the spanning tree $T$ weighs $30$. The edge $e \notin T$ weighs $4$, and its fundamental cycle $C(T,e)$ contains the tree edges with weights $3$, $9$, $6$. What is the minimum weight of a tree $T \cup \{e\} \setminus \{f\}$, with $f$ in the cycle?
  1. $31$
  2. $25$
  3. $27$
  4. $28$
Explanation. The heaviest edge of the cycle, $f$ of weight $9$, is removed: $30+4-9=25$. Removing $3$ gives $31$, removing $6$ gives $28$. The improvement shows that $T$ violates the cycle condition.
no-f-267 · level teorema · notes
The generic greedy algorithm for the MST starts from $F=\emptyset$ and adds $n-1$ times the lightest edge of a cut $\delta(S)$ not crossed by $F$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $F$ is a minimum spanning tree only if the weights are distinct
  2. The cut must always be $\delta(\{s\})$ for a fixed vertex $s$
  3. At every iteration $F$ is contained in some minimum spanning tree
  4. The added edge may close a cycle with $F$
Explanation. By the cut property, adding the lightest edge of an uncrossed cut keeps $F$ inside an MST; at the end $|F|=n-1$ and $F$ is an MST, even with equal weights. The edge joins two components of $(V,F)$, so it closes no cycle.
no-f-268 · level ipotesi · notes
In the correctness of the generic greedy algorithm for the MST, why does a nonempty cut $\delta(S)$ not crossed by $F$ exist at every iteration with $|F|<n-1$?
  1. Because the weights are positive
  2. Because $(V,F)$ has at least two components, and $G$ is connected, so the cut of a component $S$ contains edges
  3. Because $F$ is a path
  4. Because every vertex has degree at least $2$
Explanation. A forest with $|F|<n-1$ edges has $n-|F| \ge 2$ components; for a component $S$ no edge of $F$ crosses $\delta(S)$, and $\delta(S) \neq \emptyset$ because $G$ is connected. The sign of the weights does not matter.
no-f-269 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove that Kruskal is correct one shows that every accepted edge $e_k=\{u,v\}$ is the lightest edge of the cut $\delta(S)$, with $S$ the component of $u$ in $(V,F)$. Which fact guarantees it?
  1. The weights are distinct
  2. The graph is complete
  3. $S$ contains vertex $1$
  4. Every edge of $\delta(S)$ examined before $e_k$ would have been accepted, so all edges of $\delta(S)$ come later and weigh at least $w_{e_k}$
Explanation. An edge of $\delta(S)$ examined earlier would have had its endpoints in different components (components only merge) and would have been accepted, crossing $\delta(S)$: absurd. So the edges of $\delta(S)$ follow $e_k$ in the weight order. Equal weights are allowed.
no-f-270 · level facile · notes
Kruskal keeps the components of $(V,F)$ with a union–find structure by labels: every vertex $v$ has a label $\mathit{comp}(v)$, and a merge relabels the vertices of the smaller component. At most how many times is a vertex relabelled?
  1. $\log_2 n$ times
  2. $n-1$ times
  3. Once
  4. $m$ times
Explanation. When a vertex is relabelled, its component joins one at least as large, so its size at least doubles; it never exceeds $n$, so at most $\log_2 n$ relabellings. Without union by size it can be $n-1$.
no-f-271 · level facile · notes
Kruskal on a weighted graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges takes $O(m\log n)$ with union–find. Which phase dominates the time?
  1. The merges of the components
  2. The tests $\mathit{comp}(u) \neq \mathit{comp}(v)$
  3. Building the adjacency lists
  4. Sorting the edges by weight
Explanation. Sorting costs $O(m\log m)=O(m\log n)$; the tests cost $O(1)$ each, $O(m)$ in all, and the merges $O(n\log n)$. With a faster union–find the bound stays $O(m\log n)$ because of sorting.
no-f-272 · level facile · notes
Kruskal on the graph with edges and weights $w_{12}=4$, $w_{13}=1$, $w_{23}=3$, $w_{24}=6$, $w_{34}=5$, $w_{35}=8$, $w_{45}=2$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$) examines the edges by increasing weight. Which edge is the first to be rejected?
  1. $\{2,3\}$
  2. $\{1,2\}$
  3. $\{2,4\}$
  4. $\{3,5\}$
Explanation. Order: $\{1,3\}$ accepted, $\{4,5\}$ accepted, $\{2,3\}$ accepted, $\{1,2\}$ rejected, because $1$ and $2$ are already in the component $\{1,2,3\}$. $\{2,4\}$ is rejected later, when $\{3,4\}$ has joined everything.
no-f-273 · level facile · notes
Kruskal on a connected graph with $n$ vertices and $m$ edges stops as soon as $|F|=n-1$. Which statement about the edges that are never examined is correct?
  1. There are none: Kruskal always examines all $m$ edges
  2. They are the lightest edges of the graph
  3. They are the heaviest ones, and none of them is needed: the tree is already complete
  4. They are those closing cycles of length $3$
Explanation. The scan goes by increasing weight, so the unexamined edges are those after the last accepted one; with $n-1$ edges the tree is complete. On the weighted reference graph $\{3,5\}$ and $\{4,6\}$ are never examined.
no-f-274 · level facile · notes
In Kruskal every rejected edge $e=\{u,v\}$ has its endpoints already in the same component of $(V,F)$. What can be said about $e$ with respect to the cycle it closes with $F$?
  1. It is the lightest edge of the cycle
  2. The cycle has exactly $3$ edges
  3. It belongs anyway to some minimum spanning tree
  4. It is an edge of maximum weight of the cycle
Explanation. The other edges of the cycle are in $F$, accepted earlier, so not heavier than $e$: $e$ has maximum weight in the cycle, as the cycle property predicts. With distinct weights $e$ is in no MST.
no-f-275 · level facile · notes
Prim from $s=1$ on the graph with weights $w_{12}=4$, $w_{13}=1$, $w_{23}=3$, $w_{24}=6$, $w_{34}=5$, $w_{35}=8$, $w_{45}=2$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$) adds at every step the lightest edge between the tree and the rest. Which is the third added edge?
  1. $\{3,4\}$
  2. $\{4,5\}$
  3. $\{1,2\}$
  4. $\{2,4\}$
Explanation. From $\{1\}$: $\{1,3\}$ ($1$); from $\{1,3\}$: $\{2,3\}$ ($3$); from $\{1,2,3\}$ the outgoing edges weigh $6,5,8$: $\{3,4\}$ is added. $\{4,5\}$ is Kruskal's second edge, but Prim reaches it only after $4$.
no-f-276 · level facile · notes
In Prim's algorithm every vertex $v$ outside the tree $S$ has the label $d(v)$, minimum weight of an edge between $S$ and $v$. Vertex $3$ enters $S$; $v \notin S$ has $d(v)=8$ and the edge $\{3,v\}$ weighs $5$. What does $d(v)$ become?
  1. $5$, since $w_{3v}=5<8$
  2. $13$, that is, $d(3)+w_{3v}$
  3. It stays $8$
  4. $3$
Explanation. Prim updates $d(v):=\min\{d(v),w_{3v}\}=5$, with $\mathit{pred}(v)=3$. Adding $d(3)+w_{3v}$ is Dijkstra's update for shortest paths, not Prim's.
no-f-277 · level facile · notes
On a connected graph with distinct weights Kruskal and Prim (from any vertex) are run. What do the two results have in common?
  1. The order in which the edges are added
  2. Nothing: they may give trees of different weight
  3. The number of examined edges
  4. The set of edges of the tree
Explanation. With distinct weights the MST is unique, so both algorithms give the same tree, in a different order: Kruskal grows a forest, Prim a single tree from $s$. On the reference graph $\{4,5\}$ is Kruskal's second edge and Prim's fourth.
no-f-278 · level teorema · notes
Prim's algorithm starts from $S=\{s\}$ and adds at every iteration the lightest edge of $\delta(S)$ (between $S$ and $V \setminus S$). Which statement is correct?
  1. Prim is correct only with nonnegative weights
  2. Prim is a case of the generic greedy algorithm with the cut $\delta(S)$, so it returns a minimum spanning tree
  3. Prim returns the shortest path tree from $s$
  4. Prim's result depends on the vertex $s$ even with distinct weights
Explanation. The cut $\delta(S)$ is not crossed by the chosen edges (they all lie inside $S$), so Prim is an instance of the generic greedy, correct with weights of any sign. With distinct weights the MST is unique and does not depend on $s$; the shortest path tree is a different object.
no-f-279 · level teorema · notes
Let $G$ be a connected undirected graph with edge weights. Which statement about the uniqueness of the minimum spanning tree (MST) is correct?
  1. If the weights are pairwise distinct, the MST is unique; with equal weights it may or may not be
  2. The MST is unique if and only if the weights are distinct
  3. If two edges have the same weight, there are at least two MSTs
  4. The MST is always unique, since Kruskal is deterministic
Explanation. Distinct weights are enough but not necessary: with equal weights the MST is unique if every outside edge is strictly heavier than the others of its fundamental cycle. Kruskal's determinism fixes one tree, it does not exclude others.
no-f-280 · level facile · notes
Let $T$ be an MST. The edge $e \in T$ weighs $5$, and the edges of its fundamental cut (the edges joining the two parts of $T \setminus \{e\}$) other than $e$ weigh $5$, $7$, $9$. What can be said about $e$?
  1. $e$ is in every MST
  2. $e$ is in no MST
  3. $T$ is not an MST
  4. $e$ is in $T$ but there is an MST without $e$
Explanation. Another edge $f$ of the fundamental cut weighs as much as $e$: $T \cup \{f\} \setminus \{e\}$ is a spanning tree of the same weight without $e$. $e$ is in every MST only if it is strictly lighter than all the other edges of the cut.
no-f-281 · level facile · notes
A graph with vertices $1,2,3$ has the edges $\{1,2\}$, $\{2,3\}$, $\{1,3\}$, all of weight $5$. How many minimum spanning trees does it have?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $2$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Every pair of edges is a spanning tree of weight $10$, and there are $3$ pairs: three MSTs. With equal weights the uniqueness condition (every outside edge strictly heavier than its cycle) fails.
no-f-282 · level teorema · notes
Let $\delta(S)$ be a cut of a weighted connected graph (edges with one endpoint in $S$ and one outside). Which statement of the cut property is correct?
  1. If $e$ is strictly lighter than every other edge of $\delta(S)$, $e$ is in every MST
  2. The heaviest edge of $\delta(S)$ is in no MST
  3. Every edge of $\delta(S)$ is in some MST
  4. An MST contains exactly one edge of every cut
Explanation. An edge strictly lighter than all others of the cut is in every MST (with ties, only in some MST). The heaviest edge of a cut can be in an MST (if it is the only bridge); an MST can cross a cut several times.
no-f-283 · level facile · notes
Why does a connected undirected graph with weights $w_e$ always have a minimum spanning tree, even with negative weights?
  1. Because it has at least one spanning tree and finitely many of them
  2. Because negative weights can be ignored
  3. Because Prim always stops after $n$ iterations
  4. Because the problem is a maximum flow
