Explanation. A connected graph has at least one spanning tree and finitely many, so the minimum exists. Unlike shortest paths, negative weights cause no trouble: a tree has no cycles to traverse repeatedly.
no-f-284 · level facile · notes
A company must connect $5$ plants by roads; the cost $w_{uv}$ of every direct road is known. The minimum spanning tree problem is solved. How many roads will be built?
  1. $5$
  2. $10$
  3. It depends on the costs
  4. $4$
Explanation. A spanning tree on $5$ vertices always has $5-1=4$ edges, whatever the costs. $10$ are all the possible pairs; $5$ would create a cycle, that is, a useless road.
no-f-285 · level teorema · notes
Kruskal's algorithm examines the edges by nondecreasing weight (ties in lexicographic order) and accepts those joining two components of $(V,F)$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Kruskal is correct only if the weights are distinct
  2. Kruskal stops with $|F|=n-1$ and $F$ is a minimum spanning tree
  3. Kruskal may stop with a forest of two trees even if $G$ is connected
  4. Kruskal gives the shortest path tree from vertex $1$
Explanation. Every accepted edge is the lightest of the cut of its component, so Kruskal is a case of the generic greedy: it ends with an MST even with equal weights. On a connected graph it never stops with two components.
no-f-286 · level facile · notes
The weights $w_e$ of a graph are replaced by $w_e^3$ (an increasing transformation). What happens to the tree returned by Kruskal?
  1. It changes, since large weights count more
  2. It stays the same, since Kruskal uses only comparisons between weights
  3. It stays the same only if the weights are positive
  4. It becomes a maximum spanning tree
Explanation. $w^3$ is increasing on all reals, so the order of the edges does not change, and Kruskal depends only on that order. The same holds for every increasing transformation.
no-f-287 · level facile · notes
Kruskal on the graph with weights $w_{12}=2$, $w_{13}=2$, $w_{23}=1$, $w_{34}=3$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$; edges of equal weight are examined in lexicographic order) accepts which edges?
  1. $\{2,3\}$, $\{1,2\}$, $\{3,4\}$
  2. $\{2,3\}$, $\{1,3\}$, $\{3,4\}$
  3. $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$, $\{3,4\}$
  4. $\{2,3\}$, $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$
Explanation. Order: $\{2,3\}$, then $\{1,2\}$ (before $\{1,3\}$ by the lexicographic rule), accepted; $\{1,3\}$ closes a cycle and is rejected; $\{3,4\}$ accepted. With the other tie rule the second tree, of the same weight, would be obtained.
no-f-288 · level facile · notes
Prim from $s=4$ on the graph with weights $w_{12}=4$, $w_{13}=1$, $w_{23}=3$, $w_{24}=6$, $w_{34}=5$, $w_{45}=2$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$) adds at every step the lightest edge between the tree and the rest. In which order do the vertices enter?
  1. $5,3,1,2$
  2. $5,2,3,1$
  3. $3,1,2,5$
  4. $5,3,2,1$
Explanation. From $\{4\}$: $\{4,5\}$ ($2$), $5$ enters; then $\{3,4\}$ ($5$) against $\{2,4\}$ ($6$): $3$ enters; then $\{1,3\}$ ($1$): $1$ enters; finally $\{2,3\}$ ($3$): $2$ enters. The last option adds $2$ before $1$, ignoring the edge of weight $1$.
no-f-289 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: with distinct weights the MST is unique. The proof assumes two MSTs $T_1 \neq T_2$ and takes the lightest edge $e$ of their symmetric difference, with $e \in T_1$. What is built?
  1. A negative cycle in $T_1$
  2. A cut with two edges of equal weight
  3. The tree $T_2 \cup \{e\} \setminus \{f\}$, with $f \in T_2 \setminus T_1$ in the cycle $C(T_2,e)$, lighter than $T_2$
  4. A third MST different from both
Explanation. $C(T_2,e)$ has an edge $f \notin T_1$, hence in the symmetric difference and heavier than $e$ (distinct weights); the exchange gives a tree of weight $w(T_2)+w_e-w_f<w(T_2)$, against the optimality of $T_2$. Without distinct weights one would only get $w_f \ge w_e$.
no-f-290 · level teorema · notes
Let $T$ be an MST of a weighted graph. Which statement characterizes the uniqueness of the MST, even with equal weights?
  1. $T$ is the unique MST if and only if the weights of $T$ are distinct
  2. $T$ is the unique MST if and only if no outside edge has the same weight as an edge of $T$
  3. $T$ is the unique MST if and only if every edge $e \notin T$ is strictly heavier than the other edges of $C(T,e)$
  4. $T$ is the unique MST if and only if it is a path
Explanation. An outside edge with a tie in its fundamental cycle gives, by an exchange, another MST; without ties no outside edge is in an MST. Equal weights between edges not in the same fundamental cycle do not matter.
no-f-561 · level teorema · notes
Let $T$ be a spanning tree of a connected graph with weights $w_e$; the fundamental cut of $e \in T$ is the set of edges joining the two parts of $T \setminus \{e\}$. Which condition is equivalent to «$T$ is a minimum spanning tree»?
  1. Every edge $e \in T$ weighs at most as much as every edge of its fundamental cut
  2. Every edge $e \in T$ is the lightest of the whole graph
  3. Every edge $e \notin T$ is lighter than some edge of its fundamental cycle
  4. $T$ contains the $n-1$ lightest edges
Explanation. It is the cut condition, equivalent to the cycle condition and to optimality. The third option describes instead a tree that an exchange can improve; the $n-1$ lightest edges may contain cycles.

3. Shortest paths (58)

no-f-043 · level facile · notes
A directed graph with cycles has lengths $d_{ij}$ on the arcs, one of them negative, and no directed cycle of negative length. Which algorithm correctly computes the distances (lengths of shortest paths) from a node $s$?
  1. Dijkstra's algorithm
  2. The Bellman–Ford algorithm
  3. The algorithm for acyclic graphs
  4. Dijkstra after adding the same positive constant to every length
Explanation. Dijkstra can fail with negative arcs, and adding a constant changes the shortest paths (a path with $k$ arcs grows by $k$ times the constant). The algorithm for acyclic graphs needs a topological order, which does not exist with cycles.
no-f-044 · level facile · notes
On a directed graph with $n$ nodes and $m \ge n-1$ arcs, what are, in order, the running times of Bellman–Ford, Dijkstra (labels in an array) and the algorithm for acyclic graphs?
  1. $O(n^2)$, $O(n\,m)$, $O(n+m)$
  2. $O(n+m)$, $O(n^2)$, $O(n\,m)$
  3. $O(n\,m)$, $O(m\log n)$, $O(n^2)$
  4. $O(n\,m)$, $O(n^2)$, $O(n+m)$
Explanation. Bellman–Ford makes at most $n$ passes of $O(m)$; Dijkstra makes $n$ selections of $O(n)$; in acyclic graphs every arc is scanned once, in topological order.
no-f-045 · level facile · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ sends one unit of flow from $s$ to $t$ at minimum cost, with lengths $d_{ij}$ and the incidence matrix of the course ($+1$ in the tail, $-1$ in the head). Let $\tilde{d}(i)$ be the distances from $s$. What are the dual constraints and the optimal potentials $\pi_i$?
  1. $\pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}$, with $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$
  2. $\pi_j-\pi_i \le d_{ij}$, with $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$
  3. $\pi_i-\pi_j \ge d_{ij}$, with $\pi_i=\tilde{d}(i)$
  4. $\pi_i+\pi_j \le d_{ij}$, with $\pi_i=\tilde{d}(i)$
Explanation. The column of the arc $(i,j)$ has $+1$ at $i$ and $-1$ at $j$, so the dual constraint is $\pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}$, and $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ is optimal, up to a constant. The form $\pi_j-\pi_i \le d_{ij}$ with $\pi_i=\tilde{d}(i)$ belongs to the opposite convention.
no-f-046 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has lengths $d_{ij}$ of any sign. The potentials $\vet{\pi}$ are feasible: $\bar{c}_{ij}=d_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc, with $\pi_s=0$ and $\pi_t=-9$. A path $P$ from $s$ to $t$ has all its arcs tight ($\bar{c}_{ij}=0$). What do we conclude?
  1. $P$ is a shortest path and its length is $d(P)=-9$
  2. $P$ is shortest only if the lengths are non-negative
  3. $P$ is a shortest path and its length is $d(P)=9$
  4. $d(P)=9$, but $P$ may not be a shortest path
Explanation. By the theorem on potentials as optimality certificate $d(P)=\pi_s-\pi_t=0-(-9)=9$, and every other path is at least $9$ long: $P$ is shortest and the graph has no negative cycle. Non-negative lengths are not needed.
no-f-047 · level facile · notes
The Bellman–Ford algorithm from $s$, on a graph with $n$ nodes, computes in pass $k$ the labels $d^k(j)$ from the $d^{k-1}$. The labels still change in pass $n$, that is $d^n \neq d^{n-1}$. What does it mean?
  1. More passes are needed: continue until pass $2n$
  2. A cycle of negative length is reachable from $s$
  3. Some node of the graph is not reachable from $s$
  4. The graph has a cycle, not necessarily negative
Explanation. Without reachable negative cycles $d^{n-1}=d^n=\tilde{d}$, since a path has at most $n-1$ arcs; a change in pass $n$ reveals a negative cycle reachable from $s$, read by following the predecessors backwards.
no-f-048 · level facile · notes
A topological order of a directed graph is a numbering of the nodes in which every arc $(i,j)$ goes from an earlier node to a later one. When does a directed graph have a topological order?
  1. When it is strongly connected
  2. When it has no arc of negative length
  3. Always, sorting the nodes by increasing index
  4. If and only if it has no directed cycle
Explanation. A topological order exists if and only if the graph has no directed cycle: along a cycle the arcs cannot all go forward. The lengths play no role.
no-f-049 · level facile · notes
The single-source model $\mathrm{SP}(s)$ sends one unit of flow from $s$ to every other node at minimum cost. The distances from $s=1$ in a graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ are $\tilde{d}=(0,5,2,8,10,13)$. What is the optimal value $z(\mathrm{SP}(s))$?
  1. $38$
  2. $13$
  3. $45$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Every node receives its unit along a shortest path: $z(\mathrm{SP}(s))=\sum_{j \in N}\tilde{d}(j)=0+5+2+8+10+13=38$. $13$ is $z(\mathrm{SP}(1,6))$, the distance of node $6$ alone.
no-f-050 · level facile · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ (one unit of flow from $s$ to $t$, flows $x_{ij} \ge 0$ without upper bounds) is feasible, and the graph has a directed cycle of negative length not reachable from $s$. What can be said about the model?
  1. It has optimal value $\tilde{d}(t)$, since the cycle is not reachable
  2. It is infeasible
  3. It is unbounded
  4. It has an optimum, but not an integer one
Explanation. Adding to a feasible solution multiples of the incidence vector of the cycle keeps feasibility and the cost tends to $-\infty$: a negative cycle anywhere in the graph makes the model unbounded, even though Bellman–Ford from $s$ does not see it.
no-f-051 · level facile · notes
In the Bellman–Ford algorithm from $s$, what does the label $d^k(j)$ computed in pass $k$ represent?
  1. The minimum length of a path from $s$ to $j$ with exactly $k$ arcs
  2. The minimum length of a walk from $s$ to $j$ with at most $k$ arcs
  3. The distance $\tilde{d}(j)$ from $s$, for every pass $k \ge 1$
  4. The number of arcs of a shortest path from $s$ to $j$
Explanation. $d^k(j)$ is the minimum over all walks (which may repeat nodes) with at most $k$ arcs; without reachable negative cycles $d^{n-1}(j)=\tilde{d}(j)$.
no-f-052 · level facile · notes
Dijkstra's algorithm splits the nodes into permanent (final label) and temporary ones. The node $i$ has just become permanent. Which labels are updated right after ($\delta^+(i)$ are the arcs leaving $i$)?
  1. Those of all the successors of $i$, permanent ones included
  2. Those of all the temporary nodes, also those not adjacent to $i$
  3. Those of the temporary nodes with an arc towards $i$
  4. Only those of the temporary heads of the arcs of $\delta^+(i)$
Explanation. The arcs $(i,j) \in \delta^+(i)$ with $j$ temporary are scanned, and $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ if it improves; the label of a permanent node is final and is not touched again.
no-f-053 · level media · notes
A directed graph has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=4$, $d_{13}=1$, $d_{32}=2$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{34}=7$. With the potentials $\vet{\pi}=(\pi_1,\dots,\pi_4)=(0,-3,-1,-6)$, which of the listed arcs has a positive reduced cost $\bar{c}_{ij}=d_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$?
  1. $(1,3)$
  2. $(3,2)$
  3. $(1,2)$
  4. $(2,4)$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{12}=4-0-3=1$, $\bar{c}_{13}=1-0-1=0$, $\bar{c}_{32}=2+1-3=0$, $\bar{c}_{24}=3+3-6=0$, $\bar{c}_{34}=7+1-6=2$. The tight arcs $(1,3)$, $(3,2)$, $(2,4)$ form the shortest path $1,3,2,4$ of length $\pi_1-\pi_4=6$.
no-f-054 · level teorema · notes
Let (A1) be «every node is reachable from $s$» and (A2) «no negative cycle is reachable from $s$», and $\tilde{d}(j)$ the distances from $s$; $\delta^-(j)$ and $\delta^+(j)$ are the arcs entering and leaving $j$. Under (A1) and (A2), which form of Bellman's equations is correct?
  1. $\tilde{d}(j)=\max_{(i,j) \in \delta^-(j)}\{\tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}\}$ for $j \neq s$, and $\tilde{d}(s)=0$
  2. $\tilde{d}(j)=\min_{(i,j) \in \delta^-(j)}\{\tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}\}$ for $j \neq s$, and $\tilde{d}(s)=0$
  3. $\tilde{d}(j)=\min_{(j,k) \in \delta^+(j)}\{\tilde{d}(k)+d_{jk}\}$ for $j \neq s$, and $\tilde{d}(s)=0$
  4. $\tilde{d}(j)=\min_{(i,j) \in \delta^-(j)} d_{ij}$ for $j \neq s$, and $\tilde{d}(s)=0$
Explanation. The distance of $j$ comes from the best predecessor: minimum over $\delta^-(j)$ of $\tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}$. The maximum is that of the forward pass of the critical path method; the last form recalls Prim's labels.
no-f-055 · level teorema · notes
Dijkstra's algorithm from $s$ makes permanent, at every step, the temporary node with the smallest label $d$; $\tilde{d}$ are the distances from $s$ and $d_{ij}$ the lengths. Which statement on its correctness is correct?
  1. If $d_{ij} \ge 0$ on every arc, every permanent node $i$ has $d(i)=\tilde{d}(i)$
  2. If there are no negative cycles, at the end $d=\tilde{d}$, also with negative arcs
  3. If the graph is acyclic, at the end $d=\tilde{d}$, with lengths of any sign
  4. If $d_{ij} \ge 0$, every temporary node $j$ has $d(j)=\tilde{d}(j)$ at every step
Explanation. The theorem needs non-negative lengths: then the permanent labels are the distances. With a negative arc Dijkstra can fail even without negative cycles or on an acyclic graph; the temporary labels are only the minima over paths with permanent inner nodes.
no-f-056 · level teorema · notes
The Bellman–Ford algorithm from $s$, on a graph with $n$ nodes, computes the labels $d^k$ pass after pass and stops at the first pass $k$ with $d^k=d^{k-1}$. Which statement on its correctness is correct?
  1. If the graph has no cycles, already after the first pass $d^{1}=\tilde{d}$
  2. If no negative cycle is reachable from $s$, it stops after exactly $n$ passes
  3. If the lengths are non-negative, already after two passes $d^{2}=\tilde{d}$
  4. If no negative cycle is reachable from $s$, $d^{n-1}=\tilde{d}$ and it stops at a pass $k \le n$
Explanation. A path has at most $n-1$ arcs, so $d^{n-1}=\tilde{d}$; the algorithm stops at the first pass without changes, which is at most the $n$-th, often earlier. The other claims fail on long paths.
no-f-057 · level ipotesi · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ sends one unit of flow from $s$ to $t$; its dual $D(\mathrm{SP}(s,t))$ has the constraints $\pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}$ and the objective $\max\,\pi_s-\pi_t$. To prove that $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ is optimal for the dual, what is used?
  1. The correctness of Dijkstra and the non-negativity of the lengths $d_{ij}$
  2. The total unimodularity of the incidence matrix and the integrality of the vertices
  3. Bellman's equations, for dual feasibility, and weak duality
  4. The max-flow min-cut theorem applied to the network with capacities $1$
Explanation. Bellman's equations give $\tilde{d}(j) \le \tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}$, that is $\pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}$; the dual value $\pi_s-\pi_t=\tilde{d}(t)$ equals the cost of the shortest path, so by weak duality both solutions are optimal. The lengths may have any sign.
no-f-058 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof that the node $i$ made permanent by Dijkstra has $d(i)=\tilde{d}(i)$, one considers a path $P$ from $s$ to $i$ and its first temporary node $h$. Where is the assumption $d_{ij} \ge 0$ on the lengths used?
  1. To guarantee that the graph is acyclic
  2. To say that the part of $P$ after $h$ is at least $0$ long
  3. To guarantee that every node is reachable from $s$
  4. To have a topological order of the nodes
Explanation. The part of $P$ up to $h$ is at least $d(h)$ long and the rest, with non-negative lengths, at least $0$; hence $d(P) \ge d(h) \ge d(i)$, since $i$ has the smallest temporary label.
no-f-059 · level ipotesi · notes
Bellman's equations say $\tilde{d}(s)=0$ and $\tilde{d}(j)=\min_{(i,j) \in \delta^-(j)}\{\tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}\}$ for $j \neq s$. Let (A1) be «every node is reachable from $s$» and (A2) «no negative cycle is reachable from $s$». What is needed to prove them?
  1. Non-negative lengths and the correctness of Dijkstra's algorithm
  2. An acyclic graph and a topological order of it
  3. A connected graph and the rank theorem for the incidence matrix
  4. (A1), (A2), «walks and paths» and the optimality of subpaths
Explanation. (A1) makes the distances finite and (A2) gives $\tilde{d}(s)=0$; the inequality $\le$ comes from the lemma «walks and paths», the equation from the last arc of a shortest path, whose subpath up to the second-to-last node is shortest.
no-f-060 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph without negative cycles reachable from $s$, the path $P=s,a,b,c,t$ is a shortest path from $s$ to $t$. What can be said about the subpath $s,a,b$?
  1. It is shortest only if the lengths are non-negative
  2. It may be longer than another path from $s$ to $b$
  3. It is a shortest path from $s$ to $b$
  4. It is shortest only if $b$ is the middle node of $P$
Explanation. Lemma «optimality of subpaths»: a shorter path from $s$ to $b$, followed by $b,c,t$, would give a walk from $s$ to $t$ shorter than $P$, hence a shorter path. It holds for lengths of any sign.
no-f-061 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph with $n$ nodes, every node is reachable from $s$ and no negative cycle is reachable from $s$. How many arcs does a shortest path tree have (a tree in which the path from $s$ to every node is a shortest path)?
  1. $n-1$
  2. $n$
  3. As many as the arcs of the graph
  4. It depends on the arc lengths
Explanation. The shortest path tree is a spanning tree: one arc $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ for every node $j \neq s$, hence $n-1$ arcs. The lengths decide which arcs, not how many.
no-f-062 · level facile · notes
The labels $d(j)$ of a directed graph with lengths $d_{ij}$ are finite, with $d(s)=0$; for every $j \neq s$ there is a path from $s$ to $j$ of length $d(j)$. Which extra condition guarantees that the $d(j)$ are the distances from $s$?
  1. $d(j) \ge d(i)+d_{ij}$ for every arc $(i,j)$
  2. $d(j) \le d(i)$ for every arc $(i,j)$
  3. $d_{ij} \ge 0$ for every arc $(i,j)$
  4. $d(j) \le d(i)+d_{ij}$ for every arc $(i,j)$
Explanation. Optimality conditions for labels: with $d(j) \le d(i)+d_{ij}$ the potentials $\pi_i=-d(i)$ are feasible and every path from $s$ to $j$ is at least $d(j)$ long; the path of length $d(j)$ is therefore shortest. The sign of the lengths does not matter.
no-f-063 · level facile · notes
On an acyclic directed graph with the nodes numbered in topological order, the algorithm for acyclic graphs scans the nodes in that order and, for every leaving arc $(i,j)$, sets $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ if it improves. Which lengths does it allow?
  1. Only non-negative lengths, like Dijkstra
  2. Lengths of any sign
  3. Only integer lengths
  4. Negative lengths only on the arcs leaving $s$
Explanation. An acyclic graph has no cycles, hence no negative cycles; when a node is processed all its predecessors are already final, and the label is the distance whatever the sign of the lengths.
no-f-064 · level facile · notes
With the temporary labels in a binary heap, every selection of the minimum and every decrease of a label cost $O(\log n)$. What does Dijkstra's algorithm then cost on a graph with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs?
  1. $O(n^2)$
  2. $O(n\,m)$
  3. $O((n+m)\log n)$
  4. $O(n+m)$
Explanation. There are at most $n$ selections and at most $m$ decreases, each in $O(\log n)$: $O((n+m)\log n)$, better than $O(n^2)$ (labels in an array) on sparse graphs.
no-f-291 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=7$, $d_{13}=2$, $d_{32}=3$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{34}=9$. What is the minimum length of a directed path from $1$ to $4$?
  1. $10$
  2. $8$
  3. $11$
  4. $5$
Explanation. The paths from $1$ to $4$ are $1,2,4$ ($10$), $1,3,4$ ($11$) and $1,3,2,4$ ($2+3+3=8$): the minimum is $8$. The path with fewest arcs is not necessarily the shortest.
no-f-292 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph with lengths $d_{ij}$ on the arcs and source $s$, let $\tilde{d}(j)$ be the distance of $j$ (minimum length of a directed path from $s$ to $j$, $+\infty$ if $j$ is not reachable). Node $5$ is not reachable from $s$. What is $\tilde{d}(5)$?
  1. $0$
  2. $-\infty$
  3. It is undefined
  4. $+\infty$
Explanation. By convention the distance of an unreachable node is $+\infty$: there is no path, and the minimum over an empty set of lengths is $+\infty$. $-\infty$ would rather signal a reachable negative cycle.
no-f-293 · level facile · notes
The single-source shortest path problem asks for a shortest directed path from $s$ to every node; the $s$–$t$ problem only from $s$ to one node $t$. How are the two related?
  1. A single-source algorithm solves the $s$–$t$ problem for all $t$ at once
  2. The $s$–$t$ problem is harder, since it fixes the destination
  3. They are equivalent only on acyclic graphs
  4. The single-source problem is solved with $n-1$ separate runs
Explanation. The distances from $s$ to all nodes include the one to $t$, and the algorithms of the chapter (Dijkstra, Bellman–Ford) compute them in one run. $n-1$ runs are not needed.
no-f-294 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph with lengths $d_{ij}$ the length of a walk is the sum of the lengths of its arcs. How long is the walk $1,3,2,4,3,5$ with $d_{13}=2$, $d_{32}=3$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{43}=1$, $d_{35}=8$?
  1. $5$
  2. $14$
  3. $17$
  4. $13$
Explanation. $2+3+3+1+8=17$. The walk repeats node $3$: removing the cycle $3,2,4,3$ (length $7$) leaves the path $1,3,5$ of length $10$.
no-f-295 · level facile · notes
A negative cycle is a directed cycle of negative total length. The graph has the arcs $(2,3)$, $(3,4)$, $(4,2)$ with $d_{23}=4$, $d_{34}=-6$, $d_{42}=1$. Is it a negative cycle?
  1. Yes: $4-6+1=-1<0$
  2. No: it contains two positive arcs
  3. Yes, because it contains a negative arc
  4. No: a cycle cannot have negative length
Explanation. The sum counts: $4-6+1=-1$, so the cycle is negative. A negative arc alone is not enough (with $d_{42}=3$ the sum would be $1$), and the number of positive arcs does not matter.
no-f-296 · level teorema · notes
Let (A1) «every node is reachable from $s$» and (A2) «no negative cycle is reachable from $s$», in a directed graph with lengths $d_{ij}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. Under (A1) every walk from $s$ to $j$ is longer than a path from $s$ to $j$
  2. Without (A2) the distance $\tilde{d}(j)$ is always $-\infty$ for every $j$
  3. Under (A2), for every walk $W$ from $s$ to $j$ there is a path $P$ from $s$ to $j$ with arcs of $W$ and $d(P) \le d(W)$
  4. Under (A2) every walk is a path
Explanation. Removing from $W$ a cycle, of length $\ge 0$ by (A2), does not lengthen the walk: repeating, a path is reached. Without (A2) a negative cycle makes unbounded only the walks to the nodes reachable from that cycle.
no-f-297 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has a negative cycle $C$ reachable from $s$, and node $j$ is reachable from the cycle. What happens to the lengths of the walks (which may repeat nodes and arcs) from $s$ to $j$?
  1. They are all positive
  2. Their minimum equals $d(C)$
  3. They coincide with the lengths of the paths
  4. They can be negative and arbitrarily large in absolute value
Explanation. Going around $C$ $k$ times shortens the walk by $k\,|d(C)|$, without bound. This is why the algorithms of the chapter, with a reachable negative cycle, detect it instead of computing the distances.
no-f-298 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: if no negative cycle is reachable from $s$, every walk $W$ from $s$ to $j$ contains a path $P$ with $d(P) \le d(W)$. The proof removes a cycle $C$ from $W$. Where is the assumption on negative cycles used?
  1. To have $d(C) \ge 0$, hence $d(W)-d(C) \le d(W)$
  2. To guarantee that $W$ has at least one arc
  3. To guarantee that $j$ is reachable from $s$
  4. To have all lengths nonnegative
Explanation. The cycle $C$ is reachable from $s$ along $W$, so $d(C) \ge 0$ and removing it does not lengthen the walk. Single arc lengths may be negative; reachability of $j$ is given by $W$ itself.
no-f-299 · level facile · notes
The directed graph has the cycles $2,4,3,2$ with lengths $d_{24}=3$, $d_{43}=1$, $d_{32}=3$ and $3,5,4,3$ with $d_{35}=8$, $d_{54}=2$, $d_{43}=1$. Which value of $d_{54}$, instead of $2$, makes a cycle negative?
  1. $-3$
  2. $-8$
  3. $-10$
  4. $0$
Explanation. The cycle $3,5,4,3$ has length $8+d_{54}+1$, negative if $d_{54}<-9$: with $-10$ it is $-1$. With $-3$ it is $6$, with $-8$ it is $1$; the cycle $2,4,3,2$ does not use $d_{54}$.
no-f-300 · level facile · notes
With lengths $d_{12}=4$, $d_{13}=1$, $d_{32}=2$, $d_{24}=3$ and labels $d=(0,3,1,6)$ (for nodes $1,\dots,4$), the potentials $\pi_i=-d(i)$ and the reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=d_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ are used. What is $\bar{c}_{12}$, and what does it indicate?
  1. $1$: the arc $(1,2)$ is not tight and lies on no shortest path
  2. $0$: the arc is tight
  3. $-1$: the labels violate the conditions
  4. $7$: it is the length of the path $1,2$ plus $d(2)$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{12}=d_{12}+d(1)-d(2)=4+0-3=1>0$: the arc is not tight, and a path using it is longer than the minimum ($2$ is reached with $3$ along $1,3,2$).
no-f-301 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: if the potentials $\vet{\pi}$ are feasible ($\bar{c}_{ij}=d_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc) and all arcs of a path $P$ from $s$ to $t$ are tight ($\bar{c}_{ij}=0$), then $P$ is shortest. Which identity carries the proof?
  1. $d(P)=\sum_{(i,j) \in P}\pi_j$
  2. Bellman's equations $\tilde{d}(j)=\min_i\{\tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}\}$
  3. Strong duality of linear programming
  4. The telescoping sum $d(Q)=\sum_{(i,j) \in Q}\bar{c}_{ij}+\pi_s-\pi_t$ for every path $Q$ from $s$ to $t$
Explanation. Along a path the potentials cancel: $d(Q) \ge \pi_s-\pi_t=d(P)$ since the reduced costs are $\ge 0$ and those of $P$ are zero. Strong duality is not needed (it is the weak-duality argument), nor knowing the distances.
no-f-302 · level teorema · notes
In a directed graph with lengths $d_{ij}$ there are feasible potentials $\vet{\pi}$, that is, $\bar{c}_{ij}=d_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc. What can be concluded about cycles?
  1. The graph is acyclic
  2. The graph has no negative cycles, since the length of every cycle is the sum of its reduced costs
  3. The graph has at least one cycle of length zero
  4. Nothing: tight arcs are also needed
Explanation. On a cycle the potentials cancel completely: $d(C)=\sum_{(i,j) \in C}\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$. So feasible potentials certify the absence of negative cycles, but the graph may have cycles.
no-f-303 · level facile · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ of the shortest path uses a flow $x_{ij} \ge 0$ on every arc and the conservation constraints «outflow minus inflow equals $b_i$». Which balances $b_i$ are used?
  1. $b_s=-1$, $b_t=1$, $b_i=0$ elsewhere
  2. $b_s=n-1$, $b_i=-1$ elsewhere
  3. $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$, $b_i=0$ elsewhere
  4. $b_i=0$ for every node
Explanation. A path from $s$ to $t$ sends one unit: it leaves $s$ ($b_s=1$) and enters $t$ ($b_t=-1$), the other nodes pass it on. $b_s=n-1$ is the single-source model; the first option reverses the signs.
no-f-304 · level facile · notes
In the LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ ($\min \sum d_{ij}x_{ij}$, flow conservation with $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$, $x \ge 0$), what is the incidence vector $\vet{x}^P$ of a path $P$ from $s$ to $t$?
  1. $x^P_{ij}=1$ on the arcs of $P$, $0$ elsewhere: a feasible solution of value $d(P)$
  2. $x^P_{ij}=d_{ij}$ on the arcs of $P$
  3. A dual solution of the model
  4. $x^P_{ij}=1$ on all arcs leaving the nodes of $P$
Explanation. Sending the unit along $P$ respects conservation at every node, and the cost is $\sum_{(i,j) \in P}d_{ij}=d(P)$. The dual solutions are the potentials $\pi_i$.
no-f-305 · level facile · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ is written in matrix form as $\min\{\vet{d}'\vet{x} : \mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b},\ \vet{x} \ge \vet{0}\}$. What is $\mat{B}$?
  1. The adjacency matrix of the graph
  2. The matrix of the lengths $d_{ij}$
  3. An $n \times n$ identity matrix
  4. The node–arc incidence matrix, with $+1$ in the tail and $-1$ in the head of every arc
Explanation. The conservation constraints are one row per node: row $i$ of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$ is the outflow minus the inflow of $i$. The adjacency matrix is $n \times n$ and does not give the constraints.
no-f-306 · level ipotesi · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ has fractional feasible solutions (for instance half a unit on two paths). Why is its optimal value nevertheless the length of a shortest path?
  1. Because the solver always rounds the solution
  2. Because the incidence vector of a shortest path and the potentials $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ have the same value, optimal by weak duality
  3. Because fractional flows violate conservation
  4. Because the graph is acyclic
Explanation. Under (A1)–(A2) $\vet{x}^P$ of a shortest path is primal feasible with value $\tilde{d}(t)$, and $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ is dual feasible with value $\pi_s-\pi_t=\tilde{d}(t)$: equal values, so both optimal. Fractional solutions exist but are not better.
no-f-307 · level teorema · notes
Let (A1) «every node is reachable from $s$» and (A2) «no negative cycle is reachable from $s$». Which statement about the LP model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$ and its dual $\max\{\pi_s-\pi_t : \pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}\}$ is correct?
  1. Under (A1)–(A2) $z(\mathrm{SP}(s,t))=\tilde{d}(t)$ and $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ is optimal for the dual
  2. The dual is always unbounded
  3. $z(\mathrm{SP}(s,t))=\tilde{d}(t)$ even with a reachable negative cycle
  4. The dual has optimum $\pi_i=\tilde{d}(i)$
Explanation. With the course's signs the optimal potentials are $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$: $\pi_s-\pi_t=\tilde{d}(t)$ and $\pi_i-\pi_j \le d_{ij}$ is $\tilde{d}(j) \le \tilde{d}(i)+d_{ij}$. With a reachable negative cycle (and $t$ reachable from it) the primal is unbounded.
no-f-308 · level facile · notes
In the single-source model $\mathrm{SP}(s)$ the source sends one unit to each of the other $n-1$ nodes ($b_s=n-1$, $b_i=-1$). What is the optimal value, if (A1) and (A2) hold?
  1. $\max_j \tilde{d}(j)$
  2. $(n-1)\,\tilde{d}(t)$
  3. $\sum_{j \in N}\tilde{d}(j)$
  4. $\sum_{(i,j) \in A} d_{ij}$
Explanation. The optimal flow is the sum of the incidence vectors of the $n-1$ paths of the shortest path tree, so it costs $\sum_j \tilde{d}(j)$. It is neither the largest distance nor the sum of all lengths.
no-f-309 · level facile · notes
In the shortest path tree from $s=1$ of a graph with $5$ nodes, the arc $(1,3)$ leads to nodes $3$, $2$ and $4$ (their tree paths pass through $3$). In the optimal solution of the model $\mathrm{SP}(s)$ ($b_1=4$, $b_i=-1$ elsewhere), what is $x_{13}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $\tilde{d}(3)$
Explanation. On a tree arc $(i,j)$ one unit passes for every node of $T_j$, the set of nodes whose path passes through $j$: here $T_3=\{3,2,4\}$, so $x_{13}=3$. $1$ is the value in the model $\mathrm{SP}(s,t)$.
no-f-310 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: the flow $x_{ij}=|T_j|$ on the arcs of a shortest path tree is optimal for $\mathrm{SP}(s)$ and $\pi_i=-\tilde{d}(i)$ for its dual. Which tool concludes the proof once the two solutions are shown feasible with the same value?
  1. Weak duality
  2. Total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$
  3. Dijkstra's algorithm
  4. The max-flow min-cut theorem
Explanation. A feasible primal and a feasible dual solution with the same value are both optimal by weak duality. Total unimodularity guarantees integer vertices, but here optimality is proved directly.
no-f-311 · level facile · notes
A topological order of a directed graph is an ordering of the nodes in which every arc goes from an earlier node to a later one. The graph has the arcs $(1,3)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,2)$, $(2,4)$. Which ordering is topological?
  1. $1,2,3,4$
  2. $3,1,2,4$
  3. $1,2,4,3$
  4. $1,3,2,4$
Explanation. One needs $1$ before $3$ and $2$, $3$ before $2$, $2$ before $4$: only $1,3,2,4$. In $1,2,3,4$ the arc $(3,2)$ goes backward.
no-f-312 · level teorema · notes
The algorithm for acyclic graphs examines the nodes in topological order and, for every outgoing arc $(i,j)$, sets $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ if it improves. Which statement is correct?
  1. It works only with nonnegative lengths
  2. It takes time $O(n^2)$ like Dijkstra
  3. It works with lengths of any sign and takes time $O(n+m)$
  4. It also works on graphs with cycles, if the lengths are positive
Explanation. When $i$ is examined all its predecessors are already final (the topological order puts them first), so $d(i)=\tilde{d}(i)$ whatever the sign of the lengths; every arc is examined once. A graph with cycles has no topological order.
no-f-313 · level facile · notes
The acyclic graph with nodes in topological order $1,2,3,4$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=5$, $d_{23}=-4$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{34}=1$. What is the distance $\tilde{d}(4)$ from $s=1$?
  1. $6$
  2. $-1$
  3. $8$
  4. $3$
Explanation. In topological order: $d(2)=2$, $d(3)=\min\{5,2-4\}=-2$, $d(4)=\min\{2+6,-2+1\}=-1$. $6$ ignores the negative arc (it goes through $1,3,4$), $8$ uses $1,2,4$.
no-f-314 · level facile · notes
Bellman–Ford computes, in pass $k$, $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\ \min_{(i,j)}\{d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij}\}\}$. With $d^2(2)=5$, $d^2(5)=10$, $d^2(4)=10$ and the arcs entering $4$ $(2,4)$ with $d_{24}=3$ and $(5,4)$ with $d_{54}=-3$, what is $d^3(4)$?
  1. $10$
  2. $8$
  3. $2$
  4. $7$
Explanation. $d^3(4)=\min\{10,\ 5+3,\ 10-3\}=\min\{10,8,7\}=7$, with predecessor $5$. $8$ forgets the arc $(5,4)$; $2=d^2(2)-3$ attaches the length of $(5,4)$ to the wrong node.
no-f-315 · level facile · notes
On a graph with $n=6$ nodes, Bellman–Ford from $s$ produces in pass $5$ the same labels as in pass $4$ ($d^5=d^4$). What does the algorithm do?
  1. It stops: the labels are the distances, since from now on they would not change
  2. It runs pass $6$ anyway to check for negative cycles
  3. It declares a negative cycle
  4. It restarts from $d^0$ with another source
Explanation. Each pass depends only on the previous one, so $d^5=d^4$ implies $d^k=d^4$ for every $k \ge 4$: no negative cycle, and $d^4=\tilde{d}$. In the notes' example it stops after $5$ passes instead of $6$.
no-f-316 · level facile · notes
On a graph with $n=6$ nodes, Bellman–Ford from $s$ runs all $n$ passes, and in pass $6$ a label still decreases ($d^6 \neq d^5$). What is concluded?
  1. That more passes are needed to converge
  2. That the graph is not connected
  3. That there is a negative cycle reachable from $s$
  4. That the labels $d^6$ are the distances
Explanation. Without reachable negative cycles shortest paths have at most $n-1$ arcs and $d^{n-1}=d^n$. An improvement in pass $n$ certifies a negative cycle, found by tracing back the predecessors.
no-f-317 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: if in pass $n$ of Bellman–Ford a label changes, there is a negative cycle reachable from $s$. Which fact, valid without reachable negative cycles, makes the test correct?
  1. The lengths are nonnegative
  2. Every shortest path has at most $n-1$ arcs, and $d^k(j)$ is the minimum length of a walk with at most $k$ arcs
  3. The graph has a topological order
  4. Every node has at most $n-1$ incoming arcs
Explanation. $d^k(j)$ is the shortest walk with at most $k$ arcs; without reachable negative cycles the minimum is attained by a path, with at most $n-1$ arcs, so $d^n=d^{n-1}$. Bellman–Ford requires neither nonnegative lengths nor acyclicity.
no-f-318 · level facile · notes
Dijkstra from $s=1$ with $d_{12}=7$, $d_{13}=2$, $d_{32}=3$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{35}=8$, $d_{54}=2$, $d_{46}=5$, $d_{56}=7$, $d_{43}=1$ makes permanent at every step the temporary node with the smallest label. In which order do the nodes become permanent?
  1. $1,3,2,4,5,6$
  2. $1,3,5,2,4,6$
  3. $1,2,3,4,5,6$
  4. $1,3,2,5,4,6$
Explanation. Labels: after $1$, $d(2)=7$, $d(3)=2$; after $3$, $d(2)=5$, $d(5)=10$; after $2$, $d(4)=8$; after $4$, $d(6)=13$; then $5$ ($10$) and $6$ ($13$). Order $1,3,2,4,5,6$ with $\tilde{d}=(0,5,2,8,10,13)$.
no-f-319 · level facile · notes
In Dijkstra the node $i$ just made permanent has $d(i)=10$; the arc $(i,j)$ has $d_{ij}=7$ and the temporary node $j$ has $d(j)=13$. What does the algorithm do on $j$?
  1. It sets $d(j):=17$ and $\mathit{pred}(j):=i$
  2. It sets $d(j):=7$
  3. It leaves $d(j)=13$, since $10+7=17>13$
  4. It makes $j$ permanent right away
Explanation. The update is $d(j):=\min\{d(j),d(i)+d_{ij}\}=\min\{13,17\}=13$: no change. Setting $d(j):=d_{ij}$ is Prim's update.
no-f-320 · level facile · notes
At the end of Dijkstra from $s$ (nonnegative lengths), what do the arcs $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ of the reachable nodes $j \neq s$ form?
  1. A minimum spanning tree
  2. A shortest path tree from $s$
  3. A path touching all the nodes
  4. The set of tight arcs, which may contain cycles
Explanation. Every $\mathit{pred}(j)$ gives the last arc of a shortest path to $j$, and the arcs form a tree rooted at $s$ whose paths are shortest. It is not in general a minimum spanning tree (Prim and Dijkstra differ).
no-f-321 · level ipotesi · notes
The proof of correctness of Dijkstra uses $d_{ij} \ge 0$ in a single place. Which one?
  1. To guarantee that every node is reachable from $s$
  2. To guarantee that the graph is acyclic
  3. To say that the part of a path after its first temporary node has length $\ge 0$
  4. To bound the time by $O(n^2)$
Explanation. The path $P$ to the selected node $i$ leaves the permanent nodes at a temporary node $h$ with $d(h) \ge d(i)$; the rest of $P$ has length $\ge 0$ only if lengths are nonnegative. A negative arc breaks exactly this step.
no-f-322 · level facile · notes
In the graph with arcs $d_{12}=2$, $d_{13}=3$, $d_{32}=-2$ and $s=1$, Dijkstra makes $2$ permanent with $d(2)=2$, then $3$ with $d(3)=3$, and ignores the arc $(3,2)$ because $2$ is already permanent. What is the true distance $\tilde{d}(2)$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $-2$
  4. $5$
Explanation. The path $1,3,2$ has length $3-2=1<2$: Dijkstra fails because it made $2$ permanent before seeing the negative arc. Bellman–Ford, which allows negative lengths, finds $\tilde{d}(2)=1$.
no-f-323 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has some negative lengths but no negative cycle, and the distances from $s$ are wanted. Which algorithm of the notes is used if the graph has cycles?
  1. Dijkstra, since without negative cycles it is correct
  2. The algorithm for acyclic graphs
  3. Prim
  4. Bellman–Ford
Explanation. Dijkstra can fail with a single negative arc, even without negative cycles; the algorithm for acyclic graphs needs a topological order, which does not exist if the graph has cycles. Bellman–Ford allows negative lengths and detects negative cycles.
no-f-324 · level facile · notes
At the end of Bellman–Ford from $s=1$ (no negative cycles) the predecessors are $\mathit{pred}(2)=3$, $\mathit{pred}(3)=1$, $\mathit{pred}(4)=5$, $\mathit{pred}(5)=3$, $\mathit{pred}(6)=4$. What is the shortest path from $1$ to $6$?
  1. $1,3,2,4,6$
  2. $1,3,5,4,6$
  3. $1,2,4,6$
  4. $6,4,5,3,1$
Explanation. Trace back the predecessors: $6,4,5,3,1$, and read the path backwards: $1,3,5,4,6$. The last option is the predecessor sequence not reversed.
no-f-325 · level facile · notes
In Dijkstra from $s$ (nonnegative lengths) a node is temporary until it is chosen, then permanent. When an arc $(i,j)$ leaving the just-chosen node $i$ ends at an already permanent node $j$, what does the algorithm do?
  1. It updates $d(j)$ if $d(i)+d_{ij}<d(j)$
  2. It makes $j$ temporary again
  3. It declares a negative cycle
  4. It ignores it: the label of $j$ is already final
Explanation. With nonnegative lengths $d(j) \le d(i) \le d(i)+d_{ij}$ for a permanent node $j$, so the arc cannot improve it and is ignored. With negative arcs this is exactly where Dijkstra fails.
no-f-497 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: if no negative cycle is reachable from $s$, at the end of Bellman–Ford the arcs $(\mathit{pred}(j),j)$ form a shortest path tree. The proof shows that every arc $(p,j)$ with $p=\mathit{pred}(j)$ is tight, $d(j)=d(p)+d_{pj}$. Which fact gives the inequality $d(j) \ge d(p)+d_{pj}$?
  1. The lengths are nonnegative
  2. In the pass where $d(j)$ last decreased it was $d^{k-1}(p)+d_{pj}$, and labels never increase
  3. The graph is acyclic
  4. Every node has a single incoming arc
Explanation. When $j$ got the predecessor $p$, $d(j)=d^{k-1}(p)+d_{pj} \ge d(p)+d_{pj}$ since $d(p)$ can only decrease afterwards; the other inequality comes from Bellman's equations. Bellman–Ford allows negative lengths and cycles.

Part: Flows (225)

4. Network flow models (52)

no-f-065 · level facile · notes
In a flow network every node $i$ has a balance $b_i$, and flow conservation requires outflow minus inflow equal to $b_i$. Node $i$ has balance $b_i=-4$. What kind of node is it?
  1. A supply node
  2. A transshipment node
  3. A demand node
  4. A dummy node
Explanation. $b_i>0$ means a supply node, $b_i<0$ a demand node, $b_i=0$ a transshipment node: with $b_i=-4$ the node must receive $4$ net units.
no-f-066 · level facile · notes
In a flow network, two arcs leave node $i$ with flows $3$ and $2$, and one arc enters $i$ with flow $4$. Conservation requires outflow minus inflow equal to the balance $b_i$. For which $b_i$ does conservation hold at $i$?
  1. $b_i=1$
  2. $b_i=-1$
  3. $b_i=9$
  4. $b_i=0$
Explanation. Outflow minus inflow: $3+2-4=1=b_i$. The opposite sign comes from swapping outflow and inflow, $9$ from adding all the flows.
no-f-067 · level facile · notes
A network with balances $b_i$, capacities $u_{ij}$ and zero lower bounds is balanced, that is $\sum_{i \in N} b_i=0$ (total supply equal to total demand). What can be said about its set of feasible flows?
  1. It is always nonempty
  2. It is nonempty if all the capacities are positive
  3. It always contains the zero flow
  4. It may be empty: balance is necessary, not sufficient
Explanation. A network with a feasible flow is balanced, but not conversely: a cut may have too little capacity to carry out the supply of the nodes inside it (cut condition). The zero flow is feasible only with $\vet{b}=\vet{0}$.
no-f-068 · level facile · notes
In a network without lower bounds, the node set $S$ has total balance $\sum_{i \in S} b_i=7$ (net supply to be carried out of $S$), and the arcs leaving $S$ have total capacity $5$. What do we conclude?
  1. Every feasible flow sends exactly $5$ units out of $S$
  2. The network has no feasible flow
  3. The network is not balanced
  4. A dummy node must be added inside $S$
Explanation. The cut condition requires $\sum_{i \in S} b_i \le u(\delta^+(S))-l(\delta^-(S))=5-0$: with $7>5$ no feasible flow exists.
no-f-069 · level facile · notes
The minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ has bounds $\vet{l} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$ and costs $\vet{c}$. The substitution $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$ gives an equivalent model with zero lower bounds. How are the optimal values of the two models related?
  1. They are equal
  2. They differ by the constant $\vet{c}'\vet{u}$
  3. They differ by the constant $\vet{c}'\vet{l}$
  4. The second is only a lower bound on the first
Explanation. $\vet{c}'\vet{x}=\vet{c}'\vet{x}'+\vet{c}'\vet{l}$ for every flow: the optimal solutions correspond and the values differ by the constant $\vet{c}'\vet{l}$, which must be added back at the end.
no-f-070 · level facile · notes
An unbalanced minimum cost flow instance has supply nodes with $b=5$ and $b=4$ and demand nodes with $b=-3$ and $b=-4$: supply exceeds demand. A dummy node $d$ is added, with an arc $(i,d)$ from every supply node. What are the balance $b_d$ and the cost of the arcs $(i,d)$?
  1. $b_d=-2$, arcs of cost $0$
  2. $b_d=2$, arcs of cost $0$
  3. $b_d=-2$, arcs costing the largest cost
  4. $b_d=-9$, arcs of cost $0$
Explanation. The excess is $e=5+4-3-4=2$; the dummy node has $b_d=-e=-2$ and receives the unused supply along zero-cost arcs. A positive cost would charge the unused supply.
no-f-071 · level facile · notes
When is an integer matrix $\mat{M}$ (not necessarily square) called totally unimodular?
  1. When it is square with determinant $\pm 1$
  2. When every entry is $0$, $+1$ or $-1$
  3. When it has full row rank
  4. When every square submatrix has determinant $0$, $+1$ or $-1$
Explanation. The definition concerns all the square submatrices, of every order $k \ge 1$. Entries in $\{0,\pm 1\}$ are only the case $k=1$, and they are not enough.
no-f-072 · level facile · notes
A network has integer balances, lower bounds and (finite) capacities and fractional costs, and the minimum cost flow LP model $\mathrm{MCF}$ is feasible. What can be said about its optimal solutions?
  1. An integer one exists only if the costs are integer too
  2. An integer one exists, whatever the costs
  3. They are all integer, whatever the costs
  4. Branch-and-bound is needed to find an integer one
Explanation. The incidence matrix is totally unimodular: with integer $\vet{b}$, $\vet{l}$, $\vet{u}$ every vertex is integer, and the minimum is attained at a vertex. The costs do not matter; non-integer optima may also exist (between two optimal vertices).
no-f-073 · level facile · notes
With potentials $\pi$ on the nodes, the reduced cost of the arc $(i,j)$, with tail $i$ and head $j$, is $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. The arc has cost $c_{ij}=4$, and $\pi_i=10$, $\pi_j=7$. What is $\bar{c}_{ij}$?
  1. $1$
  2. $7$
  3. $-1$
  4. $21$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=4-10+7=1$: $-\pi$ of the tail and $+\pi$ of the head. With the signs swapped one gets $7$.
no-f-074 · level facile · notes
In the minimum cost flow model with $\vet{0} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$, let $\vet{x}$ be a feasible flow and $\vet{\pi}$ potentials, with reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Which condition on every arc certifies that $\vet{x}$ is optimal?
  1. $\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=0$
  2. $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on every arc, whatever the flow $\vet{x}$
  3. $\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=0$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$
  4. $x_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow \bar{c}_{ij}>0$ and $x_{ij}=0 \Rightarrow \bar{c}_{ij}<0$
Explanation. Arcs with positive reduced cost must be empty, arcs with negative reduced cost saturated: this is the reduced cost certificate, with $\alpha_{ij}=\max\{0,-\bar{c}_{ij}\}$ in the dual.
no-f-075 · level facile · notes
The minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ (balances, capacities and costs on a directed graph) has many special cases. Which of these problems is not a special case of $\mathrm{MCF}$ in the course?
  1. The shortest path from $s$ to $t$
  2. The minimum spanning tree
  3. The maximum flow from $s$ to $t$
  4. The transportation problem
Explanation. Shortest path, maximum flow, transportation and assignment are $\mathrm{MCF}$ models with special balances, capacities and costs; the minimum spanning tree is a problem on undirected graphs, solved by Kruskal and Prim.
no-f-076 · level media · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has balances $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$ and a single positive lower bound, $l_{32}=1$ on the arc $(3,2)$. With the substitution $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$ the lower bounds become zero. What are the new balances $\vet{b}'=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$ ($\mat{B}$: $+1$ in the tail, $-1$ in the head)?
  1. $(5,1,1,-3,0,-4)$
  2. $(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$
  3. $(5,3,1,-3,0,-4)$
  4. $(5,3,-1,-3,0,-4)$
Explanation. The tail $3$ has already sent $l_{32}=1$ and the head $2$ has already received it: $b'_3=0-1=-1$, $b'_2=2+1=3$. The other options swap the signs, forget an endpoint or ignore the bound.
no-f-077 · level teorema · notes
Let $\mat{B}$ be the node–arc incidence matrix of a directed graph with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs (every column has one $+1$ in the tail and one $-1$ in the head). Which statement on the total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$ is correct?
  1. $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular only if the graph is acyclic
  2. $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular for every directed graph
  3. $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular only if the graph is bipartite
  4. $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular only if $n=m$ and $\det(\mat{B})=\pm 1$
Explanation. Every column of $\mat{B}$ has one $+1$ and one $-1$, and by induction every square submatrix has determinant $0$ or $\pm 1$, whatever the directed graph. $\mat{B}$ is $n \times m$, in general not square.
no-f-078 · level teorema · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ be a vector satisfying flow conservation, $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$, and $\vet{\pi} \in \R^n$ potentials, with reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Which identity is correct?
  1. $\sum_{(i,j) \in A} c_{ij}x_{ij}=\sum_{(i,j) \in A} \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}+\sum_{i \in N} b_i\pi_i$
  2. $\sum_{(i,j) \in A} c_{ij}x_{ij}=\sum_{(i,j) \in A} \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}-\sum_{i \in N} b_i\pi_i$
  3. $\sum_{(i,j) \in A} c_{ij}x_{ij}=\sum_{(i,j) \in A} \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}$
  4. $\sum_{(i,j) \in A} \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}=\sum_{i \in N} b_i\pi_i$
Explanation. From $c_{ij}=\bar{c}_{ij}+\pi_i-\pi_j$ and conservation, $\sum(\pi_i-\pi_j)x_{ij}=\sum_i \pi_i b_i$: the cost is the reduced cost plus $\vet{b}'\vet{\pi}$.
no-f-079 · level ipotesi · notes
The polyhedron of feasible flows is $\{\vet{x} : \mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b},~\vet{l} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}\}$, with $\mat{B}$ the incidence matrix and $\vet{b}$, $\vet{l}$, $\vet{u}$ integer. In the proof that its vertices are integer, which steps are needed?
  1. The max-flow min-cut theorem and the Ford–Fulkerson algorithm
  2. The network simplex and Phase 0 with the artificial arcs
  3. Independent columns for the arcs between bounds, Cramer and total unimodularity
  4. Strong duality and complementary slackness
Explanation. At a vertex the columns of the arcs with $l_a<\bar{x}_a<u_a$ are independent; the reduced system has integer data, and by Cramer's rule each entry is $\det(\mat{M}_a)/\det(\mat{M})$ with $\det(\mat{M})=\pm 1$ by total unimodularity.
no-f-080 · level ipotesi · notes
The lemma «flow across a cut» says: if $\vet{x}$ satisfies conservation, $x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))=\sum_{i \in S} b_i$ for every node set $S$. To prove that a network with a feasible flow is balanced ($\sum_i b_i=0$), what suffices?
  1. The lemma «flow across a cut» with $S=N$
  2. The total unimodularity of the incidence matrix
  3. The cut condition for every set $S$
  4. The max-flow min-cut theorem
Explanation. With $S=N$ no arc leaves or enters $S$, so $\sum_{i \in N} b_i=0$; equivalently, the rows of the incidence matrix add up to zero.
no-f-081 · level ipotesi · notes
For the $\mathrm{MCF}$ model with $\vet{0} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$, the dual has variables $\pi_i$ and $\alpha_{ij} \ge 0$ and constraints $\pi_i-\pi_j-\alpha_{ij} \le c_{ij}$. The duality gap is $\sum(\bar{c}_{ij}+\alpha_{ij})x_{ij}+\sum\alpha_{ij}(u_{ij}-x_{ij})$, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Why is it non-negative?
  1. Because the costs $c_{ij}$ are non-negative
  2. Because the incidence matrix is totally unimodular
  3. Because the potentials are normalized with $\pi_n=0$
  4. Because of the dual constraints, of $\alpha_{ij} \ge 0$ and of $0 \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$
Explanation. The dual constraint gives $\bar{c}_{ij}+\alpha_{ij} \ge 0$; with $\alpha_{ij} \ge 0$ and the bounds of the primal flow all terms are non-negative. Neither non-negative costs nor a normalization are needed.
no-f-082 · level facile · notes
A flow vector $\vet{x}$ satisfies conservation at every node (outflow minus inflow equal to the balance $b_i$). For a node set $S$ with $\sum_{i \in S} b_i=6$, what is $x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))$, the flow leaving $S$ minus the flow entering it?
  1. $0$
  2. $6$
  3. $-6$
  4. It depends on the arc capacities
Explanation. Adding up the conservation equations of the nodes of $S$ the inner arcs cancel: the net outflow of $S$ is the total balance of its nodes, $6$.
no-f-083 · level facile · notes
In the LP model $\mathrm{MCF}$ of the minimum cost flow on a network $G=(N,A)$ with balances, lower bounds, capacities and costs, how many variables and how many conservation constraints are there?
  1. One variable per node and one constraint per arc
  2. One variable per arc and one constraint per arc
  3. One variable per arc and one constraint per node
  4. One variable per node and one constraint per node
Explanation. The variable $x_{ij}$ is the flow on the arc $(i,j)$; the conservation constraint of node $i$ requires outflow minus inflow equal to $b_i$. The arcs give the bounds $l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ instead.
no-f-084 · level facile · notes
In an unbalanced minimum cost flow instance the total supply exceeds the total demand, $\sum_{i \in N} b_i>0$. How is the constraint of a supply node $i$ ($b_i>0$) written?
  1. Outflow minus inflow $\le b_i$
  2. Outflow minus inflow $= b_i$
  3. Outflow minus inflow $\ge b_i$
  4. Outflow $\le b_i$, without counting the inflow
Explanation. Every demand must be met, while a supply node sends at most $b_i$ net units: constraint $\le b_i$. With the dummy node, which absorbs the unused supply, the constraint becomes an equation again.
no-f-085 · level facile · notes
The undirected triangle with vertices $1,2,3$ has the vertex–edge incidence matrix $\mat{M}$ with rows $(1,1,0)$, $(1,0,1)$, $(0,1,1)$, and $\det(\mat{M})=-2$. What is the only solution of $\mat{M}\vet{y}=\vet{1}$, $\vet{y} \ge \vet{0}$?
  1. $(1,0,0)$
  2. $(1,1,1)$
  3. $(0,1,0)$
  4. $(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})$
Explanation. $\mat{M}$ is nonsingular, so the solution is unique: $(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})$, a fractional vertex with integer data. $\mat{M}$ is not totally unimodular: in the incidence matrices of directed graphs this cannot happen.
no-f-086 · level teorema · notes
An unbalanced instance ($\sum_i b_i=e>0$) is balanced by adding a dummy node $d$ with $b_d=-e$ and zero-cost arcs $(i,d)$ from the supply nodes. Which statement on the balanced network is correct?
  1. Its optimum is smaller than that of the unbalanced instance, since the arcs cost $0$
  2. Its feasible flows, restricted to $A$, are the solutions of the original instance, with the same cost
  3. It is equivalent only if every supply node sends all of its supply $b_i$
  4. It has feasible flows even when the original instance has none
Explanation. Proposition «the dummy node»: $x_{id}$ measures the unused supply of node $i$, and the correspondence keeps feasibility and cost, since the arcs $(i,d)$ cost zero.
no-f-200 · level media · notes
A minimum cost flow network has nodes $1,2,3$ with balances $\vet{b}=(4,0,-4)$ and the arcs $(1,2)$: cost $1$, capacity $3$; $(2,3)$: cost $2$, capacity $3$; $(1,3)$: cost $5$, infinite capacity. What is the minimum cost of a feasible flow?
  1. $14$
  2. $12$
  3. $20$
  4. $9$
Explanation. The path $1,2,3$ costs $3$ per unit but carries at most $3$ units; the remaining unit goes on the arc $(1,3)$: $3 \cdot 3+1 \cdot 5=14$. $12$ ignores the capacities, $20$ sends everything on $(1,3)$, $9$ forgets the last unit.
no-f-201 · level facile · notes
In the network with nodes $1,2,3$ and arcs $(1,2)$ (cost $1$), $(2,3)$ (cost $2$), $(1,3)$ (cost $5$), take the potentials $\vet{\pi}=(5,4,0)$. What are the reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ of the arcs $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(1,3)$, in this order?
  1. $(2,6,10)$
  2. $(0,2,0)$
  3. $(0,-2,0)$
  4. $(-8,-2,0)$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{12}=1-5+4=0$, $\bar{c}_{23}=2-4+0=-2$, $\bar{c}_{13}=5-5+0=0$. With a flow that saturates $(2,3)$ and lies between the bounds on the other two arcs, these potentials certify optimality. $(2,6,10)$ swaps the signs of the potentials.
no-f-202 · level facile · notes
The shortest path from $s$ to $t$ is written as a minimum cost flow model, with costs equal to the lengths and infinite capacities (conservation: outflow minus inflow equal to $b_i$). Which balances are used?
  1. $b_s=-1$, $b_t=1$, $b_i=0$ elsewhere
  2. $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$, $b_i=0$ elsewhere
  3. $b_s=n-1$, $b_i=-1$ for every other node
  4. $b_i=1$ for every node other than $t$
Explanation. One unit leaves $s$ and enters $t$: $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$. The balance $b_s=n-1$ with $-1$ elsewhere is that of the single-source model.
no-f-203 · level facile · notes
A flow on a network with nodes $1,2,3$ has $x_{12}=2$, $x_{13}=4$, $x_{32}=1$ (no other arc). Conservation requires, at every node, outflow minus inflow equal to the balance $b_i$. For which balances $\vet{b}=(b_1,b_2,b_3)$ does the flow satisfy conservation?
  1. $(-6,3,3)$
  2. $(6,-3,3)$
  3. $(6,3,-3)$
  4. $(6,-3,-3)$
Explanation. Node $1$: $2+4=6$ leave; node $2$: $2+1=3$ enter, hence $-3$; node $3$: $1$ leaves and $4$ enter, hence $-3$. The balances add up to zero, as in every network with a feasible flow.
no-f-326 · level facile · notes
In a network every node $i$ has a balance $b_i$: it is a supply node if $b_i>0$, a demand node if $b_i<0$, a transshipment node if $b_i=0$. The network has $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$. Which nodes are transshipment nodes?
  1. $4$ and $6$
  2. $1$ and $2$
  3. $3$ and $5$
  4. None
Explanation. The nodes with $b_i=0$ are $3$ and $5$: they pass on everything they receive. $1$ and $2$ supply $7$ units, $4$ and $6$ demand them.
no-f-327 · level facile · notes
In a network with lower bounds $l_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$ and balances $b_i$, when is a flow $\vet{x}$ feasible?
  1. If it respects conservation at every node and $l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ on every arc
  2. If it respects conservation at every node, even with negative flows
  3. If $x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ on every arc and the cost is minimum
  4. If the outflow of the supply nodes is at most $b_i$
Explanation. Feasible means conservation ($\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$) plus bounds on the arcs; the cost does not matter (optimality is a further requirement). The «at most $b_i$» constraint concerns only unbalanced instances.
no-f-328 · level facile · notes
In a network the demand node $j$ has $b_j=-4$. With the conservation constraint «outflow minus inflow equals $b_j$», what must happen at $j$?
  1. $4$ more units leave than enter
  2. No flow enters or leaves
  3. Exactly $4$ units enter, whatever leaves
  4. $4$ more units enter than leave
Explanation. Outflow minus inflow $=-4$ means that the inflow exceeds the outflow by $4$: the node keeps $4$ units. Other units may also pass through, entering and leaving.
no-f-329 · level facile · notes
A flow respects conservation at every node of a network with balances $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$. For $S=\{1,2,3\}$, the arcs leaving $S$ carry $8$ units in total. How much do the arcs entering $S$ carry?
  1. $7$
  2. $1$
  3. $15$
  4. $0$
Explanation. By the cut lemma $x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))=\sum_{i \in S}b_i=5+2+0=7$, so $x(\delta^-(S))=8-7=1$. $15$ adds instead of subtracting.
no-f-330 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: if $\vet{x}$ respects conservation at every node, for every $S \subseteq N$ we have $x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))=\sum_{i \in S}b_i$. The proof adds up the constraints of the nodes of $S$. What happens to an arc with both endpoints in $S$?
  1. It appears once with sign $+$
  2. It must be excluded explicitly from the sum
  3. It appears with $+x_{ij}$ in the row of the tail and $-x_{ij}$ in that of the head, and cancels
  4. It makes the sum differ from $\sum_{i \in S}b_i$
Explanation. Internal arcs cancel, those of $\delta^+(S)$ stay with $+$ and those of $\delta^-(S)$ with $-$. The lemma uses neither bounds nor costs: it holds for every vector respecting conservation.
no-f-331 · level facile · notes
In a network, $\delta^+(S)$ are the arcs with tail in $S$ and head outside, $\delta^-(S)$ those with head in $S$ and tail outside. The graph has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(3,2)$, $(4,3)$. For $S=\{1,2,3\}$, what are $\delta^+(S)$ and $\delta^-(S)$?
  1. $\delta^+(S)=\{(2,4)\}$, $\delta^-(S)=\{(4,3)\}$
  2. $\delta^+(S)=\{(4,3)\}$, $\delta^-(S)=\{(2,4)\}$
  3. $\delta^+(S)=\{(1,2),(1,3)\}$, $\delta^-(S)=\emptyset$
  4. $\delta^+(S)=\{(2,4),(4,3)\}$, $\delta^-(S)=\emptyset$
Explanation. The only node outside $S$ is $4$: $(2,4)$ leaves $S$, $(4,3)$ enters it. The arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(3,2)$ have both endpoints in $S$.
no-f-332 · level facile · notes
The minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ on a network with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs has one variable $x_{ij}$ per arc and one conservation constraint per node. How many variables and equations does it have?
  1. $n$ variables and $m$ equations
  2. $n+m$ variables and $n$ equations
  3. $m$ variables and $m$ equations
  4. $m$ variables and $n$ equations
Explanation. One variable per arc ($m$) and one conservation equation per node ($n$), plus the bounds $l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$. The reference network has $9$ variables and $6$ equations.
no-f-333 · level facile · notes
In the $\mathrm{MCF}$ model of a network, node $2$ has the incoming arc $(1,2)$, the outgoing arc $(2,4)$ and the incoming arc $(3,2)$, and balance $b_2=2$. What is its conservation constraint?
  1. $x_{12}+x_{24}+x_{32}=2$
  2. $-x_{12}+x_{24}-x_{32}=2$
  3. $x_{12}-x_{24}+x_{32}=2$
  4. $x_{24}=2$
Explanation. Outflow minus inflow: $x_{24}-x_{12}-x_{32}=2$. The third option is inflow minus outflow, that is, the constraint with reversed signs.
no-f-334 · level teorema · notes
Let $G$ be a network with balances $b_i$, lower bounds $l_{ij}$ and capacities $u_{ij}$, and for $F \subseteq A$ let $u(F)$, $l(F)$ be the sums of capacities and lower bounds. Which condition is necessary for a feasible flow to exist?
  1. $\sum_{i \in S}b_i \le u(\delta^+(S))-l(\delta^-(S))$ for every $S \subseteq N$
  2. $\sum_{i \in S}b_i \ge u(\delta^+(S))$ for every $S$
  3. $\sum_{i \in S}b_i=0$ for every $S$
  4. $b_i \le u(\delta^+(i))$ only for the supply nodes
Explanation. The net supply of $S$ must leave on $\delta^+(S)$, at most $u(\delta^+(S))$, while $\delta^-(S)$ brings back into $S$ at least $l(\delta^-(S))$. Zero balance holds for $S=N$, not for every $S$; checking single nodes only is not enough.
no-f-335 · level facile · notes
In the substituted network that removes the lower bounds one sets $\vet{b}'=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$ and $u'_{ij}=u_{ij}-l_{ij}$. The arc $(3,2)$ has $l_{32}=1$, $u_{32}=2$; all other lower bounds are zero, and $b_3=0$, $b_2=2$. What are $b'_3$, $b'_2$, $u'_{32}$?
  1. $b'_3=1$, $b'_2=1$, $u'_{32}=2$
  2. $b'_3=0$, $b'_2=2$, $u'_{32}=1$
  3. $b'_3=-1$, $b'_2=3$, $u'_{32}=1$
  4. $b'_3=1$, $b'_2=3$, $u'_{32}=3$
Explanation. The forced unit is sent in advance: the tail $3$ has one unit less to send ($b'_3=0-1=-1$), the head $2$ one unit less to receive ($b'_2=2+1=3$), and the arc one unit less capacity ($u'_{32}=1$).
no-f-336 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: $\vet{x}'=\vet{x}-\vet{l}$ is a one-to-one correspondence between the feasible flows of the network with lower bounds and those of the substituted network ($\vet{b}'=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$, $\vet{u}'=\vet{u}-\vet{l}$). Which fact makes the transformation of the conservation constraints correct?
  1. Total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$
  2. The fact that costs are nonnegative
  3. The fact that the network is acyclic
  4. Linearity: $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$ if and only if $\mat{B}(\vet{x}-\vet{l})=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$
Explanation. The translation by $\vet{l}$ is linear and invertible: conservation and bounds transform exactly, and the cost changes by the constant $\vet{c}'\vet{l}$. Sign of costs, acyclicity and total unimodularity are not needed.
no-f-337 · level facile · notes
The lower bounds of a network are removed with the substituted network. The only positive bound is $l_{32}=1$, with cost $c_{32}=2$, and the optimal value of the substituted network is $58$. What is the optimal value of the original network?
  1. $60$
  2. $58$
  3. $56$
  4. $59$
Explanation. The costs differ by the constant $\vet{c}'\vet{l}=c_{32}l_{32}=2$, to be added: $58+2=60$. $59$ adds only $l_{32}$, forgetting the cost.
no-f-338 · level facile · notes
In an unbalanced instance the supply nodes have $b_1=5$, $b_2=4$ and the demand nodes $b_4=-3$, $b_6=-4$. It is balanced with a dummy node $7$ and arcs $(1,7)$, $(2,7)$ of cost $0$ and infinite capacity. What is $b_7$?
  1. $2$
  2. $-2$
  3. $-9$
  4. $-7$
Explanation. The excess is $e=\sum_i b_i=9-7=2$, and the dummy node absorbs it as a demand: $b_7=-2$. The flow on $(i,7)$ is the supply not shipped by node $i$.
no-f-339 · level facile · notes
A square submatrix of the node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ has in every column exactly one $+1$ and one $-1$. What is its determinant?
  1. $+1$
  2. $-1$
  3. $0$
  4. It depends on the order of the columns
Explanation. The rows add up to the zero vector, so they are linearly dependent and the determinant is $0$. It is the third case of the proof that $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular.
no-f-340 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: the node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ is totally unimodular. The proof, by induction on the size $k$ of a submatrix $\mat{M}$, distinguishes three cases. Which property of $\mat{B}$ makes them exhaustive?
  1. $\mat{B}$ has rank $n-1$
  2. Every column of $\mat{B}$ has exactly one $+1$ and one $-1$, so every column of $\mat{M}$ has at most two nonzero entries
  3. Every row of $\mat{B}$ has at most two nonzero entries
  4. $\mat{B}$ is square
Explanation. A column of $\mat{M}$ is zero, or has a single $\pm1$ (Laplace expansion and induction), or all have $+1$ and $-1$ (rows summing to zero, determinant $0$). Rows may have many nonzero entries, and $\mat{B}$ is not square in general.
no-f-341 · level teorema · notes
Let $P=\{\vet{x} : \mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b},\ \vet{l} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}\}$ be the flow polyhedron of a network. Which statement about integrality is correct?
  1. Every point of $P$ is integer if $\vet{b}$ is integer
  2. The vertices of $P$ are integer only if the costs are also integer
  3. The vertices of $P$ are integer only if the network is acyclic
  4. If $\vet{b}$, $\vet{l}$ are integer and $\vet{u}$ is integer or $+\infty$, every vertex of $P$ is integer
Explanation. Total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$ makes the vertices integer with integer data; the costs do not matter (they decide which vertex is optimal, not where the vertices are). Interior points of $P$ may be fractional.
no-f-342 · level facile · notes
The maximum flow problem from $s$ to $t$ is written as a minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ with all balances zero and zero costs on the arcs. What is added?
  1. A dummy node with balance $-1$
  2. Balances $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$
  3. Costs $c_{ij}=-1$ on all arcs
  4. The arc $(t,s)$ with cost $-1$ and infinite capacity: the flow on it is the value sent
Explanation. With the return arc $(t,s)$ of cost $-1$ the model minimizes $-x_{ts}$, that is, maximizes the flow coming back from $t$ to $s$, equal to the value sent from $s$ to $t$. Balances $1,-1$ give the shortest path.
no-f-343 · level facile · notes
The assignment problem is written as a special case of the minimum cost flow model. Which special data does it have?
  1. A single supply node and a single demand node
  2. Arcs from $P$ to $D$ with $|P|=|D|$, balances $b_i=1$ for $i \in P$ and $b_j=-1$ for $j \in D$
  3. Zero balances and a return arc
  4. Capacities $u_{ij}=1$ and arbitrary balances
Explanation. Assignment is a transportation problem with unit supplies and demands and $|P|=|D|$; the integer vertices of the polyhedron are exactly the assignments. A single supply and demand node give a path or a flow.
no-f-344 · level facile · notes
In the optimal solution of a minimum cost flow model with $\vet{0} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$, every arc is empty ($x_{ij}=0$), saturated ($x_{ij}=u_{ij}$) or free ($0<x_{ij}<u_{ij}$). Which property do the free arcs have in a basic solution?
  1. They all have negative reduced cost
  2. They all leave the source
  3. They form at least one cycle
  4. They form no cycle (they are a forest)
Explanation. The columns of the free arcs of a vertex are linearly independent, so they contain no cycle; in the reference network the five free arcs form a spanning tree. Free arcs have zero reduced cost.
no-f-345 · level facile · notes
An integer optimal flow has been found for a network with integer balances, capacities and bounds. Which result guarantees that a vertex-based LP solver returns an integer flow, without integrality constraints?
  1. The integer vertices theorem (total unimodularity of $\mat{B}$)
  2. Dijkstra's theorem
  3. The cut condition
  4. The handshake lemma
Explanation. With integer data every vertex of the flow polyhedron is integer, and the simplex returns an optimal vertex: no integrality constraints. The cut condition concerns feasibility.
no-f-346 · level facile · notes
In a minimum cost flow network the arc $(i,j)$ has cost $c_{ij}=3$ and capacity $u_{ij}=4$, and carries $x_{ij}=4$ in the optimal flow. With optimal potentials $\pi_i=13$, $\pi_j=7$, what is the reduced cost $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, and is it consistent with optimality?
  1. $9$: consistent, the arc is saturated
  2. $-3$: consistent, a saturated arc may have negative reduced cost
  3. $-3$: not consistent, $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ would be needed
  4. $0$: the arc is free
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{ij}=3-13+7=-3<0$, and an arc with negative reduced cost must be saturated: $x_{ij}=u_{ij}=4$ is consistent. Only arcs with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$ must be empty.
no-f-347 · level facile · notes
To certify an optimal flow one imposes zero reduced cost, $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$, on the free arcs, which form a spanning tree, with $\pi_6=0$. The arcs $(4,6)$ and $(5,4)$ are free, with $c_{46}=5$, $c_{54}=5$. What are $\pi_4$ and $\pi_5$?
  1. $\pi_4=-5$, $\pi_5=-10$
  2. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_5=10$
  3. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_5=5$
  4. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_5=0$
Explanation. $\pi_4=\pi_6+c_{46}=5$ and $\pi_5=\pi_4+c_{54}=10$: the potential is the cost of the tree path to node $6$. The first option reverses the sign of $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$.
no-f-348 · level facile · notes
In the optimality certificate of a flow with $\vet{0} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$, the dual has value $\sum_i b_i\pi_i-\sum_{(i,j)}u_{ij}\alpha_{ij}$, with $\alpha_{ij}=\max\{0,-\bar{c}_{ij}\}$. If $\sum_i b_i\pi_i=86$ and the saturated arcs with negative reduced cost have $u_{ij}\alpha_{ij}$ equal to $12$ and $14$, what is the dual value?
  1. $86$
  2. $112$
  3. $72$
  4. $60$
Explanation. $86-(12+14)=60$, equal to the cost of the flow: the gap is zero and the flow is optimal. $112$ adds instead of subtracting; $72$ subtracts only one term.
no-f-349 · level teorema · notes
For the model $\mathrm{MCF}$ with $\vet{0} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$ let $\vet{x}$ be feasible and $\vet{\pi}$ potentials, with reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Which set of conditions guarantees that $\vet{x}$ is optimal?
  1. $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on all arcs
  2. $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$ on all arcs with positive flow
  3. $\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=0$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$
  4. $x_{ij}=u_{ij}$ on all arcs with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$
Explanation. These are the complementary slackness conditions: arcs with positive reduced cost empty, negative saturated, free only with zero reduced cost. With capacities one cannot require $\bar{c} \ge 0$ everywhere: a saturated arc may have $\bar{c}<0$.
no-f-350 · level facile · notes
In an optimal minimum cost flow, the arc $(3,2)$ has reduced cost $\bar{c}_{32}=c_{32}-\pi_3+\pi_2=2>0$ with respect to the optimal potentials. What is $x_{32}$ in every optimal flow?
  1. $0$
  2. $u_{32}$
  3. $2$
  4. Any value between $0$ and $u_{32}$
Explanation. By complementary slackness an arc with positive reduced cost is empty in every optimal solution: sending flow on it would increase the cost. Arcs with negative reduced cost are instead saturated.
no-f-498 · level facile · notes
A flow network is balanced if $\sum_{i \in N} b_i=0$ (total supply equal to total demand). Which network with balances $\vet{b}$ is balanced?
  1. $\vet{b}=(5,2,-3,-3)$
  2. $\vet{b}=(4,0,0,4)$
  3. $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$
  4. $\vet{b}=(1,1,1,-2)$
Explanation. $5+2+0-3+0-4=0$. The other sums are $1$, $8$ and $1$. Being balanced is necessary for a feasible flow, but not sufficient.

5. Maximum flow (56)

no-f-087 · level facile · notes
In the maximum flow problem, the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ of a flow $\vet{x}$ has, for every arc $(i,j)$, a forward and a backward arc with positive residual capacities $r$. The arc $(i,j)$ has capacity $u_{ij}=7$ and flow $x_{ij}=5$. Which arcs does it give in $G(\vet{x})$?
  1. $(i,j)$ with $r_{ij}=2$ and $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=5$
  2. $(i,j)$ with $r_{ij}=2$ and $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=7$
  3. Only $(i,j)$, with $r_{ij}=2$
  4. $(i,j)$ with $r_{ij}=5$ and $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=2$
Explanation. The forward arc has $r_{ij}=u_{ij}-x_{ij}=2$, the backward arc $r_{ji}=x_{ij}=5$: only the flow already sent can be sent back, not the capacity.
no-f-088 · level facile · notes
An augmenting path from $s$ to $t$ in the residual network uses the backward arc $(j,i)$, which corresponds to the arc $(i,j)$ of the network. What happens to the flow $x_{ij}$ when augmenting along the path by $\delta$?
  1. It increases by $\delta$
  2. It does not change
  3. It decreases by $\delta$
  4. It becomes $u_{ij}-\delta$
Explanation. Along a backward arc part of the flow already sent is undone: $x_{ij}:=x_{ij}-\delta$, and $\delta \le r_{ji}=x_{ij}$ guarantees $x_{ij} \ge 0$.
no-f-089 · level facile · notes
In the maximum flow problem from $s$ to $t$, a feasible flow has value $v=6$ and an $s$–$t$ cut (a set $S$ with $s \in S$, $t \notin S$) has capacity $9$. Let $\tilde{v}$ be the maximum flow value. What can be concluded?
  1. The maximum flow value is $\tilde{v}=9$
  2. The maximum flow value lies between $6$ and $9$
  3. The given flow is not maximum
  4. The given cut is not minimum
Explanation. By weak duality every flow value is at most every cut capacity: $6 \le \tilde{v} \le 9$. Without more information neither the flow nor the cut is certainly optimal or non-optimal.
no-f-090 · level facile · notes
The Ford–Fulkerson algorithm augments the flow along paths from $s$ to $t$ of the residual network and has stopped with a maximum flow, since $t$ is no longer reachable. How is a minimum $s$–$t$ cut read?
  1. Taking the saturated arcs of the final flow
  2. Taking the arcs with zero flow
  3. Taking $S=\{s\}$, the source alone
  4. Taking as $S$ the nodes reachable from $s$ in the residual network
Explanation. In the residual network of the final flow the nodes reachable from $s$ form $S$, and $(S,N \setminus S)$ is a minimum cut. The saturated arcs do not form a cut in general.
no-f-091 · level facile · notes
For the $s$–$t$ cut $(S,N \setminus S)$ of a capacitated network, the forward arcs (from $S$ to $N \setminus S$) have capacities $3$ and $4$ and the only backward arc (from $N \setminus S$ to $S$) has capacity $2$. What is the cut capacity $u(S,N \setminus S)$?
  1. $7$
  2. $9$
  3. $5$
  4. $2$
Explanation. The capacity of a cut adds up only the forward arcs, from $S$ to $N \setminus S$: $3+4=7$. Adding the backward arc too gives $9$, subtracting it gives $5$.
no-f-092 · level facile · notes
The Ford–Fulkerson algorithm starts from the zero flow and augments along paths of the residual network. With integer capacities and a maximum flow of value $\tilde{v}=40$, on a network with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs, at most how many augmentations does it make?
  1. $n\,m$
  2. $n-1$
  3. $40$
  4. $2^{40}$
Explanation. With integer capacities each augmentation raises the value by at least $1$: at most $\tilde{v}=40$ augmentations. The bound is pseudopolynomial; the bound $n\,m$ is that of Edmonds–Karp.
no-f-093 · level facile · notes
The Ford–Fulkerson algorithm looks for augmenting paths from $s$ to $t$ in the residual network with any search. What distinguishes the Edmonds–Karp algorithm from Ford–Fulkerson with the depth-first search?
  1. It uses the residual network without the backward arcs
  2. It finds the paths with the breadth-first search, hence with the fewest arcs
  3. It always augments the flow by $1$ unit at a time
  4. It chooses the path with the largest bottleneck
Explanation. Edmonds–Karp is Ford–Fulkerson with the BFS ($\mathit{LIST}$ a queue): the paths have the minimum number of arcs, and there are at most $n\,m$ augmentations for any capacities.
no-f-094 · level facile · notes
A matching of a bipartite graph $H=(V_1 \cup V_2,E)$ is a set of edges without common endpoints. The network of $H$ has the arcs $(s,i)$, $i \in V_1$, $(i,j)$ for every edge, $(j,t)$, $j \in V_2$, all with capacity $1$, and the maximum flow value is $4$. What do we conclude?
  1. $H$ has exactly $4$ edges
  2. $V_1$ has exactly $4$ vertices
  3. Every matching of $H$ has $4$ edges
  4. A maximum matching of $H$ has $4$ edges
Explanation. The maximum number of edges of a matching is the maximum flow value; an integer maximum flow gives a maximum matching, $M=\{\{i,j\} : \tilde{x}_{ij}=1\}$.
no-f-095 · level facile · notes
In a directed graph the minimum number of arcs whose removal leaves no directed path from $s$ to $t$ is $3$. Two paths are arc-disjoint if they share no arc. What does Menger's theorem say?
  1. The maximum number of pairwise arc-disjoint paths from $s$ to $t$ is $3$
  2. The paths from $s$ to $t$ are $3$ in all
  3. Every path from $s$ to $t$ has exactly $3$ arcs
  4. There are $3$ paths from $s$ to $t$ with pairwise no inner node in common
Explanation. The maximum number of arc-disjoint paths from $s$ to $t$ equals the minimum number of arcs whose removal separates $t$ from $s$: both are $3$. It is the max-flow min-cut theorem with capacities $1$.
no-f-096 · level facile · notes
In the maximum flow problem on a network with source $s$, sink $t$ and arc capacities, what is the value $v$ of a feasible flow?
  1. The sum of the flows on all the arcs of the network
  2. The flow leaving $s$ minus the flow entering $s$
  3. The flow on the most loaded arc of the network
  4. The sum of the capacities of the arcs leaving $s$
Explanation. The value is the net outflow of the source, equal to the net inflow of the sink and to the net flow across every $s$–$t$ cut.
no-f-097 · level media · notes
Ford–Fulkerson stops: in the residual network of the final flow the nodes reachable from $s$ are $S=\{s,a,b\}$. The arcs of the network from $S$ to $N \setminus S$ have capacities $4$, $2$, $3$, and one arc of the network from $N \setminus S$ to $S$ has capacity $5$. What is the maximum flow value?
  1. $14$
  2. $4$
  3. $9$
  4. It cannot be said without the flow
Explanation. At the stop the forward arcs of the cut are saturated and the backward arcs empty, so $v=u(S,N \setminus S)=4+2+3=9$, and the cut is minimum. The capacity of the backward arc does not count.
no-f-098 · level teorema · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ be a feasible flow from $s$ to $t$ of value $v$, and $G(\vet{x})$ its residual network. Which statement of the max-flow min-cut theorem is correct?
