  1. $\vet{x}$ is maximum if and only if $G(\vet{x})$ has no path from $s$ to $t$, if and only if $v$ is the capacity of a cut
  2. $\vet{x}$ is maximum if and only if it saturates all the arcs leaving the source $s$
  3. $\vet{x}$ is maximum if and only if the saturated arcs form an $s$–$t$ cut
  4. The maximum flow value equals the maximum capacity of an $s$–$t$ cut
Explanation. The three conditions (maximum flow, no augmenting path, value equal to a cut capacity) are equivalent, and $\tilde{v}$ is the minimum, not the maximum, capacity of a cut.
no-f-099 · level teorema · notes
The Edmonds–Karp algorithm is Ford–Fulkerson with the augmenting paths found by the breadth-first search. On a network with $n$ nodes, $m \ge n-1$ arcs and maximum flow $\tilde{v}$, which statement is correct?
  1. It needs integer capacities and makes at most $\tilde{v}$ augmentations
  2. It makes at most $n-1$ augmentations, one per node other than $s$
  3. It terminates only if the capacities are rational
  4. For any non-negative capacities it makes at most $n\,m$ augmentations, in $O(n\,m^2)$ time
Explanation. With the BFS the distances in the residual network never decrease and every arc is critical at most $n/2$ times: at most $n\,m$ augmentations, for any real capacities. The bound $\tilde{v}$ with integer capacities is that of Ford–Fulkerson.
no-f-100 · level ipotesi · notes
In the max-flow min-cut theorem, (b) says «$G(\vet{x})$ has no path from $s$ to $t$» and (c) «$v=u(S,N \setminus S)$ for some $s$–$t$ cut». In the proof of (b) $\Rightarrow$ (c), how is $S$ chosen and what is used?
  1. $S=\{s\}$, and strong LP duality
  2. $S$ = nodes reachable from $s$ in $G(\vet{x})$, and the lemma «flow across a cut»
  3. $S$ = nodes incident to saturated arcs, and total unimodularity
  4. $S=N \setminus \{t\}$, and the Edmonds–Karp algorithm
Explanation. With $S$ the nodes reachable from $s$, an unsaturated arc from $S$ to $N \setminus S$, or a backward one carrying flow, would make a node outside $S$ reachable. Hence the net flow across the cut, which is $v$, equals $u(S,N \setminus S)$.
no-f-101 · level ipotesi · notes
The Ford–Fulkerson algorithm augments the flow, starting from zero, along paths of the residual network, by the bottleneck $\delta$ (minimum residual capacity of the path). To prove that it terminates when the capacities are integer, which fact is used?
  1. The breadth-first search finds shortest paths
  2. The distances in the residual network never decrease
  3. Every arc is critical at most $n/2$ times
  4. The flow stays integer, so $\delta \ge 1$, and the value is at most $\tilde{v}$
Explanation. With an integer flow the residual capacities are integer, every $\delta$ is a positive integer and the value is bounded by the maximum $\tilde{v}$: at most $\tilde{v}$ augmentations. The other facts belong to the proof of Edmonds–Karp.
no-f-102 · level facile · notes
A feasible flow from $s$ to $t$ has value $v=8$. For an $s$–$t$ cut $(S,N \setminus S)$, the arcs from $S$ to $N \setminus S$ carry $11$ units in total. How much flow do the arcs from $N \setminus S$ to $S$ carry in total?
  1. $0$
  2. $19$
  3. $3$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Lemma «flow across a cut»: $v=x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))$, so $x(\delta^-(S))=11-8=3$. The net flow across every $s$–$t$ cut is the flow value.
no-f-103 · level facile · notes
A feasible flow from $s$ to $t$ has value $v=10$. In the residual network there is an augmenting path from $s$ to $t$ with residual capacities $4$, $2$, $7$ (one of them backward). Augmenting along the path by the bottleneck $\delta$, what is the new flow value?
  1. $12$
  2. $23$
  3. $14$
  4. $10$, since there is a backward arc
Explanation. The bottleneck is the minimum residual capacity, $\delta=2$; by the lemma «augmentation» the new flow is feasible and has value $v+\delta=12$, even if the path uses backward arcs.
no-f-104 · level facile · notes
With integer capacities, Ford–Fulkerson takes $O((n+m)\,\tilde{v})$ time, and $\tilde{v} \le (n-1)\,U$ where $U$ is the largest capacity. Why is this bound not polynomial in the size of the instance?
  1. Because $n+m$ can be exponential in $n$
  2. Because with integer capacities the algorithm may not terminate
  3. Because the number of paths from $s$ to $t$ can be exponential
  4. Because it grows with the value $U$, not with its digits $\log U$
Explanation. The running time is pseudopolynomial: doubling the digits of the capacities can multiply the number of augmentations. Edmonds–Karp removes the dependence on $U$ with at most $n\,m$ augmentations.
no-f-105 · level facile · notes
In a network with source $s$, sink $t$ and all capacities integer, what can be said about the maximum flows?
  1. Every maximum flow has all entries integer
  2. There is a maximum flow with all entries integer
  3. An integer maximum flow exists only if the capacities are even
  4. The maximum value may be fractional
Explanation. Corollary «integrality»: Ford–Fulkerson, starting from the zero flow, always augments by integer amounts and returns an integer maximum flow. Fractional maximum flows may exist as well.
no-f-106 · level teorema · notes
In the Edmonds–Karp algorithm a residual arc $(i,j)$ is critical in an augmentation if it has the minimum residual capacity of the path; $d_{\vet{x}}(i)$ is the minimum number of arcs from $s$ to $i$ in the residual network of $\vet{x}$. Which statement is correct?
  1. An arc can be critical at most once in the whole run
  2. Between two times $(i,j)$ is critical, $d(i)$ stays the same
  3. If $(i,j)$ is critical at $\vet{x}$ and again at $\vet{y}$, then $d_{\vet{y}}(i) \ge d_{\vet{x}}(i)+2$
  4. If $(i,j)$ is critical at $\vet{x}$ and again at $\vet{y}$, then $d_{\vet{y}}(i) \le d_{\vet{x}}(i)-2$
Explanation. Lemma «critical arcs»: between the two times an augmentation used the opposite arc $(j,i)$, and with non-decreasing distances $d(i)$ grows by at least $2$. Since $d(i) \le n-1$, every arc is critical at most about $n/2$ times.
no-f-204 · level facile · notes
In the LP model of the maximum flow on a network with source $s$, sink $t$ and capacities $u_{ij}$, the flow value $v$ is maximized. Which constraint holds at every node $i \notin \{s,t\}$?
  1. Flow leaving $i$ equal to flow entering $i$
  2. Outflow minus inflow equal to $v$
  3. Zero flow entering $i$
  4. Flow leaving $i$ equal to the sum of the leaving capacities
Explanation. Intermediate nodes conserve flow; only $s$ has net outflow $v$ and only $t$ net inflow $v$. The capacities give the constraints $0 \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ on the arcs.
no-f-351 · level facile · notes
In the maximum flow problem from a source $s$ to a sink $t$, with capacities $u_{ij}$ on the arcs, which constraint does not hold for a feasible flow?
  1. $0 \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ on every arc
  2. Conservation at every node other than $s$ and $t$
  3. Conservation also at $s$ and $t$
  4. The zero flow is feasible
Explanation. At $s$ and $t$ conservation is not imposed: the net outflow of $s$ is the value $v$ and the net inflow of $t$ is also $v$. The zero flow, of value $0$, is always feasible.
no-f-352 · level facile · notes
In the maximum flow problem from a source $s$ to a sink $t$, with capacities $u_{ij}$ on the arcs, the arcs $(1,2)$ with $x_{12}=3$ and $(1,3)$ with $x_{13}=4$ leave the source $s=1$, and no arc enters it. What is the value of the flow?
  1. $7$
  2. $4$
  3. $3$
  4. It cannot be told without the flows at $t$
Explanation. The value is the net outflow of the source: $3+4-0=7$. By the cut lemma it is also the net inflow of $t$, so there is no need to look at $t$.
no-f-353 · level facile · notes
The LP model of the maximum flow has variables $x_{ij}$ on the arcs and a variable $v$, the value. What kind of variable is $v$, and what is maximized?
  1. $v \ge 0$ integer; $\sum x_{ij}$ is maximized
  2. $v$ free; $v$ is maximized
  3. $v \le \sum u_{ij}$; $-\sum x_{ij}$ is minimized
  4. $v$ binary; $v$ is maximized
Explanation. The objective is $\max v$, with $v$ free (the constraints decide its sign) and the constraints «net outflow of $s$ equal to $v$», «net inflow of $t$ equal to $v$». Maximizing $\sum x_{ij}$ would reward flows going around cycles.
no-f-354 · level facile · notes
In the LP model of the maximum flow the source constraint is «outflow of $s$ minus inflow equals $v$». What is the constraint of the sink $t$?
  1. Outflow of $t$ minus inflow equals $v$
  2. Outflow of $t$ minus inflow equals $0$
  3. Inflow of $t$ at most $v$
  4. Outflow of $t$ minus inflow equals $-v$
Explanation. The sink absorbs the value: its net outflow is $-v$. With $0$ the model would impose conservation at $t$ too, and the only possible flow would have value zero.
no-f-355 · level facile · notes
The LP model of the maximum flow ($\max v$, conservation with $v$ at $s$ and $-v$ at $t$, $0 \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$) is a case of the minimum cost flow model. How can this be seen?
  1. By adding the arc $(t,s)$ with cost $-1$ and infinite capacity, whose flow is $v$, with zero balances
  2. By setting $b_s=1$, $b_t=-1$ and zero costs
  3. By setting all costs equal to $1$
  4. It is not a case of minimum cost flow
Explanation. With the return arc $(t,s)$ all nodes conserve flow, and $\min -x_{ts}$ is equivalent to $\max v$. Balances $1,-1$ would send exactly one unit.
no-f-356 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the LP model of the maximum flow with finite capacities $u_{ij}$ is correct?
  1. It may be unbounded if the network has cycles
  2. It may be infeasible if the capacities are small
  3. It always has an optimal solution: the zero flow is feasible and $v$ is bounded by the capacity of every cut
  4. It has an optimal solution only if $s$ reaches $t$
Explanation. The zero flow is feasible, and $v \le u(S,N \setminus S)$ for every $s$–$t$ cut, so the value is bounded and an optimum exists. If $t$ is not reachable the optimum still exists and equals $0$.
no-f-357 · level facile · notes
In a network with source $s$ and sink $t$, a network with $n=6$ nodes has how many $s$–$t$ cuts $(S,N \setminus S)$ with $s \in S$, $t \notin S$?
  1. $64$
  2. $16$
  3. $6$
  4. $30$
Explanation. Each of the $n-2=4$ nodes other than $s$ and $t$ can be in $S$ or outside: $2^4=16$ cuts. $2^6=64$ counts all subsets of nodes, without fixing $s$ and $t$.
no-f-358 · level facile · notes
In maximum flow, the residual network of a flow $\vet{x}$ has the forward arc $(i,j)$ with capacity $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$ if positive, and the backward arc $(j,i)$ with capacity $x_{ij}$ if positive. An arc $(2,4)$ has $u_{24}=3$ and $x_{24}=3$. Which residual arcs does it generate?
  1. $(2,4)$ with capacity $3$
  2. $(2,4)$ with capacity $0$ and $(4,2)$ with capacity $3$
  3. None
  4. Only $(4,2)$, with capacity $3$
Explanation. A saturated arc cannot carry more flow, so it gives no forward arc; it gives the backward arc $(4,2)$, since up to $3$ units can be removed. Arcs of zero residual capacity are not in the residual network.
no-f-359 · level facile · notes
Ford–Fulkerson augments the flow along a path $P$ of the residual network by $\delta$, the minimum residual capacity on $P$. The path $1,3,4,6$ has residual capacities $4,2,3$, and its residual arc $(3,4)$ is the backward arc of the network arc $(4,3)$. What happens after the augmentation?
  1. $\delta=2$; $x_{13}$ and $x_{46}$ grow by $2$ and $x_{43}$ decreases by $2$
  2. $\delta=4$; all flows of the path grow by $4$
  3. $\delta=2$; $x_{43}$ also grows by $2$
  4. $\delta=3$; $x_{43}$ decreases by $3$
Explanation. The bottleneck is $\min\{4,2,3\}=2$. On forward arcs the flow grows, on the backward arc the flow of the original arc $(4,3)$ decreases: this is how Ford–Fulkerson corrects earlier choices.
no-f-360 · level facile · notes
Ford–Fulkerson with integer capacities stops after five augmentations with bottlenecks $\delta=2,1,2,1,1$, starting from the zero flow. What is the value of the maximum flow?
  1. $5$
  2. $2$
  3. $7$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Each augmentation increases the value by $\delta$: $2+1+2+1+1=7$. $5$ is the number of augmentations, not the value.
no-f-361 · level facile · notes
Ford–Fulkerson has stopped: in the residual network of the final flow only the nodes $S=\{1,2\}$ are reachable from $s=1$. The network arcs from $S$ to the other nodes are $(1,3)$ with $u_{13}=4$ and $(2,4)$ with $u_{24}=3$. What is concluded?
  1. The flow is not maximum yet
  2. The maximum flow is $4$
  3. The maximum flow is $3$
  4. $(S,N \setminus S)$ is a minimum cut and the maximum flow is $7$
Explanation. Without residual paths from $s$ to $t$ the forward arcs of the cut are saturated and the backward ones empty: $v=u(S,N \setminus S)=4+3=7$, and the cut is minimum by the max-flow min-cut theorem.
no-f-362 · level facile · notes
In Ford–Fulkerson an augmentation uses a backward residual arc $(j,i)$, corresponding to the network arc $(i,j)$ with positive flow. What does it mean in terms of the original flow?
  1. Flow is sent in the opposite direction on $(i,j)$, which becomes negative
  2. Flow is removed from $(i,j)$, rerouting units that used to travel on that arc
  3. The capacity of $(i,j)$ is increased
  4. A new arc $(j,i)$ is created in the network
Explanation. Decreasing $x_{ij}$ by $\delta$ reroutes $\delta$ units that reached $j$ from $i$ towards the rest of the augmenting path. The flow stays between $0$ and $u_{ij}$; the network does not change.
no-f-363 · level facile · notes
In the network with arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(3,4)$ of capacity $M$ and $(2,3)$ of capacity $1$ (with $s=1$, $t=4$), Ford–Fulkerson allows any augmenting path. In the worst case, how many augmentations can it make?
  1. $2$
  2. $4$
  3. $2M$
  4. $M$
Explanation. Alternating $1,2,3,4$ and $1,3,2,4$ (which uses $(2,3)$ backward) every bottleneck is $1$, and the maximum value $2M$ needs $2M$ augmentations. With the paths $1,2,4$ and $1,3,4$ two augmentations suffice.
no-f-364 · level facile · notes
Ford–Fulkerson with integer capacities takes time $O((n+m)\,\tilde{v})$, where $\tilde{v}$ is the maximum value. Why is this time called pseudopolynomial?
  1. Because it depends on the numerical value $\tilde{v}$, which can be exponential in the length of the data
  2. Because it is polynomial only on acyclic graphs
  3. Because it does not depend on $n$
  4. Because it holds only on average
Explanation. The number of augmentations is bounded by $\tilde{v}$, which grows with the value of the capacities and not with the number of bits to write them: with $M=10^6$ two million augmentations may be needed. Edmonds–Karp removes this dependence.
no-f-365 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: with integer capacities Ford–Fulkerson stops after at most $\tilde{v}$ augmentations. Which fact is needed to count the augmentations?
  1. Every augmenting path has at most $n-1$ arcs
  2. The residual network has at most $2m$ arcs
  3. Distances in the residual network do not decrease
  4. Flow and residual capacities stay integer, so every augmentation increases the value by at least $1$
Explanation. Starting from the zero flow and with integer capacities, every $\delta$ is a positive integer: the value grows by at least $1$ and does not exceed $\tilde{v}$. Nondecreasing distances are used for Edmonds–Karp; with irrational capacities the argument fails.
no-f-366 · level facile · notes
In the network with arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(3,4)$ of capacity $M$ and $(2,3)$ of capacity $1$ ($s=1$, $t=4$), how many augmentations does Edmonds–Karp (paths with the minimum number of arcs) make?
  1. $2$
  2. $2M$
  3. $3$
  4. $M+1$
Explanation. The BFS first finds $1,2,4$ and then $1,3,4$, both with $2$ arcs and bottleneck $M$: two augmentations and value $2M$. The path $1,2,3,4$ has $3$ arcs and is never chosen.
no-f-367 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: in Edmonds–Karp the distances $d_{\vet{x}}(i)$ (minimum number of arcs from $s$ to $i$ in the residual network) do not decrease from one augmentation to the next. Which fact about the arcs entering the residual network is the core of the proof?
  1. They are arcs towards the sink $t$
  2. They are reverses of arcs of the augmenting path, which goes by increasing distance: they go from distance $k+1$ to $k$
  3. They always have residual capacity $1$
  4. They are arcs of the original network
Explanation. An augmentation creates only arcs $(j,i)$ reversing arcs $(i,j)$ of the BFS path, with $d(j)=d(i)+1$: an arc going down one level shortens no path from $s$. Residual capacities are arbitrary.
no-f-368 · level facile · notes
Edmonds–Karp on the reference network of the chapter augments along $1,2,4,6$ ($\delta=3$), $1,3,5,6$ ($\delta=3$) and $1,3,5,4,6$ ($\delta=1$). What is observed about the number of arcs of the paths?
  1. It decreases at every augmentation
  2. It is always $3$
  3. It never decreases: $3,3,4$
  4. It grows by one at every augmentation
Explanation. Distances in the residual network do not decrease, so neither does the length of the augmenting path: $3,3,4$. It need not grow at every step: several augmentations can have the same length.
no-f-369 · level facile · notes
In Edmonds–Karp an arc of the augmenting path is critical if its residual capacity equals the bottleneck $\delta$. What happens to it after the augmentation?
  1. It leaves the residual network, since its residual capacity becomes $0$
  2. Its residual capacity doubles
  3. It becomes a backward arc of the sink
  4. It stays in the residual network with capacity $\delta$
Explanation. The residual capacity drops by $\delta$, hence to zero: the arc leaves, and the opposite arc appears (or grows). Counting how often an arc can be critical gives the $O(nm)$ bound on augmentations.
no-f-370 · level facile · notes
On the reference network of the chapter Ford–Fulkerson with depth-first search makes $5$ augmentations, Edmonds–Karp (breadth-first search) $3$. What do the two final results have in common?
  1. The same augmenting paths
  2. The same number of critical arcs
  3. The same flow on every arc, necessarily
  4. The same value, the maximum value $\tilde{v}$
Explanation. Both stop with a maximum flow, hence with the same value ($\tilde{v}=7$ on that network); the flows on single arcs may differ, since the maximum flow is not unique in general.
no-f-371 · level facile · notes
Many combinatorial problems are solved as maximum flows in a network built for the purpose. What guarantees that the maximum flow found by Ford–Fulkerson corresponds to a combinatorial object (a matching, paths)?
  1. The fact that the network is acyclic
  2. Strong duality
  3. Integrality: with integer capacities there is an integer maximum flow, and Ford–Fulkerson finds it
  4. Uniqueness of the maximum flow
Explanation. With unit capacities an integer flow has values $0$ or $1$ on the arcs, and it reads as a set of edges or of paths. The maximum flow is not unique in general, and application networks may have cycles.
no-f-372 · level facile · notes
One wants to know how many arcs must be cut, at least, to disconnect $t$ from $s$ in a directed graph. Which flow problem is solved?
  1. The maximum flow from $s$ to $t$ with all capacities equal to $1$
  2. The shortest path from $s$ to $t$ with lengths $1$
  3. The minimum cost flow with balances $1,-1$
  4. The minimum spanning tree
Explanation. With unit capacities the capacity of a cut is the number of its forward arcs, and the minimum cut equals the maximum flow (Menger). The shortest path counts the arcs of a path, not the arcs to cut.
no-f-373 · level facile · notes
In a network built for a maximum flow application, what are the added source $s$ and sink $t$ for, joined by arcs of capacity $1$ to the nodes of two groups $V_1$ and $V_2$?
  1. To make the network acyclic
  2. To give a cost to the nodes
  3. To double the value of the flow
  4. To limit to $1$ the flow through every node of $V_1$ and $V_2$
Explanation. The arc $(s,i)$ of capacity $1$ lets at most one unit into $i \in V_1$, the arc $(j,t)$ lets at most one out of $j \in V_2$: so every node is used at most once, as in a matching.
no-f-374 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the link between the max-flow min-cut theorem and its combinatorial applications is correct?
  1. It holds only for networks with all capacities equal to $1$
  2. With unit capacities it gives min–max theorems, such as «maximum number of arc-disjoint paths = minimum number of arcs separating $s$ from $t$»
  3. It gives the maximum size of an independent set in every graph
  4. It requires the graph to be bipartite
Explanation. The theorem holds for any capacities; with unit capacities and integrality it gives Menger's theorem and the correspondence between matchings and flows in bipartite graphs. It does not solve problems such as maximum independent set in general graphs.
no-f-375 · level facile · notes
In a bipartite graph with $V_1=\{a,b,c\}$, $V_2=\{x,y\}$ and edges $\{a,x\}$, $\{b,x\}$, $\{b,y\}$, $\{c,y\}$, a matching is a set of edges with no common endpoints. What is the maximum number of edges of a matching?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $1$
Explanation. $\{a,x\},\{b,y\}$ is a matching with $2$ edges, and there cannot be more since $|V_2|=2$. $3=|V_1|$ and $4=|E|$ ignore the constraint on the endpoints.
no-f-376 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: the maximum number of edges of a matching of a bipartite graph $H$ is the maximum value $\tilde{v}$ of a flow in the network of $H$ (arcs $(s,i)$, $(i,j)$, $(j,t)$ of capacity $1$). To read a matching from a maximum flow, which fact is needed?
  1. The bipartite graph has $|V_1|=|V_2|$
  2. The maximum flow is unique
  3. The capacities of the arcs $(i,j)$ are infinite
  4. There is an integer maximum flow, with values $0$ or $1$, and conservation makes every node use at most one edge
Explanation. A fractional flow (for instance $1/2$ on two edges) does not read as a matching: integrality is needed, guaranteed with integer capacities. Neither $|V_1|=|V_2|$ nor uniqueness is needed.
no-f-377 · level facile · notes
In the network of a bipartite graph (arcs $s \to V_1$, $V_1 \to V_2$, $V_2 \to t$, capacity $1$) an integer maximum flow has $x_{ij}=1$ on the arcs $(a,y)$, $(b,x)$, $(c,z)$ between $V_1$ and $V_2$. What does it represent?
  1. Three paths from $a$ to $z$
  2. A minimum cut of capacity $3$
  3. The matching $\{a,y\},\{b,x\},\{c,z\}$ with $3$ edges
  4. A spanning tree of the network
Explanation. The edges with flow $1$ between the two sides form a matching, and its number of edges is the value of the flow. The minimum cut is a set of network arcs, not of graph edges.
no-f-378 · level teorema · notes
Let $s \neq t$ be two nodes of a directed graph. Which statement of Menger's theorem (arc version) is correct?
  1. The maximum number of node-disjoint paths from $s$ to $t$ is the minimum number of arcs to remove
  2. The maximum number of pairwise arc-disjoint paths from $s$ to $t$ is the minimum number of arcs whose removal separates $t$ from $s$
  3. Every connected graph has two arc-disjoint paths from $s$ to $t$
  4. The number of arc-disjoint paths is the out-degree of $s$
Explanation. It is the max-flow min-cut theorem with capacities $1$. Arc-disjoint paths may share nodes; the out-degree of $s$ is only an upper bound.
no-f-379 · level facile · notes
A directed graph has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(3,4)$. At most how many pairwise arc-disjoint paths from $1$ to $4$ are there?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $1$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $1,2,4$ and $1,3,4$ are arc-disjoint; a third path would have to leave $1$ on a third arc, but $\deg^+(1)=2$. The cut $\{(1,2),(1,3)\}$ has $2$ arcs, so the maximum is $2$.
no-f-380 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof of Menger's theorem, from an integer flow of value $\tilde{v}$ with $x_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$ one extracts $\tilde{v}$ arc-disjoint paths by walking along arcs with flow $1$. Why can the walk, having entered a node $i \notin \{s,t\}$, always continue?
  1. Because every node has out-degree at least $2$
  2. Because the graph is acyclic
  3. By flow conservation: at $i$ there is an outgoing arc with flow $1$ not used yet
  4. Because the flow is maximum
Explanation. At $i$ the walk has used one incoming arc more than outgoing ones, and conservation guarantees an outgoing arc with flow not yet used. Maximality is needed only to get $\tilde{v}$ paths.
no-f-381 · level facile · notes
Ford–Fulkerson with depth-first search (lists by increasing index), on the network with arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(3,4)$ of capacity $M \ge 2$ and $(2,3)$ of capacity $1$ ($s=1$, $t=4$), finds the paths $1,2,3,4$, then $1,2,4$, $1,3,2,4$ and $1,3,4$. What are the bottlenecks?
  1. $M$, $M$, $1$, $1$
  2. $1$, $1$, $1$, $1$
  3. $1$, $M$, $1$, $M$
  4. $1$, $M-1$, $1$, $M-1$
Explanation. The first path is limited by $(2,3)$: $\delta=1$; then $(1,2)$ has residual $M-1$; $1,3,2,4$ uses $(2,3)$ backward with residual $1$; finally $(1,3)$ has residual $M-1$. Total $2M$ in four augmentations.
no-f-382 · level facile · notes
At the end of Edmonds–Karp on the reference network of the chapter, the distances in the residual network from $s=1$ are $d(1)=0$, $d(2)=1$ and $+\infty$ for the other nodes. Which minimum cut is read?
  1. $S=\{1\}$
  2. $S=\{1,2\}$
  3. $S=\{1,2,3,4,5\}$
  4. None: the algorithm is not finished
Explanation. The nodes reachable in the final residual network form the side $S$ of a minimum cut: $S=\{1,2\}$, since $t=6$ is not reachable. Its capacity is the maximum value, $7$.
no-f-562 · level teorema · notes
In the maximum flow problem from $s$ to $t$ with capacities $u_{ij}$, let $u(S,N \setminus S)$ be the capacity of an $s$–$t$ cut (sum of the capacities of the arcs from $S$ to $N \setminus S$). Which statement is correct for every feasible flow of value $v$ and every $s$–$t$ cut?
  1. $v \ge u(S,N \setminus S)$
  2. $v=u(S,N \setminus S)$
  3. $v \le u(S,N \setminus S)$, so the maximum flow does not exceed the minimum cut
  4. $v \le u(S,N \setminus S)$ only for the minimum cut
Explanation. By the cut lemma $v=x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S)) \le u(\delta^+(S))-0$: it is weak duality, valid for every flow–cut pair. Equality holds only for a maximum flow and a minimum cut.
no-f-563 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: for every feasible flow from $s$ to $t$ of value $v$ and every $s$–$t$ cut $(S,N \setminus S)$, $v=x(\delta^+(S))-x(\delta^-(S))$. Which fact is needed to prove it?
  1. Flow conservation at the nodes of $S$ other than $s$: adding the constraints of the nodes of $S$ leaves only the net outflow of $s$
  2. The capacities are integer
  3. The flow is maximum
  4. The cut is minimum
Explanation. The constraints of the nodes of $S$ are added (balance $v$ for $s$, $0$ for the others): internal arcs cancel and the arcs crossing the cut remain. The lemma holds for every feasible flow and every cut.
no-f-564 · level ipotesi · notes
Lemma: augmenting a feasible flow by $\delta$ (the minimum residual capacity) along an augmenting path from $s$ to $t$ of the residual network gives a feasible flow of value $v+\delta$. Why does conservation still hold at the internal nodes of the path?
  1. Because $\delta$ is integer
  2. Because the path has no backward arcs
  3. Because the path is the shortest
  4. Because every internal node gains $\delta$ (or gives back $\delta$ less) on the path arc entering it and passes on as much on the one leaving it
Explanation. At an internal node the net effect of the two residual arcs of the path is zero, whether they are forward (flow $+\delta$) or backward (flow $-\delta$ on the opposite arc). At $s$ the outflow grows by $\delta$.

6. Minimum cost flow (45)

no-f-107 · level facile · notes
In minimum cost flow, the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ has a backward arc $(j,i)$ for every arc $(i,j)$ that can be decreased. The arc $(i,j)$ has bounds $l_{ij}=1$, $u_{ij}=6$, cost $c_{ij}=4$ and flow $x_{ij}=3$. What residual capacity and cost does the backward arc have?
  1. $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=3$ and cost $4$
  2. $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=3$ and cost $-4$
  3. $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=2$ and cost $4$
  4. $(j,i)$ with $r_{ji}=2$ and cost $-4$
Explanation. The backward arc has residual capacity $x_{ij}-l_{ij}=3-1=2$ (the flow cannot go below the lower bound) and cost $-c_{ij}=-4$: decreasing $x_{ij}$ saves $c_{ij}$ per unit.
no-f-108 · level facile · notes
A feasible flow costs $80$. In its residual network the directed cycle $W$ has cost $c(W)=-3$ (sum of the residual costs) and residual capacity $r(W)=2$ (minimum of the residual capacities). What does the flow augmented along $W$ by $\theta=r(W)$ cost?
  1. $74$
  2. $77$
  3. $86$
  4. $78$
Explanation. Augmenting along a cycle changes the cost by $\theta\,c(W)=2 \cdot (-3)=-6$: the new cost is $74$. $77$ forgets to multiply by $\theta$, $86$ gets the sign wrong.
no-f-109 · level facile · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ be a feasible flow of the minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ on a network $G$, and $G(\vet{x})$ its residual network (forward arcs of cost $c_{ij}$, backward arcs of cost $-c_{ij}$). When is $\vet{x}$ optimal?
  1. When every arc of the network has zero reduced cost
  2. When the network $G$ has no directed cycle of negative cost
  3. If and only if $G(\vet{x})$ has no directed cycle of negative cost
  4. When it saturates the cheapest arcs of the network
Explanation. Negative cycle optimality: a negative cycle of $G(\vet{x})$ would improve the flow, and without negative cycles every other flow costs at least as much. The residual network counts, not $G$.
no-f-110 · level facile · notes
The minimum cost flow model $\mathrm{MCF}$ on a network $G$ with capacities $u_{ij}$ (possibly $+\infty$) and costs $c_{ij}$ is feasible. When is it unbounded (cost tending to $-\infty$)?
  1. If and only if $G$ has a directed cycle of negative cost
  2. If and only if $G$ has a directed cycle of negative cost with all capacities $u_{ij}=+\infty$
  3. If and only if some arc has negative cost and infinite capacity
  4. If and only if the residual network of a feasible flow has a negative cycle
Explanation. A negative cycle with a finite capacity can be used only a bounded number of times; a negative directed cycle with all capacities infinite is needed. A negative cycle in the residual network only says that the flow is not optimal.
no-f-111 · level facile · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ be an optimal flow of the minimum cost flow model (bounds $l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$) and $\vet{\pi}$ the potentials that certify it, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. On an arc with $l_{ij}<x_{ij}<u_{ij}$, what is $\bar{c}_{ij}$?
  1. It is positive
  2. It is negative
  3. It can have any sign
  4. It is zero, $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$
Explanation. By complementary slackness $\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=l_{ij}$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$: an arc strictly between its bounds has $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$.
no-f-112 · level facile · notes
The cycle canceling algorithm looks for negative cycles in the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ with Bellman–Ford. Why does it start it with $d^0(j)=0$ for every node $j$, instead of from a single source node?
  1. To find also the negative cycles not reachable from a single node
  2. To have all lengths non-negative
  3. Because $G(\vet{x})$ is always acyclic
  4. To reduce the number of passes to $1$
Explanation. Starting from all the nodes is like adding a node $r$ joined to all of them by zero-cost arcs: every negative cycle of $G(\vet{x})$ becomes reachable. From a single node the unreachable cycles would be missed.
no-f-113 · level facile · notes
In successive shortest paths, after Dijkstra (on the reduced costs) from the chosen excess node, every potential is updated by $\pi_i:=\pi_i-\min\{d(i),d(t)\}$, where $t$ is the chosen deficit node. Node $i$ has $\pi_i=5$ and $d(i)=7$, and $d(t)=4$. What is the new $\pi_i$?
  1. $\pi_i=-2$
  2. $\pi_i=9$
  3. $\pi_i=1$
  4. $\pi_i=12$
Explanation. $\pi_i:=5-\min\{7,4\}=5-4=1$: the nodes farther than $t$ decrease by $d(t)$ only. $-2$ uses $d(i)$, $9$ and $12$ get the sign wrong.
no-f-114 · level facile · notes
To decide whether a balanced network has a feasible flow one builds the auxiliary network: super source $s$ with arcs $(s,i)$ of capacity $b_i$ to the supply nodes, super sink $t$ with arcs $(i,t)$ of capacity $-b_i$. The total supply is $D=12$ and the maximum flow from $s$ to $t$ is $10$. What do we conclude?
  1. The maximum flow restricted to the original arcs is a feasible flow
  2. The network has no feasible flow
  3. The network has a feasible flow of cost $10$
  4. A dummy node with balance $-2$ must be added
Explanation. The network has a feasible flow if and only if the maximum flow of the auxiliary network has value $D$, that is, it saturates all the new arcs: with $10<12$ none exists.
no-f-115 · level facile · notes
The cycle canceling algorithm starts from a feasible flow and, while the residual network has a negative cycle, augments along it by its residual capacity. With integer data it starts from a flow of cost $80$, and the optimum is $60$. At most how many iterations does it make?
  1. $60$
  2. $80$
  3. $n\,m$
  4. $20$
Explanation. With integer data every cancellation lowers the cost by at least $1$: at most $\vet{c}'\vet{x}^0-z(\mathrm{MCF})=80-60=20$ iterations.
no-f-116 · level facile · notes
The successive shortest path algorithm sends flow along shortest paths of the residual network, whose arcs have costs $c^x_{ij}$ ($c_{ij}$ forward, $-c_{ij}$ backward). Why is Dijkstra run on the reduced costs $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ and not on the costs $c^x_{ij}$?
  1. Because backward arcs can have negative costs, reduced costs cannot
  2. Because the reduced costs are always integer
  3. To find the negative cycles of the residual network
  4. Because the costs $c^x_{ij}$ are not defined on backward arcs
Explanation. Backward arcs cost $-c_{ij}$, and Dijkstra with negative lengths can fail. The potentials make the lengths non-negative without changing the shortest paths.
no-f-117 · level facile · notes
A pseudoflow satisfies the arc bounds but not necessarily conservation; the imbalance of node $i$ is $e_i=b_i-(\text{outflow}-\text{inflow})$. Node $i$ has balance $b_i=5$, sends out $3$ units and receives nothing. What is $e_i$, and what kind of node is it?
  1. $e_i=-2$, a deficit node
  2. $e_i=3$, an excess node
  3. $e_i=2$, an excess node
  4. $e_i=8$, an excess node
Explanation. $e_i=5-(3-0)=2>0$: $i$ has still to send $2$ units, it is an excess node. A deficit node has $e_i<0$.
no-f-118 · level media · notes
A feasible flow $\vet{x}$ has the residual arcs (with costs $c^x$) $(1,2)$: $3$, $(2,3)$: $-1$, $(3,1)$: $-1$, $(2,1)$: $-3$. With the potentials $\vet{\pi}=(0,-3,-2)$ and reduced costs $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, which statement is correct?
  1. All the reduced costs of the residual network are $\ge 0$: $\vet{x}$ is optimal
  2. The arc $(2,3)$ has negative reduced cost: $\vet{x}$ is not optimal
  3. The arc $(2,1)$ has negative reduced cost: $\vet{x}$ is not optimal
  4. The residual network has a negative cycle: $\vet{x}$ is not optimal
Explanation. Reduced costs: $(1,2)$: $3-0-3=0$; $(2,3)$: $-1+3-2=0$; $(3,1)$: $-1+2+0=1$; $(2,1)$: $-3+3+0=0$. All $\ge 0$, so $\vet{x}$ is optimal; indeed the cycles $1,2,3,1$ and $1,2,1$ cost $1$ and $0$.
no-f-119 · level teorema · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ be a feasible flow of the minimum cost flow model, and $G(\vet{x})$ its residual network with costs $c^x_{ij}$. Which statement of reduced cost optimality is correct?
  1. $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if some $\vet{\pi}$ has $c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc of the network $G$
  2. $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if some $\vet{\pi}$ has $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on every arc of $G(\vet{x})$
  3. $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if some $\vet{\pi}$ has $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc of $G(\vet{x})$
  4. $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if $\vet{\pi}=\vet{0}$ gives non-negative reduced costs on $G(\vet{x})$
Explanation. The condition concerns the arcs of the residual network, with the costs $c^x_{ij}$. On the arcs of $G$ it reads: $\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=l_{ij}$, $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$; $\bar{c} \ge 0$ on the whole network is not needed.
no-f-120 · level teorema · notes
A circulation on a directed graph $H=(N,F)$ is a vector $\vet{y} \ge \vet{0}$ with outflow equal to inflow at every node. Let $\vet{y} \neq \vet{0}$ be a circulation with $p$ arcs where $y_a>0$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\vet{y}$ is a sum of at most $p$ positive multiples of directed cycles with arcs where $y_a>0$
  2. $\vet{y}$ is the incidence vector of a single directed cycle of $H$
  3. $\vet{y}$ is a sum of flows along paths from a source to a sink
  4. $\vet{y}$ is a sum of exactly $p$ positive multiples of directed cycles
Explanation. Decomposition lemma: find a cycle of arcs with $y>0$, subtract the minimum $\theta_1$ and repeat; every step zeroes at least one arc, so there are $k \le p$ cycles, integer if $\vet{y}$ is integer.
no-f-121 · level ipotesi · notes
We want to prove that a feasible flow $\vet{x}$ whose residual network $G(\vet{x})$ has no negative cycle is optimal, by comparing it with another feasible flow $\vet{x}'$. Which results are used?
  1. The total unimodularity of the incidence matrix and Cramer's rule
  2. The max-flow min-cut theorem on the auxiliary network
  3. Bellman's equations with an added node $r$ and zero-cost arcs
  4. The difference of the two flows is a circulation on $G(\vet{x})$, which decomposes into cycles
Explanation. One has $\vet{c}'\vet{x}'=\vet{c}'\vet{x}+\sum_h\theta_h\,c(W_h)$ with cycles $W_h$ of $G(\vet{x})$ and $\theta_h>0$ (lemmas «difference of two flows» and «decomposition into cycles»); without negative cycles the sum is $\ge 0$. Bellman's equations are used for the theorem with potentials instead.
no-f-122 · level ipotesi · notes
Reduced cost optimality says: $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if some $\vet{\pi}$ has $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every arc of the residual network $G(\vet{x})$. In the «only if» part ($\vet{x}$ optimal), how are the potentials built?
  1. With the distances $d(i)$ in $G(\vet{x})$ from an added node $r$, joined to all at cost $0$: $\pi_i=-d(i)$
  2. Setting $\pi_i=0$ for every node, since $\vet{x}$ is optimal
  3. Solving the dual model with the network simplex
  4. Setting $\pi_i$ equal to the balance $b_i$ of every node
Explanation. An optimal flow has no negative cycle in $G(\vet{x})$, so the distances from $r$ are finite; Bellman's equations give $d(j) \le d(i)+c^x_{ij}$, that is $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ with $\pi=-d$.
no-f-123 · level facile · notes
The successive shortest path algorithm starts from a pseudoflow and at every iteration sends $\delta \ge 1$ units from an excess node to a deficit node. It is applied to a network with integer data and total supply $D=15$. At most how many iterations does it make?
  1. $n\,m$
  2. $15$
  3. $n-1$
  4. It depends on the arc costs
Explanation. Every iteration reduces the sum of the positive imbalances by $\delta \ge 1$, and the sum starts at $D$: at most $D=15$ iterations.
no-f-124 · level facile · notes
In the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ of a flow, the directed cycle $W$ has three arcs with residual costs $2$, $-5$, $1$ and residual capacities $4$, $3$, $6$. What are the cost $c(W)$ (sum of the costs) and the residual capacity $r(W)$ (minimum of the capacities)?
  1. $c(W)=-2$, $r(W)=6$
  2. $c(W)=8$, $r(W)=3$
  3. $c(W)=-2$, $r(W)=13$
  4. $c(W)=-2$, $r(W)=3$
Explanation. $c(W)=2-5+1=-2$ and $r(W)=\min\{4,3,6\}=3$: the cycle is negative, and augmenting by $\theta=3$ lowers the cost by $6$.
no-f-125 · level facile · notes
A circulation on a directed graph is a vector $\vet{y} \ge \vet{0}$ on the arcs with outflow equal to inflow at every node. On the graph with arcs $(1,2)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,1)$, $(2,1)$, which vector $(y_{12},y_{23},y_{31},y_{21})$ is a circulation?
  1. $(1,1,1,1)$
  2. $(3,1,1,2)$
  3. $(2,1,2,1)$
  4. $(1,2,1,0)$
Explanation. Node $1$: out $y_{12}$, in $y_{31}+y_{21}$; node $2$: out $y_{23}+y_{21}$, in $y_{12}$; node $3$: out $y_{31}$, in $y_{23}$. With $(3,1,1,2)$: $3=1+2$, $1+2=3$, $1=1$. It is the sum of the cycles $1,2,3,1$ (once) and $1,2,1$ (twice).
no-f-126 · level facile · notes
A balanced network with zero lower bounds has balances $\vet{b}=(4,3,-2,-5)$. The auxiliary network adds the super source $s$, with an arc $(s,i)$ of capacity $b_i$ to every supply node, and the super sink $t$, with an arc $(i,t)$ of capacity $-b_i$ from every demand node. What must the maximum flow be for the network to have a feasible flow?
  1. $14$
  2. $0$
  3. $7$
  4. $5$
Explanation. It must saturate all the new arcs: the value must be the total supply $D=4+3=7$, which equals the total demand $2+5$. $14$ adds supplies and demands.
no-f-127 · level teorema · notes
Let $\vet{x}$ and $\vet{x}'$ be two feasible flows of the same network, and $G(\vet{x})$ the residual network of $\vet{x}$ with costs $c^x_{ij}$. Which statement on their difference is correct?
  1. $\vet{x}'-\vet{x}$ is always a non-negative vector on the arcs of the network
  2. The difference, written on the arcs of $G(\vet{x})$, is a circulation $\vet{y}$ and $\vet{c}'\vet{x}'-\vet{c}'\vet{x}=\sum c^x_{ij}y_{ij}$
  3. The difference is a flow from a source to a sink of $G(\vet{x})$
  4. The difference is a circulation on $G(\vet{x})$ only if the two flows have the same cost
Explanation. Lemma «difference of two flows»: increases go on forward arcs, decreases on backward arcs; equal balances give a circulation, and its cost with the $c^x$ is the difference of the costs.
no-f-383 · level facile · notes
In minimum cost flow, the arc $(1,2)$ has $l_{12}=0$, $u_{12}=4$, cost $c_{12}=2$ and flow $x_{12}=3$. Which arcs does it generate in the residual network $G(\vet{x})$, with which residual capacities $r$ and costs $c^x$?
  1. Only $(1,2)$, with $r=1$ and $c^x=2$
  2. $(1,2)$ with $r=3$, $c^x=2$ and $(2,1)$ with $r=1$, $c^x=-2$
  3. $(1,2)$ with $r=1$, $c^x=2$ and $(2,1)$ with $r=3$, $c^x=-2$
  4. $(1,2)$ with $r=1$, $c^x=2$ and $(2,1)$ with $r=3$, $c^x=2$
Explanation. Forward, $u-x=1$ units remain at cost $2$; backward, $x-l=3$ units can be removed, saving $2$ each: cost $-2$. The last option forgets the sign of the backward cost.
no-f-384 · level facile · notes
A circulation (outflow equal to inflow at every node, values $\ge 0$) has $y_{12}=3$, $y_{23}=3$, $y_{31}=2$, $y_{34}=1$, $y_{41}=1$. How does it decompose into directed cycles?
  1. $2$ times the cycle $1,2,3,1$ plus $1$ time the cycle $1,2,3,4,1$
  2. $3$ times the cycle $1,2,3,1$
  3. $1$ time the cycle $1,2,3,1$ plus $2$ times the cycle $1,2,3,4,1$
  4. It does not decompose: it is not a circulation
Explanation. The cycle $1,2,3,1$ can be taken $\min\{3,3,2\}=2$ times; what remains is $y_{12}=y_{23}=y_{34}=y_{41}=1$, the cycle $1,2,3,4,1$. Check: $3$ units enter node $3$ and $2+1$ leave.
no-f-385 · level facile · notes
In the residual network of a feasible flow there is the cycle $W$ with residual costs $-5$, $2$, $1$ and residual capacities $3$, $1$, $4$. By how much does the cost drop when augmenting along $W$ by the largest possible amount?
  1. By $6$
  2. By $8$
  3. By $4$
  4. By $2$: $\theta=r(W)=1$ and $c(W)=-2$
Explanation. $c(W)=-5+2+1=-2$ and $\theta=r(W)=\min\{3,1,4\}=1$: the cost drops by $\theta\,|c(W)|=2$. $6$ uses the capacity $3$ of the first arc, ignoring the bottleneck; $8$ and $4$ add absolute values or only the negative costs.
no-f-386 · level facile · notes
A balanced network has balances $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$. In the auxiliary network (super source $s$ joined to the supply nodes, super sink $t$ joined to the demand nodes) the maximum flow from $s$ to $t$ is $6$. What is concluded?
  1. The network has a feasible flow of cost $6$
  2. The network has no feasible flow, since $6<7$
  3. A dummy node must be added
  4. The network has a feasible flow, since $6 \ge 4$
Explanation. A feasible flow exists if and only if the maximum flow saturates the arcs leaving $s$, that is, equals the total supply $D=5+2=7$. With $6$ one unit of supply cannot reach the demand.
no-f-387 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: a balanced network with $\vet{l}=\vet{0}$ has a feasible flow if and only if the maximum flow of the auxiliary network equals the total supply $D$. For the direction «maximum flow $D$ $\Rightarrow$ feasible flow», which fact is needed?
  1. The maximum flow is unique
  2. The costs are nonnegative
  3. With value $D$ every arc $(s,i)$ and $(i,t)$ is saturated, so conservation at the original nodes gives exactly the balances $b_i$
  4. The original network is acyclic
Explanation. The value $D$ is the total capacity of the arcs leaving $s$, so they are all saturated; so are the arcs into $t$ (total capacity $D$, balanced network). Then at every node the net flow on the original arcs is $b_i$. Costs do not matter.
no-f-388 · level facile · notes
The cycle-canceling algorithm (Klein) starts from a feasible flow. What does it do at every iteration?
  1. It finds a negative cycle $W$ of the residual network and augments along $W$ by $r(W)$, the minimum residual capacity
  2. It finds a shortest path from an excess node to a deficit node
  3. It adds an arc to the spanning tree
  4. It removes from the flow a cycle of positive cost
Explanation. Augmenting by $r(W)$ saturates an arc of $W$, which leaves the residual network: the cycle is canceled and the cost drops by $r(W)\,|c(W)|$. Shortest paths from excesses to deficits are the successive shortest path algorithm.
no-f-389 · level facile · notes
When does the cycle-canceling algorithm stop, and why is the final flow optimal?
  1. After $n-1$ iterations, by the Bellman–Ford theorem
  2. When every arc is saturated
  3. When the residual network has no more negative cycles, which is the optimality condition
  4. When the cost becomes negative
Explanation. A feasible flow is optimal if and only if its residual network has no negative cycles: the algorithm stops exactly there. The number of iterations depends on the data, not on $n$.
no-f-390 · level facile · notes
In the cycle-canceling algorithm, why does an iteration augment along the negative cycle $W$ by exactly $r(W)$ and not less?
  1. Because then the cost stays the same
  2. Because a smaller augmentation is not feasible
  3. Because $r(W)$ is always $1$
  4. Because then an arc of $W$ is saturated and leaves the residual network: the cycle is canceled
Explanation. Any $\theta \le r(W)$ gives a cheaper feasible flow; with $\theta=r(W)$ the saving is largest and the cycle disappears from the residual network. With integer data $r(W)$ is a positive integer, not necessarily $1$.
no-f-391 · level teorema · notes
Which statement about the link between cycle canceling and the negative cycle optimality theorem is correct?
  1. The algorithm is correct only if the network is acyclic
  2. The algorithm follows the theorem: a negative cycle improves the flow, and its absence certifies optimality
  3. The algorithm requires nonnegative costs
  4. The algorithm finds the optimum only if it starts from the zero flow
Explanation. The theorem says that $\vet{x}$ is optimal if and only if $G(\vet{x})$ has no negative cycles; the algorithm cancels negative cycles as long as there are any. It starts from any feasible flow, with costs of any sign.
no-f-392 · level facile · notes
To look for a negative cycle in the residual network $G(\vet{x})$, which may not be reachable from a given node, Bellman–Ford is run from a new node $r$ joined to every node by an arc of cost $0$. Which labels does the first pass give?
  1. $+\infty$ to every node except $r$
  2. $0$ to every node
  3. The minimum cost of an arc entering each node
  4. $-\infty$ to the nodes of a negative cycle
Explanation. The arcs $(r,j)$ of cost $0$ give $d(j)=0$ to every node right away, and the table is written from there with $d^0(j)=0$. This way every negative cycle is reachable from $r$.
no-f-393 · level teorema · notes
Bellman–Ford on the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ with $n$ nodes starts from $d^0(j)=0$ for every $j$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $G(\vet{x})$ has a negative cycle if and only if $d^1 \neq d^0$
  2. If $d^n=d^{n-1}$ the flow $\vet{x}$ is not optimal
  3. $d^k(j)$ is the minimum cost of a path from $1$ to $j$
  4. $G(\vet{x})$ has a negative cycle if and only if $d^n \neq d^{n-1}$; if $d^k=d^{k-1}$, $\pi_i=-d^k(i)$ gives nonnegative reduced costs
Explanation. $d^k(j)$ is the minimum cost of a walk with at most $k$ arcs ending at $j$ (possibly empty, of cost $0$). A change in pass $n$ certifies a negative cycle; stability gives potentials certifying optimality.
no-f-394 · level facile · notes
Bellman–Ford on the residual network with $n=6$ nodes gives $d^6(4)<d^5(4)$. How is a negative cycle found?
  1. The predecessors are read backwards starting from node $4$, one row per pass from $6$ to $1$: the walk found contains a negative cycle
  2. The cycle is formed by the nodes with negative label
  3. The cycle is the cheapest arc entering $4$
  4. One restarts from $d^0$ with source $4$
Explanation. The reconstructed walk has $n=6$ arcs on $6$ nodes, so it repeats a node; the cycle it contains is negative. A negative label is not enough: negative arcs without negative cycles also give negative labels.
no-f-395 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: with integer balances, capacities and costs, cycle canceling stops after at most $\vet{c}'\vet{x}^0-z(\mathrm{MCF})$ iterations ($\vet{x}^0$ initial flow). Which fact is needed to count the iterations?
  1. Every iteration cancels an arc of the network
  2. Every negative cycle has at most $n$ arcs
  3. Flows stay integer, so $r(W) \ge 1$ and $c(W) \le -1$: the cost drops by at least $1$ at every iteration
  4. Distances in the residual network do not decrease
Explanation. With integer data $r(W)$ is a positive integer and $c(W)$ a negative integer, so every iteration lowers the cost by at least $1$; the cost cannot go below the optimum. Nondecreasing distances concern Edmonds–Karp.
no-f-396 · level facile · notes
Cycle canceling, in Step 0, also checks whether the model is unbounded. When is a minimum cost flow instance with a feasible flow unbounded?
  1. When some cost is negative
  2. When the network has a directed cycle
  3. When there is a negative-cost cycle made of arcs of infinite capacity
  4. When the total supply exceeds the demand
Explanation. Along a negative cycle of arcs with $u_{ij}=+\infty$ flow can be sent without limit, lowering the cost to $-\infty$. A negative cost or any cycle is not enough.
no-f-397 · level facile · notes
Cycle canceling starts from a feasible flow of cost $80$ and stops with an optimal flow of cost $60$. With integer data, at most how many iterations did it make?
  1. $60$
  2. $80$
  3. $140$
  4. $20$
Explanation. Every iteration lowers the cost by at least $1$, so at most $80-60=20$ iterations. In the notes' example far fewer are needed, since every cycle lowers the cost by more than $1$.
no-f-398 · level facile · notes
In an iteration of cycle canceling, the negative cycle found has cost $c(W)=-6$ and residual capacity $r(W)=2$. The current flow costs $80$. What does the flow cost after the iteration?
  1. $68$
  2. $74$
  3. $78$
  4. $92$
Explanation. The new cost is $80+r(W)\,c(W)=80-12=68$. $74$ augments by one unit only; $92$ gets the sign wrong.
no-f-399 · level facile · notes
On the reference network of the chapter, if cycle canceling starts from the flow found in Step 0 with the maximum flow (which is already optimal), what happens?
  1. It runs $n$ iterations anyway
  2. It stops at the first run of Bellman–Ford, which finds no negative cycle
  3. It finds a cycle of cost $0$ and cancels it
  4. It declares the model unbounded
Explanation. If the initial flow is already optimal the residual network has no negative cycles, and the first Bellman–Ford search certifies it. This is why the notes show the algorithm from a more expensive flow.
no-f-400 · level facile · notes
A pseudoflow respects $\vet{l} \le \vet{x} \le \vet{u}$ but may violate conservation; the imbalance of node $i$ is $e_i=b_i-(\text{outflow}-\text{inflow})$. Node $2$ has $b_2=2$, outflow $3$ and inflow $0$. What is $e_2$?
  1. $5$
  2. $1$
  3. $-3$
  4. $-1$
Explanation. $e_2=2-(3-0)=-1$: the node has sent one unit more than its supply, and is now in deficit. The imbalances of all nodes add up to $0$.
no-f-401 · level facile · notes
Successive shortest paths, starting from $\vet{x}=\vet{0}$, send $\delta=3$ units on a path of cost $7$, then $2$ on one of cost $8$, then $1$ on one of cost $10$ and $1$ on one of cost $13$. What does the final flow cost?
  1. $60$
  2. $38$
  3. $47$
  4. $7$
Explanation. The cost grows by $\delta\,c(P)$ at every iteration: $21+16+10+13=60$. $38$ adds the path costs without multiplying by $\delta$; $47$ stops at the third iteration.
no-f-402 · level facile · notes
In successive shortest paths, what is the amount $\delta$ sent along the shortest path $P$ from the excess node $s$ to the deficit node $t$?
  1. $\delta=e_s$
  2. $\delta=r(P)$
  3. $\delta=\min\{e_s,\,-e_t,\,r(P)\}$
  4. $\delta=1$ always
Explanation. One cannot send more than the excess of $s$, more than the deficit of $t$, nor more than the minimum residual capacity of the path. With integer data $\delta \ge 1$, but not always $1$.
no-f-403 · level facile · notes
In successive shortest paths the paths are found by Dijkstra on the reduced costs $c^x_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, and the reduced cost of the path is $d(t)$. The path $P$ from $s$ to $t$ has $d(t)=4$, with $\pi_s=0$ and $\pi_t=-6$. What is its true cost $c(P)$?
  1. $4$
  2. $-2$
  3. $6$
  4. $10$
Explanation. Along the path the potentials cancel: $d(t)=c(P)-\pi_s+\pi_t$, so $c(P)=4+0+6=10$. The reduced cost is not the true cost when the potentials are nonzero.
no-f-404 · level teorema · notes
The successive shortest path algorithm keeps a pseudoflow and potentials with nonnegative reduced costs on the residual network. Which statement is correct?
  1. Every iteration lowers the cost of the pseudoflow
  2. It also works with negative cycles in the initial residual network
  3. When all imbalances are zero the pseudoflow is an optimal feasible flow
  4. It needs an initial feasible flow
Explanation. Nonnegative reduced costs on $G(\vet{x})$ certify optimality as soon as the pseudoflow becomes feasible. The cost grows at every iteration (flow is sent), and one starts from a pseudoflow, for instance $\vet{0}$, without negative cycles.
no-f-405 · level facile · notes
In the cycle-canceling algorithm, why can an iteration not use a negative cycle of residual capacity $r(W)=+\infty$ after Step 0?
  1. Because the residual network never has arcs of infinite capacity
  2. Because Step 0 has already ruled out negative cycles made only of infinite-capacity arcs, that is, the unbounded case
  3. Because Bellman–Ford does not find cycles with infinite arcs
  4. Because the costs are all positive
Explanation. Step 0 checks the negative cycles of the graph of the arcs with $u_{ij}=+\infty$: if there is one the model is unbounded and the algorithm stops. Otherwise every negative cycle of the residual network has an arc of finite capacity, and $r(W)<+\infty$.
no-f-406 · level facile · notes
In cycle canceling the Bellman–Ford search on the residual network with $n=6$ nodes gives $d^6 \neq d^5$, and tracing back the predecessors gives a walk of $6$ arcs. Why does it surely contain a cycle?
  1. Because every walk contains a cycle
  2. Because the residual network is strongly connected
  3. Because $6$ arcs touch $7$ node positions on only $6$ nodes: a node repeats
  4. Because the first arc has negative cost
Explanation. A walk with $n$ arcs visits $n+1$ nodes (with repetitions), so it repeats at least one, and the piece between the two occurrences is a cycle, which the Proposition shows to be negative.

7. Transportation and assignment (72)

no-f-128 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem the origins $i \in P$ have supplies $a_i$ and the destinations $j \in D$ demands $d_j$; we ship $x_{ij} \ge 0$ from $i$ to $j$, without capacities, meeting supplies and demands exactly. When does an instance have a feasible solution?
  1. When all the costs $c_{ij}$ are non-negative
  2. If and only if it is balanced, $\sum_{i \in P} a_i=\sum_{j \in D} d_j$
  3. When origins and destinations are equally many
  4. Always, since there are no arc capacities
Explanation. Adding up the origin constraints and the destination constraints gives the same sum of the variables: balance is necessary; and it is sufficient, for example with $x_{ij}=a_i d_j/S$, where $S$ is the total supply.
no-f-129 · level facile · notes
A balanced transportation instance has $m=3$ origins and $n=4$ destinations; a cell $(i,j)$ of the table corresponds to the variable $x_{ij}$. How many cells does a basis of the $\mathrm{TP}$ model have?
  1. $7$
  2. $12$
  3. $6$
  4. $4$
Explanation. A basis is a spanning tree of the complete bipartite network with $m+n=7$ nodes: it has $m+n-1=6$ cells, even if some of them are $0$ (degenerate basis). $12=m\,n$ are all the cells.
no-f-130 · level facile · notes
The transportation model $\mathrm{TP}$ of a balanced instance with $m$ origins and $n$ destinations has one constraint per origin (supply) and one per destination (demand). What is the rank of these $m+n$ constraints?
  1. $m+n-1$
  2. $m+n$
  3. $m\,n$
  4. $\min\{m,n\}$
Explanation. The complete bipartite network is connected, so its incidence matrix has rank $m+n-1$; each constraint is implied by the others.
no-f-131 · level facile · notes
For an initial transportation solution, at every step a cell is filled as much as possible and a row or a column is crossed out. The north-west corner rule picks the top-left active cell, the minimum cost rule the cheapest active cell. What is the difference?
  1. The first always gives an optimal solution
  2. The second may give a non-basic solution
  3. The first fills one cell per row, the second one per column
  4. The first ignores the costs; both give a basic solution
Explanation. Both fill one cell and cross out one line per step, so they give a basic feasible solution with $m+n-1$ cells; the north-west corner does not look at the costs, and usually starts farther from the optimum.
no-f-132 · level facile · notes
In the potential method of the course the basic cells have $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$, with $\pi_i$ for origin $i$ and $\pi_j$ for destination $j$. Many textbooks use the u-v method instead, with $u_i+v_j=c_{ij}$ on the basic cells. How are $u$, $v$ and $\pi$ related?
  1. $u_i=-\pi_i$ and $v_j=\pi_j$
  2. $u_i=\pi_i$ and $v_j=-\pi_j$
  3. $u_i=\pi_i$ and $v_j=\pi_j$
  4. There is no relation: the reduced costs are different
Explanation. From $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$ on the basic cells, $u_i=\pi_i$ for the origins and $v_j=-\pi_j$ for the destinations: the reduced costs are the same, only the potentials of the destinations change sign.
no-f-133 · level facile · notes
In the potential method for transportation, the entering cell closes with the basic cells a cycle with alternating signs: the $+$ cells increase by $\theta$, the $-$ cells decrease by $\theta$. The $-$ cells hold $40$, $15$, $25$ and the $+$ cells (the entering one excluded) $10$, $5$. What is the step $\theta$?
  1. $5$
  2. $40$
  3. $15$
  4. $0$
Explanation. $\theta$ is the largest step that makes no cell negative, that is, the minimum over the $-$ cells only: $\min\{40,15,25\}=15$. The $+$ cells increase and do not limit the step.
no-f-134 · level facile · notes
In the potential method for transportation the current basic solution costs $440$. The cell $e$ with reduced cost $\bar{c}_e=-3$ enters, and the step along its cycle is $\theta=10$. What does the new basic solution cost?
  1. $410$
  2. $437$
  3. $470$
  4. $430$
Explanation. The cost changes by $\theta\,\bar{c}_e=10 \cdot (-3)=-30$: $440-30=410$. With $\theta=0$ (degenerate pivot) only the basis would change.
no-f-135 · level facile · notes
A transportation instance has total supply $70$ and total demand $55$. The balanced model requires supply equal to demand. How is the instance balanced, without changing its optimal solutions?
  1. Add a dummy origin with supply $15$
  2. Reduce all the supplies proportionally
  3. Add a dummy destination with demand $15$ and costs equal to the largest cost
  4. Add a dummy destination with demand $15$ and zero costs
Explanation. With more supply than demand a dummy destination absorbs the excess $70-55=15$, at zero cost; a dummy origin is needed when the demand exceeds the supply.
no-f-136 · level facile · notes
The assignment model $\mathrm{AP}$ assigns $n$ workers to $n$ jobs with $x_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$, every row and every column adding up to $1$. Why does its LP relaxation ($x_{ij} \ge 0$) have an optimal solution that is an assignment?
  1. Because the costs $c_{ij}$ are integer
  2. Because it is a transportation model with $a_i=d_j=1$, with integer vertices
  3. Because the constraint $x_{ij} \le 1$ is imposed explicitly
  4. It is not true: branch-and-bound is always needed
Explanation. The relaxation is a transportation instance with unit supplies and demands; its vertices are integer by total unimodularity, and an integer solution with rows and columns adding up to $1$ is a permutation matrix.
no-f-137 · level facile · notes
We want an assignment of $n$ workers to $n$ jobs of maximum profit, with profits $p_{ij}$ and largest profit $p_{\max}$. The Hungarian algorithm minimizes non-negative costs. With which costs $c_{ij}$ is it run?
  1. $c_{ij}=p_{ij}$
  2. $c_{ij}=1/p_{ij}$
  3. $c_{ij}=p_{\max}-p_{ij}$
  4. $c_{ij}=p_{ij}-p_{\max}$
Explanation. Minimizing $-p_{ij}$ is the same as maximizing the profits, and $c_{ij}=p_{\max}-p_{ij}$ gives the same optimum (every assignment changes by the constant $n\,p_{\max}$) with non-negative costs.
no-f-138 · level facile · notes
In a reduced $4 \times 4$ matrix (non-negative entries) some zeros are independent if no two of them lie in the same row or column. The maximum number of independent zeros is $3$. What is the minimum number of lines (rows or columns) covering all the zeros?
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. It depends on where the zeros are
Explanation. By the theorem of König and Egerváry the maximum number of independent zeros equals the minimum number of lines covering the zeros: $3$.
no-f-139 · level facile · notes
In Step 3 of the Hungarian algorithm on an $n \times n$ matrix with $n=5$, the zeros are covered by $k=3$ lines and the smallest uncovered entry is $\delta=2$. By how much does the dual value $\sum_{i \in P}\pi_i-\sum_{j \in D}\pi_j$, a lower bound on the cost of every assignment, grow?
  1. $2$
  2. $4$
  3. $6$
  4. $10$
Explanation. Step 3 increases the dual value by $\delta\,(n-k)=2\,(5-3)=4$.
no-f-140 · level facile · notes
In Step 3 of the Hungarian algorithm the zeros of the reduced matrix are covered by the minimum number of lines, and $\delta$ is the smallest uncovered entry. How does the reduced matrix change?
  1. $\delta$ is subtracted from all the entries
  2. $\delta$ is subtracted from the covered entries
  3. $\delta$ is added to the uncovered entries
  4. $\delta$ is subtracted from the uncovered ones and added to those covered twice
Explanation. The uncovered entries drop by $\delta$ (at least one new zero appears), those covered by a row and a column grow by $\delta$, the others stay the same.
no-f-141 · level media · notes
The cost matrix of a $3 \times 3$ assignment has rows $(4,2,5)$, $(3,6,4)$, $(7,5,3)$. Step 0 of the Hungarian algorithm subtracts from every row its minimum, then from every column its minimum. What is the resulting dual value, that is, the sum of the subtracted minima?
  1. $7$
  2. $9$
  3. $8$
  4. $10$
Explanation. Row minima: $2,3,3$ (sum $8$). Matrix: $(2,0,3)$, $(0,3,1)$, $(4,2,0)$; every column already has a zero, so no column reduction. The value is $8$, and the assignment $(1,2),(2,1),(3,3)$ costs $2+3+3=8$: it is optimal.
no-f-142 · level teorema · notes
In transportation, let $\bar{\vet{x}}$ be the basic feasible solution of the basis $T$, with potentials $\vet{\pi}$ such that $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the cells of $T$. Which statement of the optimality criterion is correct?
  1. If $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ for every cell, $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is optimal and $\vet{\pi}$ is optimal for the dual $D(\mathrm{TP})$
  2. If $\bar{c}_{ij} \le 0$ for every cell, $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is optimal and $\vet{\pi}$ is optimal for the dual $D(\mathrm{TP})$
  3. If $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ for every cell, $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is always the unique optimal solution
  4. $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is optimal if and only if none of its basic cells is $0$
Explanation. With $\bar{c} \ge 0$ every feasible solution costs at least $\sum a_i\pi_i-\sum d_j\pi_j$, which is the cost of $\bar{\vet{x}}$. Uniqueness needs $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$ on all the nonbasic cells.
no-f-143 · level teorema · notes
The transportation model $\mathrm{TP}$ has $m$ origins and $n$ destinations; every cell $(i,j)$ corresponds to an arc of the complete bipartite network. Which statement on its bases is correct?
  1. Every set of $m+n-1$ cells is a basis of the model
  2. A basis has $m\,n$ cells, one for every origin–destination pair
  3. A basis has several basic solutions, one for each order of the leaves
  4. $m+n-1$ cells form a basis if and only if their arcs form a spanning tree
Explanation. The bases are the spanning trees of the complete bipartite network; the basic solution is found leaf by leaf, it is unique and integer when supplies and demands are integer. A set of cells with a cycle is not a basis.
no-f-144 · level ipotesi · notes
The optimality criterion of the potential method says: if $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$ on every cell and $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$ on the basic cells, the basic solution $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is optimal. Which facts are used in the proof?
  1. The total unimodularity of the constraint matrix and Cramer's rule
  2. Cost $=\sum \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}+\sum a_i\pi_i-\sum d_j\pi_j$, $\bar{c} \ge 0$, $\vet{x} \ge \vet{0}$, weak duality
  3. The theorem of König and Egerváry on independent zeros
  4. The termination of the potential method when $\theta>0$
Explanation. For $\bar{\vet{x}}$ the term $\sum \bar{c}_{ij}\bar{x}_{ij}$ is zero, for every other $\vet{x} \ge \vet{0}$ it is $\ge 0$: $\bar{\vet{x}}$ is optimal; $\vet{\pi}$ is feasible for the dual with the same value, hence optimal by weak duality.
no-f-145 · level ipotesi · notes
The potential method for transportation lets a cell with $\bar{c}_e<0$ enter and moves $\theta$ units along its cycle. To prove that it terminates when $\theta>0$ at every iteration, which results are used?
  1. The max-flow min-cut theorem on the bipartite network
  2. The north-west corner rule and the minimum cost rule
  3. The theorem of König and Egerváry on independent zeros
  4. The cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_e|>0$, a basis has one solution, the bases are finitely many
Explanation. A basis visited twice would have two different costs, which is impossible since the basic solution is unique; the bases are finitely many, so the method stops, and at the end the optimality criterion applies.
no-f-146 · level facile · notes
An uncapacitated network with costs $c_{ij} \ge 0$ has supply, demand and transshipment nodes. It is reduced to a transportation problem between supply and demand nodes. Which cost is used between origin $i$ and destination $j$?
  1. The cost $c_{ij}$ of the arc $(i,j)$, or $+\infty$ if it is missing
  2. The largest cost of a path from $i$ to $j$
  3. The distance $\tilde{d}(i,j)$, length of a shortest path from $i$ to $j$
  4. The number of arcs of a path from $i$ to $j$
Explanation. Proposition «transshipment as transportation»: without capacities every unit is best sent along a shortest path, so the cost of the pair $(i,j)$ is the distance $\tilde{d}(i,j)$ with lengths $c_{ij}$.
no-f-147 · level facile · notes
The north-west corner rule, on a balanced transportation instance with $m=3$ origins and $n=5$ destinations, fills one cell and crosses out one row or column per step. After how many steps does it stop?
  1. $7$
  2. $8$
  3. $15$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Theorem «the initial solution is basic»: it stops after $m+n-1=7$ steps, with a basis of $7$ cells (some may be $0$). $15=m\,n$ are all the cells.
no-f-148 · level facile · notes
In the potential method the nonbasic cell $e$ closes a unique cycle with the basic cells; starting from $e$ the signs alternate $+,-,+,-,\dots$. How many $+$ cells and $-$ cells does every row or column touched by the cycle contain?
  1. Two $+$ cells and no $-$ cell
  2. One $+$ cell and one $-$ cell
  3. Any number, as long as it is even
  4. A single cell, $+$ or $-$
Explanation. Lemma «the cycle of a nonbasic cell»: the cycle enters and leaves every line with two consecutive cells of opposite sign, so moving $\theta$ along the cycle leaves supplies and demands unchanged.
no-f-149 · level facile · notes
In the Hungarian algorithm the reduced matrix has entries $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j \ge 0$, and the dual value is $\sum_{i}\pi_i-\sum_{j}\pi_j=17$. An assignment uses only cells with $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$. What do we conclude?
  1. It costs $0$, since it uses only zeros of the reduced matrix
  2. It costs at least $17$, but it may not be optimal
  3. It is optimal only if it is the unique assignment of zeros
  4. It costs $17$ and it is optimal, also for the LP relaxation
Explanation. Corollary «zero-cost assignments are optimal»: the cost of every assignment is $\sum \bar{c}_{ij}x_{ij}+17$, and with $\bar{c} \ge 0$ none costs less than $17$. Zero is the reduced cost, not the cost.
no-f-150 · level facile · notes
In a $3 \times 3$ matrix with non-negative entries the zeros are in the cells $(1,1)$, $(1,2)$, $(2,1)$, $(3,3)$. Zeros are independent if no two of them lie in the same row or column. At most how many independent zeros can be chosen?
  1. $3$
  2. $2$
  3. $4$
  4. $1$
Explanation. For example $(1,2)$, $(2,1)$, $(3,3)$: three zeros in different rows and columns, that is, an assignment of zero reduced cost. With $3$ rows no more can be chosen.
no-f-407 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the network associated with $m$ origins and $n$ destinations has an arc from every origin to every destination. How many nodes and arcs does it have?
  1. $m+n$ nodes and $m\,n$ arcs
  2. $m\,n$ nodes and $m+n$ arcs
  3. $m+n$ nodes and $m+n-1$ arcs
  4. $m+n+2$ nodes and $m\,n+m+n$ arcs
Explanation. One node per origin and one per destination; one arc per origin–destination pair: the complete bipartite network. $m+n-1$ is the number of cells of a basis, not of arcs.
no-f-408 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the origins are numbered $1,\dots,m$ and the destinations $m+1,\dots,m+n$. With $m=3$ and $n=4$, what are the destinations called?
  1. $1,2,3,4$
  2. $0,1,2,3$
  3. $4,5,6,7$
  4. $3,4,5,6$
Explanation. The destinations follow the origins: $m+1=4$ up to $m+n=7$. This gives every node its own number, and later its own potential $\pi_v$.
no-f-409 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, which constraints does the model $\mathrm{TP}$ of a balanced instance have?
  1. $\sum_j x_{ij} \le a_i$ and $\sum_i x_{ij} \ge d_j$, with integer $x_{ij}$
  2. $\sum_j x_{ij}=a_i$ for every origin, $\sum_i x_{ij}=d_j$ for every destination, $x_{ij} \ge 0$
  3. $\sum_j x_{ij}=1$ and $\sum_i x_{ij}=1$
  4. $x_{ij} \le \min\{a_i,d_j\}$ and nothing else
Explanation. Every origin ships all its supply and every destination receives all its demand; the $x_{ij}$ are continuous and nonnegative (integrality comes from total unimodularity). The equations with $1$ are assignment.
no-f-410 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the transportation table has one row per origin and one column per destination, with the supplies in the last column and the demands in the last row. When is a vector $\vet{x} \ge \vet{0}$ written in the table feasible?
  1. When every cell has value at most $\min\{a_i,d_j\}$
  2. When the sum of all cells is the total supply
  3. When it has exactly $m+n-1$ positive cells
  4. When every row adds up to its supply and every column to its demand
Explanation. The constraints of the model are exactly the row and column sums. The total sum alone is not enough; $m+n-1$ cells characterize bases, not feasibility.
no-f-411 · level facile · notes

  1. Yes: total supply and total demand are both $90$
  2. No: the supply is $30$ and the demand $90$
  3. No: there are more shops than warehouses
  4. Yes, but only if all costs are equal
Explanation. Balanced means total supply equal to total demand: $3 \cdot 30=90=10+25+15+40$. The number of origins and destinations and the costs do not matter.
no-f-412 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, a solution has $x_{14}=10$, $x_{15}=20$, $x_{25}=5$ and costs $c_{14}=1$, $c_{15}=9$, $c_{25}=4$ (the other used cells are not in question). What do these three cells cost?
  1. $14$
  2. $35$
  3. $210$
  4. $200$
Explanation. The cost is $\sum c_{ij}x_{ij}$: $1 \cdot 10+9 \cdot 20+4 \cdot 5=10+180+20=210$. $35$ adds the quantities, $14$ the costs.
no-f-413 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, why are neither transshipment nodes nor arc capacities needed in the transportation problem?
  1. Because the costs are all positive
  2. Because every arc goes directly from an origin to a destination, and the amounts are limited only by supplies and demands
  3. Because solutions are always degenerate
  4. Because the destinations have unit demand
Explanation. The network is complete bipartite: a unit goes from an origin to a destination along a single arc. The only limitations are the row and column sums. Demands are arbitrary (unit only in assignment).
no-f-414 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the total supply is $100$ and the total demand $90$, and every origin may ship less than its supply. What is added to balance the instance?
  1. A dummy origin with supply $10$
  2. A dummy destination with demand $100$
  3. All supplies are reduced by $10\%$
  4. A dummy destination with demand $10$ and cost $0$ from every origin
Explanation. The dummy destination collects the unused supply, $100-90=10$, at cost $0$: the amount it receives from $i$ is the unused supply of $i$. A dummy origin is needed when supply is short.
no-f-415 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the total demand ($120$) exceeds the total supply ($100$), and every unit of unmet demand of destination $j$ costs a penalty $p_j$. How is the instance balanced?

  2. With a dummy origin of supply $20$ and cost $p_j$ in its cell $(\cdot,j)$
  3. With a dummy origin of supply $20$ and cost $0$ in every cell, always
  4. It cannot be: the instance is infeasible
Explanation. The dummy origin «supplies» the missing demand, $120-100=20$; sending a unit to $j$ from the dummy origin means leaving it unmet, at cost $p_j$. Cost $0$ is used only if there are no penalties.
no-f-416 · level facile · notes

  2. Origins $2$ and $3$ are unused
  3. Origin $1$ does not ship $10$ units of its supply, origins $2$ and $3$ ship everything
  4. The solution is infeasible
Explanation. The amount sent to the dummy destination is the unused supply: origin $1$ keeps $10$, the others ship all their supply. The cost of those cells is $0$.
no-f-417 · level teorema · notes

  3. The dummy node makes the instance degenerate, so it cannot be solved
  4. The dummy destination must have positive cost, otherwise it receives everything
Explanation. With costs $0$ the dummy destination absorbs only the unused supply, and the cost does not change; it is the dummy node of the chapter on flow models, as a column of the table. It does not receive «everything»: its demand is only the excess.
no-f-418 · level facile · notes
A transshipment network without capacities, with costs $c_{ij} \ge 0$, is reduced to a transportation instance between the supply nodes and the demand nodes. Which costs are used in the table?
  1. The costs $c_{ij}$ of the direct arcs, $+\infty$ if the arc is missing
  2. The number of arcs of the shortest path
  3. The distances $\tilde{d}(i,j)$ of the shortest paths from $i$ to $j$
  4. The sum of all costs of the arcs leaving $i$
Explanation. Without capacities every unit travels along a shortest path, and only the distance between origin and destination matters. Using only direct arcs would ignore the routes through transshipment nodes.
no-f-419 · level ipotesi · notes
Proposition: a transshipment network with costs $c_{ij} \ge 0$ has the same optimal value as the transportation instance with costs $\tilde{d}(i,j)$ (distances). Which assumption is essential for the reduction?
  1. The arcs have no capacities: every unit can follow a shortest path without limits
  2. The network is acyclic
  3. Every node is a supply or demand node
  4. The balances are all equal
Explanation. With capacities two units could not always use the same shortest path, and distances would not be enough. Nonnegative costs ensure the distances exist; transshipment nodes are allowed and the network may have cycles.
no-f-420 · level facile · notes
