  1. $2$ units on every arc leaving $1$
  2. $13$ units on the arc $(1,6)$
  3. $1$ unit on each of two paths from $1$ to $6$
  4. $2$ units along the path $1,3,2,4,6$, of cost $2 \cdot 13=26$
Explanation. The $y_{16}$ units are sent along a shortest path from $1$ to $6$: every arc of the path carries $2$ more units, and the cost is $y_{16}\tilde{d}(1,6)$. The arc $(1,6)$ might not exist.
no-f-421 · level ipotesi · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, a basic feasible solution (basic cells forming a spanning tree of the bipartite network) is a vertex of the polyhedron of the model $\mathrm{TP}$. Which fact makes it integer when supplies and demands are integer?
  1. There are at most $m+n-1$ basic cells
  2. The system of the basic cells is solved from the leaves of the tree, one cell at a time, with only sums and differences of $a_i$ and $d_j$
  3. The costs are integer
  4. The potential method rounds the amounts
Explanation. A leaf of the tree is a row or column with a single basic cell, which gets all the residual supply or demand; going on this way every amount is a sum and difference of integers. Costs do not matter for vertices.
no-f-422 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the initial solution algorithm fills a cell with the largest possible amount and crosses out a row or a column. When the residual supply $\bar{a}_i$ and the residual demand $\bar{d}_j$ run out together, what does it cross out?
  1. Both, the row and the column
  2. Only the column
  3. Neither
  4. Only the row (if another row is active); the column stays with residual demand $0$
Explanation. Crossing out a single line guarantees that the solution has $m+n-1$ basic cells (a spanning tree): a later cell of the column gets the amount $0$, and the solution is degenerate. Crossing out both would give one cell less.
no-f-423 · level teorema · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, which statement about the initial basic feasible solution algorithm (northwest corner or minimum cost rule) is correct?
  1. It stops after $m+n-1$ steps, and the filled cells form a basis: a spanning tree of the bipartite network
  2. It always gives an optimal solution with the minimum cost rule
  3. It always gives a nondegenerate solution
  4. It fills $m\,n$ cells
Explanation. Every step crosses out exactly one line, the last one two: $m+n-1$ steps and cells, forming a spanning tree. The minimum cost rule usually gives a better solution, but not an optimal one; the solution may be degenerate.

  2. $9$
  3. $24$
  4. $6$
Explanation. A basis has $m+n-1=9$ cells, some possibly with amount $0$ (degeneracy). $24=m\,n$ is the number of all cells, $10=m+n$ the number of constraints.

no-f-426 · level facile · notes

  3. Because it does not respect the demands
  4. Because it uses more than $m+n-1$ cells

  4. A random one

no-f-430 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, on the reference instance of the chapter the northwest corner rule gives cost $440$ and the minimum cost rule $170$. What can be said about the optimal cost $z^\star$?

  2. $z^\star=440$
  3. $z^\star \ge 440$
  4. $z^\star \le 170$: both are feasible solutions, hence upper bounds
Explanation. Every feasible solution gives an upper bound on the minimum, so $z^\star \le \min\{440,170\}=170$. Whether the minimum cost solution is optimal must be checked with the potentials.
no-f-431 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, in the potential method the basic cells have zero reduced cost, $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$, with $\pi_7=0$ (last destination).   4. $\pi_2=2$, $\pi_5=2$

no-f-432 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the dual of the model $\mathrm{TP}$ has a free potential $\pi_v$ for every node and constraints $\pi_i-\pi_j \le c_{ij}$. What is its objective function?
  1. $\min \sum_i a_i\pi_i+\sum_j d_j\pi_j$
  2. $\max \sum_{i,j} c_{ij}\pi_i$
  3. $\max \sum_i \pi_i-\sum_j \pi_j$
  4. $\max \sum_i a_i\pi_i-\sum_j d_j\pi_j$
Explanation. The destination constraints are written $-\sum_i x_{ij}=-d_j$, so the right-hand side for $\pi_j$ is $-d_j$: $\max \sum_i a_i\pi_i-\sum_j d_j\pi_j$. The third option is the assignment case $a_i=d_j=1$.
no-f-433 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, with the potentials $\pi_3=6$ (origin $3$) and $\pi_5=-2$ (destination $5$) and the cost $c_{35}=1$, what is the reduced cost $\bar{c}_{35}=c_{35}-\pi_3+\pi_5$, and what does it indicate?
  1. $9$: the cell is not convenient
  2. $-5$: the cell is convenient
  3. $-7$: the cell is a candidate to enter the basis
  4. $0$: the cell is basic
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{35}=1-6-2=-7<0$: moving flow onto $(3,5)$ lowers the cost by $7$ per unit, so the basis is not optimal and $(3,5)$ may enter. $9$ gets the sign of $\pi_5$ wrong.
no-f-434 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, in the potential method the cell $(3,5)$ enters, with $\bar{c}_{35}=-7$. Its cycle is $(3,5)^+,(2,5)^-,(2,7)^+,(3,7)^-$, with $\bar{x}_{25}=5$ and $\bar{x}_{37}=30$. The solution costs $440$. What is $\theta$, and what does the new solution cost?
  1. $\theta=5$, cost $405$
  2. $\theta=30$, cost $230$
  3. $\theta=5$, cost $475$
  4. $\theta=10$, cost $370$
Explanation. $\theta$ is the minimum over the cells with a minus sign: $\min\{5,30\}=5$, and $(2,5)$ leaves. The cost changes by $\theta\,\bar{c}_{35}=-35$: $440-35=405$. $475$ gets the sign wrong.
no-f-435 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, in an iteration of the potential method the reduced costs of the nonbasic cells are $-5$, $-4$, $-7$, $7$, $-2$, $9$. With the most negative reduced cost rule, which cell enters?
  1. The one with reduced cost $9$
  2. The one with reduced cost $-5$, the first negative one
  3. None: there are positive reduced costs
  4. The one with reduced cost $-7$
Explanation. The cell with the most negative reduced cost, $-7$, enters, promising the largest saving per unit. The basis would be optimal only if all reduced costs were $\ge 0$.
no-f-436 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, the potential method stops when all reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ of the cells are $\ge 0$. What does it guarantee then?
  1. That the solution is unique
  2. That the current basic solution is optimal, since the potentials are dual feasible with the same value
  3. That no cell has amount $0$
  4. That the potentials are all nonnegative
Explanation. With $\bar{c} \ge 0$ the potentials satisfy $\pi_i-\pi_j \le c_{ij}$, and the basic cells have $\bar{c}=0$: primal and dual have the same value, so both are optimal. Uniqueness needs strictly positive reduced costs outside the basis.
no-f-437 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, in the potential method a cell enters, and on its cycle a cell with minus sign has amount $0$ (a degenerate basis). What happens?
  1. The method stops: the solution is optimal
  2. The cost decreases by $|\bar{c}_e|$
  3. $\theta=0$: the basis changes but the solution and the cost stay the same (degenerate pivot)
  4. The solution becomes infeasible
Explanation. With $\theta=0$ no amount changes: one cell enters and the cell with amount $0$ leaves. It is a degenerate pivot; the new basis may lead to improving pivots.
no-f-438 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, when is a basic feasible solution of the transportation problem called degenerate?
  1. When a basic cell has amount $0$
  2. When it has fewer than $m+n-1$ basic cells
  3. When two cells have the same cost
  4. When a nonbasic reduced cost is zero
Explanation. A basis always has $m+n-1$ cells; it is degenerate if some of them equal $0$, for instance when a row and a column run out together in the northwest corner rule. Zero nonbasic reduced costs indicate multiple optima.
no-f-439 · level teorema · notes
In the transportation problem with origins $i$ (supplies $a_i$), destinations $j$ (demands $d_j$) and unit costs $c_{ij}$, which statement about termination of the potential method with degeneracy is correct?
  1. Degeneracy makes it impossible to find the optimum
  2. Every pivot lowers the cost, even if degenerate
  3. With degeneracy the method always stops in $m+n-1$ pivots
  4. Without degeneracy every pivot strictly lowers the cost; with degenerate pivots ($\theta=0$) rules that prevent cycling are needed
Explanation. With $\theta>0$ the cost drops by $\theta|\bar{c}_e|$ and no basis repeats; with $\theta=0$ the cost stays the same and in theory bases could repeat. The notes show examples where degenerate pivots still lead to the optimum.
no-f-440 · level facile · notes
In the assignment problem there are $n$ workers and $n$ jobs, and $c_{ij}$ is the cost of assigning worker $i$ to job $j$. How many feasible assignments (every worker one job, every job one worker) are there with $n=4$?
  1. $16$
  2. $24$
  3. $4$
  4. $256$
Explanation. An assignment is a permutation of the jobs: $4!=24$. $16=n^2$ is the number of cells, $256=4^4$ would allow giving the same job to several workers.
no-f-441 · level teorema · notes
The assignment model $\mathrm{AP}$ has variables $x_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$ and constraints «every row adds up to $1$», «every column adds up to $1$». Which statement about its linear relaxation ($0 \le x_{ij}$ instead of $x_{ij} \in \{0,1\}$) is correct?
  1. The relaxation always has a fractional optimum
  2. The relaxation has an integer optimal vertex, that is, an assignment, since it is a transportation problem with supplies and demands $1$
  3. The relaxation is unbounded
  4. The relaxation has the same value only if the costs are distinct
Explanation. The relaxation is a transportation model (totally unimodular constraint matrix and integer right-hand sides), so its vertices are integer: an optimal vertex is an assignment. Fractional optima may exist, but they are not better.
no-f-442 · level facile · notes
A $3 \times 3$ assignment instance has costs with rows $(4,2,5)$, $(3,6,4)$, $(7,5,3)$ (rows = workers, columns = jobs). What does the assignment worker $1 \to$ job $2$, $2 \to 1$, $3 \to 3$ cost?
  1. $8$
  2. $10$
  3. $9$
  4. $7$
Explanation. $c_{12}+c_{21}+c_{33}=2+3+3=8$. It is also the minimum: every row uses its smallest entry, and the three minima are in different columns.
no-f-443 · level facile · notes
The linear relaxation of the $n \times n$ assignment is a transportation problem with supplies and demands $1$. How many basic cells with value $0$ does each of its basic feasible solutions have?
  1. $0$
  2. $n$
  3. $n-1$
  4. $2n-1$
Explanation. A basis has $2n-1$ cells, but an assignment has only $n$ cells with value $1$: the other $n-1$ basic cells are $0$. Every basic solution is therefore degenerate.
no-f-444 · level facile · notes
Why is the potential method not well suited to assignment, even though it solves its linear relaxation?
  1. Because every basis is degenerate, and many pivots have $\theta=0$ without improving the cost
  2. Because it does not find integer solutions
  3. Because it requires positive costs
  4. Because an initial solution cannot be built
Explanation. With $n-1$ basic zeros, many pivots move $\theta=0$: in the notes' example $4$ of the $6$ pivots are degenerate. This is why the Hungarian algorithm is used.
no-f-445 · level facile · notes
The northwest corner rule is applied to a $4 \times 4$ assignment instance (supplies and demands $1$). Which solution is obtained?
  1. The cells $(1,1)$, $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(1,4)$ with value $1$
  2. A solution with $4$ basic cells, all equal to $1$
  3. A fractional solution
  4. The diagonal with value $1$, plus $3$ basic cells with value $0$
Explanation. Every cell $(i,i)$ exhausts row and column together: the row is crossed out, and the cell below, $(i+1,i)$, enters with $0$. One gets the diagonal and $3$ basic zeros: $2n-1=7$ cells.
no-f-446 · level ipotesi · notes
In the linear relaxation of assignment every basic solution has $n-1$ zero basic cells. Which general fact about transportation bases explains it?
  1. Bases have $m\,n$ cells
  2. A basis always has $m+n-1$ cells (a spanning tree), whatever the supplies and demands
  3. The reduced costs of the basic cells are positive
  4. Basic cells lie on the diagonal
Explanation. The number of basic cells depends only on $m$ and $n$ ($m+n-1=2n-1$), while the cells with positive value in an assignment are $n$. The difference, $n-1$, are basic zeros.
no-f-447 · level facile · notes
In assignment, $3$ is subtracted from all the entries of row $2$ of the cost matrix. What happens to the optimal assignments?
  1. They change: row $2$ is now more convenient
  2. They change only if an entry becomes negative
  3. Their cost decreases by $3 \cdot n$
  4. They stay the same: every assignment uses exactly one entry of row $2$, so it costs $3$ less
Explanation. Every assignment takes exactly one entry of row $2$, so all costs drop by $3$ and the order does not change. It is the potential change $\pi_2=3$.
no-f-448 · level facile · notes
After Step 0 of the Hungarian algorithm (row and column reduction) the reduced matrix has all entries $\ge 0$ and the dual value is $8$. An assignment uses only cells with reduced entry $0$. What is concluded?
  1. It costs $0$
  2. It is optimal and costs $8$
  3. It is feasible but not necessarily optimal
  4. It costs at least $8$, but another iteration is needed
Explanation. The cost of an assignment is the sum of its reduced costs plus the dual value: $0+8=8$, and no assignment can cost less since the reduced costs are $\ge 0$. The true cost is not $0$.
no-f-449 · level teorema · notes
Let $\bar{c}$ be an $n \times n$ matrix with nonnegative entries. Which statement of the König–Egerváry theorem is correct?
  1. The maximum number of independent zeros (never two in the same row or column) equals the minimum number of lines (rows or columns) covering all the zeros
  2. The number of zeros equals the number of lines
  3. The zeros are always covered by $n$ lines and never fewer
  4. The maximum number of independent zeros is always $n$
Explanation. It is a min–max theorem, like max-flow min-cut: independent zeros as a matching in the bipartite network of the zeros, lines as a cover. With fewer than $n$ independent zeros, fewer than $n$ lines suffice.
no-f-450 · level facile · notes
In a $3 \times 3$ matrix with nonnegative entries the zeros are in the cells $(1,1)$, $(2,1)$, $(3,1)$, $(3,2)$. What is the minimum number of lines (rows or columns) covering all the zeros?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $2$
Explanation. Column $1$ covers three zeros and row $3$ (or column $2$) the fourth: $2$ lines. By König–Egerváry the maximum number of independent zeros is also $2$, for instance $(1,1)$ and $(3,2)$.
no-f-451 · level ipotesi · notes
Theorem: in the Hungarian algorithm, Step 3 (subtract the minimum $\delta$ of the uncovered entries, add it to those covered twice) keeps $\bar{c} \ge 0$ and increases the dual value. Why does the dual value grow?
  1. Because $\delta$ is always $1$
  2. Because the uncovered rows outnumber the covered columns: with $k<n$ lines, the dual grows by $(n-k)\,\delta>0$
  3. Because a row is added to the matrix
  4. Because the independent zeros decrease
Explanation. $\pi_i$ grows by $\delta$ for uncovered rows and $\pi_j$ by $\delta$ for covered columns; the dual $\sum\pi_i-\sum\pi_j$ gains $\delta$ per uncovered row and loses $\delta$ per covered column, $(n-k)\,\delta$ in total. $\delta$ is any positive number.
no-f-452 · level facile · notes
In Step 0 of the Hungarian algorithm on a $4 \times 4$ instance the row minima are $2,1,2,1$ and, after subtracting them, the column minima are $0,2,0,0$. What is the lower bound (dual value) on the cost of every assignment?
  1. $6$
  2. $2$
  3. $4$
  4. $8$
Explanation. The dual value is the sum of all subtracted amounts: $2+1+2+1+2=8$, and every assignment costs at least that much. $6$ forgets the column reduction.
no-f-453 · level facile · notes
In an iteration of the Hungarian algorithm on a $4 \times 4$ matrix $3$ independent zeros are found, covered by $3$ lines, and the minimum uncovered entry is $\delta=1$. How does the dual value change after Step 3?
  1. It stays the same
  2. It grows by $1$
  3. It grows by $3$
  4. It decreases by $1$
Explanation. The dual value grows by $(n-k)\,\delta=(4-3) \cdot 1=1$. This is how the algorithm progresses: the lower bound rises until $n$ independent zeros are found.
no-f-454 · level facile · notes
In the transportation problem with supplies $a=(30,30,30)$ (origins $1,2,3$) and demands $d=(10,25,15,40)$ (destinations $4,5,6,7$), the northwest corner rule fills the cell $(3,7)$ last. With what amount?
  1. $40$
  2. $30$
  3. $10$
  4. $0$
Explanation. At the end only row $3$, with supply $30$, and column $7$ are active; column $7$ still needs $40-10=30$ units (the first $10$ come from row $2$): $x_{37}=30$, and row and column are crossed out together.
no-f-455 · level facile · notes
In the Hungarian algorithm for the $n \times n$ assignment, when does the algorithm stop?
  1. When the minimum uncovered entry is $0$
  2. After $n$ iterations
  3. When all entries of the reduced matrix are $0$
  4. When a maximum set of independent zeros of the reduced matrix has $n$ elements: it is an optimal assignment
Explanation. $n$ independent zeros form an assignment of zero reduced cost, optimal since the reduced costs are $\ge 0$. The number of iterations depends on the data.

Part: The network simplex (74)

8. The network simplex (74)

no-f-151 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex a tree structure $(T,L,U)$ splits the arcs into: $T$ (spanning tree), $L$ (outside the tree, at the lower bound $0$) and $U$ (outside the tree, at capacity $u_{ij}$). An arc of $U$ has flow $u_{ij}>0$. Is it a basic arc?
  1. No: the basic variables are the arcs of $T$, and $U$ is nonbasic
  2. Yes, because its flow is positive
  3. Yes, as long as its capacity is finite
  4. Only if the tree solution is degenerate
Explanation. The basic arcs are those of $T$, even with flow $0$ or $u_{ij}$; the arcs of $L$ and $U$ are nonbasic, fixed at $0$ or at their capacity.
no-f-152 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow, with structure $(T,L,U)$ ($L$: nonbasic arcs at $0$, $U$: nonbasic arcs at capacity) and reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, which arcs are eligible to enter the basis?
  1. The arcs of $L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$ and those of $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$
  2. All the arcs with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, wherever they are
  3. The arcs of $L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$ and those of $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$
  4. The arcs of $T$ with $\bar{c}_{ij} \neq 0$
Explanation. An arc of $L$ is worth increasing if $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, an arc of $U$ is worth decreasing if $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$. The first option is the test of a maximization; the arcs of $T$ have $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$.
no-f-153 · level facile · notes
In the pivot of the network simplex the entering arc $e=(k,l)$ closes a cycle with the tree $T$, oriented so that the flow of $e$ moves away from its bound. The entering arc is in $U$ (nonbasic, at capacity). How is the cycle oriented?
  1. In the direction of $e$, from $k$ to $l$
  2. Against $e$, because its flow must decrease
  3. From the root of the tree towards node $k$
  4. So that the apex of the cycle is node $k$
Explanation. An arc of $U$ enters by decreasing its flow: the cycle traverses it backwards. If $e \in L$ the cycle goes in the direction of $e$.
no-f-154 · level facile · notes
The potentials of a tree $T$ with root $n$ have $\pi_n=0$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$. The path from $i$ to the root traverses the arc $(i,a)$ (cost $4$) from tail to head, then the arc $(n,a)$ (cost $6$) from head to tail. What is $\pi_i$?
  1. $10$
  2. $2$
  3. $-10$
  4. $-2$
Explanation. $\pi_i$ is the cost of the tree path from $i$ to $n$, with $+c_{ij}$ for the arcs traversed from tail to head and $-c_{ij}$ for the others: $4-6=-2$. Check: $\pi_a=-6$ from $c_{na}-\pi_n+\pi_a=0$, and $\pi_i=c_{ia}+\pi_a=-2$.
no-f-155 · level facile · notes
Let $\vet{\pi}$ be the potentials of a spanning tree $T$ ($\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$). What does the reduced cost $\bar{c}_{kl}$ of an arc $(k,l)$ outside the tree represent?
  1. The cost of the fundamental cycle of $(k,l)$, traversed in the direction of $(k,l)$
  2. The cost of the tree path from $k$ to the root
  3. The residual capacity of the fundamental cycle of $(k,l)$
  4. The change of the dual value when $(k,l)$ enters
Explanation. The cost of a cycle does not change when the costs are replaced by the reduced costs; the tree arcs have $\bar{c}_{ij}=0$, so only $\bar{c}_{kl}$ remains (lemma «the cost of a cycle»).
no-f-156 · level facile · notes
In the pivot of the network simplex $\theta$ units move along the cycle of the entering arc; the blocking arcs are those reaching a bound with step $\theta$, and the apex is the first node shared by the paths to the root. If several arcs are blocking, which one leaves the tree?
  1. The first blocking arc met starting from the apex
  2. The blocking arc with the smallest index
  3. The blocking arc with the largest cost
  4. The last blocking arc met traversing the cycle from the apex
Explanation. The leaving arc rule chooses the last blocking arc from the apex, in the direction of the cycle: it keeps the tree strongly feasible and prevents cycling.
no-f-157 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex (root $n$, $\pi_n=0$) the arc $e=(k,l)$ with reduced cost $\bar{c}_{kl}$ enters and the arc $f \neq e$ leaves. Which potentials change after the pivot?
  1. All of them, by the same amount $\bar{c}_{kl}$
  2. Only the potentials $\pi_k$ and $\pi_l$
  3. Only those of the part cut off by $f$ without the root, by $\pm\bar{c}_{kl}$
  4. None, if the pivot is degenerate
Explanation. Removing $f$ splits the tree into $R$ (with the root) and $S$; the potentials of $R$ stay, those of $S$ change by $-\bar{c}_{kl}$ if $l \in S$ and by $+\bar{c}_{kl}$ if $k \in S$, also in a degenerate pivot.
no-f-158 · level facile · notes
In the pivot of the network simplex the step $\theta$ is the largest flow that can be moved along the cycle of the entering arc without violating the bounds. We get $\theta=+\infty$. What do we conclude on the minimum cost flow model?
  1. The current tree solution is optimal
  2. It is unbounded: the cycle is directed, negative and with infinite capacities
  3. It is infeasible: no feasible flow exists
  4. The pivot is degenerate and the basis does not change
Explanation. A backward arc or an arc with finite capacity would give a finite $\theta$: all the arcs of the cycle are forward with $u_{ij}=+\infty$, and the cost of the cycle is $-|\bar{c}_{kl}|<0$.
no-f-159 · level facile · notes
Phase 0 of the network simplex adds an artificial arc between every node and the root, of cost $c^0=1$ (the original arcs cost $0$), and starts by sending $|b_i|$ units on the artificial arc of every node. A network has balances $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$. What does the initial tree cost?
  1. $14$
  2. $7$
  3. $0$
  4. $6$
Explanation. The initial cost is $\sum_i |b_i|=5+2+0+3+0+4=14$. $7$ counts only the supplies (or only the demands).
no-f-160 · level facile · notes
Phase 0 of the network simplex minimizes the flow on the artificial arcs (cost $1$ on them, $0$ on the original arcs of the network $G$). It ends with optimal value $z_0=3$. What do we conclude?
  1. Continue with the original costs from the final tree
  2. The minimum cost flow model is unbounded
  3. A dummy node with balance $-3$ must be added
  4. The network $G$ has no feasible flow
Explanation. $G$ has a feasible flow if and only if $z_0=0$: with $z_0>0$ some units stay on the artificial arcs in every solution.
no-f-161 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow, a pivot with an entering arc of reduced cost $\bar{c}_{kl}=-4$ (from the set $L$) has step $\theta=3$. How does the cost of the tree solution change?
  1. It grows by $12$
  2. It decreases by $12$
  3. It decreases by $4$
  4. It does not change
Explanation. The cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|=3 \cdot 4=12$; it would not change only with $\theta=0$ (degenerate pivot).
no-f-162 · level facile · notes
A feasible tree structure $(T,L,U)$ with root $n$ may have degenerate tree arcs, with $x_{ij}=0$ or $x_{ij}=u_{ij}$. When is it strongly feasible?
  1. When no tree arc is degenerate
  2. When all the tree arcs point towards the root
  3. When the arcs with $x_{ij}=0$ point towards the root and those with $x_{ij}=u_{ij}$ away from it
  4. When the set $U$ of arcs at capacity is empty
Explanation. Equivalently every node can push a positive amount of flow to the root along the tree; a nondegenerate structure always is, but the definition allows degenerate arcs oriented the right way.
no-f-163 · level media · notes
In the network simplex the current potentials are $\vet{\pi}=(7,5,6,2,3,0)$, root $6$. The arc $(k,l)=(2,5)$ enters with $\bar{c}_{25}=-2$; removing the leaving arc, the part of the tree without the root is $S=\{2,4\}$. The potentials of $S$ change by $+\bar{c}_{kl}$ if $k \in S$, by $-\bar{c}_{kl}$ if $l \in S$. What are the new potentials?
  1. $(7,3,6,0,3,0)$
  2. $(7,7,6,4,3,0)$
  3. $(5,3,4,0,1,0)$
  4. $(7,3,6,2,3,0)$
Explanation. Here $k=2 \in S$, so $\pi_2=5-2=3$, $\pi_4=2-2=0$; the others stay. Check: $\bar{c}_{25}$ becomes $c_{25}-3+3=0$, since it was $c_{25}-5+3=-2$.
no-f-164 · level teorema · notes
Let $(T,L,U)$ be a feasible tree structure ($L$: nonbasic arcs at $0$, $U$: nonbasic arcs at capacity), with potentials $\vet{\pi}$ and reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$. Which statement on the tree solution is correct?
  1. It is optimal if $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on all the arcs of $L \cup U$
  2. It is optimal if $\bar{c}_{ij} \le 0$ on the arcs of $L$ and $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on those of $U$
  3. It is optimal only if it is not degenerate, whatever the reduced costs
  4. It is optimal if $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on the arcs of $L$ and $\bar{c}_{ij} \le 0$ on those of $U$
Explanation. By the lemma «cost and reduced cost» $\vet{c}'\vet{y}-\vet{c}'\vet{x}=\sum \bar{c}_{ij}(y_{ij}-x_{ij}) \ge 0$: on $L$ $y \ge 0=x$ and $\bar{c} \ge 0$, on $U$ $y \le u=x$ and $\bar{c} \le 0$. With strict inequalities it is the unique optimum.
no-f-165 · level teorema · notes
In the minimum cost flow model on a connected network with $n$ nodes the redundant constraint of node $n$ is removed; a basis is a set of independent columns (arcs) as many as the rank. Which statement on the bases is correct?
  1. A basis is a set of $n$ arcs without cycles
  2. $n-1$ arcs form a basis if and only if they form a spanning tree
  3. A basis is a directed path from a supply node to a demand node
  4. The bases are the sets of arcs with positive flow
Explanation. The columns of a tree are independent, those of a cycle dependent; a leaf other than $n$ gives a row with a single $\pm 1$, and by induction the basis matrix is triangular with determinant $\pm 1$.
no-f-166 · level teorema · notes
The network simplex can make degenerate pivots ($\theta=0$). The leaving arc rule chooses the last blocking arc from the apex of the cycle. Which statement on the finiteness of the algorithm is correct?
  1. It always terminates, with any rule for choosing the leaving arc
  2. It can never make degenerate pivots, so it terminates
  3. With a strongly feasible initial structure and that rule it terminates, for any choice of the entering arc
  4. It terminates only if the arc with the largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$ always enters
Explanation. With strongly feasible trees and the last-blocking-arc rule, a degenerate pivot lowers the potentials of a part of the tree, and no structure repeats; the entering arc rule is free.
no-f-167 · level ipotesi · notes
In the proof of the optimality of a tree solution $\vet{x}$ its cost is compared with that of another feasible flow $\vet{y}$, writing $\vet{c}'\vet{y}-\vet{c}'\vet{x}=\sum_{(i,j) \in A}\bar{c}_{ij}(y_{ij}-x_{ij})$. Which result gives this identity?
  1. The lemma «cost and reduced cost», applied to two flows with the same balances
  2. The max-flow min-cut theorem
  3. The total unimodularity of the incidence matrix
  4. The cycle decomposition lemma
Explanation. $\vet{c}'\vet{x}=\bar{\vet{c}}'\vet{x}+\vet{b}'\vet{\pi}$ for every $\vet{x}$ with $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$; subtracting the two identities, the term $\vet{b}'\vet{\pi}$ cancels.
no-f-168 · level ipotesi · notes
In the pivot of the network simplex the new flow is $\vet{x}'=\vet{x}+\theta\vet{x}^C$, where $\vet{x}^C$ is $+1$ on the cycle arcs traversed forward, $-1$ on those traversed backward and $0$ elsewhere. Why does $\vet{x}'$ keep the balances, $\mat{B}\vet{x}'=\vet{b}$?
  1. Because $\theta$ is the minimum of the residual capacities of the cycle
  2. Because the current tree is strongly feasible
  3. Because the columns of a cycle, with these signs, add up to zero: $\mat{B}\vet{x}^C=\vet{0}$
  4. Because the potential of the root is zero
Explanation. The signed incidence vector of the cycle is in the null space of $\mat{B}$ (lemma «columns of a cycle»); $\theta$ is used to respect the bounds instead, and the lemma «the cost of a cycle» to compute the change of cost.
no-f-169 · level ipotesi · notes
Phase 0 applies the network simplex to the network with the artificial arcs, with costs $c^0=1$ on the artificial arcs and $0$ on the original ones, starting from the tree of the artificial arcs. To prove that it terminates and does not stop for unboundedness, what is used?
  1. The max-flow min-cut theorem and the cut condition
  2. The initial tree is strongly feasible, and with costs $c^0 \ge 0$ there are no negative cycles
  3. The network with the artificial arcs is acyclic
  4. The original problem has at least one feasible flow
Explanation. Finiteness comes from the theorem on strongly feasible trees; with non-negative costs no cycle has negative cost, so $\theta=+\infty$ cannot occur. Whether the network is feasible is what Phase 0 decides, not an assumption.
no-f-170 · level facile · notes
A connected network with $n$ nodes is balanced, $\sum_i b_i=0$. The network simplex removes from the constraints $\mat{B}\vet{x}=\vet{b}$ the row of node $n$. Why can it be removed without changing the set of solutions?
  1. Because node $n$ has balance $b_n=0$
  2. Because node $n$ is a leaf of the tree
  3. Because the rows of $\mat{B}$ add up to zero, so row $n$ follows from the others
  4. Because row $n$ of $\mat{B}$ is zero
Explanation. Every column has one $+1$ and one $-1$, so the rows add up to zero; with $\sum_i b_i=0$ the constraint of node $n$ is minus the sum of the others. The rank stays $n-1$.
no-f-171 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex with a strongly feasible structure, the pivot cycle is traversed from the apex $w$: tree path from $w$ to $p$, entering arc, path from $q$ to $w$. The pivot is degenerate ($\theta=0$). Where are the blocking arcs?
  1. All on the path from $w$ to $p$, before the entering arc
  2. All on the path from $q$ to $w$, after the entering arc
  3. One of them is always the entering arc itself
  4. They are spread at random along the cycle
Explanation. Lemma «degenerate pivots»: by strong feasibility the entering arc and the arcs from $q$ up to the apex have positive residual capacity, so with $\theta=0$ the blocking arcs lie on the part from $w$ to $p$.
no-f-172 · level facile · notes
Phase 0 ends with $z_0=0$ (no flow on the artificial arcs) and Phase 0 reduced costs $\bar{c}^0_{ij}$. The network simplex continues with the original costs from the final tree. Which arcs are allowed to enter?
  1. All the original arcs, with any $\bar{c}^0_{ij}$
  2. Only the artificial arcs
  3. Only the arcs with $\bar{c}^0_{ij}>0$
  4. Only the arcs with $\bar{c}^0_{ij}=0$
Explanation. Proposition «from Phase 0 to the network simplex»: the arcs with $\bar{c}^0_{ij} \neq 0$ stay «frozen» at their bound, and this guarantees that the flow on the artificial arcs stays zero in every flow met.
no-f-173 · level facile · notes
The tree solution of an uncapacitated network is computed by removing one leaf $\ell \neq n$ at a time: the arc of the leaf carries its residual balance $\hat{b}_\ell$. A leaf with $\hat{b}_\ell=3$ is the head of its arc $(j,\ell)$. What is the flow on the arc?
  1. $3$
  2. $-3$
  3. $0$
  4. $6$
Explanation. If the arc enters the leaf, $x_{j\ell}=-\hat{b}_\ell=-3$: the leaf should send $3$ units but can only receive them, and the tree structure is not feasible. If the arc left the leaf it would be $+3$.
no-f-205 · level facile · notes
In an uncapacitated network the network simplex lets an arc of $L$ (flow $0$) with $\bar{c}_{kl}=-3$ enter; the cycle of the entering arc, oriented as the arc, has two backward arcs with flows $4$ and $2$, and the others forward. The tree solution costs $50$. What does it cost after the pivot?
  1. $38$
  2. $47$
  3. $44$
  4. $56$
Explanation. Without capacities only backward arcs block: $\theta=\min\{4,2\}=2$, and the cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|=6$, to $44$. $38$ uses $\theta=4$, $47$ forgets $\theta$, $56$ gets the sign wrong.
no-f-206 · level facile · notes
In an uncapacitated network (all $u_{ij}=+\infty$) the network simplex lets an arc of $L$ enter and moves $\theta$ units along its cycle, oriented as the entering arc. Which arcs of the cycle can limit $\theta$?
  1. Only the forward arcs
  2. Only the backward arcs, which lose flow
  3. Only the entering arc
  4. All the arcs of the cycle
Explanation. Forward arcs increase and, without capacities, never reach a bound; backward arcs decrease and block when they reach $0$. If the cycle has no backward arc, $\theta=+\infty$ and the model is unbounded.
no-f-207 · level facile · notes
In a capacitated network, the cycle of a network simplex pivot (oriented as the flow moves) has a forward arc with $x=2$, $u=5$, a backward arc with $x=4$, and a forward arc with $x=1$, $u=2$. What is the step $\theta$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $4$
  4. $3$
Explanation. A forward arc can grow by $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, a backward arc decrease by $x_{ij}$: $\theta=\min\{5-2,\,4,\,2-1\}=1$. $2$ ignores the capacities of the forward arcs, $3$ looks only at the first arc.
no-f-208 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex a pivot is degenerate: the step is $\theta=0$, because a blocking tree arc is already at its bound. What changes after the pivot?
  1. Flow, cost and tree all stay the same
  2. The cost drops by $|\bar{c}_{kl}|$
  3. The flow changes but the cost stays the same
  4. The tree and the potentials change, not the flow or the cost
Explanation. With $\theta=0$ no flow moves, so the cost stays; but the entering arc replaces the leaving arc in the tree, and the potentials of the cut-off part change. This is why a rule against cycling is needed.
no-f-209 · level teorema · notes
A tree structure $(T,L,U)$ on a network with $n$ nodes and $m$ arcs is a spanning tree $T$ plus a partition of the other arcs into $L$ (at $0$) and $U$ (at capacity). Which statement on the termination of the network simplex is correct?
  1. It always stops after at most $n-1$ pivots
  2. If every pivot has $\theta>0$ it stops, since the cost always drops and the structures are finitely many
  3. It stops only if the network is uncapacitated
  4. If every pivot has $\theta>0$ it stops after at most $m$ pivots
Explanation. A structure determines its solution and hence its cost; with $\theta>0$ the cost drops by $\theta|\bar{c}_{kl}|>0$ and no structure repeats. There are at most $\binom{m}{n-1}2^{m-n+1}$ structures, a finite but not small number.
no-f-210 · level facile · notes
An iteration of the network simplex for minimum cost flow, from a feasible tree structure $(T,L,U)$, consists of some steps. In which order are they carried out?
  1. Cycle and step $\theta$, then potentials, then entering arc
  2. Leaving arc, then entering arc, then potentials
  3. Potentials and reduced costs, entering arc, cycle with $\theta$ and leaving arc, update
  4. Potentials, update of the tree, then choice of the entering arc
Explanation. The potentials give the reduced costs, which say which arcs are eligible; the entering arc defines the cycle, the ratio test gives $\theta$ and the leaving arc, then flow, tree and potentials are updated. If no arc is eligible the solution is optimal.
no-f-211 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex, given a basis $T$ (spanning tree), the bound status splits the nonbasic arcs into $L$ (flow $0$) and $U$ (flow equal to the capacity $u_{ij}$, with $0<u_{ij} \le +\infty$). Which arcs can be in $U$?
  1. Only the arcs with negative cost
  2. All the nonbasic arcs
  3. Only the arcs belonging to a cycle of the tree
  4. Only the nonbasic arcs with finite capacity
Explanation. An arc with $u_{ij}=+\infty$ cannot sit at its capacity, so it is in $L$. The cost does not matter for the status: it only decides whether the arc is worth entering.
no-f-456 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), a basis is a set of $n-1$ arcs with independent columns in the incidence matrix without the row of node $n$. With $n=6$ nodes and $m=9$ arcs, how many arcs are nonbasic?
  1. $5$
  2. $6$
  3. $3$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The basis has $n-1=5$ arcs, so $m-(n-1)=4$ arcs are nonbasic, split between $L$ (flow $0$) and $U$ (flow at capacity).
no-f-457 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), why is the conservation constraint of node $n$ removed?
  1. Because the rows of the incidence matrix and the balances add up to zero: that constraint is implied by the others
  2. Because node $n$ has no arcs
  3. Because node $n$ always has zero balance
  4. Because then the matrix becomes square
Explanation. With $\sum_i b_i=0$ every constraint is a combination of the others, and the rank is $n-1$: removing one row leaves a matrix of full row rank. The matrix stays $(n-1) \times m$, not square.
no-f-458 · level facile · notes
In the simplex method with bounds, for a minimization problem, when is a nonbasic variable at its upper bound a convenient entering variable?
  1. When its reduced cost is negative
  2. When its reduced cost is zero
  3. When its reduced cost is positive: decreasing it lowers the cost
  4. Never: only variables at the lower bound enter
Explanation. A variable at its upper bound can only decrease; decreasing it lowers the cost if the reduced cost is positive. Variables at the lower bound are instead convenient with negative reduced cost.
no-f-459 · level facile · notes
In the simplex method with bounds, what does the ratio test decide when a variable enters?
  1. The largest step keeping all basic variables within their bounds, and which variable leaves
  2. The reduced cost of the entering variable
  3. Which nonbasic variable enters
  4. The new simplex multipliers
Explanation. The entering variable moves until a basic variable (or the entering one itself) reaches a bound; that variable leaves. In the network simplex the ratio test becomes the computation of $\theta$ along the cycle.
no-f-460 · level facile · notes
How do the revised simplex method (with matrices) and the network simplex for minimum cost flow correspond?
  1. Not at all: the network simplex is a different combinatorial algorithm
  2. The network simplex solves only the transportation problem
  3. The network simplex uses the same matrices, explicitly inverted
  4. Bases are spanning trees, multipliers are potentials computed along the tree, the ratio test is a shift along a cycle
Explanation. The network simplex performs exactly the operations of the bounded simplex, without matrices: everything is computed on the tree. The potential method for transportation is a special case.
no-f-461 · level teorema · notes
In the bounded simplex for a minimization problem, with nonbasic variables at the lower bound ($L$) or the upper bound ($U$), which condition certifies the optimality of a basic feasible solution?
  1. All reduced costs are $\ge 0$
  2. The reduced costs are $\ge 0$ on the variables of $L$ and $\le 0$ on those of $U$
  3. The reduced costs are $\le 0$ on the variables of $L$ and $\ge 0$ on those of $U$
  4. All basic variables are strictly between their bounds
Explanation. No nonbasic variable can reduce the cost: those in $L$ can only increase (reduced cost $\ge 0$ needed), those in $U$ only decrease ($\le 0$ needed). «All $\ge 0$» is the case without upper bounds.
no-f-462 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), with integer balances and capacities, why is every tree solution integer?
  1. Because the costs are integer
  2. Because the basis matrix of a spanning tree has determinant $\pm 1$ (triangular with $\pm 1$ on the diagonal)
  3. Because the algorithm rounds the flows
  4. Because the network is acyclic
Explanation. The basis of a spanning tree is solved by substitution from the leaves, with only sums and differences of balances and capacities: with integer data the flows are integer. Costs affect the potentials, not the flows.
no-f-463 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), why, if every pivot has $\theta>0$, does the algorithm stop after finitely many pivots?
  1. Because every pivot adds an arc to the tree
  2. Because the potentials grow at every pivot
  3. Because the number of arcs in $U$ decreases
  4. Because every pivot strictly lowers the cost, so no tree structure repeats, and there are finitely many structures
Explanation. A structure $(T,L,U)$ determines the solution and the cost; with strictly decreasing cost one never returns to a structure already seen, and there are finitely many. With degenerate pivots the rule of strongly feasible trees is needed.
no-f-464 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), Step 1 of the algorithm looks for an eligible entering arc. Which arcs are candidates?
  1. The tree arcs with zero reduced cost
  2. All arcs with negative reduced cost
  3. The arcs of $L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$ and the arcs of $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$
  4. The arcs of $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$
Explanation. An arc at flow $0$ is convenient if increasing it costs less ($\bar{c}<0$), a saturated arc if decreasing it saves ($\bar{c}>0$). An arc of $U$ with $\bar{c}<0$ is already in the right position.
no-f-465 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), the «second system» of the revised simplex, $\mat{M}_T\vet{d}=\mat{M}_e$ for the entering arc $e=(k,l)$, is solved on the tree. What is $\vet{d}$?

  3. The vector of reduced costs
  4. The flow of the tree solution
Explanation. $d_a=+1$ on the arcs of the path from $k$ to $l$ traversed from tail to head, $-1$ on the others, $0$ outside: increasing $x_e$ by $t$ changes the basic variables by $-t\,\vet{d}$, that is, sends flow along the fundamental cycle.
no-f-466 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), the «first system» of the revised simplex, $\vet{\pi}'\mat{M}_T=\vet{c}_T'$, gives what in the language of networks?

  4. The potentials, with $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$ on the tree arcs and $\pi_n=0$
Explanation. The column of $(i,j)$ has $+1$ in row $i$ and $-1$ in row $j$ (without row $n$), so the equation of the arc is $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$: zero reduced cost on the basic arcs. It is solved along the tree from the root.
no-f-467 · level facile · notes
The potential method for the transportation problem and the network simplex: how are they related?
  1. The potential method is the network simplex on the bipartite graph of origins and destinations
  2. They are independent algorithms with different optimality criteria
  3. The network simplex is a special case of the potential method
  4. The potential method uses trees, the network simplex does not
Explanation. The basic cells of transportation form a spanning tree of the bipartite network, and potentials, reduced costs and cycles are those of the network simplex. Transportation is the special case.
no-f-468 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), the second system $\mat{M}_T\vet{d}=\mat{M}_e$ is solved with the tree path from $k$ to $l$ ($e=(k,l)$ entering).   3. The correctness of the BFS
  4. The integer vertices theorem
Explanation. The columns of a cycle, with signs given by the direction of traversal, add up to zero: this expresses $\mat{M}_e$ as a combination of the columns of the tree path, with coefficients $\pm 1$.
no-f-469 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), without capacities, the initial structure sends the $5$ units of node $1$ along $1,3,5,4$ and the $2$ of node $2$ along $(2,4)$; node $4$ has $b_4=-3$ and forwards the rest to node $6$.   3. $2$
  4. $4$

no-f-470 · level facile · notes

  3. When the cost stops decreasing for two pivots

  3. A strongly feasible structure never has degenerate pivots
  4. Strong feasibility requires all tree flows to be positive
Explanation. With the last blocking arc rule strong feasibility is preserved, and this rules out cycling. Degenerate pivots may still occur; tree arcs with flow $0$ are allowed if they point towards the root.

  3. $13$: yes
  4. $7$: yes, since it is positive

no-f-473 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (connected network with $n$ nodes, balances $b_i$ summing to zero, capacities $u_{ij}$, costs $c_{ij}$), the arc $(1,3) \in U$ with $\bar{c}_{13}=2>0$ enters, and its flow decreases along the cycle.   3. The flow changes but the cost does not
  4. The tree (hence the potentials), while flow and cost stay the same

no-f-499 · level facile · notes
