  3. The shortest path tree from $r$
  4. All original arcs in $U$
Explanation. The artificial arcs form a star centred at $r$, a spanning tree of $G^0$: every node sends or receives its balance on them. Phase 0 then minimizes the flow on these arcs.
no-f-500 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), which statement about computing the tree solution is correct?
  1. A linear system is solved by Gaussian elimination, with no shortcut
  2. The flows of $U$ are fixed, the residual balances computed, then one leaf $\ell \neq n$ at a time is removed: its tree arc carries $\pm$ the residual balance of $\ell$
  3. All supplies are sent to the root along the tree, ignoring $U$
  4. The flow on the tree arcs is always positive
Explanation. A leaf has a single tree arc, which must carry its residual balance (with the sign given by the arc's direction); the balance passes to the neighbour. Tree flows may be zero (degeneracy) or out of bounds (infeasible structure).
no-f-501 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), which statement about the potentials of a spanning tree $T$ is correct?
  1. They exist only if $T$ is a path
  2. They are unique only if the costs are distinct
  3. They coincide with the shortest path distances from $n$
  4. They exist and are unique; $\pi_i$ is the cost of the tree path from $i$ to $n$, with $+c_{ij}$ on arcs traversed forward and $-c_{ij}$ backward
Explanation. The $n-1$ equations $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$ on the tree arcs and $\pi_n=0$ have a unique solution, computed along the tree from the root; they are the simplex multipliers of the basis. They are not distances: backward arcs count with a minus sign.
no-f-502 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), let $C$ be the fundamental cycle of the arc $(k,l) \notin T$, traversed in the direction of $(k,l)$, and $c(C)$ its cost (forward arcs with $+c_{ij}$, backward with $-c_{ij}$). Which statement is correct?
  1. $c(C)=\bar{c}_{kl}$: the reduced cost of the nonbasic arc is the cost of its fundamental cycle
  2. $c(C)=c_{kl}$
  3. $c(C)=0$ for every cycle
  4. $c(C)=\pi_k-\pi_l$
Explanation. On every cycle the potentials cancel, so $c(C)$ is the signed sum of the reduced costs; those of the tree arcs are zero and $\bar{c}_{kl}$ remains. It is the saving (or cost) per unit sent along the cycle.
no-f-503 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Lemma: the cost of the fundamental cycle of $(k,l)$ is $\bar{c}_{kl}$. Which fact, besides the cancellation of the potentials along the cycle, is needed to conclude?
  1. The costs are nonnegative
  2. The cycle is directed
  3. The tree arcs have zero reduced cost, by definition of the potentials
  4. The arc $(k,l)$ is in $L$
Explanation. The signed sum of the reduced costs on the cycle contains $\bar{c}_{kl}$ and the reduced costs of the tree arcs, which are zero. The lemma holds for every fundamental cycle, directed or not, and for $(k,l)$ in $L$ or $U$.
no-f-504 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), in a pivot $\theta$ units are moved along the cycle $C$ of the entering arc $e=(k,l)$ (in the direction of $e$ if $e \in L$, opposite if $e \in U$). Which statement is correct?
  1. The cost changes by $\theta\,\bar{c}_{kl}$ in every case, so it grows if $e \in U$
  2. The new flow violates conservation at the nodes of the cycle
  3. The new flow is feasible only if $\theta=1$
  4. The new flow is feasible and the cost decreases by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|$
Explanation. Moving flow along a cycle preserves the balances; with $\theta$ at most the minimum residual capacity the bounds stay respected. The direction is chosen so that the cycle costs $-|\bar{c}_{kl}|$ per unit, both for $e \in L$ and $e \in U$.
no-f-505 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), $e=(k,l)$ enters and $f \neq e$ leaves; removing $f$ from the tree leaves the components $R$ (with the root $n$) and $S$. Which statement about updating the potentials is correct?
  1. All potentials change by the same amount
  2. Only $\pi_k$ and $\pi_l$ change
  3. The potentials of $R$ stay the same, those of $S$ all change by $-\bar{c}_{kl}$ (if $l \in S$) or by $+\bar{c}_{kl}$ (if $k \in S$)
  4. They are recomputed from scratch because the tree changed
Explanation. Nodes of $R$ reach the root by the same path; those of $S$ now reach it through $e$, and a uniform shift makes the reduced cost of $e$ zero. There is no need to recompute everything.
no-f-506 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Proposition: after a pivot ($e=(k,l)$ enters, $f$ leaves) the potentials of the nodes of the component $S$ of $T \setminus \{f\}$ without the root all change by $\mp\bar{c}_{kl}$. Which fact justifies that this shift is enough?
  1. The potentials of the new tree are unique, and the shift gives zero reduced cost on all arcs of the new tree
  2. The costs are integer
  3. The leaving arc is always adjacent to the root
  4. The pivot is nondegenerate
Explanation. Arcs inside $S$ or inside $R$ keep zero reduced cost (both endpoints move together), and $e$, between $R$ and $S$, becomes zero with the right shift: by uniqueness these are the potentials of $T'$. It also holds for degenerate pivots.
no-f-507 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the pivot step is $\theta=+\infty$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The structure is degenerate and another entering arc is needed
  2. The model is unbounded: the cycle is directed, its arcs have infinite capacity and it costs $-|\bar{c}_{kl}|<0$
  3. The current solution is optimal
  4. The entering arc is in $U$
Explanation. $\theta=+\infty$ means no arc blocks: all arcs of the cycle are forward with infinite capacity, and sending $t$ units lowers the cost by $t\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|$ without limit. An arc of $U$ has finite capacity, so it blocks.
no-f-508 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Theorem: if $\bar{c}_{ij} \ge 0$ on $L$ and $\bar{c}_{ij} \le 0$ on $U$, the tree solution is optimal; if the inequalities are strict, it is the unique optimum. What more is needed for uniqueness?
  1. That the flow is integer
  2. That every other feasible flow differs on some nonbasic arc, where a strict inequality makes the cost grow
  3. That the tree is a path
  4. That all costs are positive
Explanation. The cost of another flow $\vet{y}$ exceeds that of $\vet{x}$ by $\sum \bar{c}_{ij}(y_{ij}-x_{ij})$ over the nonbasic arcs; with strict inequalities every deviation costs something, and a flow equal outside the tree coincides on the tree too.
no-f-509 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the structure is strongly feasible and the pivot cycle is traversed from the apex $w$: path $P$ from $w$ to an endpoint of $e$, the arc $e$, path $Q$ from the other endpoint to $w$. Which statement is correct?
  1. The blocking arcs always lie on $Q$
  2. A degenerate pivot cannot happen
  3. If $\theta=0$, all blocking arcs lie on $P$; the arcs of $Q$ and $e$ have positive residual capacity
  4. If $\theta=0$, the blocking arc is $e$
Explanation. With strong feasibility flow can be sent to the root from every node, so along $Q$ (towards the apex) there is positive residual capacity; and $e$ has residual capacity $u_{kl}>0$. A block at $\theta=0$ can only come from $P$.
no-f-510 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Theorem: starting from a strongly feasible structure and using as leaving arc the last blocking arc of the cycle traversed from the apex, no structure repeats. In degenerate pivots, what guarantees progress?
  1. The cost, which always drops
  2. The number of arcs in $U$, which grows
  3. The lexicographic order of the entering arcs
  4. A measure on the potentials (their sum) that strictly decreases in degenerate pivots
Explanation. In a degenerate pivot the cost does not change, so another monotone quantity is needed: with the leaving arc rule the potentials of the nodes that change decrease, and their sum strictly decreases. The entering rule can be any.
no-f-511 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Phase 0 applies the network simplex to the auxiliary network (artificial arcs to or from the artificial root $r$, of cost $1$; original arcs of cost $0$). Which statement is correct?
  1. The original network has a feasible flow if and only if the optimal value of Phase 0 is $z_0=0$
  2. Phase 0 finds an optimal flow of the original network
  3. Phase 0 may cycle forever
  4. If $z_0>0$ the original network is unbounded
Explanation. With $z_0=0$ no flow passes on the artificial arcs, and the flow restricted to the original arcs is feasible; with $z_0>0$ no feasible flow exists (infeasibility, not unboundedness). The true optimum is sought afterwards, with the original costs.
no-f-512 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Theorem: Phase 0 stops after finitely many pivots. Which fact about the initial tree of artificial arcs guarantees it?
  1. It is a path
  2. All its flows are positive
  3. It is strongly feasible, and the capacities of the auxiliary network are positive: the finiteness theorem applies
  4. It has zero cost
Explanation. Every node sends or receives its balance on the artificial arc to the root, in the right direction: the initial structure is strongly feasible, and the leaving arc rule prevents cycling. Flows may be zero (zero balances).
no-f-513 · level teorema · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Phase 0 has ended with $z_0=0$, and one continues with the original costs on the auxiliary network, letting enter only arcs with zero Phase 0 reduced cost. Which statement is correct?
  1. One must restart from a tree of original arcs only
  2. The artificial arcs may receive positive flow
  3. The result is optimal only if no artificial arc is in the tree
  4. Every flow met has zero flow on the artificial arcs, and an optimal flow of the original network is obtained (or it is found to be unbounded)
Explanation. Arcs with zero Phase 0 reduced cost cannot bring flow back onto the artificial arcs (which would cost), so the flow stays in the original network. Artificial arcs with flow $0$ may stay in the tree.
no-f-514 · level ipotesi · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$ with $\pi_n=0$; reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), Corollary: with integer balances and capacities every tree solution is integer; with integer costs the potentials are integer too. Which fact is needed for the potentials?
  1. Total unimodularity holds only for flows
  2. The capacities are integer
  3. The flows are integer
  4. The potentials are computed along the tree from the root with $\pi_i=\pi_j \pm c_{ij}$: only sums and differences of costs
Explanation. Starting from $\pi_n=0$ every potential is the signed cost of the tree path to the root, so it is integer if the costs are. Capacities and flows do not enter the computation of the potentials.
no-f-536 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), when is a tree structure feasible?
  1. When all reduced costs are nonnegative
  2. When the tree is a path
  3. When its tree solution satisfies $0 \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ on the arcs of $T$
  4. When $U$ is empty
Explanation. Nonbasic flows are fixed ($0$ on $L$, $u_{ij}$ on $U$) and tree flows are determined by conservation: the structure is feasible if the latter are within bounds. Reduced costs concern optimality.
no-f-537 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the arc $(1,3) \in U$ has capacity $u_{13}=4$, and node $1$ has balance $b_1=5$. Before computing the tree flows, what is the residual balance $\hat{b}_1=b_1-\sum_{(1,j) \in U}u_{1j}+\sum_{(j,1) \in U}u_{j1}$, if no other arc of $U$ touches node $1$?
  1. $1$
  2. $9$
  3. $5$
  4. $-4$
Explanation. The $4$ units on the saturated arc $(1,3)$ already leave node $1$: $5-4=1$ unit remains to be sent along the tree. $9$ adds instead of subtracting.
no-f-538 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the tree has the arcs $(4,5)$ with $c_{45}=5$ and $(3,4)$ with $c_{34}=2$, and the root is $5$. What are $\pi_4$ and $\pi_3$?
  1. $\pi_4=-5$, $\pi_3=-7$
  2. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_3=7$
  3. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_3=3$
  4. $\pi_4=5$, $\pi_3=2$
Explanation. On a tree arc $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$: $\pi_4=\pi_5+5=5$ and $\pi_3=\pi_4+2=7$, the cost of the path $3,4,5$. $\pi_3=2$ forgets to add the potential of $4$.
no-f-539 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the arcs eligible to enter have reduced costs: $(1,2) \in L$ with $\bar{c}=-3$, $(2,4) \in U$ with $\bar{c}=5$, $(3,5) \in L$ with $\bar{c}=-4$. With the rule of the notes (largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$), which arc enters?
  1. $(1,2)$
  2. $(3,5)$, the most negative
  3. None: there is a positive reduced cost
  4. $(2,4)$
Explanation. Absolute values are compared: $|5|>|-4|>|-3|$, so $(2,4)$ enters, and its flow will decrease. Looking only at negative reduced costs works when $U$ is empty.
no-f-540 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), the pivot cycle (oriented as the flow shift) has forward arcs with residual capacities $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$ equal to $3$ and $5$, and backward arcs with flows $x_{ij}$ equal to $2$ and $4$. What is $\theta$?
  1. $3$
  2. $4$
  3. $2$
  4. $5$
Explanation. $\theta$ is the minimum residual capacity along the cycle: forward one can increase by at most $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, backward decrease by at most $x_{ij}$: $\min\{3,5,2,4\}=2$. Looking only at forward arcs gives $3$.
no-f-541 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), an arc with $\bar{c}_{kl}=-3$ enters, the step is $\theta=2$, and the current solution costs $80$. What does the new solution cost?
  1. $86$
  2. $74$
  3. $77$
  4. $78$
Explanation. The cost changes by $\theta\,\bar{c}_{kl}=2 \cdot (-3)=-6$: $80-6=74$. $77$ uses $\bar{c}$ without $\theta$; $86$ gets the sign wrong.
no-f-542 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), in a feasible tree structure a tree arc has flow $0$. When does the structure stay strongly feasible?
  1. If that arc points towards the root
  2. If that arc points away from the root
  3. Never: strong feasibility requires positive flows on the tree
  4. Always
Explanation. From every node positive flow must be sendable to the root along the tree: an empty arc must be traversed forward, so it must point towards the root. A saturated arc must instead point away from the root.
no-f-543 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), in Phase 0 the auxiliary network adds the artificial node $r$ and, for every node $i$, the artificial arc $(i,r)$ if $b_i \ge 0$ or $(r,i)$ if $b_i<0$. With balances $\vet{b}=(5,2,0,-3,0,-4)$, what is the initial value of Phase 0 (total flow on the artificial arcs)?
  1. $7$
  2. $0$
  3. $5$
  4. $14$
Explanation. Every node sends or receives its balance on its own artificial arc: $5+2+0+3+0+4=14$. $7$ counts only the supplies (or only the demands).
no-f-544 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), in Step 3 of the algorithm the pivot step turns out to be $\theta=+\infty$. What does the algorithm return?
  1. The current solution, optimal
  2. That the problem is unbounded
  3. That the problem is infeasible
  4. A degenerate structure to restart from
Explanation. With $\theta=+\infty$ the cycle of the entering arc is directed, of infinite capacity and negative cost: the cost drops without limit. Infeasibility is discovered instead in Phase 0.
no-f-545 · level facile · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow (tree structure $(T,L,U)$: $T$ spanning tree, $L$ nonbasic arcs at flow $0$, $U$ nonbasic arcs at capacity; root $n$, $\pi_n=0$; $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$), without capacities ($U=\emptyset$), the entering arc $(k,l) \in L$ closes a cycle with the tree; the flow increases on the arcs of the cycle in the direction of $(k,l)$ and decreases on the others. Which arcs can block the step $\theta$?
  1. Only the arcs of the cycle in the direction of $(k,l)$
  2. Only the entering arc
  3. Only the arcs traversed backward, whose flow decreases
  4. All arcs of the cycle
Explanation. Without capacities a forward arc can grow without limit; only backward arcs block, when their flow reaches $0$. If there are none, $\theta=+\infty$.

Part: Project management (70)

9. Project management (70)

no-f-174 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method the forward pass computes the earliest starts $\mathit{ES}_j$ and the earliest finishes $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$. Activity $j$ has three immediate predecessors with $\mathit{EF}=7$, $12$, $9$. What is $\mathit{ES}_j$?
  1. $7$
  2. $12$
  3. $28$
  4. $9$
Explanation. $j$ can start only when all its predecessors are finished: $\mathit{ES}_j=\max \mathit{EF}_i=12$. The minimum is the typical mistake; the sum ignores that the predecessors run in parallel.
no-f-175 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method the backward pass computes the latest finishes $\mathit{LF}_j$ and the latest starts $\mathit{LS}_j=\mathit{LF}_j-d_j$ that do not delay the project. Activity $j$, of duration $d_j=4$, has two immediate successors with $\mathit{LS}=20$ and $17$. What are $\mathit{LF}_j$ and $\mathit{LS}_j$?
  1. $\mathit{LF}_j=20$, $\mathit{LS}_j=16$
  2. $\mathit{LF}_j=17$, $\mathit{LS}_j=21$
  3. $\mathit{LF}_j=37$, $\mathit{LS}_j=33$
  4. $\mathit{LF}_j=17$, $\mathit{LS}_j=13$
Explanation. $\mathit{LF}_j=\min \mathit{LS}_k=17$ (it must finish before the most urgent successor starts) and $\mathit{LS}_j=\mathit{LF}_j-d_j=13$.
no-f-176 · level facile · notes
The total slack of an activity is $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$, the free slack is $\mathit{FS}_j=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_j$ over the successors $k$. Activity $j$ has $\mathit{ES}_j=5$, $\mathit{LS}_j=9$, duration $3$, and its successors have $\mathit{ES}=10$ and $\mathit{ES}=12$. What are $\mathit{TS}_j$ and $\mathit{FS}_j$?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_j=4$, $\mathit{FS}_j=2$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_j=4$, $\mathit{FS}_j=4$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_j=2$, $\mathit{FS}_j=4$
  4. $\mathit{TS}_j=4$, $\mathit{FS}_j=0$
Explanation. $\mathit{TS}_j=9-5=4$; $\mathit{EF}_j=5+3=8$ and $\mathit{FS}_j=\min\{10,12\}-8=2$. Always $\mathit{FS}_j \le \mathit{TS}_j$.
no-f-177 · level facile · notes
In a project, the total slack $\mathit{TS}_j$ is the largest delay of $j$ that does not lengthen the project, the free slack $\mathit{FS}_j$ the largest one that moves the earliest start of no successor. Activity $j$ has $\mathit{TS}_j=5$ and $\mathit{FS}_j=2$, and it is delayed by $3$ units. What happens?
  1. The project becomes $3$ units longer
  2. The project becomes $1$ unit longer
  3. The project does not become longer, but some successor must move
  4. Nothing: no other activity moves
Explanation. A delay up to $\mathit{TS}_j=5$ does not delay the project; one beyond $\mathit{FS}_j=2$ moves some successor. With $3$ we are between the two slacks.
no-f-178 · level facile · notes
The project network has one node for every activity, plus the dummy nodes START and FINISH (duration $0$), and one arc for every precedence. A project has $14$ activities. How many nodes does its project network have?
  1. $14$
  2. $16$
  3. $15$
  4. $28$
Explanation. One node per activity plus START (node $0$) and FINISH (node $n+1$): $14+2=16$.
no-f-179 · level facile · notes
A schedule of a project gives every activity a start $t_j$ with $t_j \ge t_i+d_i$ for every precedence ($i$ before $j$), with durations $d_i>0$. The precedences contain a cycle: $1$ before $2$, $2$ before $3$, $3$ before $1$. What can be said?
  1. The minimum duration is the sum of the durations of the cycle
  2. The critical path method ignores the arc that closes the cycle
  3. The cycle becomes a critical path of the project
  4. The project has no schedule at all
Explanation. A schedule exists if and only if the project network is acyclic: adding up the constraints along the cycle would give $0 \ge \sum d_i>0$.
no-f-180 · level facile · notes
In the project network the length of a path from START to FINISH is the sum of the durations of its activities (START and FINISH last $0$). The durations are $d_1=4$, $d_3=2$, $d_5=6$. How long is the path START$,1,3,5,$FINISH?
  1. $12$
  2. $8$
  3. $6$
  4. $14$
Explanation. $4+2+6=12$. The maximum length of a path from START to FINISH is the minimum project duration.
no-f-181 · level facile · notes
A project has minimum duration $T=20$. Activity $j$ has total slack $\mathit{TS}_j=3$ (largest delay that does not lengthen the project), and its duration grows by $\delta=5$, with no other change. How long does the project last now?
  1. $25$
  2. $20$
  3. $22$
  4. $23$
Explanation. $T'=T+\max\{0,\delta-\mathit{TS}_j\}=20+2=22$: the first $3$ units of delay are absorbed by the slack.
no-f-182 · level facile · notes
A path from START to FINISH is critical if it has maximum length $T$; an activity is critical if $\mathit{TS}_j=0$; the arc $(i,j)$ is tight if $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{EF}_i$. A path goes only through critical activities. Is it necessarily critical?
  1. Yes, always
  2. No: it is critical if and only if all its arcs are tight
  3. Yes, as long as it contains the largest number of activities
  4. No: a critical path must contain all the critical activities
Explanation. A path is critical if and only if it is made of tight arcs. With two critical paths, a path crossing them can go only through critical activities without being critical.
no-f-183 · level facile · notes
A project has two critical paths (paths from START to FINISH of maximum length $T$), and an activity lying on only one of them is shortened by one week. How does the minimum duration $T$ change?
  1. It decreases by one week
  2. It decreases by half a week
  3. It increases, because the critical path changes
  4. It does not change
Explanation. To shorten the project every critical path must become shorter: the other critical path still has length $T$.
no-f-184 · level facile · notes
A project is a sequence of $10$ pairs of parallel activities: every activity of a pair has as immediate predecessors the two activities of the previous pair. How many paths from START to FINISH are there in the project network?
  1. $2^{10}=1024$
  2. $20$
  3. $10$
  4. $100$
Explanation. At every pair one of the two activities is chosen: $2^{10}$ paths. This is why the critical path method does not list the paths, but computes the maximum length in $O(n+m)$.
no-f-185 · level facile · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ of the minimum duration minimizes $t_{n+1}-t_0$ subject to $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$ on the arcs of the project network. What does its dual $D(\mathrm{PD})$ represent?
  1. A shortest path from START to FINISH
  2. A maximum flow from START to FINISH
  3. One unit of flow from START to FINISH along a path of maximum length
  4. A minimum spanning tree
Explanation. The dual maximizes $\sum d_i x_{ij}$ with a unit flow from START to FINISH: it is the longest path model, with optimal value $T$.
no-f-186 · level media · notes
A project has minimum duration $T=13$ and two critical paths (of maximum length), START$,1,4,6,8,$FINISH and START$,1,5,7,8,$FINISH; the other paths are at most $11$ long. Which change reduces $T$?
  1. Shortening activity $5$ by $1$
  2. Shortening activity $8$ by $1$
  3. Shortening activity $4$ by $1$
  4. Shortening a non-critical activity by $1$
Explanation. Only an activity common to all the critical paths shortens the project: $1$ and $8$ lie on both, $4$ and $5$ on one only. Shortening $8$ by $1$ makes both critical paths $12$ long, the others stay $\le 11$, and $T=12$.
no-f-187 · level teorema · notes
The forward pass computes the earliest starts $\mathit{ES}_j$; a schedule is a vector of starts $\vet{t}$ with $t_0=0$ that respects the precedences. Which statement on the earliest times is correct?
  1. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the maximum length from START to $j$, and every schedule has $t_j \ge \mathit{ES}_j$
  2. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the minimum length of a path from START to $j$
  3. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the maximum length of a path from $j$ to FINISH
  4. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the maximum length from START to $j$, and every schedule has $t_j \le \mathit{ES}_j$
Explanation. The forward pass computes longest paths from START; $\mathit{ES}$ is the earliest schedule, so every schedule has $t_j \ge \mathit{ES}_j$. The maximum length from $j$ to FINISH is $T-\mathit{LS}_j$.
no-f-188 · level teorema · notes
In a project of minimum duration $T$, let $L_j$ be the maximum length of a path from START to FINISH through activity $j$, and $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$ its total slack. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_j=L_j$ for every activity $j$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_j=T+L_j$ for every activity $j$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{FS}_j$ for every activity $j$
  4. $\mathit{TS}_j=T-L_j$, zero if and only if $j$ is critical
Explanation. $L_j=\mathit{ES}_j+(T-\mathit{LS}_j)=T-\mathit{TS}_j$: the total slack is how much the longest path through $j$ lacks to become critical. The free slack may be smaller.
no-f-189 · level teorema · notes
The LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ minimizes the duration $t_{n+1}-t_0$ of a schedule; its dual $D(\mathrm{PD})$ sends one unit of flow from START to FINISH maximizing $\sum d_i x_{ij}$. Which statement is correct ($T$ minimum duration)?
  1. In general $z(\mathrm{PD})<z(D(\mathrm{PD}))$
  2. $z(\mathrm{PD})=z(D(\mathrm{PD}))=T$, and every critical path is optimal for the dual
  3. Among the schedules, only $\mathit{ES}$ is optimal for $\mathrm{PD}$
  4. The dual has optimal value equal to the sum of all the durations
Explanation. $\mathit{ES}$ and $\mathit{LS}$ have value $T$, the incidence vector of a critical path has value $L(P)=T$: by weak duality they are all optimal.
no-f-190 · level ipotesi · notes
In a project of minimum duration $T$, $L_j$ is the maximum length of a path from START to FINISH through activity $j$, and $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$. To prove that $\mathit{TS}_j=T-L_j$, which results are used?
  1. The total unimodularity of the $\mathrm{PD}$ model
  2. The max-flow min-cut theorem
  3. $\mathit{ES}_j$ and $T-\mathit{LS}_j$ are the maximum lengths from START to $j$ and from $j$ to FINISH
  4. The proposition on the free slack
Explanation. A path from START to FINISH through $j$ is a path to $j$ followed by one from $j$: taking the longest ones (theorems on earliest and latest times), $L_j=\mathit{ES}_j+T-\mathit{LS}_j=T-\mathit{TS}_j$.
no-f-191 · level ipotesi · notes
A path $P=i_0,\dots,i_k$ from START to FINISH is critical if $L(P)=T$; the arc $(i,j)$ is tight if $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{EF}_i$. In the proof that $P$ is critical if and only if all its arcs are tight, what is used?
  1. The telescoping sum $\sum(\mathit{ES}_{i_{s+1}}-\mathit{EF}_{i_s})=T-L(P)$, with terms $\ge 0$
  2. Menger's theorem on disjoint paths
  3. The fact that the free slack of every activity is zero
  4. The uniqueness of the optimal solution of the $\mathrm{PD}$ model
Explanation. Along the path the earliest times cancel in pairs and $T-L(P)$ remains; every term is $\ge 0$ by the forward pass, and a sum of non-negative terms is zero if and only if every term is zero.
no-f-192 · level ipotesi · notes
The theorem «minimum project duration» ($T=\mathit{ES}_{n+1}$, length of a longest path) holds under (H1) «deterministic durations» and (H2) «unlimited resources». What are the two assumptions for?
  1. To guarantee that the project network is acyclic
  2. To make the activity durations integer
  3. To guarantee a unique critical path
  4. (H1) fixes the path lengths; (H2) leaves the precedences as the only constraints
Explanation. With deterministic durations and no resource constraints, the minimum duration is the length of a longest path; acyclicity is needed instead for the existence of a schedule.
no-f-193 · level facile · notes
The critical path method computes a topological order, the forward pass, the backward pass and the slacks. What is its running time on a project network with $n$ activities and $m$ arcs?
  1. $O(n^2)$
  2. $O(n+m)$
  3. $O(n\,m)$
  4. $O(2^n)$
Explanation. Each of the four phases scans every arc a constant number of times: $O(n+m)$, even though the paths may be exponentially many.
no-f-194 · level facile · notes
A schedule of a project is a vector of starts $\vet{t}=(t_0,\dots,t_{n+1})$ with $t_0=0$ and $t_j \ge t_i+d_i$ for every arc $(i,j)$ of the project network. Activity $2$ (duration $d_2=5$) is the only predecessor of activity $4$, and $t_2=3$. Which start of $4$ is feasible?
  1. $t_4=3$
  2. $t_4=5$
  3. $t_4=9$
  4. $t_4=7$
Explanation. We need $t_4 \ge t_2+d_2=3+5=8$: among the options only $t_4=9$ respects the precedence. Starting at $t_4=5$ confuses the duration with the finishing time.
no-f-195 · level facile · notes
In a project a path from START to FINISH has length $L(P)=23$ (sum of the durations of its activities). A schedule $\vet{t}$ respects all the precedences, and $t_{n+1}$ is the start of FINISH, that is, the end of the project. What can be said about $t_{n+1}$?
  1. $t_{n+1} \ge 23$
  2. $t_{n+1}=23$
  3. $t_{n+1} \le 23$
  4. Nothing, without knowing the other paths
Explanation. Lemma «every path bounds the duration»: adding up the constraints $t_{i_{s+1}} \ge t_{i_s}+d_{i_s}$ along the path gives $t_{n+1} \ge L(P)=23$, since the activities of the path run one after the other.
no-f-196 · level teorema · notes
The backward pass computes the latest starts $\mathit{LS}_j$ for a project of minimum duration $T$. Which statement on the latest times is correct?
  1. $\mathit{LS}_j$ is the maximum length of a path from START to $j$
  2. Every schedule, even longer than $T$, has $t_j \le \mathit{LS}_j$
  3. $T-\mathit{LS}_j$ is the maximum length from $j$ to FINISH, and schedules of duration $\le T$ have $t_j \le \mathit{LS}_j$
  4. $T-\mathit{LS}_j$ is the minimum length of a path from $j$ to FINISH
Explanation. Theorem «latest times»: $\mathit{LS}$ is the latest schedule of duration $T$, and the longest tail from $j$ to FINISH is $T-\mathit{LS}_j$. A schedule longer than $T$ may start $j$ later.
no-f-197 · level facile · notes
The Gantt chart of a schedule $\vet{t}$ has one row per activity, with a bar from $t_j$ to $t_j+d_j$. In the chart of the earliest schedule, activity $j$ has $\mathit{ES}_j=4$, $d_j=3$ and $\mathit{LF}_j=10$. Where does its bar go?
  1. From $4$ to $10$
  2. From $7$ to $10$
  3. From $0$ to $3$
  4. From $4$ to $7$
Explanation. The bar goes from $\mathit{ES}_j=4$ to $\mathit{EF}_j=4+3=7$; the stretch from $7$ to $\mathit{LF}_j=10$ is the total slack ($3$), drawn separately in the chart.
no-f-474 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, on which two assumptions is the method based?
  1. Random durations and limited resources
  2. Deterministic durations and a single worker
  3. (H1) deterministic durations, without interruptions; (H2) unlimited resources, so independent activities in parallel
  4. Cyclic precedences and integer durations
Explanation. With (H1) every activity lasts exactly $d_j$; with (H2) activities not linked by precedences may run together. So the precedences are the only constraints of a schedule.
no-f-475 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, activity $5$ has the immediate predecessors $2$ and $3$, which end at times $9$ and $12$. When can activity $5$ start at the earliest?
  1. At time $12$
  2. At time $9$
  3. At time $21$
  4. At time $10.5$
Explanation. An activity with several immediate predecessors starts only when all of them have ended: $\max\{9,12\}=12$. The minimum, the sum or the average do not respect all precedences.

  1. The activities, the costs and the profits
  2. The resources, the shifts and the deadlines
  3. The critical path, the slacks and the Gantt chart
  4. The activities, the precedences (immediate predecessors) and the durations
Explanation. They are the input data of the method; critical path, slacks and Gantt chart are its results. Costs and resources do not enter the basic model, by (H2).
no-f-477 · level facile · notes

  2. Arcs from START to the activities without predecessors and from the activities without successors to FINISH
  3. A single arc from START to FINISH
  4. None: the dummy nodes have no arcs
Explanation. START precedes the activities without predecessors and FINISH follows those without successors; START and FINISH have duration $0$. Joining them to every activity would add useless arcs.
no-f-478 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, Proposition: a project has a schedule ($t_j \ge t_i+d_i$ for every precedence $(i,j)$) if and only if the activity network is acyclic. For «schedule $\Rightarrow$ acyclic», what is done with a cycle $i_0,\dots,i_k=i_0$?
  1. The constraints of its arcs are added up: the times cancel and $0 \ge \sum d_{i_s}>0$ remains, a contradiction
  2. Dijkstra is applied to the cycle
  3. One shows that the cycle contains START
  4. One shows that the durations of the cycle are zero
Explanation. Adding the constraints $t_{i_{s+1}}-t_{i_s} \ge d_{i_s}$ along the cycle gives $0$ on the left and a sum of positive durations on the right. Positive durations are essential; START and FINISH are not on cycles.
no-f-479 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, a schedule assigns the starts $t_j$ with $t_0=0$ and $t_j \ge t_i+d_i$ for every precedence. The precedence $(2,4)$ has $d_2=5$ and $t_2=3$. Which start of $4$ is feasible?
  1. $t_4=5$
  2. $t_4=3$
  3. $t_4=7$
  4. $t_4=9$
Explanation. Activity $2$ ends at time $t_2+d_2=8$, so $t_4 \ge 8$ is needed: only $9$ works. $5$ compares with the duration instead of the end.
no-f-480 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, why is the duration of every schedule at least the length of every path from START to FINISH (sum of the durations of its activities)?
  1. Because the activities of a path cannot run in parallel by (H2)
  2. Because the path is critical
  3. Because along the path every activity starts after the end of the previous one: the constraints added up give $t_{n+1} \ge L(P)$
  4. Because the durations are integer
Explanation. Adding $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$ along the path the intermediate times cancel and $t_{n+1}-t_0 \ge L(P)$ remains. It holds for every path, critical or not; by (H2) activities of different paths may instead run in parallel.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, the forward pass computes $\mathit{ES}_j=\max_i \mathit{EF}_i$ over the immediate predecessors and $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$. Activity $6$ has predecessors $3$ ($\mathit{EF}_3=10$) and $4$ ($\mathit{EF}_4=14$), and $d_6=5$. What are $\mathit{ES}_6$ and $\mathit{EF}_6$?
  1. $\mathit{ES}_6=10$, $\mathit{EF}_6=15$
  2. $\mathit{ES}_6=14$, $\mathit{EF}_6=19$
  3. $\mathit{ES}_6=24$, $\mathit{EF}_6=29$
  4. $\mathit{ES}_6=14$, $\mathit{EF}_6=14$
Explanation. $\mathit{ES}_6=\max\{10,14\}=14$ and $\mathit{EF}_6=14+5=19$. The minimum would give a start before $4$ ends; the sum makes no sense in time.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, the backward pass computes $\mathit{LF}_j=\min_k \mathit{LS}_k$ over the immediate successors and $\mathit{LS}_j=\mathit{LF}_j-d_j$. Activity $3$ has successors $5$ ($\mathit{LS}_5=16$) and $6$ ($\mathit{LS}_6=12$), and $d_3=4$. What are $\mathit{LF}_3$ and $\mathit{LS}_3$?
  1. $\mathit{LF}_3=12$, $\mathit{LS}_3=8$
  2. $\mathit{LF}_3=16$, $\mathit{LS}_3=12$
  3. $\mathit{LF}_3=12$, $\mathit{LS}_3=16$
  4. $\mathit{LF}_3=28$, $\mathit{LS}_3=24$
Explanation. Activity $3$ must end in time for all successors: $\mathit{LF}_3=\min\{16,12\}=12$, and $\mathit{LS}_3=12-4=8$. With the maximum, activity $6$ would start late.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, Theorem: $T-\mathit{LS}_j$ is the length of a longest path from $j$ to FINISH. Which fact makes the backward pass correct, node by node?
  1. The durations are integer
  2. The network has a single critical path
  3. The activities are independent by (H2)
  4. The reverse topological order: when $j$ is processed, all its successors already have their final times
Explanation. It is the same idea as (longest) paths in acyclic graphs: in reverse topological order the successors of $j$ come first, and $\mathit{LF}_j=\min_k \mathit{LS}_k$ uses values already computed. There may be several critical paths.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, in what time does the whole method (topological order, forward and backward passes, slacks) run on a network with $n+2$ nodes and $m$ arcs?
  1. $O(n^2 m)$
  2. $O(m \log n)$
  3. $O(n+m)$
  4. $O(2^n)$
Explanation. Every step examines every node and every arc a constant number of times: linear time $O(n+m)$. It is the cost of paths in acyclic graphs.
no-f-485 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, Step 0 looks for a topological order by removing at each step a node without incoming arcs. What is concluded if nodes remain that all have incoming arcs?
  1. The precedences contain a cycle: the project has no schedule
  2. The project has infinite duration but one can go on
  3. The node with fewest incoming arcs is chosen and one goes on
  4. The project has several critical paths
Explanation. A graph in which every node has an incoming arc contains a cycle (walking backwards a node repeats), and with a cycle of precedences no schedule exists. The method stops.

  2. To find the activities with positive free slack
  3. To find a topological order
  4. The critical paths are exactly the paths of tight arcs from START to FINISH
Explanation. On a tight arc activity $j$ starts as soon as $i$ ends: a path of tight arcs from START to FINISH has length $T$, so it is critical, and conversely. $T$ comes from the forward pass.

  2. The duration of the activity
  3. The period in which the activity is suspended
  4. Its free slack
Explanation. $\mathit{LF}_j-\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{TS}_j$, the total slack. The free slack may be smaller: it is about not delaying the successors.

  3. (H2), unlimited resources: activities without precedences between them may run in parallel
  4. That the network is acyclic
Explanation. Without resource limits several teams work in parallel; with limited resources different scheduling models would be needed. The three activities need not be critical.
no-f-489 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, in the Gantt chart of the earliest schedule the bars of the critical activities form an uninterrupted chain from $0$ to $T$. What does it mean?
  1. That the critical activities all run together
  2. That the critical activities have positive slack
  3. That the project can be shortened by moving them
  4. That a delay of any of them delays the project
Explanation. Critical activities have zero total slack and follow each other without pauses along a path of length $T$: every delay propagates to FINISH. To shorten the project their durations must be reduced.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ of the minimum duration has one variable $t_j$ per node. What is its objective function?

  3. $\min \sum_j t_j$
  4. $\min \sum_j d_j t_j$
Explanation. One minimizes the time between the start of the project (START) and FINISH, with the constraints $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$ on the precedences. Minimizing $\sum_j t_j$ gives the earliest schedule, not just the duration.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, in the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ ($\min t_{n+1}-t_0$, constraints $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$), what sign do the variables $t_j$ have?

  2. $t_j \in \{0,1\}$
  3. $t_j \le T$
  4. Free: only differences matter, and shifting all times changes nothing
Explanation. Constraints and objective depend only on differences of times; the variables are free. This is why the dual equations have right-hand sides given by the objective coefficients.
no-f-492 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, the earliest schedule ($t_j=\mathit{ES}_j$) is a solution of the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$. What can be said?
  1. It is optimal, with value $T=\mathit{ES}_{n+1}$
  2. It is feasible but not optimal
  3. It is infeasible
  4. It is optimal only if there is a single critical path
Explanation. The forward pass gives a schedule (constraints respected) of duration $\mathit{ES}_{n+1}=T$, the minimum. The latest schedule is optimal too; uniqueness of the critical path does not matter.

In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, Which statement about the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ of the minimum duration ($\min t_{n+1}-t_0$, $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$ on every precedence, free $t$) is correct?
  1. It is unbounded if there are activities in parallel
  2. It has an optimal solution if and only if the network is acyclic, and the optimal value is the length of a longest path from START to FINISH
  3. It always has an optimal solution, even with cycles of precedences
  4. Its optimal value is the sum of all durations
Explanation. With a cycle the model is infeasible (no schedule); with an acyclic network the optimal value is $T$, the length of a critical path. The sum of all durations holds only if the activities are in series.
no-f-494 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, the dual of the model $\mathrm{PD}$ has variables $x_{ij} \ge 0$ on the arcs, flow conservation with $b_0=1$, $b_{n+1}=-1$ and objective $\max \sum d_i x_{ij}$. What is an integer optimal solution?
  1. An earliest schedule
  2. The incidence vector of a critical path: one unit from START to FINISH along a longest path
  3. A shortest path tree
  4. A flow with one unit on every arc
Explanation. The dual sends one unit from START to FINISH maximizing the length: it is the longest path problem, and the integer optimal solutions are critical paths. By strong duality the value is $T$.
no-f-495 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (CPM) for a project of activities with durations $d_j$ and precedences, Theorem: the optimal value of $\mathrm{PD}$ and that of its dual (longest path from START to FINISH) are both $T$. Which pair of feasible solutions proves it directly?
  1. The latest schedule and a spanning tree
  2. Any two schedules
  3. The zero flow and the earliest schedule
  4. The earliest schedule, of duration $T$, and the incidence vector of a critical path, of length $T$: by weak duality they are optimal
Explanation. A feasible primal and a feasible dual solution with the same value are optimal by weak duality. The zero flow violates $b_0=1$; a tree is not a dual solution.

  2. $11$, since two paths get shorter
  3. $12$
  4. $13-\mathit{TS}_8$
Explanation. Every path goes through $8$, so all of them get $1$ shorter and the longest is $12$. The second critical path blocks the gain only when it does not contain the shortened activity; $\mathit{TS}_8=0$ since $8$ is critical.
no-f-515 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), which statement about the earliest starts is correct?
  1. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the length of a shortest path from START to $j$
  2. $\mathit{ES}_j$ depends on the chosen topological order
  3. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the length of a longest path from START to $j$, and every schedule has $t_j \ge \mathit{ES}_j$
  4. $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{LS}_j$ for every activity
Explanation. An activity waits for the predecessor finishing last, so $\mathit{ES}_j$ is a longest path; and no schedule can start $j$ earlier. The topological order changes the order of computation, not the values.
no-f-516 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), Theorem: every schedule $\vet{t}$ has $t_j \ge \mathit{ES}_j$ for every node. The proof is by induction on the topological order. Which step carries it?
  1. If $t_i \ge \mathit{ES}_i$ for the immediate predecessors $i$ of $j$, then $t_j \ge \max_i(t_i+d_i) \ge \max_i \mathit{EF}_i=\mathit{ES}_j$
  2. The durations are integer
  3. The earliest schedule is unique
  4. The network has a single critical path
Explanation. The constraints $t_j \ge t_i+d_i$ and the inductive hypothesis on the predecessors, which come first in the topological order, give the claim. Integrality and uniqueness of critical paths are not needed.
no-f-517 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), which statement about the minimum duration of the project is correct?
  1. $T$ is the sum of all durations
  2. $T=\mathit{ES}_{n+1}$, the length of a longest path from START to FINISH, and it is attained by the earliest schedule
  3. $T$ is the largest duration of an activity
  4. $T$ is the length of a shortest path from START to FINISH
Explanation. Every schedule lasts at least as long as every path, and the earliest schedule lasts exactly as long as the longest path. By (H2) parallel activities do not add up.

In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), Theorem: the latest schedule $t_j=\mathit{LS}_j$ is a schedule of duration $T$.   3. The durations are positive
  4. The network is acyclic

In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), let $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$ be the total slack and $\mathit{FS}_j=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_j$ (over the successors) the free slack. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\mathit{FS}_j \ge \mathit{TS}_j$ for every activity
  2. $\mathit{FS}_j=\mathit{TS}_j$ for every activity
  3. $\mathit{FS}_j>0$ implies that $j$ is critical
  4. $0 \le \mathit{FS}_j \le \mathit{TS}_j$: the free slack is the delay that moves the earliest start of no successor
Explanation. Delaying $j$ by $\mathit{FS}_j$ does not affect the successors, hence not the project: the free slack does not exceed the total one. They coincide, for instance, for activities directly preceding FINISH.

In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), Proposition: $\mathit{FS}_j \le \mathit{TS}_j$.   1. $\mathit{ES}_k \ge \mathit{LS}_k$ for every successor
  2. $\mathit{EF}_j \ge \mathit{LF}_j$
  3. $\mathit{ES}_k \le \mathit{LS}_k$ for every successor $k$, so $\min_k \mathit{ES}_k \le \min_k \mathit{LS}_k=\mathit{LF}_j$
  4. $d_j \le T$
Explanation. $\mathit{FS}_j=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_j \le \mathit{LF}_j-\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{TS}_j$, since every successor has earliest start not after its latest start.
no-f-521 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), an arc $(i,j)$ is tight if $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{EF}_i$. Which statement is correct?
  1. A path from START to FINISH is critical if and only if all its arcs are tight and its activities have zero total slack
  2. Every arc between two critical activities is tight
  3. A path of tight arcs has minimum length
  4. The tight arcs always form a tree
Explanation. On a critical path every activity starts as soon as the previous one ends, and all have zero slack; conversely a path of tight arcs from START to FINISH has length $T$. Two critical activities may be joined by a non-tight arc (on different critical paths).
no-f-522 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), the duration $d_j$ of an activity grows by $\delta>0$. Which statement about the new minimum duration $T'$ is correct?
  1. $T'=T+\delta$ always
  2. $T'=T$ always, thanks to the slacks
  3. $T'=T+\delta$ only if $j$ has zero free slack
  4. $T'=T+\max\{0,\,\delta-\mathit{TS}_j\}$
Explanation. The longest paths through $j$ have length $T-\mathit{TS}_j$ and grow by $\delta$; the project gets longer only by the part of $\delta$ exceeding the total slack. The total slack counts, not the free one.
no-f-523 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), Proposition: if $d_j$ decreases by $\delta<d_j$, then $T-\delta \le T' \le T$, and $T'=T$ if some critical path does not contain $j$. Which fact carries it?
  1. The free slacks do not change
  2. $T'$ is the maximum length of the paths, and the change alters by $-\delta$ only the paths through $j$
  3. The critical activities stay the same
  4. The network is a path
Explanation. A critical path without $j$ keeps length $T$, so $T'=T$; paths through $j$ get shorter by at most $\delta$, so $T' \ge T-\delta$. The critical activities may change.
no-f-524 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), the dual of the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ is a flow of one unit from START to FINISH maximizing $\sum d_i x_{ij}$. Why does it have an integer optimal solution, that is, a path?
  1. Because the constraints are flow conservation with a totally unimodular incidence matrix and integer balances
  2. Because the durations are integer
  3. Because the dual has a single variable
  4. Because the network has a single critical path
Explanation. The flow polyhedron with integer balances has integer vertices; an integer flow of one unit on an acyclic network is a path. The durations (the costs) do not affect the integrality of the vertices.
no-f-525 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), which statement about the critical path method as an algorithm is correct?
  1. It is exponential, since it enumerates the paths
  2. It is linear, $O(n+m)$: it is the computation of longest paths in an acyclic graph, in both directions
  3. It needs the simplex method to compute $T$
  4. It also works with cyclic precedences
Explanation. The two passes are the acyclic-graph algorithm of the chapter on shortest paths, with maxima instead of minima; every node and arc is examined a constant number of times. With a cycle there is no topological order.
no-f-526 · level ipotesi · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), Theorem: $T=\mathit{ES}_{n+1}$ is the length of a longest path. Without assumption (H2) (unlimited resources), what would change?
  1. Nothing: resources never matter
  2. The network would become cyclic
  3. The durations would become random
  4. Two activities without precedences might not be able to run together, and $T$ could exceed the longest path
Explanation. With limited resources parallel activities compete, and a schedule may have to stagger them beyond what precedences require: the longest path stays only a lower bound.
no-f-527 · level teorema · notes
In the critical path method (durations $d_j$, activity network with START and FINISH, $T$ minimum duration, earliest starts and finishes $\mathit{ES}_j,\mathit{EF}_j$ and latest ones $\mathit{LS}_j,\mathit{LF}_j$), let $L_j$ be the maximum length of a path from START to FINISH through activity $j$. Which statement about the total slack $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$ is correct?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_j=L_j$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_j=T-d_j$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_j=T-L_j$: it is what the longest path through $j$ lacks to become critical
  4. $\mathit{TS}_j$ depends on the chosen schedule
Explanation. $\mathit{ES}_j$ is the longest path up to $j$ and $T-\mathit{LS}_j$ the one from $j$ to FINISH; their sum is $L_j$, so $\mathit{TS}_j=T-L_j$. It is defined by the earliest and latest times, not by a schedule.
no-f-546 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), a project has the activities $1$ ($d_1=3$, no predecessor), $2$ ($d_2=5$, no predecessor), $3$ ($d_3=2$, predecessors $1,2$). What is the minimum duration $T$?
  1. $7$
  2. $10$
  3. $5$
  4. $8$
Explanation. $1$ and $2$ run in parallel by (H2); $3$ starts at time $\max\{3,5\}=5$ and ends at $7$. $10$ adds all durations, as if the activities were in series.
no-f-547 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), in the activity network every arc $(i,j)$ has length $d_i$, the duration of the tail. Why the duration of the tail?
  1. By convention, without consequences
  2. Because the head always has duration $0$
  3. Because then the arcs have positive lengths
  4. Because the arc constraint is $t_j \ge t_i+d_i$: $j$ starts after $i$ has lasted $d_i$
Explanation. The precedence $(i,j)$ separates the starts of $i$ and $j$ by at least $d_i$; so the length of a path from START to FINISH is the sum of the durations of its activities. Arcs from START have length $0$.
no-f-548 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), an activity network has the paths from START to FINISH: $1,3,5$ ($d_1+d_3+d_5=14$), $1,4,5$ ($16$), $2,4,5$ ($13$). Which is the critical path and what is $T$?
  1. $1,3,5$ with $T=14$
  2. $2,4,5$ with $T=13$
  3. $1,4,5$ with $T=16$
  4. All of them, with $T=43$
Explanation. The critical path is the longest, $1,4,5$, and the minimum duration is its length, $16$. The shortest does not constrain the project; $43$ adds lengths of different paths.
no-f-549 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), in a network with paths $1,3,5$ ($14$), $1,4,5$ ($16$), $2,4,5$ ($13$) from START to FINISH, what is the total slack of activity $3$ (largest delay not lengthening the project)?
  1. $0$
  2. $2$
  3. $3$
  4. $16$
Explanation. The longest path through activity $3$ is $1,3,5$, of length $14$: $\mathit{TS}_3=T-L_3=16-14=2$. $3$ is the difference between the critical path and $2,4,5$, which does not pass through $3$.
no-f-550 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), activity $j$ has $\mathit{ES}_j=4$, $\mathit{EF}_j=9$, $\mathit{LS}_j=7$, and its immediate successors have earliest starts $10$ and $12$. What are the total slack $\mathit{TS}_j=\mathit{LS}_j-\mathit{ES}_j$ and the free slack $\mathit{FS}_j=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_j$?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_j=3$, $\mathit{FS}_j=1$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_j=1$, $\mathit{FS}_j=3$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_j=3$, $\mathit{FS}_j=3$
  4. $\mathit{TS}_j=5$, $\mathit{FS}_j=1$
Explanation. $\mathit{TS}_j=7-4=3$ and $\mathit{FS}_j=\min\{10,12\}-9=1$: $j$ can be delayed by $1$ without affecting the successors, by $3$ without affecting the project. $5=d_j$ is not a slack.
no-f-551 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), which activity is surely critical?
  1. The one with the longest duration
  2. The one with most successors
  3. The one starting first
  4. The one with $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{LS}_j$, that is, zero total slack
Explanation. Critical means zero total slack: every delay lengthens the project, and the activity lies on a critical path. Duration, number of successors and position are not enough.
no-f-552 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), the LP model $\mathrm{PD}$ has the constraint $t_j-t_i \ge d_i$ for the precedence $(i,j)$. With $d_2=4$ and the precedence $(2,5)$, which constraint appears?
  1. $t_5-t_2 \le 4$
  2. $t_2-t_5 \ge 4$
  3. $t_5-t_2 \ge 4$
  4. $t_5 \ge 4$
Explanation. Activity $5$ starts at least $d_2=4$ after the start of $2$: $t_5 \ge t_2+4$. With $\le$ one would require starting within $4$, which is not a precedence.
no-f-553 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), the forward pass processes the nodes in topological order. Why can an activity not be processed before its immediate predecessors?
  1. Because durations are integer
  2. Because the activity might be critical
  3. Because the backward pass forbids it
  4. Because $\mathit{ES}_j=\max_i \mathit{EF}_i$ uses the earliest finishes of the predecessors, which must already be known
Explanation. Every $\mathit{ES}_j$ depends on the values $\mathit{EF}_i$ of the predecessors; the topological order puts them all before $j$. It is the same idea as paths in acyclic graphs.
no-f-554 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), the project has minimum duration $T=20$. Activity $7$ precedes only FINISH and lasts $d_7=6$. What are $\mathit{LF}_7$ and $\mathit{LS}_7$?
  1. $\mathit{LF}_7=14$, $\mathit{LS}_7=8$
  2. $\mathit{LF}_7=26$, $\mathit{LS}_7=20$
  3. $\mathit{LF}_7=20$, $\mathit{LS}_7=14$
  4. $\mathit{LF}_7=20$, $\mathit{LS}_7=20$
Explanation. The backward pass starts from $\mathit{LF}_{\mathrm{FINISH}}=\mathit{LS}_{\mathrm{FINISH}}=T=20$; the only successor of $7$ is FINISH, so $\mathit{LF}_7=20$ and $\mathit{LS}_7=20-6=14$.
no-f-555 · level facile · notes
In the critical path method (activities with durations $d_j$ and immediate predecessors, nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), the project has minimum duration $T=30$. Activity $j$, not critical, has total slack $\mathit{TS}_j=5$, and its duration grows by $3$. What is the new minimum duration?
  1. $33$
  2. $30$
  3. $35$
  4. $28$
Explanation. $T'=T+\max\{0,3-5\}=30$: the increase is absorbed by the total slack. The project would get longer only with an increase above $5$.

1. Graphs (7)

Example 1 · no-g-bfs · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the adjacency lists (sorted by increasing index) $L(1)=(2,3,4)$, $L(2)=(1)$, $L(3)=(2,6)$, $L(4)=(1)$, $L(5)=\emptyset$, $L(6)=(2,5)$. The generic search from $s=1$ keeps in $\mathit{LIST}$ the labelled nodes to be examined: it examines a node $i$ of $\mathit{LIST}$ and labels the first unlabelled head of the list $L(i)$, or removes $i$ from $\mathit{LIST}$ if there is none. In the breadth-first search (BFS) $i$ is always the first node of $\mathit{LIST}$ (a queue). In which order does the BFS label the nodes?
  1. $1,\,2,\,3,\,6,\,5,\,4$
  2. $1,\,2,\,3,\,4,\,6,\,5$
  3. $1,\,4,\,3,\,2,\,6,\,5$
  4. $1,\,2,\,3,\,4,\,5,\,6$
Explanation. The BFS always selects the first node of $\mathit{LIST}$ (a queue) and the first admissible arc of its list: order $1,\,2,\,3,\,4,\,6,\,5$. The order $1,\,2,\,3,\,6,\,5,\,4$ is that of the DFS, which selects the last node.

Example 1 · no-g-dfs · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the adjacency lists (sorted by increasing index) $L(1)=(2,3,4)$, $L(2)=(1)$, $L(3)=(2,6)$, $L(4)=(1)$, $L(5)=\emptyset$, $L(6)=(2,5)$. The generic search from $s=1$ keeps in $\mathit{LIST}$ the labelled nodes to be examined: it examines a node $i$ of $\mathit{LIST}$ and labels the first unlabelled head of the list $L(i)$, or removes $i$ from $\mathit{LIST}$ if there is none. In the depth-first search (DFS) $i$ is always the last node of $\mathit{LIST}$ (a stack). In which order does the DFS label the nodes?
  1. $1,\,2,\,3,\,4,\,6,\,5$
  2. $1,\,2,\,3,\,6,\,5,\,4$
  3. $2,\,5,\,6,\,3,\,4,\,1$
  4. $1,\,4,\,3,\,6,\,5,\,2$
Explanation. The DFS always selects the last node of $\mathit{LIST}$ (a stack) and the first admissible arc of its list; a node without admissible arcs leaves $\mathit{LIST}$. Labelling order $1,\,2,\,3,\,6,\,5,\,4$; $2,\,5,\,6,\,3,\,4,\,1$ is the order in which the nodes leave $\mathit{LIST}$.

Example 1 · no-g-rank · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,7$ has the arcs $(2,4)$, $(4,7)$, $(5,1)$, $(5,6)$, $(7,2)$. What is the rank of its node–arc incidence matrix $\mat{B}$ (one row per node, one column per arc, $+1$ in the tail and $-1$ in the head)?
  1. $6$
  2. $7$
  3. $2$
  4. $4$
Explanation. The underlying undirected graph has $k=3$ connected components, so $\rank(\mat{B})=|N|-k=7-3=4$. $|N|-1$ holds only for a connected graph.

Example 1 · no-g-strong-component · level media · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,7$ has the arcs $(1,6)$, $(1,7)$, $(2,4)$, $(2,6)$, $(3,2)$, $(3,6)$, $(4,3)$, $(5,4)$, $(7,3)$, $(7,5)$. Two nodes $i,j$ are strongly connected if there are directed paths from $i$ to $j$ and from $j$ to $i$. What is the strongly connected component of node $2$, that is, the set of the nodes strongly connected to $2$?
  1. $\{2,3,4,6\}$
  2. $\{1,2,3,4,5,7\}$
  3. $\{2,3,4\}$
  4. $\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7\}$
Explanation. Nodes reachable from $2$: $R=\{2,3,4,6\}$; nodes from which $2$ is reachable: $R'=\{1,2,3,4,5,7\}$. The component is $R \cap R'=\{2,3,4\}$; $R$ alone, $R'$ alone or the union contain nodes with no way back.

Example 1 · no-g-net-outflow · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with the values $x_{13}=5$, $x_{15}=1$, $x_{21}=2$, $x_{23}=6$, $x_{34}=3$, $x_{35}=6$, $x_{42}=4$, $x_{54}=6$. Let $\mat{B}$ be its node–arc incidence matrix ($+1$ in the row of the tail, $-1$ in that of the head of every arc). What is component $5$ of the vector $\mat{B}\vet{x}$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $1$
  3. $13$
  4. $6$
Explanation. Row $5$ of $\mat{B}\vet{x}$ is what leaves the node minus what enters it: $6-7=-1$. The opposite sign gives inflow minus outflow.

Example 1 · no-g-bfs-dist · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,7$ has the adjacency lists (sorted by increasing index) $L(1)=(3,4,6)$, $L(2)=(1)$, $L(3)=(2,4)$, $L(4)=(1,3,5)$, $L(5)=\emptyset$, $L(6)=(1,7)$, $L(7)=\emptyset$. What is the minimum number of arcs of a directed path from node $1$ to node $5$ (as computed by the breadth-first search from $1$, which keeps the labelled nodes to examine in a queue)?
  1. $2$
  2. $3$
  3. $5$
  4. $1$
Explanation. In the BFS $d(j)=d(\mathit{pred}(j))+1$ is the minimum number of arcs: tracing back the predecessors from $5$ reaches $1$ in $2$ arcs. The DFS may give a longer path ($3$ arcs); the position in the labelling order is not a distance.

Example 1 · no-g-dfs-tree · level complessa · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the adjacency lists (sorted by increasing index) $L(1)=(2,4,5)$, $L(2)=(4)$, $L(3)=(2)$, $L(4)=(5)$, $L(5)=(3,4,6)$, $L(6)=\emptyset$. The depth-first search (DFS) from $s=1$ keeps in a stack $\mathit{LIST}$ the labelled nodes to examine: it examines the last node $i$ of $\mathit{LIST}$, labels the first unlabelled head $j$ of $L(i)$ setting $\mathit{pred}(j)=i$, or removes $i$ from $\mathit{LIST}$ if there is none. What are the predecessors of nodes $2,\dots,6$?
  1. $(\mathit{pred}(2),\dots,\mathit{pred}(6))=(1,5,1,1,5)$
  2. $(\mathit{pred}(2),\dots,\mathit{pred}(6))=(3,5,5,1,5)$
  3. $(\mathit{pred}(2),\dots,\mathit{pred}(6))=(1,5,2,4,3)$
  4. $(\mathit{pred}(2),\dots,\mathit{pred}(6))=(1,5,2,4,5)$
Explanation. Labelling order $1,\,2,\,4,\,5,\,3,\,6$; predecessors $(1,5,2,4,5)$. When a node has no more admissible arcs the DFS goes back to the previous node of the stack, so the predecessor is not always the node labelled just before; the BFS would give $(1,5,1,1,5)$.

2. Minimum spanning trees (5)

Example 1 · no-g-kruskal · level facile · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{16}=4$, $w_{23}=7$, $w_{24}=15$, $w_{25}=13$, $w_{34}=2$, $w_{35}=3$, $w_{45}=6$, $w_{46}=5$, $w_{56}=1$ (where $w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). What is the weight of a minimum spanning tree, that is, of minimum total weight?
  1. $15$
  2. $20$
  3. $19$
  4. $17$
Explanation. Kruskal accepts, by increasing weight, the edges joining two components: $\{5,6\}$, $\{3,4\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, $\{2,3\}$, weight $17$. The sum of the $5$ smallest weights ($15$) ignores the cycles.

What is the weight of a minimum spanning tree, that is, of minimum total weight?
  1. $32$
