  3. $40$
  4. $33$
Explanation. Kruskal accepts, by increasing weight, the edges joining two components: $\{2,5\}$, $\{3,6\}$, $\{2,4\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, weight $32$. The sum of the $5$ smallest weights ($31$) ignores the cycles.
Example 1 · no-g-prim · level facile · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{12}=6$, $w_{13}=13$, $w_{14}=5$, $w_{15}=7$, $w_{16}=14$, $w_{24}=10$, $w_{26}=2$, $w_{36}=3$, $w_{45}=9$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). Prim's algorithm starts from $S=\{1\}$ and at every step inserts into $S$ the vertex outside $S$ joined to $S$ by the lightest edge. In which order does it insert the other vertices?
  1. $4,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,5$
  2. $4,\,2,\,5,\,6,\,3$
  3. $2,\,6,\,3,\,4,\,5$
  4. $4,\,2,\,5,\,3,\,6$
Explanation. Prim selects the vertex outside $S$ with minimum label and updates $d(u):=w_{vu}$ if $w_{vu}<d(u)$: order $4,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,5$. With $d(v)+w_{vu}$ (Dijkstra) one would get $4,\,2,\,5,\,6,\,3$.
Example 2 · no-g-prim · level facile · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{13}=12$, $w_{16}=10$, $w_{24}=7$, $w_{25}=1$, $w_{34}=13$, $w_{35}=8$, $w_{36}=6$, $w_{45}=14$, $w_{46}=9$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). Prim's algorithm starts from $S=\{1\}$ and at every step inserts into $S$ the vertex outside $S$ joined to $S$ by the lightest edge. In which order does it insert the other vertices?
  1. $6,\,3,\,5,\,2,\,4$
  2. $6,\,3,\,4,\,5,\,2$
  3. $2,\,5,\,3,\,6,\,4$
  4. $6,\,3,\,2,\,4,\,5$
Explanation. Prim selects the vertex outside $S$ with minimum label and updates $d(u):=w_{vu}$ if $w_{vu}<d(u)$: order $6,\,3,\,5,\,2,\,4$. With $d(v)+w_{vu}$ (Dijkstra) one would get $6,\,3,\,4,\,5,\,2$.
Example 1 · no-g-mst-exchange · level media · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{12}=6$, $w_{13}=13$, $w_{14}=5$, $w_{15}=7$, $w_{16}=14$, $w_{24}=10$, $w_{26}=2$, $w_{36}=3$, $w_{45}=9$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). The spanning tree $T=\{\{1,3\},\{1,5\},\{2,6\},\{3,6\},\{4,5\}\}$ weighs $34$. The edge $\{2,4\}$ is added to $T$ and one edge is removed so as to obtain a spanning tree again. What is the weight of the lightest tree obtained in this way?
  1. $31$
  2. $42$
  3. $34$
  4. $44$
Explanation. Only an edge of the fundamental cycle of $\{2,4\}$ can be removed, that is $\{1,3\}$, $\{1,5\}$, $\{2,6\}$, $\{3,6\}$, $\{4,5\}$ (exchange lemma); the heaviest is the best choice: $34+10-13=31$. The heaviest edge of $T$ may lie outside the cycle.
Example 2 · no-g-mst-exchange · level media · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{12}=3$, $w_{14}=9$, $w_{15}=11$, $w_{16}=7$, $w_{23}=14$, $w_{24}=5$, $w_{25}=1$, $w_{26}=12$, $w_{46}=13$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). The spanning tree $T=\{\{1,5\},\{1,6\},\{2,3\},\{2,4\},\{4,6\}\}$ weighs $50$. The edge $\{2,5\}$ is added to $T$ and one edge is removed so as to obtain a spanning tree again. What is the weight of the lightest tree obtained in this way?
  1. $46$
  2. $38$
  3. $37$
  4. $50$
Explanation. Only an edge of the fundamental cycle of $\{2,5\}$ can be removed, that is $\{1,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, $\{2,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$ (exchange lemma); the heaviest is the best choice: $50+1-13=38$. The heaviest edge of $T$ may lie outside the cycle.
Example 1 · no-g-cut-lightest · level facile · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{14}=7$, $w_{16}=10$, $w_{24}=2$, $w_{25}=11$, $w_{26}=13$, $w_{35}=3$, $w_{36}=14$, $w_{45}=6$, $w_{46}=12$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). The generic greedy algorithm for the minimum spanning tree, with $F=\emptyset$, chooses the cut $\delta(S)$ with $S=\{1,3\}$ (the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and the other outside). Which edge does it add to $F$?
  1. $\{2,4\}$
  2. $\{1,4\}$
  3. $\{3,5\}$
  4. $\{3,6\}$
Explanation. It adds the minimum-weight edge of the cut: $\{3,5\}$, of weight $3$; by the cut property it lies in a minimum spanning tree. The lightest edge of the graph, $\{2,4\}$, does not cross $\delta(S)$.
Example 2 · no-g-cut-lightest · level facile · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{12}=3$, $w_{14}=9$, $w_{15}=11$, $w_{16}=7$, $w_{23}=14$, $w_{24}=5$, $w_{25}=1$, $w_{26}=12$, $w_{46}=13$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). The generic greedy algorithm for the minimum spanning tree, with $F=\emptyset$, chooses the cut $\delta(S)$ with $S=\{1,4\}$ (the edges with one endpoint in $S$ and the other outside). Which edge does it add to $F$?
  1. $\{1,2\}$
  2. $\{2,5\}$
  3. $\{2,4\}$
  4. $\{4,6\}$
Explanation. It adds the minimum-weight edge of the cut: $\{1,2\}$, of weight $3$; by the cut property it lies in a minimum spanning tree. The lightest edge of the graph, $\{2,5\}$, does not cross $\delta(S)$.
Example 1 · no-g-kruskal-run · level complessa · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{13}=13$, $w_{14}=1$, $w_{16}=10$, $w_{23}=15$, $w_{34}=14$, $w_{35}=5$, $w_{36}=7$, $w_{45}=12$, $w_{46}=2$, $w_{56}=6$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). Kruskal's algorithm examines the edges by increasing weight and accepts those joining two different components of the current forest. Which edges does it accept, in order?
  1. $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{2,3\}$
  2. $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{3,6\}$
  3. $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{3,6\}$, $\{2,3\}$
  4. $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{2,3\}$
Explanation. Kruskal accepts $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{2,3\}$ and rejects the edges closing a cycle; the $5$ lightest edges do not necessarily form a tree. Prim from $1$ adds the edges of the same tree in another order ($\{1,4\}$, $\{4,6\}$, $\{5,6\}$, $\{3,5\}$, $\{2,3\}$).
Example 2 · no-g-kruskal-run · level complessa · notes
The undirected graph with vertices $1,\dots,6$ has the edges with weights $w_{12}=12$, $w_{14}=7$, $w_{15}=10$, $w_{16}=4$, $w_{23}=15$, $w_{24}=6$, $w_{25}=2$, $w_{26}=13$, $w_{45}=3$, $w_{46}=14$ ($w_{uv}$ is the weight of $\{u,v\}$). Kruskal's algorithm examines the edges by increasing weight and accepts those joining two different components of the current forest. Which edges does it accept, in order?
  1. $\{1,6\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{2,5\}$, $\{2,3\}$
  2. $\{2,5\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{2,3\}$
  3. $\{2,5\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, $\{2,4\}$, $\{1,4\}$
  4. $\{1,6\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{1,5\}$, $\{1,2\}$, $\{2,3\}$
Explanation. Kruskal accepts $\{2,5\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{1,6\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{2,3\}$ and rejects the edges closing a cycle; the $5$ lightest edges do not necessarily form a tree. Prim from $1$ adds the edges of the same tree in another order ($\{1,6\}$, $\{1,4\}$, $\{4,5\}$, $\{2,5\}$, $\{2,3\}$).

3. Shortest paths (6)

Example 1 · no-g-dijkstra · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=9$, $d_{13}=4$, $d_{16}=8$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{32}=1$, $d_{35}=1$, $d_{43}=9$, $d_{45}=2$, $d_{56}=7$, $d_{62}=1$, $d_{64}=6$ ($d_{ij}$ is the length of the arc $(i,j)$). The distance $\tilde{d}(j)$ is the minimum length of a directed path from $s=1$ to $j$. Which distances $\tilde{d}=(\tilde{d}(1),\dots,\tilde{d}(6))$ does Dijkstra's algorithm from $s=1$ compute?
  1. $(0,9,4,14,5,8)$
  2. $(0,9,4,12,5,8)$
  3. $(0,1,4,3,1,7)$
  4. $(0,5,4,8,5,8)$
Explanation. Dijkstra makes permanent the temporary node with the smallest label and updates $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ when it improves; a label can improve several times before it becomes permanent. Distances $(0,5,4,8,5,8)$.
Example 2 · no-g-dijkstra · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=9$, $d_{13}=9$, $d_{14}=5$, $d_{16}=9$, $d_{25}=6$, $d_{26}=2$, $d_{35}=1$, $d_{42}=3$, $d_{62}=2$ ($d_{ij}$ is the length of the arc $(i,j)$). The distance $\tilde{d}(j)$ is the minimum length of a directed path from $s=1$ to $j$. Which distances $\tilde{d}=(\tilde{d}(1),\dots,\tilde{d}(6))$ does Dijkstra's algorithm from $s=1$ compute?
  1. $(0,9,9,5,15,9)$
  2. $(0,8,9,5,10,9)$
  3. $(0,9,9,5,10,9)$
  4. $(0,3,9,5,6,2)$
Explanation. Dijkstra makes permanent the temporary node with the smallest label and updates $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ when it improves; a label can improve several times before it becomes permanent. Distances $(0,8,9,5,10,9)$.
Example 1 · no-g-bellman-ford · level media · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=6$, $d_{14}=1$, $d_{24}=1$, $d_{31}=4$, $d_{32}=-1$, $d_{43}=8$, $d_{45}=1$, $d_{53}=2$. The Bellman–Ford algorithm from $s=1$ starts from $d^0=(0,+\infty,\dots,+\infty)$ and in pass $k$ sets $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$, using only the labels of the previous pass. What are the labels $d^2=(d^2(1),\dots,d^2(5))$?
  1. $(0,6,+\infty,1,+\infty)$
  2. $(0,3,4,1,2)$
  3. $(0,6,9,1,2)$
  4. $(0,6,4,1,2)$
Explanation. $d^k(j)$ is the minimum length of a walk from $s$ to $j$ with at most $k$ arcs: $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$, using only the labels of the previous pass. $d^1=(0,6,+\infty,1,+\infty)$, $d^2=(0,6,9,1,2)$.
Example 2 · no-g-bellman-ford · level media · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{13}=8$, $d_{15}=-2$, $d_{21}=5$, $d_{34}=5$, $d_{42}=7$, $d_{45}=-2$, $d_{53}=4$. The Bellman–Ford algorithm from $s=1$ starts from $d^0=(0,+\infty,\dots,+\infty)$ and in pass $k$ sets $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$, using only the labels of the previous pass. What are the labels $d^2=(d^2(1),\dots,d^2(5))$?
  1. $(0,+\infty,2,13,-2)$
  2. $(0,+\infty,8,+\infty,-2)$
  3. $(0,14,2,7,-2)$
  4. $(0,20,2,7,-2)$
Explanation. $d^k(j)$ is the minimum length of a walk from $s$ to $j$ with at most $k$ arcs: $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$, using only the labels of the previous pass. $d^1=(0,+\infty,8,+\infty,-2)$, $d^2=(0,+\infty,2,13,-2)$.
Example 1 · no-g-dag · level facile · notes
The acyclic directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ (topological order $1,2,\dots,6$) has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=3$, $d_{13}=1$, $d_{24}=-2$, $d_{25}=-2$, $d_{26}=3$, $d_{34}=0$, $d_{35}=0$, $d_{36}=8$, $d_{45}=4$, $d_{56}=3$. What is the distance $\tilde{d}(6)$, that is, the minimum length of a directed path from $s=1$ to $6$?
  1. $8$
  2. $4$
  3. $9$
  4. $6$
Explanation. In acyclic graphs the nodes are scanned in topological order and each label is final when its node is processed, with lengths of any sign: $\tilde{d}=(0,3,1,1,1,4)$. Dijkstra can fail with negative arcs; the maximum gives the longest path.
Example 2 · no-g-dag · level facile · notes
The acyclic directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ (topological order $1,2,\dots,6$) has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=5$, $d_{14}=-3$, $d_{16}=6$, $d_{23}=-3$, $d_{24}=2$, $d_{25}=4$, $d_{26}=-2$, $d_{36}=-3$, $d_{45}=4$, $d_{46}=7$. What is the distance $\tilde{d}(6)$, that is, the minimum length of a directed path from $s=1$ to $6$?
  1. $4$
  2. $14$
  3. $-1$
  4. $6$
Explanation. In acyclic graphs the nodes are scanned in topological order and each label is final when its node is processed, with lengths of any sign: $\tilde{d}=(0,5,2,-3,1,-1)$. Dijkstra can fail with negative arcs; the maximum gives the longest path.
Example 1 · no-g-dijkstra-run · level complessa · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=8$, $d_{13}=9$, $d_{14}=2$, $d_{15}=8$, $d_{16}=6$, $d_{21}=4$, $d_{35}=2$, $d_{43}=2$, $d_{56}=6$. Dijkstra's algorithm from $s=1$ makes permanent at every step the temporary node with the smallest label (ties: the smallest index) and updates $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ on the arcs leaving the node $i$ just made permanent, if it improves. In which order do the nodes become permanent, and what are the final distances $\tilde{d}=(\tilde{d}(1),\dots,\tilde{d}(6))$?
  1. order $1,\,4,\,6,\,2,\,5,\,3$; $\tilde{d}=(0,8,9,2,8,6)$
  2. order $1,\,4,\,3,\,5,\,6,\,2$; $\tilde{d}=(0,8,2,2,2,6)$
  3. order $1,\,4,\,3,\,5,\,6,\,2$; $\tilde{d}=(0,8,9,2,8,6)$
  4. order $1,\,4,\,3,\,5,\,6,\,2$; $\tilde{d}=(0,8,4,2,6,6)$
Explanation. With positive lengths the nodes become permanent in order of distance: order $1,\,4,\,3,\,5,\,6,\,2$, distances $(0,8,4,2,6,6)$. A label can improve several times before becoming permanent; keeping the first label found, or using $d_{ij}$ instead of $d(i)+d_{ij}$ (as in Prim), gives wrong results.
Example 2 · no-g-dijkstra-run · level complessa · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=3$, $d_{15}=1$, $d_{23}=6$, $d_{24}=7$, $d_{26}=3$, $d_{41}=5$, $d_{46}=8$, $d_{51}=7$, $d_{52}=1$, $d_{54}=7$. Dijkstra's algorithm from $s=1$ makes permanent at every step the temporary node with the smallest label (ties: the smallest index) and updates $d(j):=d(i)+d_{ij}$ on the arcs leaving the node $i$ just made permanent, if it improves. In which order do the nodes become permanent, and what are the final distances $\tilde{d}=(\tilde{d}(1),\dots,\tilde{d}(6))$?
  1. order $1,\,5,\,2,\,6,\,4,\,3$; $\tilde{d}=(0,3,9,8,1,6)$
  2. order $1,\,5,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,2,8,8,1,5)$
  3. order $1,\,5,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,1,6,7,1,3)$
  4. order $1,\,5,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,3,9,8,1,6)$
Explanation. With positive lengths the nodes become permanent in order of distance: order $1,\,5,\,2,\,6,\,3,\,4$, distances $(0,2,8,8,1,5)$. A label can improve several times before becoming permanent; keeping the first label found, or using $d_{ij}$ instead of $d(i)+d_{ij}$ (as in Prim), gives wrong results.
Example 1 · no-g-sp-violation · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=6$, $d_{13}=3$, $d_{14}=9$, $d_{21}=3$, $d_{32}=4$, $d_{36}=1$, $d_{41}=2$, $d_{51}=5$, $d_{62}=7$, $d_{65}=6$. One wants to check whether the labels $d=(0,6,3,11,10,4)$ (for nodes $1,\dots,6$, with $d(1)=0$) are the distances from $s=1$. The optimality conditions require $d(j) \le d(i)+d_{ij}$ on every arc $(i,j)$. Which arc violates them?
  1. $(3,2)$
  2. $(2,1)$
  3. $(1,4)$
  4. $(6,5)$
Explanation. On the arc $(1,4)$: $d(4)=11>0+9$, so the label of $4$ is not the distance: going through that arc would do better. On the other arcs the inequality holds.
Example 2 · no-g-sp-violation · level facile · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,6$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{15}=2$, $d_{23}=3$, $d_{24}=3$, $d_{26}=1$, $d_{41}=6$, $d_{46}=7$, $d_{51}=3$, $d_{52}=5$, $d_{54}=8$. One wants to check whether the labels $d=(0,7,10,10,4,8)$ (for nodes $1,\dots,6$, with $d(1)=0$) are the distances from $s=1$.   1. $(1,5)$
  2. $(4,6)$
  3. $(2,4)$
  4. $(5,4)$
Explanation. On the arc $(1,5)$: $d(5)=4>0+2$, so the label of $5$ is not the distance: going through that arc would do better. On the other arcs the inequality holds.
Example 1 · no-g-bf-run · level complessa · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{14}=3$, $d_{15}=7$, $d_{23}=9$, $d_{24}=6$, $d_{32}=-4$, $d_{42}=6$, $d_{43}=7$, $d_{53}=6$ (no negative cycle). Bellman–Ford from $s=1$ starts from $d^0=(0,\infty,\dots,\infty)$, computes $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$ and stops at the first pass $k$ with $d^k=d^{k-1}$. In which pass does it stop, and what are the distances $\tilde{d}$?
  1. pass $k=4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,6,10,3,7)$
  2. pass $k=4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,9,10,3,7)$
  3. pass $k=3$; $\tilde{d}=(0,6,10,3,7)$
  4. pass $k=5$; $\tilde{d}=(0,6,10,3,7)$
Explanation. The labels are $d^{1}=(0,+\infty,+\infty,3,7)$, $d^{2}=(0,9,10,3,7)$, $d^{3}=(0,6,10,3,7)$, $d^{4}=(0,6,10,3,7)$: pass $4$ repeats the previous one. Dijkstra, with the negative arc, would give $(0,9,10,3,7)$.
Example 2 · no-g-bf-run · level complessa · notes
The directed graph with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with lengths $d_{12}=3$, $d_{13}=3$, $d_{23}=6$, $d_{24}=9$, $d_{32}=-5$, $d_{35}=3$, $d_{42}=9$, $d_{54}=7$ (no negative cycle). Bellman–Ford from $s=1$ starts from $d^0=(0,\infty,\dots,\infty)$, computes $d^k(j)=\min\{d^{k-1}(j),\min_{(i,j)}(d^{k-1}(i)+d_{ij})\}$ and stops at the first pass $k$ with $d^k=d^{k-1}$. In which pass does it stop, and what are the distances $\tilde{d}$?
  1. pass $k=4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,-2,3,7,6)$
  2. pass $k=4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,3,3,12,6)$
  3. pass $k=3$; $\tilde{d}=(0,-2,3,7,6)$
  4. pass $k=4$; $\tilde{d}=(0,-2,3,12,6)$
Explanation. The labels are $d^{1}=(0,3,3,+\infty,+\infty)$, $d^{2}=(0,-2,3,12,6)$, $d^{3}=(0,-2,3,7,6)$, $d^{4}=(0,-2,3,7,6)$: pass $4$ repeats the previous one. Dijkstra, with the negative arc, would give $(0,3,3,12,6)$.

4. Network flow models (3)

Example 1 · no-g-lower-bounds · level facile · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs $(1,2)$, $(1,3)$, $(1,5)$, $(2,3)$, $(3,4)$, $(5,3)$, $(5,4)$, balances $\vet{b}=(2,3,0,-4,-1)$ and the lower bounds $l_{23}=2$ and $l_{53}=2$ (the others are zero). With the substitution $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$, what are the balances $\vet{b}'=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$ ($\mat{B}$: incidence matrix, $+1$ in the tail and $-1$ in the head)?
  1. $(2,1,4,-4,-3)$
  2. $(2,5,-4,-4,1)$
  3. $(2,1,0,-4,-3)$
  4. $(2,3,4,-4,-1)$
Explanation. The tail of $(i,j)$ loses $l_{ij}$ units of balance and the head gains them: $\vet{b}'=(2,1,4,-4,-3)$.
Example 2 · no-g-lower-bounds · level facile · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs $(1,3)$, $(1,5)$, $(2,4)$, $(2,5)$, $(3,2)$, $(3,4)$, $(3,5)$, balances $\vet{b}=(5,3,0,-1,-7)$ and the lower bounds $l_{24}=2$ and $l_{15}=2$ (the others are zero). With the substitution $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$, what are the balances $\vet{b}'=\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$ ($\mat{B}$: incidence matrix, $+1$ in the tail and $-1$ in the head)?
  1. $(3,1,0,1,-5)$
  2. $(7,5,0,-3,-9)$
  3. $(3,1,0,-1,-7)$
  4. $(5,3,0,1,-5)$
Explanation. The tail of $(i,j)$ loses $l_{ij}$ units of balance and the head gains them: $\vet{b}'=(3,1,0,1,-5)$.
Example 1 · no-g-reduced-costs · level facile · notes
The potentials are $\vet{\pi}=(10,8,8,0)$ and the arcs $(3,2)$, $(2,4)$, $(1,3)$ have costs $c_{32}=1$, $c_{24}=11$, $c_{13}=3$. What are their reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ (convention of the course), in the same order?
  1. $(1,19,5)$
  2. $(1,3,1)$
  3. $(-15,3,-15)$
  4. $(-1,-3,-1)$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$: $-\pi$ of the tail and $+\pi$ of the head. Result $(1,3,1)$.
Example 2 · no-g-reduced-costs · level facile · notes
The potentials are $\vet{\pi}=(7,11,4,0)$ and the arcs $(1,4)$, $(3,2)$, $(1,3)$ have costs $c_{14}=10$, $c_{32}=8$, $c_{13}=11$. What are their reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$ (convention of the course), in the same order?
  1. $(17,1,14)$
  2. $(3,-7,0)$
  3. $(-3,-15,-8)$
  4. $(3,15,8)$
Explanation. $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$: $-\pi$ of the tail and $+\pi$ of the head. Result $(3,15,8)$.
Example 1 · no-g-mcf-lower-bound · level media · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has balances $\vet{b}=(5,0,0,0,-5)$ (outflow minus inflow) and the arcs, with cost $c$, lower bound $l$ and capacity $u$: $(1,3):\ c=1,\ l=0,\ u=6$; $(2,1):\ c=7,\ l=0,\ u=3$; $(2,4):\ c=9,\ l=0,\ u=4$; $(3,2):\ c=7,\ l=2,\ u=6$; $(3,4):\ c=5,\ l=0,\ u=5$; $(3,5):\ c=5,\ l=0,\ u=6$; $(4,5):\ c=1,\ l=0,\ u=6$. What is the minimum cost $\sum c_{ij}x_{ij}$ of a feasible flow ($l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ and conservation at every node)?
  1. $54$
  2. $30$
  3. $40$
  4. $68$
Explanation. With $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$ the arc $(3,2)$ starts with $2$ units (fixed cost $\vet{c}'\vet{l}=14$) and the balances become $\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$; the problem without lower bounds costs $40$, so the optimum is $54$. Ignoring the lower bound gives $30$; sending flow only along forward arcs, without corrections, may not be optimal.
Example 2 · no-g-mcf-lower-bound · level media · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has balances $\vet{b}=(3,0,0,0,-3)$ (outflow minus inflow) and the arcs, with cost $c$, lower bound $l$ and capacity $u$: $(1,3):\ c=8,\ l=0,\ u=5$; $(1,4):\ c=6,\ l=0,\ u=4$; $(1,5):\ c=3,\ l=0,\ u=5$; $(3,2):\ c=6,\ l=0,\ u=5$; $(4,2):\ c=2,\ l=0,\ u=5$; $(4,5):\ c=7,\ l=1,\ u=4$; $(5,2):\ c=9,\ l=0,\ u=5$. What is the minimum cost $\sum c_{ij}x_{ij}$ of a feasible flow ($l_{ij} \le x_{ij} \le u_{ij}$ and conservation at every node)?
  1. $9$
  2. $19$
  3. $12$
  4. $26$
Explanation. With $\vet{x}=\vet{l}+\vet{x}'$ the arc $(4,5)$ starts with $1$ units (fixed cost $\vet{c}'\vet{l}=7$) and the balances become $\vet{b}-\mat{B}\vet{l}$; the problem without lower bounds costs $12$, so the optimum is $19$. Ignoring the lower bound gives $9$; sending flow only along forward arcs, without corrections, may not be optimal.

5. Maximum flow (5)

Example 1 · no-g-cut-capacity · level facile · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{12}=2$, $u_{13}=4$, $u_{25}=9$, $u_{26}=5$, $u_{35}=8$, $u_{45}=7$, $u_{61}=9$, $u_{64}=9$, $u_{65}=1$. What is the capacity $u(S,N \setminus S)$ of the cut with $S=\{1,3\}$?
  1. $10$
  2. $19$
  3. $14$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Only the forward arcs, from $S$ to $N \setminus S$, are added: $(1,2)$, $(3,5)$, for a total of $10$. The backward arcs ($(6,1)$) do not count.
Example 2 · no-g-cut-capacity · level facile · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{13}=3$, $u_{14}=8$, $u_{15}=5$, $u_{16}=7$, $u_{26}=6$, $u_{42}=9$, $u_{54}=1$, $u_{63}=2$, $u_{64}=2$. What is the capacity $u(S,N \setminus S)$ of the cut with $S=\{1,2,3,4\}$?
  1. $18$
  2. $23$
  3. $38$
  4. $13$
Explanation. Only the forward arcs, from $S$ to $N \setminus S$, are added: $(1,5)$, $(1,6)$, $(2,6)$, for a total of $18$. The backward arcs ($(5,4)$, $(6,3)$, $(6,4)$) do not count.
Example 1 · no-g-residual · level facile · notes
In the network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ ($s=1$, $t=5$) the arcs have capacity and flow $(1,2):\ u=7,\ x=3$; $(1,3):\ u=4,\ x=0$; $(1,4):\ u=6,\ x=0$; $(2,4):\ u=3,\ x=2$; $(2,5):\ u=8,\ x=1$; $(3,2):\ u=5,\ x=0$; $(3,5):\ u=7,\ x=2$; $(4,3):\ u=6,\ x=2$. What is the bottleneck $\delta$ (minimum residual capacity of its arcs) of the augmenting path $1,\,3,\,4,\,2,\,5$ of the residual network $G(\vet{x})$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2$
  3. $3$
  4. $4$
Explanation. Residual capacities along the path: $r_{13}=4$, $r_{34}=2$ (backward, $=x_{43}$), $r_{42}=2$ (backward, $=x_{24}$), $r_{25}=7$; the minimum is $\delta=2$. A backward arc has $r_{ji}=x_{ij}$, not $u_{ij}$.
Example 2 · no-g-residual · level facile · notes
In the network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ ($s=1$, $t=5$) the arcs have capacity and flow $(1,3):\ u=4,\ x=1$; $(1,4):\ u=9,\ x=0$; $(1,5):\ u=6,\ x=1$; $(2,5):\ u=9,\ x=1$; $(3,4):\ u=4,\ x=1$; $(3,5):\ u=9,\ x=0$; $(4,2):\ u=3,\ x=1$. What is the bottleneck $\delta$ (minimum residual capacity of its arcs) of the augmenting path $1,\,4,\,3,\,5$ of the residual network $G(\vet{x})$?
  1. $1$
  2. $3$
  3. $4$
  4. $9$
Explanation. Residual capacities along the path: $r_{14}=9$, $r_{43}=1$ (backward, $=x_{34}$), $r_{35}=9$; the minimum is $\delta=1$. A backward arc has $r_{ji}=x_{ij}$, not $u_{ij}$.
Example 1 · no-g-max-flow · level media · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{13}=4$, $u_{14}=9$, $u_{16}=3$, $u_{26}=6$, $u_{34}=8$, $u_{35}=4$, $u_{36}=1$, $u_{52}=8$, $u_{56}=8$, $u_{64}=4$. What is the maximum flow value $\tilde{v}$, that is, the largest net outflow of $s$ over the feasible flows?
  1. $16$
  2. $7$
  3. $18$
  4. $8$
Explanation. A maximum flow has value $\tilde{v}=7$; the minimum cut $S=\{1,4\}$ (nodes reachable from $s$ in the final residual network) has the same capacity. The cuts $\{s\}$ and $N \setminus \{t\}$ give only upper bounds, and the saturated arcs do not form a cut in general.
Example 2 · no-g-max-flow · level media · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{12}=9$, $u_{14}=4$, $u_{15}=9$, $u_{26}=2$, $u_{31}=5$, $u_{36}=5$, $u_{43}=8$, $u_{46}=6$, $u_{54}=5$. What is the maximum flow value $\tilde{v}$, that is, the largest net outflow of $s$ over the feasible flows?
  1. $22$
  2. $13$
  3. $17$
  4. $11$
Explanation. A maximum flow has value $\tilde{v}=11$; the minimum cut $S=\{1,2,5\}$ (nodes reachable from $s$ in the final residual network) has the same capacity. The cuts $\{s\}$ and $N \setminus \{t\}$ give only upper bounds, and the saturated arcs do not form a cut in general.
Example 1 · no-g-edmonds-karp · level complessa · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{12}=3$, $u_{13}=1$, $u_{14}=2$, $u_{23}=3$, $u_{24}=3$, $u_{26}=7$, $u_{36}=2$, $u_{45}=6$, $u_{46}=6$. The Edmonds–Karp algorithm is run from the zero flow (breadth-first search of the residual network, residual arcs leaving each node scanned by increasing index of the head, stop as soon as $t$ is labelled). What are, in order, the bottlenecks $\delta$ of the augmentations?
  1. $\delta=2,\,1,\,1,\,2$
  2. $\delta=3,\,1,\,2$
  3. $\delta=2,\,1,\,3$
  4. $\delta=3,\,2,\,1$
Explanation. Edmonds–Karp augments along paths with the fewest arcs, found by the breadth-first search: $\delta=3,\,1,\,2$, value $\tilde{v}=6$. The sequence $2,\,1,\,1,\,2$ is that of Ford–Fulkerson with the depth-first search.
Example 2 · no-g-edmonds-karp · level complessa · notes
The network with nodes $1,\dots,6$, $s=1$ and $t=6$, has the arcs with capacities $u_{14}=4$, $u_{15}=5$, $u_{16}=1$, $u_{24}=4$, $u_{26}=6$, $u_{32}=4$, $u_{43}=8$, $u_{46}=8$, $u_{52}=2$, $u_{54}=6$. The Edmonds–Karp algorithm is run from the zero flow (breadth-first search of the residual network, residual arcs leaving each node scanned by increasing index of the head, stop as soon as $t$ is labelled). What are, in order, the bottlenecks $\delta$ of the augmentations?
  1. $\delta=4,\,2,\,2,\,1,\,1$
  2. $\delta=1,\,4,\,4,\,1$
  3. $\delta=1,\,5,\,3,\,1$
  4. $\delta=1,\,4,\,2,\,3$
Explanation. Edmonds–Karp augments along paths with the fewest arcs, found by the breadth-first search: $\delta=1,\,4,\,2,\,3$, value $\tilde{v}=10$. The sequence $4,\,2,\,2,\,1,\,1$ is that of Ford–Fulkerson with the depth-first search.
Example 1 · no-g-ford-fulkerson · level complessa · notes
The network with source $s=1$ and sink $t=6$ has the arcs with capacities $u_{13}=1$, $u_{15}=5$, $u_{16}=3$, $u_{24}=6$, $u_{35}=5$, $u_{36}=5$, $u_{56}=2$, $u_{62}=3$, $u_{64}=6$. Ford–Fulkerson starts from the zero flow and, at every iteration, looks for a path from $1$ to $6$ in the residual network (forward arcs with capacity $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, backward arcs with capacity $x_{ij}$) by depth-first search (the last node of the stack is examined, outgoing arcs by increasing head, stop as soon as $6$ is labelled) and augments by the bottleneck $\delta$. What is the sequence of the $\delta$?
  1. $3,\,1,\,2$
  2. $1,\,1,\,1,\,3$
  3. $3,\,2,\,1$
  4. $1,\,1,\,3$
Explanation. Sequence $1,\,1,\,1,\,3$, maximum value $6$. With breadth-first search (Edmonds–Karp) one gets $3,\,1,\,2$; without backward arcs the sequence is $1,\,1,\,3$ and the flow may not be maximum.
Example 2 · no-g-ford-fulkerson · level complessa · notes
The network with source $s=1$ and sink $t=6$ has the arcs with capacities $u_{12}=6$, $u_{14}=4$, $u_{15}=2$, $u_{16}=5$, $u_{25}=2$, $u_{36}=2$, $u_{43}=3$, $u_{46}=1$, $u_{54}=6$. Ford–Fulkerson starts from the zero flow and, at every iteration, looks for a path from $1$ to $6$ in the residual network (forward arcs with capacity $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, backward arcs with capacity $x_{ij}$) by depth-first search (the last node of the stack is examined, outgoing arcs by increasing head, stop as soon as $6$ is labelled) and augments by the bottleneck $\delta$. What is the sequence of the $\delta$?
  1. $2,\,1,\,5$
  2. $5,\,1,\,2$
  3. $5,\,1,\,1,\,1$
  4. $5,\,2,\,1$
Explanation. Sequence $2,\,1,\,5$, maximum value $8$. With breadth-first search (Edmonds–Karp) one gets $5,\,1,\,2$; without backward arcs the sequence is $2,\,1,\,5$ and the flow may not be maximum.

6. Minimum cost flow (3)

Example 1 · no-g-mcf-optimality · level media · notes
A flow $\vet{x}$ (zero lower bounds) and the potentials $\vet{\pi}=(11,7,7,0)$ are given on the arcs $(3,2):\ c=2,\ u=8,\ x=1$; $(2,4):\ c=10,\ u=2,\ x=0$; $(1,3):\ c=2,\ u=2,\ x=2$; $(2,3):\ c=3,\ u=7,\ x=0$; $(3,4):\ c=7,\ u=3,\ x=1$. With the reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, which arc violates the complementary slackness conditions ($\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=0$, $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$)?
  1. $(3,4)$
  2. $(2,3)$
  3. $(3,2)$
  4. $(2,4)$
Explanation. Reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$: $\bar{c}_{32}=2$, $\bar{c}_{24}=3$, $\bar{c}_{13}=-2$, $\bar{c}_{23}=3$, $\bar{c}_{34}=0$. Only $(3,2)$ has a nonzero reduced cost without sitting at the right bound.
Example 2 · no-g-mcf-optimality · level media · notes
A flow $\vet{x}$ (zero lower bounds) and the potentials $\vet{\pi}=(8,6,8,0)$ are given on the arcs $(3,2):\ c=2,\ u=8,\ x=4$; $(3,4):\ c=10,\ u=7,\ x=0$; $(2,3):\ c=1,\ u=6,\ x=6$; $(2,4):\ c=3,\ u=8,\ x=8$; $(1,3):\ c=2,\ u=8,\ x=0$. With the reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, which arc violates the complementary slackness conditions ($\bar{c}_{ij}>0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=0$, $\bar{c}_{ij}<0 \Rightarrow x_{ij}=u_{ij}$)?
  1. $(2,3)$
  2. $(3,4)$
  3. $(2,4)$
  4. $(3,2)$
Explanation. Reduced costs $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$: $\bar{c}_{32}=0$, $\bar{c}_{34}=2$, $\bar{c}_{23}=3$, $\bar{c}_{24}=-3$, $\bar{c}_{13}=2$. Only $(2,3)$ has a nonzero reduced cost without sitting at the right bound.
Example 1 · no-g-residual-cycle · level facile · notes
A feasible flow $\vet{x}$ costs $118$. On the arcs $(4,3):\ c=6,\ u=6,\ x=0$; $(5,3):\ c=7,\ u=4,\ x=4$; $(1,5):\ c=9,\ u=6,\ x=5$; $(4,1):\ c=3,\ u=3,\ x=2$ (zero lower bounds), the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ contains the cycle $W=4,\,3,\,5,\,1,\,4$. What does the flow augmented along $W$ by $\theta=r(W)$, the minimum residual capacity of the arcs of $W$, cost?
  1. $105$
  2. $168$
  3. $79$
  4. $92$
Explanation. Forward arcs have cost $c_{ij}$ and residual capacity $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, backward arcs cost $-c_{ij}$ and residual capacity $x_{ij}$: $c(W)=-13$, $r(W)=2$, new cost $118+2\cdot(-13)=92$.
Example 2 · no-g-residual-cycle · level facile · notes
A feasible flow $\vet{x}$ costs $120$. On the arcs $(4,5):\ c=8,\ u=6,\ x=1$; $(2,4):\ c=7,\ u=6,\ x=5$; $(1,2):\ c=1,\ u=6,\ x=5$; $(1,5):\ c=4,\ u=3,\ x=0$ (zero lower bounds), the residual network $G(\vet{x})$ contains the cycle $W=5,\,4,\,2,\,1,\,5$. What does the flow augmented along $W$ by $\theta=r(W)$, the minimum residual capacity of the arcs of $W$, cost?
  1. $140$
  2. $108$
  3. $84$
  4. $132$
Explanation. Forward arcs have cost $c_{ij}$ and residual capacity $u_{ij}-x_{ij}$, backward arcs cost $-c_{ij}$ and residual capacity $x_{ij}$: $c(W)=-12$, $r(W)=1$, new cost $120+1\cdot(-12)=108$.
Example 1 · no-g-ssp-cost · level media · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has balances $\vet{b}=(4,3,0,0,-7)$ (outflow minus inflow) and the arcs, with cost $c$ and capacity $u$ (zero lower bounds): $(1,2):\ c=5,\ u=4$; $(1,4):\ c=4,\ u=2$; $(1,5):\ c=3,\ u=2$; $(2,3):\ c=8,\ u=4$; $(2,4):\ c=2,\ u=4$; $(3,1):\ c=1,\ u=4$; $(3,5):\ c=5,\ u=4$; $(4,5):\ c=1,\ u=2$. The successive shortest path algorithm repeatedly sends flow from an excess node to a deficit node along a shortest path of the residual network (backward arcs included, with cost $-c_{ij}$). What does the final flow cost, that is, the minimum cost of a feasible flow?
  1. $61$
  2. $55$
  3. $21$
  4. $16$
Explanation. Successive shortest paths end with an optimal flow of cost $55$. Without backward arcs (no corrections) one gets $61$, ignoring capacities $21$.
Example 2 · no-g-ssp-cost · level media · notes
A network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has balances $\vet{b}=(4,0,0,3,-7)$ (outflow minus inflow) and the arcs, with cost $c$ and capacity $u$ (zero lower bounds): $(1,3):\ c=2,\ u=5$; $(1,4):\ c=5,\ u=4$; $(2,3):\ c=9,\ u=5$; $(2,5):\ c=9,\ u=2$; $(3,5):\ c=3,\ u=4$; $(4,2):\ c=7,\ u=3$; $(4,3):\ c=1,\ u=5$; $(4,5):\ c=1,\ u=2$. The successive shortest path algorithm repeatedly sends flow from an excess node to a deficit node along a shortest path of the residual network (backward arcs included, with cost $-c_{ij}$). What does the final flow cost, that is, the minimum cost of a feasible flow?
  1. $42$
  2. $23$
  3. $38$
  4. $20$
Explanation. Successive shortest paths end with an optimal flow of cost $38$. Without backward arcs (no corrections) one gets $42$, ignoring capacities $23$.

7. Transportation and assignment (6)

Example 1 · no-g-northwest · level facile · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,3$ with supplies $a=(20,25,35)$, destinations $4,\dots,7$ with demands $d=(5,20,10,45)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(9,3,4,1)$; row 2: $(2,5,7,6)$; row 3: $(5,3,1,6)$. What does the initial basic feasible solution built with the north-west corner rule cost?
  1. $250$
  2. $265$
  3. $455$
  4. $290$
Explanation. The north-west corner fills at every step the active cell with the smallest $i$ and $j$, with $\min\{\bar{a}_i,\bar{d}_j\}$, without looking at the costs: $x_{1,4}=5$, $x_{1,5}=15$, $x_{2,5}=5$, $x_{2,6}=10$, $x_{2,7}=10$, $x_{3,7}=35$, cost $455$.
Example 2 · no-g-northwest · level facile · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,3$ with supplies $a=(20,35,20)$, destinations $4,\dots,7$ with demands $d=(5,15,30,25)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(8,6,9,7)$; row 2: $(6,8,8,2)$; row 3: $(9,9,7,7)$. What does the initial basic feasible solution built with the north-west corner rule cost?
  1. $520$
  2. $395$
  3. $570$
  4. $415$
Explanation. The north-west corner fills at every step the active cell with the smallest $i$ and $j$, with $\min\{\bar{a}_i,\bar{d}_j\}$, without looking at the costs: $x_{1,4}=5$, $x_{1,5}=15$, $x_{2,5}=0$, $x_{2,6}=30$, $x_{2,7}=5$, $x_{3,7}=20$, cost $520$.
Example 1 · no-g-transport-potentials · level media · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,2$ with supplies $a=(10,35)$, destinations $3,\dots,5$ with demands $d=(20,20,5)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(8,1,8)$; row 2: $(2,3,1)$. The basis is that of the north-west corner, with the cells $(1,3)$, $(2,3)$, $(2,4)$, $(2,5)$. What are the potentials $(\pi_1,\dots,\pi_5)$ of the basis, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the basic cells and $\pi_5=0$?
  1. $(7,1,-1,-2,0)$
  2. $(7,1,1,2,0)$
  3. $(0,-6,-8,-9,-7)$
  4. $(-7,-1,1,2,0)$
Explanation. Start from $\pi_5=0$ and use $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$ along the tree of the basic cells: $\vet{\pi}=(7,1,-1,-2,0)$. With the u-v convention ($u_i+v_j=c_{ij}$) the potentials of the destinations change sign.
Example 2 · no-g-transport-potentials · level media · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,2$ with supplies $a=(20,35)$, destinations $3,\dots,5$ with demands $d=(10,5,40)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(4,8,8)$; row 2: $(6,9,7)$. The basis is that of the north-west corner, with the cells $(1,3)$, $(1,4)$, $(1,5)$, $(2,5)$. What are the potentials $(\pi_1,\dots,\pi_5)$ of the basis, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the basic cells and $\pi_5=0$?
  1. $(8,7,-4,0,0)$
  2. $(0,-1,-4,-8,-8)$
  3. $(8,7,4,0,0)$
  4. $(-8,-7,-4,0,0)$
Explanation. Start from $\pi_5=0$ and use $\pi_i-\pi_j=c_{ij}$ along the tree of the basic cells: $\vet{\pi}=(8,7,4,0,0)$. With the u-v convention ($u_i+v_j=c_{ij}$) the potentials of the destinations change sign.
Example 1 · no-g-transport-pivot · level complessa · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,3$ with supplies $a=(25,30,35)$, destinations $4,\dots,6$ with demands $d=(30,5,55)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(8,6,6)$; row 2: $(8,1,8)$; row 3: $(2,3,1)$. Start from the north-west corner solution (cost $440$) and perform one iteration of the potential method ($\pi_6=0$; the cell with the most negative reduced cost enters). What are the entering cell, the step $\theta$ and the new cost?
  1. enters $(1,6)$, $\theta=20$, new cost $400$
  2. enters $(1,6)$, $\theta=5$, new cost $430$
  3. enters $(1,6)$, $\theta=20$, new cost $480$
  4. enters $(3,4)$, $\theta=5$, new cost $445$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(8,8,1,0,7,0)$; the cell $(1,6)$ has $\bar{c}=-2$, the minimum. On its cycle $\theta$ is the minimum over the $-$ cells only: $\theta=20$, and the cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_e|$: $440-40=400$.
Example 2 · no-g-transport-pivot · level complessa · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,3$ with supplies $a=(40,40,30)$, destinations $4,\dots,6$ with demands $d=(25,10,75)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(6,5,1)$; row 2: $(2,3,3)$; row 3: $(1,6,7)$.   2. enters $(3,4)$, $\theta=25$, new cost $260$
  3. enters $(3,4)$, $\theta=25$, new cost $810$
  4. enters $(2,4)$, $\theta=25$, new cost $385$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(1,3,7,-5,-4,0)$; the cell $(3,4)$ has $\bar{c}=-11$, the minimum. On its cycle $\theta$ is the minimum over the $-$ cells only: $\theta=25$, and the cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_e|$: $535-275=260$.
Example 1 · no-g-assignment · level media · notes
The workers are $1,\dots,4$ and the jobs $5,\dots,8$; the cost matrix $c_{ij}$ has rows $(6,6,8,8)$, $(1,8,6,6)$, $(8,1,8,2)$, $(3,1,1,3)$. What does a minimum cost assignment cost?
  1. $16$
  2. $9$
  3. $10$
  4. $25$
Explanation. An optimal assignment is $(1,6)$, $(2,5)$, $(3,8)$, $(4,7)$, of cost $10$ (the Hungarian algorithm finds it). The greedy choice row by row costs $16$; the reduction of Step 0 gives only the lower bound $10$.
Example 2 · no-g-assignment · level media · notes

Example 1 · no-g-hungarian-bound · level facile · notes
The workers are $1,\dots,4$ and the jobs $5,\dots,8$; the cost matrix $c_{ij}$ has rows $(6,6,8,8)$, $(1,8,6,6)$, $(8,1,8,2)$, $(3,1,1,3)$. In Step 0 of the Hungarian algorithm each row is reduced by its minimum, then each column of the result by its minimum. What is the resulting dual value $\sum_{i}\pi_i-\sum_{j}\pi_j$, a lower bound on the cost of every assignment?
  1. $9$
  2. $14$
  3. $10$
  4. $5$
Explanation. Sum of the row minima ($9$) plus sum of the column minima of the row-reduced matrix: $10$. The column minima are taken after the row reduction.
Example 2 · no-g-hungarian-bound · level facile · notes

Example 1 · no-g-min-cost-rule · level facile · notes
A transportation instance has origins $1,\dots,3$ with supplies $a=(20,25,35)$, destinations $4,\dots,7$ with demands $d=(5,20,10,45)$, and costs $c_{ij}$ (rows = origins, columns = destinations) row 1: $(9,3,4,1)$; row 2: $(2,5,7,6)$; row 3: $(5,3,1,6)$. The minimum cost rule fills at every step, with the largest possible amount, the cheapest cell among the active rows and columns (ties: smallest row, then smallest column), and crosses out the exhausted row or column. What does the initial solution it produces cost?
  1. $455$
  2. $290$
  3. $250$
  4. $265$

8. The network simplex (5)

Example 1 · no-g-tree-solution · level facile · notes
An uncapacitated network has nodes $1,\dots,5$ and balances $\vet{b}=(-1,-3,-4,-2,10)$. The tree $T$ has the arcs $(2,1)$, $(4,2)$, $(4,5)$, $(5,3)$ (root $5$), and the arcs outside the tree are in $L$ with flow $0$. What are the flows on the arcs of $T$, in the given order?
  1. $(-1,-4,6,-4)$
  2. $(-1,-4,-6,-4)$
  3. $(1,4,-6,4)$
  4. $(1,3,-2,4)$
Explanation. Repeatedly remove a leaf $\ell \neq 5$: $x_a=\hat{b}_\ell$ if the arc leaves $\ell$, $x_a=-\hat{b}_\ell$ if it enters $\ell$, and $\hat{b}_\ell$ is added to the balance of the neighbour. Result $(1,4,-6,4)$ (the structure is not feasible).
Example 2 · no-g-tree-solution · level facile · notes
An uncapacitated network has nodes $1,\dots,5$ and balances $\vet{b}=(0,2,4,2,-8)$. The tree $T$ has the arcs $(4,1)$, $(5,1)$, $(5,2)$, $(5,3)$ (root $5$), and the arcs outside the tree are in $L$ with flow $0$. What are the flows on the arcs of $T$, in the given order?
  1. $(2,-2,-2,-4)$
  2. $(-2,2,2,4)$
  3. $(2,2,2,4)$
  4. $(2,0,-2,-4)$
Explanation. Repeatedly remove a leaf $\ell \neq 5$: $x_a=\hat{b}_\ell$ if the arc leaves $\ell$, $x_a=-\hat{b}_\ell$ if it enters $\ell$, and $\hat{b}_\ell$ is added to the balance of the neighbour. Result $(2,-2,-2,-4)$ (the structure is not feasible).
Example 1 · no-g-tree-potentials · level facile · notes
The spanning tree $T$ of a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{21}=3$, $c_{42}=1$, $c_{45}=1$, $c_{53}=3$. What are the potentials $\vet{\pi}$ of the tree, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$ and root $\pi_5=0$?
  1. $(3,0,3,-1,0)$
  2. $(0,3,0,4,3)$
  3. $(-3,0,-3,1,0)$
  4. $(5,2,3,1,0)$
Explanation. $\pi_i$ is the cost of the tree path from $i$ to the root, with $+c_{ij}$ on the arcs traversed from tail to head and $-c_{ij}$ on the others: $\vet{\pi}=(-3,0,-3,1,0)$.
Example 2 · no-g-tree-potentials · level facile · notes
The spanning tree $T$ of a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{41}=3$, $c_{51}=5$, $c_{52}=8$, $c_{53}=7$. What are the potentials $\vet{\pi}$ of the tree, with $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$ and root $\pi_5=0$?
  1. $(-5,-8,-7,-2,0)$
  2. $(5,8,7,2,0)$
  3. $(0,-3,-2,3,5)$
  4. $(5,8,7,8,0)$
Explanation. $\pi_i$ is the cost of the tree path from $i$ to the root, with $+c_{ij}$ on the arcs traversed from tail to head and $-c_{ij}$ on the others: $\vet{\pi}=(-5,-8,-7,-2,0)$.
Example 1 · no-g-network-simplex-pivot · level complessa · notes
An uncapacitated network has nodes $1,\dots,5$, balances $\vet{b}=(-5,-4,10,-4,3)$ and the arcs with costs $c_{12}=1$, $c_{14}=4$, $c_{23}=2$, $c_{24}=1$, $c_{31}=2$, $c_{34}=8$, $c_{51}=2$, $c_{52}=4$. The current tree is $T=\{(1,2),(3,1),(3,4),(5,1)\}$ (root $5$, the other arcs in $L$), with a tree solution of cost $54$. Perform one pivot of the network simplex (potentials with $\pi_5=0$, $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$; among the arcs of $L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, the one with the largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$ enters). What are the entering arc, the leaving arc and the new cost?
  1. enters $(2,4)$, leaves $(1,2)$, cost $70$
  2. enters $(2,4)$, leaves $(3,4)$, cost $38$
  3. enters $(2,4)$, leaves $(3,4)$, cost $70$
  4. enters $(1,4)$, leaves $(3,4)$, cost $46$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(-2,-3,0,-8,0)$; $(2,4)$ enters with $\bar{c}=-4$. The cycle, oriented as the entering arc, is blocked only by backward arcs: $\theta=4$, $(3,4)$ leaves, and the cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|$ to $38$.
Example 2 · no-g-network-simplex-pivot · level complessa · notes
An uncapacitated network has nodes $1,\dots,5$, balances $\vet{b}=(7,1,3,-4,-7)$ and the arcs with costs $c_{14}=9$, $c_{15}=7$, $c_{24}=7$, $c_{25}=7$, $c_{31}=4$, $c_{32}=9$, $c_{34}=7$, $c_{54}=7$. The current tree is $T=\{(1,4),(1,5),(2,5),(3,2)\}$ (root $5$, the other arcs in $L$), with a tree solution of cost $112$. Perform one pivot of the network simplex (potentials with $\pi_5=0$, $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$; among the arcs of $L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, the one with the largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$ enters). What are the entering arc, the leaving arc and the new cost?
  1. enters $(3,4)$, leaves $(1,5)$, cost $79$
  2. enters $(3,4)$, leaves $(1,5)$, cost $145$
  3. enters $(3,4)$, leaves $(3,2)$, cost $145$
  4. enters $(3,4)$, leaves $(3,2)$, cost $79$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(7,7,16,-2,0)$; $(3,4)$ enters with $\bar{c}=-11$. The cycle, oriented as the entering arc, is blocked only by backward arcs: $\theta=3$, $(3,2)$ leaves, and the cost drops by $\theta\,|\bar{c}_{kl}|$ to $79$.
Example 1 · no-g-ns-reduced · level facile · notes
In the network simplex, the spanning tree $T$ of a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{12}=7$, $c_{32}=8$, $c_{35}=5$, $c_{43}=9$. The tree potentials have $\pi_5=0$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$. What is the reduced cost of the nonbasic arc $(4,5)$, whose cost is $c_{45}=1$?
  1. $15$
  2. $-13$
  3. $13$
  4. $1$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(4,-3,5,14,0)$ (cost of the tree path to the root); hence $\bar{c}_{45}=1-(14)+(0)=-13$, the cost of the arc's fundamental cycle.
Example 2 · no-g-ns-reduced · level facile · notes
In the network simplex, the spanning tree $T$ of a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{21}=9$, $c_{25}=5$, $c_{43}=7$, $c_{54}=7$. The tree potentials have $\pi_5=0$ and $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j=0$ on the arcs of $T$. What is the reduced cost of the nonbasic arc $(4,1)$, whose cost is $c_{41}=2$?
  1. $-1$
  2. $-5$
  3. $5$
  4. $13$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(-4,5,-14,-7,0)$ (cost of the tree path to the root); hence $\bar{c}_{41}=2-(-7)+(-4)=5$, the cost of the arc's fundamental cycle.
Example 1 · no-g-ns-entering · level media · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow, a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{21}=3$, $c_{23}=7$, $c_{31}=4$, $c_{41}=6$, $c_{42}=1$, $c_{43}=6$, $c_{45}=1$, $c_{52}=4$, $c_{53}=3$. The current tree structure has $T=\{(2,1),(4,2),(4,5),(5,3)\}$ (root $5$, $\pi_5=0$), $L=\{(2,3),(3,1)\}$ (nonbasic at flow $0$) and $U=\{(4,1),(4,3),(5,2)\}$ (nonbasic at capacity). With $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, the eligible arc ($L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, or $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$) with the largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$ enters. Which arc enters?
  1. $(5,2)$
  2. $(2,3)$
  3. $(3,1)$
  4. $(4,1)$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(-3,0,-3,1,0)$; nonbasic reduced costs: $\bar{c}_{23}=4$, $\bar{c}_{31}=4$, $\bar{c}_{41}=2$, $\bar{c}_{43}=2$, $\bar{c}_{52}=4$. $(5,2)$ enters. An arc of $U$ with $\bar{c}<0$ or of $L$ with $\bar{c}>0$ is already in its best position.
Example 2 · no-g-ns-entering · level media · notes
In the network simplex for minimum cost flow, a network with nodes $1,\dots,5$ has the arcs with costs $c_{13}=8$, $c_{15}=8$, $c_{21}=2$, $c_{24}=5$, $c_{32}=4$, $c_{34}=3$, $c_{35}=9$, $c_{41}=9$, $c_{54}=1$. The current tree structure has $T=\{(1,3),(2,1),(3,5),(4,1)\}$ (root $5$, $\pi_5=0$), $L=\{(1,5),(3,2),(3,4),(5,4)\}$ (nonbasic at flow $0$) and $U=\{(2,4)\}$ (nonbasic at capacity). With $\bar{c}_{ij}=c_{ij}-\pi_i+\pi_j$, the eligible arc ($L$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}<0$, or $U$ with $\bar{c}_{ij}>0$) with the largest $|\bar{c}_{ij}|$ enters. Which arc enters?
  1. $(5,4)$
  2. $(3,4)$
  3. $(2,4)$
  4. $(3,2)$
Explanation. Potentials $\vet{\pi}=(17,19,9,26,0)$; nonbasic reduced costs: $\bar{c}_{15}=-9$, $\bar{c}_{24}=12$, $\bar{c}_{32}=14$, $\bar{c}_{34}=20$, $\bar{c}_{54}=27$. $(2,4)$ enters. An arc of $U$ with $\bar{c}<0$ or of $L$ with $\bar{c}>0$ is already in its best position.

9. Project management (7)

Example 1 · no-g-project-duration · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). An activity starts when all its immediate predecessors are finished. What is the minimum project duration $T$, that is, the earliest time by which all the activities can be finished?
  1. $29$
  2. $14$
  3. $23$
  4. $43$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_j=\max \mathit{EF}_i$ over the immediate predecessors, $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$; $T=\mathit{ES}_{\mathrm{FINISH}}=29$, the length of the critical path $\text{START},2,3,5,6,7,\text{FINISH}$. With the minimum instead of the maximum one gets $14$.
Example 2 · no-g-project-duration · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=2$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=7$; predecessors: $1$), $3$ ($d_{3}=7$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=5$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=7$; predecessors: $3,4$), $6$ ($d_{6}=3$; predecessors: $2,4,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=3$; predecessors: $4,5$). An activity starts when all its immediate predecessors are finished. What is the minimum project duration $T$, that is, the earliest time by which all the activities can be finished?
  1. $8$
  2. $26$
  3. $23$
  4. $34$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_j=\max \mathit{EF}_i$ over the immediate predecessors, $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$; $T=\mathit{ES}_{\mathrm{FINISH}}=26$, the length of the critical path $\text{START},1,2,3,5,6,\text{FINISH}$. With the minimum instead of the maximum one gets $8$.
Example 1 · no-g-critical-path · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). In the project network (nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), which is the critical path, that is, the path from START to FINISH of maximum length (sum of the durations)?
  1. $\text{START},2,3,5,6,7,\text{FINISH}$
  2. $\text{START},2,3,4,\text{FINISH}$
  3. $\text{START},1,5,6,7,\text{FINISH}$
  4. $\text{START},2,3,5,7,\text{FINISH}$
Explanation. A critical path is a path from START to FINISH of maximum length (sum of the durations of its activities): here it is unique, of length $T=29$; all its arcs are tight, $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{EF}_i$.
Example 2 · no-g-critical-path · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=4$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=8$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=3$; predecessors: —), $4$ ($d_{4}=1$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=4$; predecessors: $1$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=8$; predecessors: $1,4$). In the project network (nodes START and FINISH of duration $0$), which is the critical path, that is, the path from START to FINISH of maximum length (sum of the durations)?
  1. $\text{START},1,7,\text{FINISH}$
  2. $\text{START},1,5,6,\text{FINISH}$
  3. $\text{START},4,7,\text{FINISH}$
  4. $\text{START},2,\text{FINISH}$
Explanation. A critical path is a path from START to FINISH of maximum length (sum of the durations of its activities): here it is unique, of length $T=16$; all its arcs are tight, $\mathit{ES}_j=\mathit{EF}_i$.
Example 1 · no-g-slacks · level complessa · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=9$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=4$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=4$; predecessors: $1$), $4$ ($d_{4}=5$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=7$; predecessors: $3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=5$; predecessors: $4,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=7$; predecessors: —), $8$ ($d_{8}=9$; predecessors: $3,7$). The total slack $\mathit{TS}_{2}=\mathit{LS}_{2}-\mathit{ES}_{2}$ is the largest delay of activity $2$ that does not lengthen the project; the free slack $\mathit{FS}_{2}$ is the largest delay that moves the earliest start of no successor. What are they?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_{2}=9,\ \mathit{FS}_{2}=11$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_{2}=11,\ \mathit{FS}_{2}=11$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_{2}=11,\ \mathit{FS}_{2}=9$
  4. $\mathit{TS}_{2}=11,\ \mathit{FS}_{2}=0$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_{2}=0$, $\mathit{EF}_{2}=4$, $T=25$; backward pass ($\mathit{LF}_j=\min \mathit{LS}_k$): $\mathit{LS}_{2}=11$. Hence $\mathit{TS}_{2}=\mathit{LS}_{2}-\mathit{ES}_{2}=11$ and $\mathit{FS}_{2}=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_{2}=9$.
Example 2 · no-g-slacks · level complessa · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=2$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=9$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: —), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: —), $7$ ($d_{7}=4$; predecessors: $5$), $8$ ($d_{8}=2$; predecessors: $1,7$). The total slack $\mathit{TS}_{1}=\mathit{LS}_{1}-\mathit{ES}_{1}$ is the largest delay of activity $1$ that does not lengthen the project; the free slack $\mathit{FS}_{1}$ is the largest delay that moves the earliest start of no successor. What are they?
  1. $\mathit{TS}_{1}=0,\ \mathit{FS}_{1}=8$
  2. $\mathit{TS}_{1}=8,\ \mathit{FS}_{1}=8$
  3. $\mathit{TS}_{1}=8,\ \mathit{FS}_{1}=0$
  4. $\mathit{TS}_{1}=13,\ \mathit{FS}_{1}=0$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_{1}=0$, $\mathit{EF}_{1}=2$, $T=17$; backward pass ($\mathit{LF}_j=\min \mathit{LS}_k$): $\mathit{LS}_{1}=8$. Hence $\mathit{TS}_{1}=\mathit{LS}_{1}-\mathit{ES}_{1}=8$ and $\mathit{FS}_{1}=\min_k \mathit{ES}_k-\mathit{EF}_{1}=0$.
Example 1 · no-g-pm-earliest · level facile · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). An activity can start only when all its immediate predecessors are finished, and the project starts at time $0$. What is the earliest start $\mathit{ES}_{4}$ of activity $4$?
  1. $6$
  2. $22$
  3. $14$
  4. $21$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_j=\max \mathit{EF}_i$ over the immediate predecessors, $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$. For activity $4$: $\mathit{ES}_{4}=\max\{6,14\}=14$. With the minimum one would get $6$.
Example 2 · no-g-pm-earliest · level facile · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=4$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=8$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=3$; predecessors: —), $4$ ($d_{4}=1$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=4$; predecessors: $1$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=8$; predecessors: $1,4$). An activity can start only when all its immediate predecessors are finished, and the project starts at time $0$. What is the earliest start $\mathit{ES}_{7}$ of activity $7$?
  1. $1$
  2. $12$
  3. $4$
  4. $8$
Explanation. Forward pass: $\mathit{ES}_j=\max \mathit{EF}_i$ over the immediate predecessors, $\mathit{EF}_j=\mathit{ES}_j+d_j$. For activity $7$: $\mathit{ES}_{7}=\max\{4,1\}=4$. With the minimum one would get $1$.
Example 1 · no-g-pm-latest · level facile · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). The project must end within its minimum duration $T$. What is the latest start $\mathit{LS}_{1}$ of activity $1$, that is, the last time at which it can start without delaying the project?
  1. $0$
  2. $14$
  3. $8$
  4. $16$
Explanation. $T=29$. Backward pass: $\mathit{LF}_j=\min \mathit{LS}_k$ over the immediate successors, $\mathit{LS}_j=\mathit{LF}_j-d_j$: $\mathit{LF}_{1}=\min\{14,15\}=14$ and $\mathit{LS}_{1}=14-6=8$. With the maximum one would get $16$.
Example 2 · no-g-pm-latest · level facile · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=2$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=7$; predecessors: $1$), $3$ ($d_{3}=7$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=5$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=7$; predecessors: $3,4$), $6$ ($d_{6}=3$; predecessors: $2,4,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=3$; predecessors: $4,5$). The project must end within its minimum duration $T$. What is the latest start $\mathit{LS}_{4}$ of activity $4$, that is, the last time at which it can start without delaying the project?
  1. $0$
  2. $16$
  3. $18$
  4. $11$
Explanation. $T=26$. Backward pass: $\mathit{LF}_j=\min \mathit{LS}_k$ over the immediate successors, $\mathit{LS}_j=\mathit{LF}_j-d_j$: $\mathit{LF}_{4}=\min\{16,23,23\}=16$ and $\mathit{LS}_{4}=16-5=11$. With the maximum one would get $18$.
Example 1 · no-g-pm-change · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). The duration of activity $1$ grows by $9$ (from $6$ to $15$). What is the new minimum duration of the project?
  1. $38$
  2. $29$
  3. $30$
  4. $37$
Explanation. Before the change $T=29$ and the total slack of the activity is $\mathit{TS}_{1}=8$. The new duration is $T+\max\{0,\delta-\mathit{TS}_{1}\}=29+1=30$: the delay is absorbed up to the total slack (not the free slack, $\mathit{FS}_{1}=8$).
Example 2 · no-g-pm-change · level media · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=4$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=8$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=3$; predecessors: —), $4$ ($d_{4}=1$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=4$; predecessors: $1$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=8$; predecessors: $1,4$). The duration of activity $3$ grows by $14$ (from $3$ to $17$). What is the new minimum duration of the project?
  1. $30$
  2. $16$
  3. $29$
  4. $17$
Explanation. Before the change $T=16$ and the total slack of the activity is $\mathit{TS}_{3}=13$. The new duration is $T+\max\{0,\delta-\mathit{TS}_{3}\}=16+1=17$: the delay is absorbed up to the total slack (not the free slack, $\mathit{FS}_{3}=13$).
Example 1 · no-g-pm-two-changes · level complessa · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=6$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=6$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=8$; predecessors: $2$), $4$ ($d_{4}=8$; predecessors: $2,3$), $5$ ($d_{5}=1$; predecessors: $1,2,3$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $1,5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=6$; predecessors: $5,6$). At the same time the duration of activity $1$ grows by $9$ and that of activity $6$ decreases by $5$. What is the new minimum duration of the project?
  1. $33$
  2. $25$
  3. $24$
  4. $30$
Explanation. Before, $T=29$, with $\mathit{TS}_{1}=8$ and activity $6$ critical. The two changes interact, so the forward pass is redone with the new durations: the minimum duration is the maximum length of a path, $25$. Adding the separate effects is not enough.
Example 2 · no-g-pm-two-changes · level complessa · notes
A project has the activities (duration; immediate predecessors) $1$ ($d_{1}=4$; predecessors: —), $2$ ($d_{2}=8$; predecessors: —), $3$ ($d_{3}=3$; predecessors: —), $4$ ($d_{4}=1$; predecessors: —), $5$ ($d_{5}=4$; predecessors: $1$), $6$ ($d_{6}=8$; predecessors: $5$), $7$ ($d_{7}=8$; predecessors: $1,4$). At the same time the duration of activity $3$ grows by $14$ and that of activity $5$ decreases by $3$. What is the new minimum duration of the project?
  1. $17$
  2. $27$
  3. $13$
  4. $14$
Explanation. Before, $T=16$, with $\mathit{TS}_{3}=13$ and activity $5$ critical. The two changes interact, so the forward pass is redone with the new durations: the minimum duration is the maximum length of a path, $17$. Adding the separate effects is not enough.
